===== ICLE-US-IND-0001.1.txt =====
Feedback Report: Exxon Valdez Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an important topic with genuine emotional conviction, and your personal connection to the event gives the writing authenticity. However, the essay currently reads more as a personal narrative and emotional response than as an *argumentative* essay. To strengthen it academically, you need a clearer thesis, more structured argumentation, and stronger evidence to support your claims. Below are specific areas for revision.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your opening line—"Money is the root of all evil"—is a strong hook, but it's a very broad philosophical claim that the rest of the essay doesn't directly return to or prove. What is your actual argument? Is it:
That corporations should be held legally/financially accountable for environmental damage?
That profit motives inherently lead to negligence?
That regulatory oversight of big business needs to be strengthened?

Choose one clear claim and state it explicitly in your introduction. Right now, the essay drifts between "I hate Exxon," "here is what happened," and "Exxon should pay more"—these need to be unified under one thesis statement that you then defend systematically.

Structure and Organization

The essay currently follows a mostly chronological/narrative structure (what happened, how I felt, what happened next). For an argumentative essay, consider organizing by points of argument instead:
Paragraph 1: Introduction + thesis (What should happen and why)
Paragraph 2: Evidence of corporate negligence (facts, not just feelings)
Paragraph 3: Evidence of inadequate response/accountability
Paragraph 4: Counterargument (what would Exxon or defenders of big business say?) + your rebuttal
Paragraph 5: Your proposed solution + conclusion

Adding a counterargument is especially important for academic argumentation—right now the essay only presents one side. Even briefly acknowledging "Exxon might argue that no company could have prevented human error" and then refuting it would make your argument more persuasive and scholarly.

Evidence and Specificity

Your strongest asset is your first-hand emotional memory, but for an *argumentative* essay, you need more factual, verifiable evidence:
You mention "Exxon's notes and court transcriptions" but don't cite or quote anything specific. What did these documents actually say?
The bracketed  suggests you intended to insert a quotation from Exxon spokespersons but never did—please fill this in with real or illustrative content.
Consider adding statistics: cost of cleanup, settlement amount ($900 million+), number of animals killed, current oil residue findings, etc
Citing at least 2–3 outside sources (news articles, court records, environmental studies) would significantly strengthen your credibility.

Emotional Language vs. Academic Tone

Phrases like "I loathed the corporation," "carnage was unbelievable," and "rape our land" are powerful but too emotionally charged for academic writing. Consider:
Keeping *one or two* personal emotional details (eg, "I remember watching the news that evening...") to establish your personal stake, but then shifting to analytical, evidence-based language for the body of the essay.
Replacing "rape our land" with more precise, professional phrasing such as "irreversibly damage our natural resources."

Sentence-Level and Grammar Issues

A few recurring issues to address:
Run-on/awkward sentences: "I will take the side of the everyday person like myself and the issues of big business" — this sentence is unclear; revise to state your position directly (eg, "In this essay, I will argue that ordinary citizens must hold large corporations accountable for environmental harm.")
Article usage: "ran aground the Exxon" should be "the Exxon Valdez ran aground."
Verb tense consistency: You shift between past and present tense when discussing ongoing effects ("still felt," "still is") — this is fine when discussing present consequences, but double-check consistency within paragraphs.
Word choice: "monies" should be "funds" or "money" (uncountable noun, no plural).
Missing citation placeholder (): Please complete this before submission.

Conclusion

Your conclusion introduces new ideas (funding research, buying land for sanctuaries) that would be better developed as full points in the body of the essay, with brief evidence/reasoning, rather than introduced only at the end. Save the conclusion for restating your thesis and summarizing your argument's implications.

Summary of Priority Revisions
Write one clear, specific thesis statement.
Reorganize by argument, not by chronology.
Add a counterargument and rebuttal.
Insert factual evidence/citations (fill in the  gap).
Tone down emotional language in body paragraphs.
Proofread for grammar and word choice.

You have compelling personal motivation for this topic—now channel that into a tightly argued, evidence-based essay. Good luck with your revision!

===== ICLE-US-IND-0002.1.txt =====
Feedback Report: "Crime Does Not Pay"

Overall Impression

Your essay has a clear moral argument and a personal, illustrative example that makes the abstract claim ("crime does not pay") concrete and memorable. This is a real strength—using a specific case study to support a general thesis is an effective argumentative strategy. However, the essay would benefit from clearer organization, stronger analytical development, and more careful attention to grammar and word choice. Below are specific suggestions organized by category.

Thesis and Structure

Your thesis—that people who choose crime to get rich quickly ultimately suffer negative consequences—is implied but never stated as a single, clear sentence. Consider adding an explicit thesis statement at the end of your introduction, something like: *"Although illegal shortcuts to wealth may seem tempting, this essay argues that crime ultimately destroys both the individual and the people who depend on him."*

The essay also lacks clear paragraph transitions. Right now you have three paragraphs: (1) general claims about why people turn to crime, (2) the extended narrative example, (3) a brief conclusion. Consider adding a transition sentence at the start of paragraph two that explicitly connects the general claim to the specific example (eg, "To illustrate this pattern, consider the case of a man I know personally...").

Development of the Argument

Right now, the essay relies almost entirely on one anecdote. This is a good start, but a stronger argumentative essay would:
Briefly acknowledge a counterargument (eg, "Some might argue that in a system where legal paths to wealth are unfair, illegal shortcuts are justified—but...")
Draw a general lesson more explicitly from the anecdote, explaining *why* crime doesn't pay (eg, loss of trust, legal punishment, psychological/family cost) rather than simply restating that it doesn't.
Consider adding a second, shorter example or a statistic to show the pattern is not isolated to one person.

Clarity and Logic

Some sentences make claims that are not fully supported or are slightly contradictory. For example, you say the man "had a good life" and "made good money," which raises the question—why would such a person turn to crime? You gesture at "greed," but this could be developed further. Try adding a sentence analyzing his motivation (eg, was it social pressure, dissatisfaction, ambition?) to strengthen your causal argument.

Grammar, Word Choice, and Mechanics

Here are recurring issues to review, with corrected examples:

Article/possessive errors: "societies rules" → *society's rules*
Spelling: "catigory" → *category*; "beleive" → *believe*
Word confusion: "find it expectable" → *find it acceptable* (or *unremarkable*); "not being will to go about" → *not being willing to go about*
Verb tense/agreement: "His family way very proud" → *His family was very proud*; "he did" (referring to getting rich) is fine, but check tense consistency throughout the narrative—you shift between past simple and past perfect somewhat inconsistently.
Sentence fragments/run-ons: "The one day they saw..." → *One day, they saw...* Watch for missing commas after introductory phrases (eg, "Right after, they received a call" is good, but "The bottom line is that money and riches are hard to come by" could be smoother as *"In short, wealth is hard to come by."*
Redundancy: "riches and money" and "money and riches" appear multiple times—vary your vocabulary (eg, *wealth, prosperity, financial success*).
Awkward phrasing: "see and easier way" → *see an easier way*

I recommend reading your essay aloud, or using a grammar-checking tool, to catch these smaller errors before your next draft.

Style and Tone

Your writing has a genuine, conversational voice, which is good for engaging the reader. However, academic essays typically use a slightly more formal register. Phrases like "The bottom line is" are fine occasionally but overused here (it appears three times). Try varying your concluding phrases: *"In essence," "Ultimately," "This case demonstrates that..."*

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Write one clear thesis sentence.
Add a transition connecting your general claims to your example.
Deepen your analysis of *why* crime fails as a strategy for wealth, not just *that* it does.
Address a brief counterargument for balance.
Proofread carefully for spelling, verb tense, and article usage.

Your instinct to use a real, personal example is strong—with tighter structure and cleaner language, this essay could be quite persuasive. Keep developing your analytical voice alongside your storytelling ability.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0003.1.txt =====
Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You're grappling with a genuinely sophisticated idea: that our stated belief in "equal pay for equal work" is undermined by unconscious cultural hierarchies of value, and that child care work exposes this contradiction well. This is a strong conceptual foundation. However, the essay needs revision in three main areas: establishing a clear thesis, strengthening logical transitions between claims, and polishing language for precision.

Thesis and Structure

The placeholder  in your opening sentence suggests you may not have finalized your thesis statement, or it was lost in editing—but as it stands, a reader cannot identify the specific claim you are testing. Your introduction should state explicitly: (a) the popular belief you're examining, and (b) your counter-argument about "deeper cultural assumptions." Right now the reader must infer your thesis from context; make it explicit in one clear sentence at the end of your first paragraph.

Your essay has a clear shape—general claim → example (child care) → complication → economic argument → paradoxical conclusion—but the connections between these steps are sometimes implicit when they need to be explicit. For instance, the transition from "Americans profess to value children" to "child care work is not seen as valuable" is a real logical leap. Consider adding a sentence that explicitly names the contradiction: *"This creates a paradox: we claim to value children, but we do not extend that value to the people who care for them."*

The Concluding Paradox

Your final paragraph contains your most interesting idea—a circular trap where child care workers won't be seen as valuable until they're paid well, but won't be paid well until they're seen as valuable. This is a compelling point, but it appears suddenly, compressed into one dense sentence at the very end. Since this circularity is essentially your essay's payoff, give it more room. Spell out the loop step by step: (1) status depends on pay, (2) pay depends on status, (3) therefore no single intervention breaks the cycle. This deserves its own paragraph, not a hurried closing line.

Paragraph Development

Your second paragraph does good work but could be tightened. The sentence "If we consider that our society rewards valuable labor... then the child care worker may reasonably be judged as not providing a 'valuable' contribution" risks sounding like you agree with this judgment. Since your essay is *critiquing* this logic, clarify with a phrase like: *"By this flawed logic, the child care worker would be judged..."* This protects you from being misread as endorsing a view you're actually challenging.

The third paragraph makes a strong economic argument (child care enables workforce participation, especially for women and men with family duties), but it moves quickly through several claims—diversity of the workforce, gender participation, national economic strength—without fully connecting them. Consider breaking this into two paragraphs: one on the workforce-participation argument, another on the underpayment problem and its implications.

Language and Grammar Notes

A few recurring issues to address in revision:

Word choice/idiom: "profess to the belief" should be "profess a belief" or "profess belief in." "Profess" already implies verbal claiming, so the "to" is unnecessary.
Spelling: "pereniel" → "perennial."
Agreement/typo: "as well as man with family responsibilities" → "as well as men with family responsibilities."
Awkward phrasing: "Americans have not reached a level of conscious acknowledgment that labor performed in the child care industry is valuable labor" is wordy. Consider: "Americans have not consciously acknowledged that child care work is valuable labor."
Repetition: "value" and "valuable" appear many times in close proximity (paragraph 2 especially). Vary your vocabulary: consider "worth," "significance," "merit," or "contribution" as alternatives in some instances.
Vague pronoun reference: "the issue will be difficult to resolve without involving employers or the government" — clarify *which* issue (underpayment) explicitly, since several issues have been discussed by this point.

Suggested Next Steps

Write an explicit one-sentence thesis for your introduction.
Add a transitional sentence after your child care example explicitly naming the contradiction between professed values and economic reward.
Expand your final paragraph's circular-logic insight into its own developed paragraph.
Proofread for the grammar/spelling issues noted above, and vary repeated vocabulary.

Your ideas here are genuinely strong—this revision should focus on making your logical connections visible to the reader rather than assumed. You have the argumentative instinct; now it needs a clearer scaffold.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0004.1.txt =====
Feedback on "The Value of Home-Making Labor"

Overall Impression

You've chosen a compelling and socially relevant topic, and your central claim—that unpaid domestic labor deserves the same recognition as marketplace labor—is thought-provoking and well worth arguing. Your use of rhetorical questions ("But what is 'equal work'?") is effective in guiding readers through your reasoning. However, the essay would benefit from tighter organization, stronger evidence, and a more fully developed conclusion. Below are suggestions organized from global (argument/structure) to local (sentence-level) concerns.

Argument and Structure

Sharpen your thesis statement. Your introduction builds nicely toward a claim, but the final sentence of paragraph 1 is long and slightly buried: "some sort of compensation should be made for 'home-making service' as well as for he or she who works outside the home." Consider stating this more directly and prominently, perhaps as a separate, shorter sentence, so readers know exactly what you will argue.

Clarify the purpose of paragraph 2. This paragraph discusses "equal pay for equal work" in traditional careers, but it isn't yet clear why you're establishing this point before turning to homemaking. Consider adding a transitional sentence explaining that you are first establishing how we *currently* measure worthy contributions before showing why homemaking doesn't fit this model.

Support your strongest claim with evidence. In paragraph 3, you assert that "many of the ills of society in the U.S. today have been traced by study after study to the lack of firm guidance and support from the family." This is a powerful claim, but without a citation or specific example, it risks appearing unsubstantiated. Even one concrete reference (a study, statistic, or named expert) would considerably strengthen your credibility here.

Develop your proposed solution more fully. Your conclusion offers an interesting idea—paying a joint "family paycheck"—but it raises questions you don't address:
How does this help *unmarried* homemakers or single parents, whom you mention earlier as also performing unpaid labor?
Where does the additional money come from? Is this simply relabeling an existing paycheck as "joint," or are you proposing new compensation?

Since this solution is central to your argument, it deserves at least a full paragraph of elaboration and should acknowledge its limitations. Right now, the essay ends somewhat tentatively ("Perhaps this is a solution worth its salt"), which undercuts the confidence of your earlier reasoning. Consider a stronger closing that either commits more firmly to this solution or explicitly frames it as one possibility among several, inviting further societal debate.

Reconsider the "Indian chiefs" reference. This phrase (from the children's rhyme "doctor, lawyer, Indian chief") feels stylistically out of place in an academic essay and may seem reductive. Consider replacing it with another profession or omitting it.

Sentence-Level and Grammar Issues

A few recurring errors to address in revision:

Article usage: "how do we compare raising a family... to the job of an neurosurgeon" → should be "a neurosurgeon." Since English articles (a/an/the) don't have direct equivalents in Chinese, it's worth doing a careful final pass specifically checking a/an/the throughout the essay.

Subject-verb agreement/typo: "this contribution to society in so immense as to be practically immeasurable" → should be "is so immense." This looks like a typo rather than a grammar misunderstanding, but proofread carefully for this error type.

Garbled sentence: "How about making the paycheck out to the couple, or to the entire family, instead of the individual person (it, of course, the worker is married)?" This parenthetical is unclear—I believe you mean "if, of course, the worker is married." Revise for clarity.

Repetitive phrasing: You use "he or she" frequently (eg, "for he or she who works outside the home"). While this is a reasonable way to avoid gendered language, consider varying your phrasing—using "workers," "employees," or restructuring sentences to use plural subjects ("those who work outside the home")—to improve readability.

Punctuation with quotation marks: You use scare quotes somewhat inconsistently around words like "jobs," "homemaker," and "equal work." Make sure each instance of scare-quoting is intentional and serves a clear rhetorical purpose (eg, signaling irony or a contested term), rather than becoming a stylistic habit.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

State your thesis more directly at the end of the introduction.
Add a transition explaining the purpose of paragraph 2.
Support your claim about "societal ills" with concrete evidence.
Expand your proposed solution and address its limitations (especially for single homemakers).
Proofread carefully for articles (a/an/the) and subject-verb agreement.

You have a genuinely interesting and timely argument here—with revision, this essay could be quite persuasive.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0005.1.txt =====
Feedback on Your Essay: "Money, Desire, and the Limits of Moral Measurement"

Overall Impression

You're tackling a genuinely difficult philosophical problem: whether "money is the root of all evil" is empirically meaningful. Your instinct to interrogate the *logic* of the saying—rather than simply agreeing or disagreeing with it—is sophisticated and shows real critical thinking. The prose is ambitious and your vocabulary is often precise ("negative correlation," "utilitarian scale"). However, the essay's argument becomes difficult to follow in the middle sections, and the connections between paragraphs need strengthening. Below are specific areas to revise.

Clarify the Logical Thread

Your first paragraph sets up a strong, clear claim: if the saying were true, desire and evil should correlate with wealth, but this cannot be objectively measured. This is a good thesis. However, I lost the thread in paragraph 2. You write:

> "Desire cannot be measured on some utilitarian scale of pleasure. It increases and decreases with the acruement of wealth."

These two sentences seem to contradict each other—first you say desire *can't* be measured, then you describe how it changes (which sounds like a measurement). Consider whether you mean something like: "even if we tried to measure desire, it fluctuates unpredictably with wealth, making any fixed correlation between money and evil impossible to establish." Make this reasoning more explicit.

The "Happiness" Section Needs a Clearer Purpose

Your second paragraph shifts to discussing happiness as "contentedness," and suggests wealthy households may trend toward virtue. This seems to work *against* your thesis (that desire/virtue can't be linked to wealth), rather than supporting it. If this is meant to be a counter-argument you will refute, say so explicitly—something like: "One might object that happiness, understood as contentment, correlates with wealth, suggesting affluence breeds virtue. But this view is flawed because…" Right now, the reader can't tell whether you're proposing this idea sincerely or setting it up to knock down.

Missing Text and Abrupt Transitions

There is a gap in your final paragraph (marked )—it looks like a sentence or transition is missing before "A truer statement, I think…" Please check your draft; this gap makes it hard to evaluate the logical connection to what follows.

Additionally, the final paragraph introduces a *new* type of argument—a historical claim that the powerful are "just as often oppressors as saviors." This is a compelling point, but it argues something different from your opening thesis. Your thesis was about the impossibility of objectively measuring desire against wealth (an epistemological/methodological claim). The final paragraph makes an empirical/historical claim (the wealthy show no consistent moral pattern). These are related, but you need a sentence explicitly bridging them—something like: "This is not merely a logical puzzle: history itself confirms that wealth does not reliably predict virtue, providing empirical support for the logical point above."

Suggested Structural Revision

Consider this shape:
Paragraph 1: State the thesis (already strong).
Paragraph 2: Present the "happiness leads to virtue" idea explicitly as an objection, then refute it.
Paragraph 3: Explain why desire and virtue can't be reconciled into a measurable formula (tighten the logic here).
Paragraph 4: Use history as *evidence* for your logical claim, explicitly connecting the two.

Language and Mechanics

A few recurring issues to proofread for:
Spelling: "privelaged" → *privileged*; "acruement" → *accrual*/*accumulation*; "wheighed" → *weighed*; "serrendipitously" → *serendipitously*
Redundancy: "a world where in which money was" — choose either "where" or "in which," not both.
Article omissions: "It is not subtle argument" → "It is not a subtle argument"
"at a least" → "at least"

These are minor but numerous enough that careful proofreading (perhaps reading the essay aloud) will help.

Final Thought

Your essay demonstrates real philosophical ambition—you're not just discussing whether money is "bad," but whether the *concept* of moral correlation with wealth is even coherent. This is exactly the kind of argument that impresses at the university level. Your revision task is not to add more ideas, but to slow down and make the logical connections between your excellent ideas explicit. Trust your reader less to infer your reasoning, and show it step by step.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0006.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a genuinely interesting question—whether Marx's egalitarian ideal can realistically operate within Western society—and you bring in family, religion, individualism, and relativism as lenses for examining it. This is ambitious and shows real intellectual engagement. However, the essay currently has significant gaps in clarity, logical development, and structure that prevent your argument from coming through clearly. Below are the main areas to address in revision.

Missing Quotations

The bracketed placeholders  appear three times where quotations should be (Marx's principle, the New Testament teaching, and the "giving back" idea). Without these, a reader cannot evaluate your argument, since your entire essay is built around responding to specific claims. This must be fixed first—insert the actual quotations and make sure your surrounding sentences directly engage with their wording.

Unclear Thesis

Your last paragraph reveals your real claim: that Marx's egalitarian goal, though "admirable," is unlikely to be realized in Western culture. This is a strong, arguable thesis—but it appears only in your conclusion. Readers need this stated clearly in your introduction so they know what claim your evidence is building toward. Right now, the essay reads as an exploration rather than an argument, because we don't know your position until the end.

Logical Gaps Between Paragraphs

There is a conceptual problem in your first two body paragraphs that needs attention. You use the family and Christian institutions as evidence *supporting* egalitarianism, but then in the following paragraphs you argue that individualism and relativism *undermine* it. The transition between these two movements is abrupt and confusing. Ask yourself: are family loyalty and Christian charity actually the same thing as Marx's egalitarian principle (from each according to ability, to each according to need)? Protecting and nurturing *your own* family is arguably closer to particularism than egalitarianism—these are actually in tension with Marx's more universalist ideal, not supportive of it. You may want to reframe this paragraph to acknowledge that distinction, since it would strengthen rather than weaken your final argument.

Underdeveloped Reasoning

Several claims are asserted but not fully explained or supported:
Why does the "individual" being "paramount" in the postmodern world contradict family, religion, and language? This connection needs to be spelled out.
The claim that moral relativism results "in part" from individualism needs a sentence of explanation—how does one lead to the other?
The rhetorical questions in paragraph 4 (about the homeless man, the battered woman) are effective, but you don't follow up with an answer or a clear point. What do these examples prove? State it explicitly.

Language and Grammar

As requested, here are patterns worth reviewing (I recommend proofreading aloud, which often catches these):

Typos/spacing: "on e would" → "one would"
Subject-verb agreement: "who it to define" → "who is to define"
Preposition errors: "learned primarily form the family" → "from the family"
Article usage: Chinese does not require articles the way English does, so double-check phrases like "a mother nurtures her helpless infant" (correct) versus places where articles are missing, eg, "There are several problems with this notion" (correct) but check phrasing like "the concept provided is an admirable goal but one whose ends..." — consider simplifying to "This is an admirable goal, but one unlikely to be achieved..."
Word choice: "inculcated" is a strong, precise word—good usage. But phrases like "absent a deviation from our general nature to propagate" are overly dense; consider breaking into two shorter sentences for clarity.
Ampersands: Avoid "&" in formal academic writing (eg, "social institutions & practices," "philosophers, poets & politicians")—write out "and."

Paragraph Structure

Each paragraph would benefit from a clear topic sentence that states its main point *before* the supporting details, and a closing sentence that links back to your thesis. Right now, some paragraphs (especially the "affluent society" and "freedom" paragraphs) present ideas somewhat associatively, without a clear internal structure.

Suggestions for Revision

Insert the missing quotations and make sure each following paragraph responds directly to the quoted words.
Move your thesis (Marx's ideal is admirable but unrealizable in Western society) to your introduction.
Reconsider whether family/religion are truly *evidence for* egalitarianism or whether they might complicate it—this could become a more sophisticated, self-aware argument.
Add 1–2 sentences of explanation after each major claim, especially in the individualism and relativism paragraphs.
Proofread carefully for articles, prepositions, and stray typos.

You have the makings of a thoughtful, well-organized argument here. With a clearer thesis stated up front and tighter logical connections between paragraphs, this essay will be much more persuasive. I'd be happy to discuss any of these points further in office hours.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0007.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Overall Impression

This is a genuinely creative and well-organized essay. Your central conceit—treating crime as a business with investments, overhead, and risk—is intellectually engaging and gives your argument a clear analytical framework. You move logically from defining "pays," to enumerating types of loss (capital, physical/mental health, legal), to a conclusion. This is a strong structural skeleton. My feedback below focuses on sharpening your thesis, strengthening your evidence, and cleaning up some grammar issues that a non-native speaker of English commonly encounters.

Strengthen and Clarify Your Thesis

Right now, your position on "does crime pay" is somewhat elusive until the final paragraph, and even there it remains hedged: "In some cases it may." For an argumentative essay, readers need a clearer stance early on—even if that stance is nuanced (eg, "crime rarely pays once true costs are accounted for, except in specific circumstances X and Y"). Consider adding a thesis sentence at the end of your introduction that previews your actual claim, not just your method of analysis. Right now you tell us *how* you will define "pays," but not *what conclusion* that definition leads to.

Develop the Counterargument

Your essay only weighs monetary/health losses against monetary gains. But part of what makes "crime pays" a compelling question is that criminals may gain things that are hard to monetize—status, thrill, sense of power, or an escape from a worse legal alternative (poverty, discrimination in the job market, etc). Acknowledging this would make your argument more sophisticated and show you've considered the strongest version of the opposing view. Even a short paragraph addressing "gains beyond dollars" would round out your business analogy nicely, since businesses also weigh intangible benefits (reputation, satisfaction) alongside profit.

Support Claims with Evidence

The essay currently relies entirely on hypothetical scenarios (the jewelry store thief, the gunshot wound). These are useful for illustrating your logic, but an academic argumentative essay benefits from concrete evidence—criminology studies, recidivism statistics, data on average "criminal wages" versus legitimate wages, or cited examples of organized crime economics. Even one or two real statistics (eg, studies estimating the "hourly wage" of drug dealing) would substantially strengthen your credibility and move the essay from speculative to evidence-based.

Conclusion Needs More Weight

Your final line about taxes is witty, but as the last note of the essay, it undercuts the seriousness of your analysis. Consider ending with a firmer restatement of your thesis, then use the tax comment as a closing rhetorical flourish rather than the final substantive point. Right now, the essay's most memorable line is a joke, when it might be better to leave readers with your strongest analytical insight.

Language and Grammar Notes

Your English is generally strong and fluent, especially given the conceptual complexity of the essay. A few recurring issues to review:

Missing comparative words: "he does better the legitimate businessman on one count" → should be "he does *better than* the legitimate businessman." Watch for dropped "than" after comparatives.
Awkward causal logic: "If the criminal can only expect to earn minimum wage in a legal job, then crimes can earn less to justify time spent." This sentence is logically reversed/unclear—I think you mean that a criminal with low earning potential needs to gain *less* from crime to make it "worth it," compared to someone who could earn more legally. Consider rewriting as: "A criminal who could only earn minimum wage legally needs a smaller criminal gain to justify the time invested; a criminal with professional-level earning potential needs a much larger gain."
Spelling: "equiptment" → "equipment."
Article/number agreement: "no taxes" is fine, but check phrases like "Mafia and police pay-offs could be involved" for clarity—consider "paying off the Mafia or corrupt police."
Informal diction: phrases like "ten dollars in the hole" and "hideout hotel room" are vivid but casual. In academic writing, you can keep the illustrative example but consider phrasing it slightly more formally, eg, "left him with a net loss of ten dollars" rather than "in the hole."
Repetition of "pay/pays": deliberate repetition works for argumentative emphasis, but vary some instances with synonyms (yield, compensate, offset) to avoid monotony.

Paragraph-Level Suggestion

Your paragraph on legal problems is somewhat thin compared to the others. Consider expanding it—perhaps discussing plea deals, the disproportionate costs of legal defense for poorer defendants, or the long-term economic effect of a criminal record on future legitimate earning potential (which ties back to your "investment" metaphor nicely).

Summary of Priorities for Revision
Add a clear thesis statement.
Address non-monetary gains as a counterargument.
Incorporate at least some real-world evidence/statistics.
Strengthen the conclusion so it doesn't end on a joke.
Fix minor grammar/spelling issues noted above.

You have a genuinely original argumentative structure here—the business analogy is your strongest asset. Focus your revision on making the "so what" of your argument unmistakable to the reader.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0008.1.txt =====
Feedback Report: "Is Money the Root of All Evil?"

Overall Impression

Your essay takes a clear and thoughtful position: money itself is neutral, but the *love* of money is what leads to harm. This is a nuanced and defensible argument, and you support it with relatable examples (families breaking apart, businesses failing, children learning to hoard). However, the essay would benefit from stronger structural organization, clearer topic sentences, and more careful proofreading. Below are specific suggestions organized by area.

Introduction and Context

The essay opens with "This idea is completely erroneous," but the reader has no idea what "idea" you are referring to. Since this appears to be a response to a prompt (likely the claim "money is the root of all evil"), you should state that claim explicitly in your first sentence. For example:

> "The claim that money itself is the root of all evil is fundamentally mistaken."

This immediately orients your reader and makes your thesis clear from the start.

Structure and Paragraphing

Your essay moves between several related ideas—money's practical necessity, the danger of desire, cross-cultural examples, and childhood development—but the transitions between them are abrupt. Consider organizing your paragraphs around a clearer progression:

Paragraph 1: State the claim you are refuting and your counter-thesis.
Paragraph 2: Explain why money itself is necessary and neutral (your current paragraphs 2–3).
Paragraph 3: Introduce the distinction between money and the *love* of money, citing the biblical origin of this idea if relevant to your argument.
Paragraph 4: Develop your examples (family, business, nation, individual) with more depth—perhaps focus on *one* extended example rather than listing several briefly.
Paragraph 5: Discuss the cross-cultural and developmental evidence (children, different currencies).
Conclusion: Restate your thesis with a forward-looking or memorable closing statement.

Currently, several paragraphs are only one or two sentences long, which fragments your argument. Combining related ideas into fuller paragraphs will make your essay feel more cohesive and persuasive.

Development of Ideas

Your examples (broken families, failing companies, imprisoned individuals) are effective but remain general. Academic essays are strengthened by *specific* evidence—a brief anecdote, a well-known case (eg, a corporate scandal like Enron), or even a statistic about debt or greed-driven crime. This would move your essay from broad assertion to substantiated argument.

Additionally, some sentences restate the same point in slightly different words (eg, the idea that love of money "urges them on" and "causes them to neglect their families" appears more than once in different forms). Consider tightening these repetitions so each paragraph advances a *new* aspect of your argument rather than circling back.

Language and Grammar

A few specific corrections to watch for:

"all together" → "altogether" ("This is a different concept altogether.")
"capitivates" → "captivates"
"sued" → "used" ("It can be used for good.")
"it's" vs. "its": "already has it's needs met" should be "already has its needs met" (possessive, not contraction).
Capitalization: "he begins to hide it" should begin with a capital "He."
Comma splices: "Money perhaps, is a necessary evil in order to conduct the daily affairs of life" has an unnecessary comma after "perhaps." Revise to: "Money, perhaps, is a necessary evil for conducting the daily affairs of life."
Repetition of "in order to": You use this phrase multiple times in close proximity. Vary your phrasing (eg, "to conduct," "for," "so that").

I recommend reading your essay aloud or using a grammar-checking tool (like Grammarly) after revising content, since these small errors are easy to miss but affect the reader's impression of polish.

Tone and Register

Phrases like "It is a cross-cultural thing" are conversational. For an academic essay, consider a more formal phrasing: "This pattern transcends cultural boundaries" or "This phenomenon is evident across cultures." Similarly, rhetorical questions ("How many times have we seen...?") are effective for engagement but should not replace analysis—follow them with deeper reflection rather than simply another example.

Conclusion

Your final paragraph does a good job of summarizing your thesis, but it largely repeats earlier statements. Consider ending with a broader implication—why does this distinction matter? What does it suggest about how individuals or societies should relate to wealth? This would give your essay a stronger sense of closure and significance.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Clarify your thesis and the claim you're responding to in the introduction.
Reorganize paragraphs for a clearer logical progression.
Add one or two specific, developed examples rather than several brief ones.
Proofread carefully for typos, capitalization, and word confusion (its/it's).
Elevate tone slightly for academic register.

You have a strong, defensible argument here—these revisions will help your writing match the sophistication of your ideas.

Feedback Report: "Crime Does Not Pay"

Overall Impression

This is a thoughtful and engaging essay with a clear argumentative stance: that the phrase "crime does not pay" functions more as wishful thinking than as objective truth. You support this claim with a variety of examples—murder statistics, Michael Milken, Richard Nixon, and the prison industry—which gives the essay good breadth. However, the essay would benefit from tightening its argumentative structure, strengthening its evidence, and polishing certain language issues. Below are specific areas to address in revision.

Content and Argument

Missing phrase. Your essay opens with "The phrase ," but the actual phrase is never inserted. Since your entire argument hinges on this saying, it must be stated explicitly and in quotation marks from the very first sentence (eg, "The phrase 'crime does not pay' is often invoked..."). Readers should not have to guess your subject.

Unsupported claims need citations. Phrases like "I recently read that..." and "reportedly made more money..." signal that you are drawing on outside sources, but you do not cite them. In academic writing, especially when making factual claims (statistics on murder sentences, financial details about Milken), you need proper citations. This strengthens your credibility and allows readers to verify your evidence.

Factual accuracy. The name "Michael Milliken" should be "Michael Milken." Since he is a central example, an error in his name undermines the reader's confidence in your research. Double-check all proper nouns and factual details before submission.

Consider counterarguments. Your essay would be more persuasive if you acknowledged the opposing view more directly—for instance, that many criminals *do* face severe consequences, or that the deterrent effect of punishment, even if imperfect, still reduces crime rates. Addressing this counterargument (even briefly) and then explaining why your examples still support your thesis would make your reasoning more rigorous and less one-sided.

Conclusion is underdeveloped. Your final paragraph restates the thesis but does not synthesize your examples or suggest broader implications. Consider ending with a reflection on why this matters—does the persistence of the phrase serve a social function (eg, reinforcing moral norms) even if it isn't literally true? This would give your conclusion more depth.

Organization

Your paragraph structure is logical: you move from the phrase's social function, to a challenge to its validity, to specific examples, and finally to a broader economic argument about the "prison industry." However, transitions between paragraphs could be smoother. For example, the shift from discussing individual criminals (Milken, Nixon) to the prison industry in Indiana feels abrupt. Consider adding a transitional sentence that explicitly connects "crime pays for individuals" to "crime also pays for institutions and industries."

Language and Style

Tone consistency. Phrases like "a more cold-blooded statistician than I am" are vivid and engaging, but be mindful of balancing personal voice with academic register. This is a stylistic choice, and yours works reasonably well, but ensure the essay doesn't drift into overly casual language (eg, "I think" appears multiple times—consider varying with more formal constructions like "This suggests that..." or "Evidence indicates that...").

Article and preposition usage. As is common for Chinese-speaking writers of English, watch for missing or misused articles. For example: "Behavior that is not reinforced in some way will not persist" is fine, but elsewhere check phrases like "living by the rules" and "make it cost effective"—these are correct, but proofread carefully for consistency, especially with countable/uncountable nouns (eg, "a token wage" vs. "token wages").

Sentence length and clarity. Some sentences are long and contain multiple clauses that could be split for clarity. For instance: "Richard Nixon, certainly a criminal at least in the sense of having violated public trust, was deprived of public office and lived a sequestered life for several years, but somehow managed to become a representative of the nation again..." Consider breaking this into two sentences to improve readability.

Word choice precision. "Miscreants" is a strong word choice, but make sure your vocabulary remains consistent in register throughout. Some phrases like "cold cutters" (if this was meant to be "cost cutters") should be checked for typos.

Summary of Priority Revisions

Insert the actual phrase in quotation marks at the start.
Add citations for factual claims and statistics.
Correct "Milliken" to "Milken" and verify other facts.
Strengthen the conclusion with synthesis and broader implications.
Add a brief counterargument for balance.
Smooth transitions between paragraphs.

Overall, this is a promising draft with an interesting, well-organized argument. With more attention to evidence, citation, and polish, it will be significantly stronger.

Feedback Report: Argumentative Essay on Financial Reward and Effort

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an interesting and complex question about how societies should reward effort. You clearly have strong opinions and try to support them with a historical progression (past → present → hypothetical future). However, the essay currently struggles with thesis consistency, paragraph organization, and some grammar and word-choice issues typical of translating complex reasoning from Chinese into English. Below are specific areas for revision.

---

Thesis Clarity and Consistency

Your introduction states that "men and women should be financially reward[ed] for their individual efforts," suggesting your essay will argue for a *money-based* reward system. However, your conclusion undercuts this by asking, "Our system of rewards is money... but is it really about money? I don't think so." This creates a significant contradiction: are you arguing *for* financial reward, or are you arguing that reward is fundamentally about something *other than* money (such as self-improvement or opportunity)?

Suggestion: Decide on your central claim before revising further. If your real argument is that money is simply a *symbol* or *tool* for a deeper principle—effort deserves opportunity for advancement—say so explicitly in your thesis, and make sure every paragraph supports that refined claim.

---

Organization and Paragraph Development

Your essay follows a loose chronological structure (past, present, hypothetical alternative, conclusion), which is a reasonable framework. However, each paragraph needs a clearer topic sentence that connects back to your thesis. Right now, paragraphs 2 and 3 mostly *describe* how effort and reward worked historically and today, but they don't strongly *argue* why this justifies financial reward specifically. Ask yourself after each paragraph: "How does this prove my thesis?"

The fourth paragraph (the hypothetical equal society) is your most persuasive section because it engages in cause-and-effect reasoning. However, it relies entirely on speculation ("I would venture to guess," "worst case scenario") without any supporting evidence—historical examples, research, or even a real-world analogy (eg, economic systems that minimize reward-based incentives). Strengthening this paragraph with concrete examples would make your argument far more convincing to an academic reader.

Suggestion: Consider adding at least one historical or contemporary example (eg, comparing economic systems, workplace incentive studies, or a specific society/era) to ground your reasoning in something more than personal speculation.

---

Language and Grammar

Several sentence-level issues need correction:

Verb form errors: "should be financially reward" → "should be financially rewarded"; "help us meet" not "held us meet."
Word confusion: "jealously" should be "jealousy."
Awkward phrasing: "took up of majority of each person's day" → "took up the majority of each person's day."
Sentence fragments/choppiness: Several sentences in paragraph 2 ("In order to feed your family you had to plant...") are short and repetitive. Consider combining them with subordinating conjunctions for more sophisticated academic sentence variety: *"In order to survive, individuals had to plant crops to eat, build shelters to live in, and sew garments to stay clothed."*
Pronoun/tense shifts: You move between second person ("you had to plant"), first person ("I feel"), and generalized statements ("Each of us is measured..."). Academic essays typically maintain a consistent point of view—third person or first-person plural ("we") is often more appropriate for this genre.

---

Tone and Style

Phrases like "much more!" and rhetorical exclamations feel more conversational than academic. While personal voice is fine in argumentative writing, consider moderating exclamation points and casual phrasing ("Let's imagine," "I would venture to guess") to maintain a more formal register appropriate for an academic essay. Instead, use phrases like "Consider a hypothetical society in which..." or "One might reasonably conclude that..."

---

Conclusion

Your conclusion introduces a new and potentially more nuanced idea—that reward is really about *opportunity* rather than money itself—but this idea appears too late and isn't given enough room to develop. This could actually be a compelling thesis if moved earlier and explored throughout the essay, rather than appearing as an afterthought in the final paragraph.

---

Summary of Priority Revisions

Clarify and unify your thesis — decide whether your argument is about money specifically or about opportunity/motivation more broadly.
Add concrete evidence to support your hypothetical scenario (paragraph 4).
Revise grammar and word choice issues noted above.
Strengthen topic sentences so each paragraph clearly connects to your thesis.
Adjust tone for a more formal academic register.

With these revisions, your essay's compelling central insight—that human motivation and effort are deeply tied to systems of reward—will come through much more clearly and persuasively.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You are grappling with a genuinely interesting and complex question—whether compensation should be tied to "contribution to society"—and your instincts about the topic's difficulty are sound. However, the essay currently reads more like a series of observations and questions than a structured argument. To strengthen this piece, focus on clarifying your thesis, tightening your organization, and developing your evidence more fully.

Thesis and Argument

Your strongest claim appears in the first paragraph: *"'Contribution to society' is subjective and therefore impossible to compensate 'equally.'"* This is a debatable, specific claim—good work. However, the essay doesn't consistently return to or build on this idea. Instead, it drifts through related but distinct questions: Do the wealthy deserve their leisure? Is inherited wealth fair? Do doctors deserve high salaries? Each of these could support your thesis, but right now they feel like separate tangents rather than connected evidence.

Suggestion: After introducing your thesis, explicitly signal how each subsequent example will support it. For instance, before discussing 1920s investors, you might write: "One clear illustration of this subjectivity can be seen in..."

Organization

The essay's structure needs revision:

Paragraph 2 (1920s investors) introduces a historical example but doesn't fully connect it to your thesis about "compensation." These investors weren't being *paid*—they were living off *existing* wealth. This is a different issue from compensation for labor, and the connection needs to be made explicit or the example reconsidered.

Paragraph 3 raises the migrant worker example, which is powerful, but it appears almost as an aside ("What about people who work very hard...") rather than as a developed counterpoint. This deserves its own fuller treatment.

Paragraph 4 introduces "Democracy" and competing political philosophies, which is a significant conceptual pivot. This might work better as an earlier paragraph, since it frames the entire debate.

Paragraph 5 (doctors) offers a nice concrete example but ends the essay somewhat abruptly. Consider a concluding paragraph that returns explicitly to your thesis and states its implications.

Suggestion: Try outlining your argument in one sentence per paragraph before revising. This will help you see whether each paragraph logically follows from the last.

Several ideas need more support:

The migrant worker example is vivid but could be expanded into a full paragraph with more concrete details (wages, hours, comparison to executive pay, etc).
The doctor example is effective, but you could push further: *why* do people believe doctors are overpaid, yet accept their fees? This tension could be explored more deeply to support your subjectivity thesis.

Language and Grammar

As a non-native English speaker, you're doing well with complex sentence structures, but here are some recurring issues to address:

Articles and prepositions:
   - "In the 1920s in American" → "in America"
   - "A migrant work may slave away" → "A migrant worker"

Spelling:
   - "Democracry" → "Democracy"
   - "responsibly" → "responsibility"

Subject-verb agreement:
   - "none of the doctors are lowering their fees" → "none of the doctors is lowering" (or rephrase: "not one doctor lowers")
   - "everyone seems to think that doctors make much too money" → "make too much money"

Sentence fragments/run-ons:
   - "The answer this question lies..." → "The answer to this question lies..."
   - Consider breaking up long sentences like the one in paragraph 4 about "menial or physically taxing job" for clarity.

Word choice:
   - "relished the idea of retiring early" is good, natural phrasing—well done here.
   - "live as parasites" is a strong, vivid metaphor—keep this kind of figurative language, but make sure it's clearly attributed if it echoes a specific author's phrasing.

Revision Priorities

For your next draft, I suggest:

Rewrite your introduction to state your thesis more prominently and preview your main supporting examples.
Reorganize paragraphs so each one clearly advances a single point tied to your thesis.
Expand at least two examples (the migrant worker and the "great authors" reference) with concrete, specific evidence.

Strengthen your conclusion by explicitly returning to your central claim about subjectivity and its implications for how society should—or shouldn't—attempt to link pay to "contribution."

You have compelling ideas here. With tighter organization and more developed evidence, this essay could become a genuinely persuasive piece. I look forward to seeing your revision.

Feedback on "Crime Can Be a Profitable Profession"

Overall Impression
This is an ambitious and provocative essay with a clear three-part structure (historical context, material gain, consequences/justice system) that supports your thesis. Your voice is confident and the topic is engaging. However, the essay needs revision in three main areas: the strength and consistency of your argument, some grammatical issues common to ESL writers, and precision in your use of evidence.

Argument and Logic

Thesis clarity: Your thesis—"crime can be a profitable profession"—is stated only in the final sentence of paragraph one, almost as an afterthought. State it more prominently and make it more precise. Do you mean crime is *always* profitable, *sometimes* profitable, or that American culture *rewards* certain criminals? Right now the essay wavers between these claims.

Logical consistency: Some of your examples work against your own argument. The Boston Tea Party is a poor example because those "criminals" were fighting an *unjust* law under a government they didn't recognize—this is a case where "crime" is really about perspective and legitimacy, not profit. This actually undermines your thesis (that crime pays *within* a functioning system) rather than supporting it. Similarly, Benedict Arnold is remembered as a *traitor*, not someone who "profited"—he died in poverty and disgrace. This example contradicts your point rather than proving it. Choose examples that clearly and directly show *material or reputational gain from criminal acts*, and remove or reframe examples that complicate your argument.

Category confusion: You blend different kinds of "crime"—historical rebellion (Boston Tea Party), scandal (Divine Brown, Buttafuco), organized illegal enterprise (Prohibition, Las Vegas), and violent crime (O.J. Simpson). These are not equivalent, and lumping them together weakens your credibility. Consider narrowing your focus (eg, only "crimes that led to fame and fortune") or clearly organizing your paragraphs by *type* of crime so readers can follow your logic.

Evidence specificity: Claims like "the city of Los Vegas was founded on ill-gotten gains" need brief explanation—*whose* ill-gotten gains? Similarly, explain *why* O.J. Simpson's case supports your point (be specific: book deals, media appearances, etc), rather than assuming readers will infer this.

Organization

Your paragraph structure is logical (history → money → justice system), but the conclusion doesn't add anything new—it mostly repeats the thesis. Try ending with a stronger final thought: a call to reconsider assumptions about crime, or a comment on what this reveals about American culture/media.

Language and Grammar

You have a strong vocabulary and sentence variety, but there are several recurring issues to address:

Typos/proofreading: "vents" → "events"; "Los Vegas" → "Las Vegas"; "tes." at the end appears to be a stray fragment—proofread carefully before submitting.

Subject-verb agreement: "crime is a recurrent theme" is fine, but check sentences like "No one can argue that crime is not a means of making money, if it were not why would people commit them" — this sentence has both a comma splice and unclear pronoun reference ("them" should refer to "crime," but "crime" is singular). Revise to: "No one can argue that crime is not a means of making money; if it were not, why would people commit it?"

Article usage: Common ESL issue—check phrases like "convicted killers autobiographies" (missing apostrophe: killers') and consider whether articles like "the" are needed before certain nouns (eg, "the historical context" is fine, but review similar constructions throughout).

Run-on sentences: "At best criminals learn to use the criminal justice system should they become arrested again and at worst they receive free room and board..." — this needs a semicolon or period between the two contrasting ideas for clarity.

Word choice: "avow" is a strong word—make sure it fits (it typically implies public declaration, which fits here). "Speak-easies" should be hyphenated correctly. Watch idiomatic expressions like "if one has the right agent," which is clever but slightly informal for academic writing—consider whether your target audience expects a more formal register throughout.

Suggestions for Revision

Rewrite your thesis to be sharper and place it more prominently.
Reconsider or reframe the Boston Tea Party and Benedict Arnold examples.
Add 1–2 sentences of explanation after each major example so readers understand *why* it proves your point.
Proofread carefully for typos and sentence fragments.
Strengthen the conclusion with a final insight, not just repetition.

This essay has a clear point of view and interesting examples—with revision to tighten the logic and polish the language, it will be much more persuasive.

Feedback on Your Essay

Thank you for tackling a genuinely complex and controversial topic. You clearly have opinions and are willing to take a position, which is a strength. However, the essay needs significant revision in several areas: argument development, evidence, organization, and language precision. Below is feedback organized by category.

Argument and Position

Your central claim is unclear. You open by agreeing that women have faced historical inequality, but by the end you seem to argue that gender roles are biologically fixed and that feminism has gone "too far." These are two very different arguments, and the essay doesn't clearly signal this shift or defend it with reasoning.

Some specific claims will weaken your credibility with an academic reader:
"Women just can't fight in a war very well. They are not strong enough and if captured could be raped." This is a sweeping generalization not supported by evidence. Physical strength varies by individual, not simply by sex, and many countries now have women in combat roles. Rape as a risk applies to male POWs as well. This claim needs either strong evidence or should be removed.
"I felt that these people were crazy" — calling people "crazy" for holding a different opinion is not persuasive; it dismisses rather than engages with the opposing argument.
"God would have created us all the same if this was not true" — this is a religious/faith-based claim, which is not appropriate as evidence in a secular academic argument, since not all readers share this belief, and it cannot be tested or verified.

Revision suggestion: Decide on one clear, defensible thesis. For example, you might argue that feminism has achieved important progress but has not represented the diversity of women's preferences equally. This is a more nuanced, arguable claim than "nature cannot be changed."

Evidence and Support

Right now, most claims are asserted without support:
"Divorce is at fifty percent and rising, women are having more heart attacks, being more stressful, and kids are being more neglected." These are strong statistical claims. Where do these numbers come from? You need citations. Also, you assert a causal link between modernization/feminism and these trends without demonstrating it — this is a common logical fallacy (correlation ≠ causation). Divorce rates, for instance, are influenced by many social, legal, and economic factors, not simply "modernization."
The military combat example is good in principle (a concrete case is helpful!), but you need to cite an actual policy, date, or source rather than relying on personal impression ("I felt").

Revision suggestion: For every major claim, ask "How do I know this?" and find a source. This will also help you avoid overgeneralizations like "all women" or "many women."

Organization

Each paragraph currently mixes several sub-topics (workplace pay, combat roles, harassment lawsuits, biological difference, divorce rates) without clear transitions. Consider restructuring with one clear idea per paragraph:

Para 1: Historical inequality (brief, since this is background, not your main argument)
Para 2: Feminism's achievements
Para 3: Your critique — feminism doesn't represent all women (develop this with your military example, but revise the claims about women's physical ability)
Para 4: Workplace/harassment claims (needs evidence)
Para 5: Conclusion — what you think should change, and why

Add clear topic sentences that state the paragraph's main point, and use transition phrases ("However," "In contrast," "For example") to guide the reader.

Language and Style

There are recurring language issues to address:
Word confusion: "piers" → *peers*; "verses" → *versus*; "The are not strong" → *They are not*
Informal phrasing: "I felt," "I think" — in academic writing, prefer "I argue," "this suggests," or simply state the claim and support it, rather than framing everything as personal feeling.
Run-on/comma splice sentences: eg, "Women just can't fight in a war very well. The are not strong enough and if captured could be raped." Consider combining and refining: "Critics argue that women are physically disadvantaged in combat and at greater risk if captured."
Avoid absolute words ("impossible," "can only") unless you can fully support them — qualify claims with "may," "tend to," "in many cases."

Summary of Priorities for Revision
Clarify and narrow your thesis.
Remove or heavily qualify biologically deterministic claims; support any claim about physical/mental differences with credible sources.
Add citations for all statistics.
Reorganize paragraphs around single, clear ideas.
Proofread for word confusion and informal phrasing.

You have an interesting angle — that feminism doesn't reflect the diversity of women's actual preferences. Focus your revision on developing *that* argument with evidence and nuance, rather than making broad claims about biological destiny. I'm happy to discuss further in office hours.

Overall Impression

Following the example with explicit analysis: *What does this example prove about the general principle that "crime does not pay"?* Spell out the connecting logic rather than assuming the reader will draw your intended conclusion.

Body paragraph 2 – the "punishment escalates" argument, using a *shortened* version of your example.
Counterargument paragraph – address the "but some criminals get away with it" objection.
Conclusion – restate the thesis and explain its broader significance (why should the reader care?).

Currently, the essay ends abruptly after the example, with no separate concluding paragraph. Please add one that returns explicitly to your opening claim.

Tone and Register

This essay uses a very conversational voice ("a cool million," "run like hell," "guess what happens... you know what happens"). This works well in storytelling but is too informal for academic argumentative writing. Consider:
Replacing slang and rhetorical asides ("Sounds like trouble to me") with more analytical phrasing.
Reducing direct address to the reader ("let's hypothetically say," "you know what happens") in favor of a more consistent authorial voice.
Avoiding self-referential asides like "especially me having a 'weakness' for children"—this is a personal aside that doesn't build the argument.

Language and Mechanics

There are several spelling and typing errors that a careful proofreading pass will catch:
committs → commits
indivudual → individual
convenient store → convenience store
strats → starts
hurridly → hurriedly
whrn → when
persistant → persistent
deterent → deterrent

I'd also encourage you to break up some very long run-on sentences (eg, the sentence beginning "Let's hypothetically say that these two guys rob...") into shorter units. This will make your ideas easier to follow and reduce comma splices.

Suggested Next Steps

Rewrite your thesis statement so it's a single, precise claim.
Shorten the narrative example by half; add explicit analysis linking it to your thesis.
Add a counterargument and rebuttal.
Draft a proper concluding paragraph.
Proofread carefully for spelling and run-on sentences, ideally reading the essay aloud.

Feedback Report: "Money is the Root of All Evil"

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a genuinely interesting philosophical question—whether money itself is inherently evil or whether its moral value depends on human intention. You have a clear and defensible thesis (money is neutral; the person's attitude determines its effect), and you attempt to complicate a simplistic saying with more nuanced thinking. This is a strong intellectual instinct. However, the essay needs significant development in structure, evidence, and grammatical precision to fully realize this argument. Below are specific areas to focus on in revision.

Content and Argument Development

Strengths: Your central claim—that money is a neutral tool whose moral valence depends on the user's psychology (need, addiction, greed)—is a sophisticated position. You also correctly identify that "money" functions as a proxy for power, status, and survival, which is a useful analytical move.

Areas needing work:

Evidence and examples. Right now, your claims are almost entirely abstract assertions ("The list of addictions people develop are endless," "greedy people usually have one objective"). An academic essay needs concrete support: a specific case study, a historical example, a statistic, or even a hypothetical scenario developed in detail. For instance, when you mention gambling or prostitution as "obvious" money-driven evils, show *how* the need for money transforms behavior in one specific case, rather than listing categories.

Logical connection to the opening quote. You open with "money is the root of all evil" but your actual argument disputes this saying (you argue money is neutral, not inherently evil). This is a legitimate and even more interesting position, but you need to explicitly frame it that way early on. Right now, the essay reads as if it's proving the saying true, then subtly shifts to disproving it, which confuses the reader about your actual thesis.

The conclusion is too abrupt. "Money does good or evil—depending on who has the money" is a strong final idea, but it appears with no transition and no restatement of your fuller argument (the addiction/greed reasoning you built in paragraph two). Consider a proper concluding paragraph that synthesizes your two main paragraphs and returns to the opening saying to explicitly say whether you agree, disagree, or complicate it.

Counterargument. The essay would be stronger if you acknowledged an opposing view—for example, someone who argues money *itself* creates temptation independent of the individual's psychology (eg, "power corrupts" arguments). Addressing this would deepen your analysis of "attitude" as the deciding factor.

Organization

Your two body paragraphs each pursue one clear idea (money as power/need; money and greed), which is good. But consider adding topic sentences that explicitly state the paragraph's function in your overall argument, and add a distinct introduction (with your thesis clearly stated) and conclusion (that returns to and complicates the opening proverb).

Language and Mechanics

Because grammar accuracy affects how clearly your argument comes across, here are the recurring issues to address:

Sentence boundaries/run-ons: "Enough money gives you the power to get whatever you need to survive, lack of money may mean you can't survive." This is a comma splice—two independent clauses joined only by a comma. Use a period or semicolon, or a conjunction ("survive, while a lack of money...").

Capitalization after commas/mid-sentence: "when the need for money becomes..." should begin with a capital "When" since it starts a new sentence after a period-equivalent pause; check throughout for lowercase letters starting sentences.

Apostrophe errors: "Ones's attitude" should be "One's attitude." "a greedy persons life" should be "a greedy person's life."

Subject-verb agreement: "The list of addictions people develop are endless" — "list" is singular, so it should be "is endless."

Article usage: "in structure of a greedy persons life" needs an article: "in the structure of a greedy person's life." Chinese does not require articles the way English does, so this is a common area to double-check throughout your draft (eg, "the definition of the term 'money'").

Punctuation of titles/sayings: The opening phrase should be in quotation marks: "'Money is the root of all evil' is an ancient saying."

Final sentence lacks a period: "money is just a tool to gain what is needed" ends without punctuation before the next sentence.

Revision Priorities

Clarify your thesis explicitly in relation to the opening proverb.
Add concrete examples/evidence in both body paragraphs.
Write a real concluding paragraph, not just a final line.
Proofread carefully for comma splices, apostrophes, and capitalization—reading your essay aloud can help you catch these.

You have the makings of a thoughtful essay here. With more developed evidence and tighter grammatical control, this argument will be much more persuasive.

Feedback Report on Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

This essay tackles a genuinely interesting and debatable topic—how feminism has changed dating norms and chivalry—and your personal voice comes through clearly. You have lived experience spanning several decades, which gives you a unique perspective that many writers on this topic lack. However, the essay currently reads more like a personal reflection or opinion piece than a structured academic argument. Below are some specific areas to strengthen for your revision.

Thesis and Focus

Your central claim doesn't emerge clearly until the fourth paragraph: "My argument is that in the realm of dating, courting, and chivalry in general, it's nearly a lost art." This should appear early—ideally in your first paragraph—so readers know exactly what you're arguing from the start. Right now, the essay opens with a broad statement about disagreeing with feminism generally, which is a much larger and more contentious claim than what you actually argue. Consider revising your opening to state precisely: "While the feminist movement has achieved important social gains, it has inadvertently contributed to the erosion of traditional dating etiquette." This narrows your scope and prevents readers from expecting a broader critique of feminism that you don't deliver.

Structure and Paragraphing

The essay would benefit from clearer organizational signposting. Currently, ideas about feminism's positive achievements (equal pay, glass ceiling, career choice) are mixed with your central argument about chivalry, which creates some structural confusion. Consider this structure:

Introduction: State your thesis clearly.
Concession paragraph: Acknowledge feminism's legitimate achievements (this paragraph works well already, but move it earlier and frame it explicitly as a concession before your main argument).
Main argument (60s vs. 90s): Develop your comparison with more specific detail.
Counterargument and rebuttal: Address why women might avoid traditional gestures (fear of appearing weak, past experiences with harassment) more seriously before dismissing it.
Conclusion: Return to your thesis with a call for balance.

Evidence and Support

Your evidence is almost entirely anecdotal—your own dating experience and comments from friends. This is a common weakness in personal argumentative essays: it makes your reasoning vulnerable to the fallacy of hasty generalization (moving from a few personal examples to broad claims like "women in general are offended"). To strengthen this:

Distinguish between "some women" and "women in general." Your current phrasing overgeneralizes based on limited testimony.
Consider acknowledging that dating norms vary by region, culture, and individual, which would make your argument more credible and nuanced.
If this is for an academic assignment, look for at least one or two external sources (a sociological study on dating norms, or commentary on the "chivalry debate") to support your claims beyond personal anecdote.

Addressing the Counterargument

You mention that women might avoid traditional gestures because they "want to appear strong" or fear dependency, but you dismiss this quickly with "I say and conclude that this is bunk!" This is the weakest point in your argument. An academic essay needs to *engage* seriously with the opposing view, not just dismiss it. Ask yourself: why might a woman prefer to pay her own way? Is it really about weakness, or could it be about autonomy, past negative experiences, or not wanting to feel a sense of obligation? Engaging honestly with this perspective—even if you ultimately disagree—will make your argument far more persuasive and less one-sided.

Tone and Word Choice

Some phrases are appropriate for casual speech but feel too informal for academic writing: "this is bunk," "hanging around this continent," "Miss Susie Homemaker." Consider replacing these with more measured language suited to argumentative essays, eg, "I find this reasoning unconvincing" instead of "this is bunk."

Language and Grammar Notes

"Stigmatism" should be "stigma."
Watch parallel structure: "confused whether to open a door... pay for their dinner, or even touch..." is fine, but check tense consistency throughout (you shift between past and present when describing the 60s vs. today).
The long parenthetical asides (eg, about hiring a housekeeper) interrupt your argument's flow. Consider moving supporting details into full sentences within the paragraph rather than parentheses.
Watch comma splices, eg, "Men were gentlemen in the 60's, felt that the woman was someone special..." — this needs a coordinating conjunction or restructuring.

Suggestions for Revision

Write a clear one-sentence thesis and place it in your introduction.
Reorganize paragraphs following the structure suggested above.
Add at least one substantial paragraph addressing the strongest counterargument.
Revise generalizations ("women in general") into qualified claims.
Proofread for tense consistency and comma splices.

You have a strong personal voice and a clear point of view—now it's a matter of shaping that voice into a more disciplined, balanced academic argument. I look forward to seeing your revision.
