Feedback on "Crime Doesn't Pay" Essay

Thank you for sharing this draft. You have chosen an interesting angle—looking at the financial and social "costs" of crime rather than simply its illegality. Below are some observations and suggestions organized from global to local issues.

Thesis and Structure

Your introduction opens with a rhetorical question about a quoted phrase, but the phrase itself is missing (shown as ), so readers cannot follow your reasoning. Please make sure the actual quotation appears in your final draft, since your whole essay is built around unpacking its meaning.

More importantly, your thesis statement needs to be sharper. Currently you write that "crime actually costs money," but your paragraphs actually cover four different *kinds* of cost: (1) financial cost to taxpayers, (2) emotional/moral cost to the criminal, (3) financial cost to property owners, and (4) social cost of stigma to the criminal. These are worth discussing, but your thesis should explicitly preview this range so readers know what to expect. For example: *"While the phrase 'crime doesn't pay' is often used casually, it captures a literal truth: crime imposes measurable financial burdens, psychological damage, and lasting social consequences on both offenders and law-abiding citizens."*

Your essay currently lacks a conclusion. After four body paragraphs, the essay simply stops. Please add a paragraph that synthesizes your points and returns to the opening quotation, explaining how your evidence has proven its truth.

Paragraph Development and Evidence

Each paragraph introduces a good idea but relies heavily on generalization ("in most cases," "for the most part," "sometimes") without concrete evidence. For an academic essay, you should support claims with data or citations:

Paragraph 2 (incarceration costs): Cite actual statistics—eg, the average annual cost of incarcerating one prisoner in a specific country or state. This would make your claim about tax dollars much more persuasive than a general statement.
Paragraph 3 (moral/emotional cost): This paragraph is more speculative and psychological than economic, so consider either connecting it more explicitly to your financial theme (eg, the cost of therapy, lost relationships, lost future earning potential) or clearly framing it as a *different kind* of cost—the "hidden" or "non-monetary" cost.
Paragraph 4 (real estate): Good example, but again, a real statistic (eg, "% decline in home value") or a citation to a study would strengthen this claim considerably.
Paragraph 5 (criminal record/stigma): This connects well to cost (lost job opportunities = lost income), but you should state that connection explicitly rather than leaving it implied.

Coherence and Transitions

Right now, the four body paragraphs feel like a list rather than a connected argument. Consider adding transitional sentences that link each paragraph to the previous one and to your thesis. For example, moving from the incarceration paragraph to the moral paragraph, you might write: *"Beyond the direct financial burden on taxpayers, crime also imposes a psychological cost on the offender."* This helps the reader see the logical progression of your argument.

Language and Mechanics

Since English is your second language, here are some specific patterns to watch for:

Spelling errors: "freinds" → *friends*; "acquantances" → *acquaintances*; "oppurtunity" → *opportunity*; "so to" → *so too*.
Pronoun agreement: You mix singular ("the individual," "his or her") with plural ("they," "their") when referring to the same person. Academic style now generally accepts "they/their" as a gender-neutral singular pronoun, but choose one approach and use it consistently rather than switching between "his or her" and "they."
Homophone confusion: "their is no escape" should be "there is no escape."
Comma and sentence-boundary issues: Several sentences run together without proper punctuation, eg, "As we read the daily paper, listen to the evening news or speak to other people we hear stories..." This needs a comma before "we hear" to separate the introductory clause from the main clause.
Word choice: "too often overlooked" (not "to often"); revise "as long as they live" (paragraph 3) since it slightly repeats "for the rest of their lives" (paragraph 5)—vary your phrasing to avoid redundancy.

Suggestions for Revision

Insert the missing quotation and build your thesis explicitly around it.
Add a concluding paragraph that ties your four types of "cost" together.
Strengthen each body paragraph with at least one concrete statistic or citation.
Add transitional sentences between paragraphs.
Proofread carefully for spelling, homophones, and pronoun consistency—reading your essay aloud can help you catch these errors.

Overall, you have a solid conceptual foundation and an engaging opening strategy. With clearer organization, more evidence, and careful proofreading, this essay will be much stronger. Keep up the good work!

Feedback on "Organized vs. Unorganized Crime" Essay

Overall Impression
You've chosen an interesting angle—challenging popular assumptions about organized crime and connecting it to money and power. Your firsthand interest in the subject (through your course) comes through, and the point about organized crime being broader than "mafioso" stereotypes is a strong contribution. However, the essay needs significant work on thesis clarity, structure, evidence, and register before it reads as a persuasive academic argument.

Thesis and Argument
Right now, readers must infer your central claim. Is it "Organized crime pays because of money and corruption, while unorganized crime does not"? State this explicitly in your introduction, and make sure every paragraph works to prove it.

There's also an internal contradiction: you say an unorganized criminal "must be very good and have considered all aspects... in order not to get caught," but then argue this crime "very rarely pays because the criminals just are not that good." Decide which claim you're making and support it consistently.

Additionally, be careful with moral and factual framing. Calling robbery, assault, rape, and theft "petty offenses" because they lack "high dollar value" is a problematic generalization—these are serious violent crimes regardless of financial gain. Revise this characterization, or clarify that you mean "low-profit" rather than "petty" in a moral sense.

Structure
Academic essays need a clear shape: introduction with thesis → body paragraphs with topic sentences and evidence → conclusion that synthesizes your argument. Currently:

The introduction spends time on your personal interest but doesn't state your claim.
The conclusion is missing. Instead, the essay ends with a personal biography ("My name is Thomas Vessely... I am currently a second semester junior"), which does not belong in an argumentative essay. This paragraph should be replaced with a conclusion that restates your thesis and reflects on its implications (eg, what this means for policy, ethics, or public trust).

Consider adding a brief roadmap sentence at the end of your introduction ("This essay argues that... by first examining X, then Y").

Evidence and Support
You make several strong factual claims—eg, "the average amount of money spent on illegal gambling alone is $30 per person," or that police receive "two or three fold" their salary from protecting organized crime—but provide no citations. Since you reference your Organized Crime & American Politics course, cite specific readings, lecture material, or statistics from credible sources (DOJ reports, academic studies, etc). This will strengthen your credibility significantly and is expected in argumentative writing.

Similarly, the claim that unorganized criminals are "usually somewhat less educated" needs support or should be softened with qualifying language ("research suggests," "in many cases").

Tone and Register
Academic essays generally avoid:
All-caps emphasis ("MONEY!", "BIG sum," "ANYTHING they want") — use precise wording or italics sparingly instead.
Colloquial expressions ("both sides of the track," "save the best for last," "on point exactly") — replace with more formal phrasing.
Direct address to the reader ("Now that you have read this and may be trying to figure me out...") — this breaks the argumentative frame and shifts into personal narrative.

Aim for a more measured, analytical voice throughout.

Language and Sentence-Level Issues
A few patterns to watch for as a non-native English writer:

Fragments: "crime of passion; heat of the moment" is a fragment—integrate it into a full sentence.
Subject-verb/word form errors: "The persons must be patient and will to deal with others" → "willing to deal with others."
Ambiguous phrasing: "I believe it to be a question and not a statement" is unclear—specify what "it" refers to and why the distinction matters to your argument.
Comma splices and run-ons: eg, "This is false. Organized crime involves two or more individuals working together for the same cause, that is ongoing and an integral part of their daily lives" — consider breaking this into clearer, separate ideas.

I recommend reading your paragraphs aloud or using a grammar-checking tool to catch smaller errors, then double-checking flagged items against a style guide.

Suggested Revision Priorities
Write a clear, one-sentence thesis and place it at the end of your introduction.
Remove the biographical paragraph; replace with a real conclusion.
Add citations for statistics and factual claims.
Revise "petty offense" language for accuracy and sensitivity.
Adjust tone—remove all-caps and casual idioms.
Proofread for sentence fragments and awkward phrasing.

With these revisions, your genuinely interesting insight into organized crime's structural nature can become a much more convincing, polished argument. I'd be happy to look at a revised draft.

Feedback on Your Essay

Thank you for sharing this deeply personal and moving account. Your story about the break-in and its aftermath is compelling, and your emotional honesty makes the essay memorable. However, since this was assigned as an argumentative essay, there are some structural and rhetorical issues to address, along with several language points common among Chinese-English learners. Below is my feedback organized by priority.

Genre and Argument (Most Important)

Right now, this essay reads as a personal narrative, not an argument. You tell a powerful story, but you never clearly state a debatable claim that you are trying to prove. Ask yourself: what is the argument here?

Possible arguable theses hiding in your narrative:
"Plea bargaining undermines justice for crime victims."
"The criminal justice system should give victims more voice in sentencing decisions."
"Victims of property crime suffer psychological harm that the legal system often ignores."

Your final paragraphs (about the plea bargain and your meeting with the prosecutor) contain your strongest argumentative material. I suggest restructuring the essay so this becomes your main claim, with the narrative serving as *evidence* rather than the entire essay. State your thesis explicitly in the introduction, then use the story to support it, and end with a clear statement of what you think should change (eg, victims should have more input into plea negotiations).

Organization

Your chronological storytelling is clear and easy to follow — this is a strength. However, for an argumentative essay, consider this structure:
Introduction: Briefly introduce the incident and state your thesis/claim.
Body: Use the narrative selectively to support specific points (eg, emotional trauma, the failure of plea bargains, the importance of victim advocacy).
Conclusion: Return to your thesis and perhaps call for a specific change or offer a broader reflection.

Currently, the essay ends on a repeated proverb ("crime doesn't pay") rather than a strong closing argument. Consider ending with a statement about what *should* happen for future victims.

Sentence-Level Language Issues

There's an incomplete sentence with a placeholder: *"they saying that "* — please complete this thought; right now the reader doesn't know what your parents said crime would result in.

Grammar patterns to review:

Verb tense agreement: "my mother and father always taught me the stealing was wrong" → "taught me that stealing was wrong." (Missing "that" as a connector, and remember: gerunds like "stealing" don't need "the" here.)

Possessive apostrophes: You are missing apostrophes in several places:
  - "husbands firearms" → husband's firearms
  - "prosecutors office" → prosecutor's office (multiple instances)
  - This is a small but frequent issue — please proofread specifically for possessives.

Subject-verb agreement in reported speech:
  - "I kept remembering what my father told be" → told me
  - "they stolen property came from" → "the stolen property came from"

Comma splices / run-on sentences: Several sentences string together too many clauses with commas. For example:
  > "I did not see them, but they did see me and they made it out of the house before I could call for police help."
  
  This works, but elsewhere sentences run long without clear breaks (eg, the sentence beginning "We would have to go to court..."). Try breaking these into two sentences for clarity.

Article usage: Chinese does not have articles (a/an/the), so this is often tricky. Example: "I received a call from the Indianapolis Police Department" (correct!) vs. "trunk of an old car" (correct) — you're generally doing well here, but double-check phrases like "the police said that we would probably never recover" — consider whether "the police" or "our belongings" needs an article adjustment for precision.

Style Suggestions

The repetition of "crime doesn't pay" (used 3 times) is a nice unifying device, but by the third repetition it feels like a substitute for actual analysis. Consider using it once memorably, then replacing later repetitions with more specific reflection on justice, victim rights, or systemic change.

Some phrases are overly informal for academic argument (eg, "For whatever it is worth"). Consider more direct, analytical language when you shift toward your argument.

Summary of Next Steps

Write an explicit thesis statement — decide what claim you want to argue (likely about plea bargains or victims' rights).
Trim the narrative to focus on details that support that argument; cut less relevant details (eg, the exact list of stolen items).
Proofread for possessives, subject-verb agreement, and comma splices.
Strengthen your conclusion by connecting back to your thesis and suggesting a broader implication or call to action.

You have excellent raw material here — a genuinely powerful personal experience. With a clearer argumentative frame, this could become a strong persuasive essay about victims' rights in the justice system. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You've tackled a genuinely difficult and controversial topic, and your passion for the subject comes through clearly. You have a real argument here—that the term "feminism" has been distorted from its original egalitarian purpose—and you support it with a specific historical reference (Susan B. Anthony) and some thoughtful reasoning about social wellbeing. However, the essay needs significant revision in three areas: the clarity of your central claim, the quality of your evidence, and some issues with word choice and grammar that undermine your credibility on a sensitive topic.

Clarify the Prompt and Your Thesis

You open by responding to "this statement," but never tell your reader what the statement actually is. Since this is an argumentative essay, your reader needs to know exactly what claim you are defending or refuting from the very first sentence. Consider opening with something like: *"The claim that feminism has done more harm than good rests on a misunderstanding of what feminism originally meant."* This immediately orients your reader and gives your essay a clear thesis to return to.

Strengthen Your Evidence

Your strongest paragraph is the one on Susan B. Anthony because it grounds your argument in a specific historical figure. The rest of the essay, however, relies heavily on unsupported generalizations:

"The concept many people today have of a 'feminist' is that of a big chunky lesbian who hates all men." This stereotype is not only unsupported by any source, but its phrasing (especially "big chunky") is dismissive and could alienate your reader, undermining your credibility. If you want to describe a stereotype in order to critique it, you need to cite where this stereotype comes from (media commentary, polling data, a specific critic) rather than presenting it as self-evident.
Similarly, "some feminist groups will take cases to an extreme" and "many people see the feminist movement moving in the wrong direction" are vague. *Which* groups? *Which* people? Academic argument requires you to name specific examples, cases, or sources. Without them, these claims read as opinion rather than argument, which weakens your overall credibility.

Suggestion: Try to find at least two or three concrete examples—a specific controversy, statement, or statistic—to replace generalized claims about "radical feminists." This will make your critique far more persuasive and less like an unsubstantiated stereotype.

Watch for Internal Contradiction

Your essay does something interesting but risky: it defends feminism's legitimacy in one section and then criticizes "modern feminism" in another. This can work as a nuanced argument (feminism's origins were good; its current practice has problems), but right now the transition between these two positions is abrupt and not signposted. Consider adding a clear transitional sentence such as: *"While the historical goals of feminism remain justified, the way the movement is sometimes practiced today has created new problems."* This will help your reader follow the logical shift rather than feeling like you're arguing two different things.

Conclusion Needs to Resolve the Tension

Your conclusion returns to the idea of "roots" and "solidarity," which is good, but it ends on a personal note ("many women, including myself... have separated themselves from modern feminism") rather than a clear final statement about what should change or what readers should conclude. Consider ending with a forward-looking claim: What should feminism look like moving forward? What would "returning to its roots" actually require in practice?

Language and Grammar Notes

A few recurring issues to address in revision:
Capitalization after periods: "by this I do not mean" should begin with a capital B.
Article errors: "A women is just as much a citizen" → "A woman is..."; "his fellow brother" is a slightly awkward phrase—consider "fellow citizen" or "male counterpart."
Singular/plural agreement: "take cases to an extremes" → "to an extreme" or "to extremes."
Comma splices: Several sentences join independent clauses only with a comma (eg, "The feminists have grown, they were radicals of their time..."). Use a semicolon or period instead.
Word choice: "dirty words" and "home free" are very informal for academic writing; consider more precise, academic phrasing.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

State your thesis explicitly in the introduction.
Replace stereotypes and vague claims with specific, cited examples.
Add a clear transition between your defense and critique of feminism.
Strengthen your conclusion with a forward-looking claim.
Proofread carefully for capitalization, articles, and comma splices.

You have a compelling argument buried in here—the revision process should focus on making your evidence as strong as your convictions.

Feedback on Your Essay: Women's Liberation Movement and Public Perception

Overall Impression

You have chosen an interesting and genuinely debatable thesis: that the failure of feminist movements to gain public acceptance stems not from the actions of "radical" members but from the biased perception of outside observers. This is a sophisticated argumentative move—rather than blaming the movement itself, you shift responsibility onto the audience. This kind of reframing is exactly what strong argumentative essays do. However, the essay currently struggles to deliver this argument with enough clarity, consistency, and evidential support. Below are suggestions organized by area.

Thesis and Structure

Your central claim ("the fault does not lie with the radical members of the movement, but rather, in the perception of those observing the movement") is buried in the third paragraph and not restated clearly until near the end. Academic readers benefit from knowing your argument early. Consider revising your introduction so that this thesis appears in the first paragraph, giving readers a roadmap for what follows.

Additionally, the essay moves chronologically (19th century → 1960s → today), which is a reasonable structure, but each section needs a clear topic sentence connecting it back to your thesis. Currently, paragraphs describe historical figures without explicitly explaining *how* each example proves that perception—not reality—is the problem. Ask yourself after each paragraph: "Have I shown that the *public's* interpretation was flawed, rather than just described what happened?"

Evidence and Historical Accuracy

This is the area that most needs attention. Several of your examples are questionable or factually inaccurate, which will undermine your credibility with an academic reader:

Emily Dickinson is not conventionally classified as part of the feminist movement; she was a poet with limited public engagement in political causes during her lifetime. If you want to use her as an example of an "ignored" feminist, you need to explain and justify why she counts as one—otherwise this claim will seem unsupported.
Gertrude Stein died in 1946, so she cannot be a "radical" figure of the late 1960s Women's Liberation movement. This is a factual error that a careful reader will notice immediately and that weakens trust in your other claims.
George Sand and Susan B. Anthony are grouped together, but Sand was a French novelist working decades earlier in a different national and cultural context. You note "although French" in parentheses, which suggests you sense the problem yourself—but a parenthetical aside isn't sufficient. Either justify the comparison explicitly or choose a more fitting example.

Suggestion: Replace these examples with well-documented, verifiable case studies (eg, comparing how media covered Betty Friedan versus more radical figures like the Redstockings, or examining specific news coverage of NOW versus more confrontational groups). Concrete evidence—quotations from newspapers, statistics on public opinion, or scholarly analysis—will make your argument far more persuasive than broad, loosely supported historical sketches.

Tone and Voice

The line "So what am I getting at?" is too informal for academic writing and also signals that the essay's argument has become unclear even to the writer. Rather than using this as a rhetorical reset button, revise earlier paragraphs so the argument builds logically toward your claim, making this kind of clarifying aside unnecessary.

Conclusion

Your conclusion trails off rather than synthesizing your argument. The final sentence ("they would be more readily received if the public it was being aimed was not so jaded") is grammatically unclear and ends the essay on a vague note. Consider closing with a stronger statement: What should change? Is this a call for media literacy, better journalism, deeper historical education, or something else? Give the reader a sense of the argument's stakes and implications.

Language and Mechanics

Since English is your second language, here are some specific patterns to watch for in revision:

Verb tense/agreement: "Whenever a movement is began" → "begins"
Spelling: "oppostion" → "opposition"; "therfore" → "therefore"; "readiliy" → "readily"
Preposition/clarity issues: "the public it was being aimed" → "the public at which it was aimed" or "the public toward which it was aimed"
Parallel structure: "the good that feminists have promote" → "have promoted"

I recommend reading your essay aloud after revising—this often reveals awkward phrasing and missing words that are easy to overlook.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

State your thesis clearly and early.
Verify all historical facts and dates.
Strengthen evidence with specific, documented examples.
Revise the conclusion to offer a clear takeaway.
Proofread carefully for grammar and spelling.

You have a genuinely compelling argument here—with more precise evidence and a tighter structure, this essay could be quite strong. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback on Your Essay on Feminism and American Culture

Your essay engages with an important topic and uses the Beijing conference as a timely, effective hook. You also handle some sophisticated rhetorical moves, like the rhetorical question "Does this 'battle' continue?" Narrow your thesis in the introduction to focus explicitly on America, perhaps framing the U.S. as a case study or starting point for a broader (future) discussion.
Expand the essay to include brief comparative examples from other countries, which would better fulfill your original framing.

Given the essay's length, I recommend the first option—simply be honest with your reader about the essay's actual scope from the start.

There's a tension in your essay that needs to be addressed directly: you state that "[n]o one today would say that women are not equal to men in the voting booths of America," suggesting the suffrage battle is resolved. There is a placeholder  in paragraph 3 where you intended to state what feminists like Friedan and Steinem "preached." A specific example of an institution that opened its doors (you mention military schools—could you name one, like The Citadel or VMI?)
A brief quote from Friedan or Steinem to substantiate what they argued

This would move your essay from general claims to persuasive, well-supported argumentation.

Your final paragraph—"America continues to set an example for women around the world"—is quite abrupt and underdeveloped for a concluding claim. This is actually a significant assertion that deserves support: *how* does America set an example? What global evidence supports this? Since your introduction gestures toward a global conversation, your conclusion is the natural place to return to that global framing. Consider expanding this into a fuller paragraph, or softening the claim if you don't have room to support it (eg, "America's own struggles with gender equality may offer both models and cautionary lessons for other nations".)

"suffragates" → suffragists
"Gloria Steinam" → Gloria Steinem
"the anniversary of the Women's Suffrage Movement" — specify *which* anniversary (75th, presumably, if this is 1995) for precision and credibility.
Watch subject-verb agreement: "Women's access... have expanded" should be "Women's access... has expanded" (access is singular).

Summary of Priorities

If you have limited time to revise, focus first on:
Filling in the missing sentence (the  placeholder)
Narrowing your thesis to match your actual content, OR adding global comparison
Clarifying the "battle won vs. battle continuing" tension
Expanding your conclusion to justify the claim about America "setting an example"

You have a clear, engaging writing voice and a good sense of rhetorical structure (introduction, rising complexity, conclusion). With more precise argumentation and evidence, this essay will be very effective. Nice work overall—I look forward to seeing your revision!

Overall Impression

You've chosen an interesting philosophical topic, and your instinct to complicate the absolute claim "money is the root of all evil" is a solid critical thinking move. Your conclusion—that individual choice, not money itself, is the true root of evil—is a thoughtful and original idea. However, the essay needs work in three main areas: clarifying your opening argument, tightening your logic, and polishing your grammar. Below are specific suggestions.

Clarify Your Opening

Your first paragraph is confusing because it's unclear what "this phrase" and "this corrected statement" refer to. You write that a phrase is "incorrect," then say you believe a "corrected statement" is true, but you never actually state what that corrected version is (the placeholder  suggests something is missing here). Before revising further, make sure your reader knows:

What is the commonly cited (misquoted) phrase?
What is the "correct" biblical/original wording?
Why does this distinction matter to your argument?

A clearer opening might be: *"The commonly quoted phrase 'Money is the root of all evil' is actually a misquotation. The original biblical text states, 'The love of money is the root of all evil.' This distinction matters because..."* Then state your thesis explicitly.

Strengthen Your Logic

Several of your arguments rely on reasoning that needs more support:

"If money were the root of all evil, we all would have to be evil because we all have money." This is a good start, but it assumes that merely *possessing* money makes someone evil, which oversimplifies the counterargument. Consider explaining *why* the "love of money" (desire, obsession) is different from simply having money.

"If one is attaining money honestly, how can honesty be associated with evil?" This is circular—you're using "honesty" to prove money isn't evil, but the essay is about money, not honesty. Try instead: "If money is earned through legitimate means such as labor or investment, the act of earning it does not involve wrongdoing; therefore, the money itself cannot be inherently evil."

The paragraph about agencies and donations is a good real-world example, but it needs a clearer connecting sentence to your main argument. Explain explicitly: this shows money can be a force for good, which further disproves the idea that money is *inherently* evil.

Your conclusion introduces a new claim (individual choice as the "root of all evil") without connecting it back to your earlier paragraphs. Add a sentence that bridges this idea: since money itself is neutral, and both evil deeds and charitable deeds arise from human choices, the true "root" must be the human will, not the object of desire.

Organize Paragraphs with Clear Topic Sentences

Each paragraph should open with a clear claim that supports your thesis. Right now, some paragraphs (like paragraph 3, about "money is the root of *some* evil") shift the argument slightly without warning. Consider adding transition phrases such as "While money is not inherently evil, it can still contribute to evil acts when..." This will help readers follow your reasoning as it evolves from paragraph to paragraph.

Grammar and Word Choice

A few recurring issues to fix:

"wold" → "would" (typo)
"honestly" → "honesty" (word confusion: honestly is an adverb, honesty is a noun)
"morals (lack of)" → rephrase as "a lack of morals" for smoother reading
"worth-while" → "worthwhile" (one word, no hyphen)
Watch subject-verb agreement: "If money was the root..." should be "If money were the root..." (subjunctive mood for hypothetical conditions)
Some sentences are quite long and combine multiple ideas with commas or semicolons where periods or conjunctions would improve clarity (eg, "Other things as well can be the root of evil: attitudes, morals (lack of), greed; there is an endless list...").

Suggestions for Revision

Rewrite the introduction to clearly state the misquotation, the correct phrase, and your thesis.
Add a transition sentence connecting each paragraph back to your thesis.
Strengthen the logical connection in your conclusion by explicitly linking "choice" to the earlier discussion of money as neutral.
Proofread carefully for grammar issues, especially subjunctive mood and word form errors (honesty/honestly).
Consider adding a short example (a specific historical or contemporary case) to illustrate both "money causing evil" and "money enabling good," which will make your argument more vivid and persuasive.

Overall, your ideas are promising and show real critical engagement with a philosophical claim. With clearer transitions, tighter logic, and careful proofreading, this essay can become a much stronger piece of argumentative writing.

Feedback Report on Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an interesting philosophical question about the relationship between money, power, and personal values. You have real insights here—particularly the distinction between people who "live to use money" versus those who "use money to live." However, the essay would benefit significantly from a clearer thesis, tighter organization, and attention to grammar and spelling. Below are specific areas to address in revision.

Clarify Your Thesis and the Saying You're Addressing

Your opening sentence references "this saying," but you never state what the saying actually is. Presumably you are responding to a prompt like "Money is the root of all evil," but the reader has to guess. Revise your introduction to explicitly quote the saying you are examining, then state clearly whether you agree, disagree, or want to complicate it. Right now, your position shifts across paragraphs: paragraph 1 says money is necessary and powerful; paragraph 2 pivots to power corrupting people; paragraphs 3–4 discuss earned vs. unearned money and different values systems; paragraph 5 concludes that money should be a tool, not a master. These are all worthwhile ideas, but as written they feel like four different essays. Decide on ONE central argument (for example: "Money itself is neutral; it is our relationship to money that determines whether it corrupts us") and make every paragraph serve that argument.

Strengthen Paragraph Transitions and Logic

The jump from money (para. 1) to power (para. 2) is abrupt. If you want to connect money and power as parallel forces that can corrupt, say so explicitly: "Money is not the only force that can control us; power operates the same way."
Paragraph 3 introduces the idea of "earned vs. easy money" without linking it back to the power discussion. Add a transition sentence explaining why this point matters to your overall argument.
Paragraph 4's key sentence is unclear: "Others rate success one heir more substantive accomplishments" — this appears to be a typo. Did you mean "based on"? Revise for clarity, as this sentence contains your important distinction between two types of people.

Develop Your Best Idea More Fully

Your strongest and most original point is in paragraph 4: money can be either "a slave for us" or something that "makes us slaves." This is a compelling metaphor and could serve as the *thesis* of your entire essay rather than being buried in the fourth paragraph. Consider restructuring so that this idea appears early and everything else (the discussion of power, earned income, etc) becomes evidence supporting it.

Grammar, Spelling, and Word Choice

You have a strong idea sense, but frequent errors interrupt the reader's understanding. Please proofread carefully or use spell-check for these words:

"currupts" → corrupts
"cicle" → circle
"sence" → sense
"sucess" → success
"indespencable" → indispensable
"begats" → begets
"ones" → one's (possessive apostrophe needed)
"somewhere" is one word, not "some where"

Also revise this sentence for grammatical completeness: *"It's probably more correct to say that, 'Absolute power currupts absolute'"* — the quotation is missing a word. The common saying is "Absolute power corrupts absolutely." Make sure your quotations are accurate and grammatically complete.

Sentence-Level Clarity

Some sentences would benefit from revision for flow and precision:

"This begats the will for more power and the cicle goes on." → Consider: "This creates a desire for even more power, and the cycle continues."
"Anything that comes easy most probably won't be as appreciated..." → "Anything that comes easily is probably not as appreciated..."
The final paragraph's short, choppy sentences ("It is for us to use, not for it to use us. We are to be the masters...") could be combined for a more sophisticated conclusion: "Money should serve us, not enslave us; we must remain its master, using it to meet our needs rather than allowing it to define our worth."

Suggested Revision Strategy

Write a one-sentence thesis stating your position on the original saying.
Reorder paragraphs so each one builds logically toward that thesis.
Add transition sentences between paragraphs.
Proofread for the spelling and grammar issues listed above.
Strengthen your conclusion by explicitly returning to the saying you introduced at the start.

Final Note

Your ideas about power, self-worth, and the psychological dimension of money are genuinely thought-provoking. With a clearer structure and more careful editing, this essay could be quite persuasive. Focus your revision on establishing one clear argument and supporting it step by step—rather than covering multiple related but distinct ideas. Well done on tackling a complex philosophical topic!

Feedback Report: "Is Money the Root of All Evil?"

Overall Impression

You engage with an interesting and debatable question, and your final position—that money itself is neutral, but human choices about money can be evil—is a thoughtful one. However, the essay's structure and argument need tightening so that this nuanced conclusion feels earned rather than contradictory. Below are suggestions organized by priority.

Clarify Your Thesis Early

Right now, the essay first argues that seeking money leads people away from a "Christian way of life" and into evil (paragraphs 2–4), and then reverses to say money itself cannot be evil (paragraphs 5–6). A reader may feel whiplash, wondering which claim you actually support.

Suggestion: State your full thesis in the introduction—something like: *"While pursuing money can lead people toward selfish or harmful behavior, money itself is a neutral tool; it is the human heart, not the currency, that is capable of evil."* This lets readers see from the start that your essay will explore both sides before arriving at this synthesis, rather than seeming to contradict itself.

Fix the Placeholder

The  at the beginning should be replaced with the actual quotation you are analyzing (presumably "money is the root of all evil"). Essays should not rely on the reader guessing the subject.

Strengthen the Religious Framing

You root much of your argument in a "Christian way of life," which is a reasonable interpretive choice given the biblical origin of the phrase. However:
Not all readers share this framework, so briefly acknowledge that you are interpreting the saying through a religious lens, or broaden the argument to a more universal ethical framework (eg, "many philosophical and religious traditions warn against greed").
The claim that Christians must be "subservient" and "without material things" is a strong generalization. Consider qualifying it (eg, "many Christian teachings emphasize...") or citing a specific scripture or theologian to support the claim.

Add Concrete Evidence and Examples

Your strongest paragraph is the one about money's positive uses (housing, food, education), but even there, the claims remain abstract. The essay would be far more persuasive with specific examples:
A real-world case of philanthropy (eg, a foundation that funds education).
A historical or literary example of someone corrupted by greed (Ebenezer Scrooge, a Gordon Gekko-type figure, or a real historical figure).
Data or a quotation from a source discussing wealth and behavior.

Academic essays are strengthened when personal opinion ("I believe," "I think not") is paired with outside evidence, not left to stand alone.

Reorganize for Logical Flow

Consider this structure:
Introduction: Present the quotation, pose your guiding questions, and state your thesis.
Body 1: Explain how the *pursuit* of money can corrupt values and relationships (your paragraphs 2–4, tightened and supported with examples).
Body 2 (counterpoint): Argue that money itself is neutral—an object without agency (your paragraph 5).
Body 3: Show money's constructive potential when used well (your paragraph 6).
Conclusion: Synthesize: it's not money but human choice that determines good or evil outcomes.

This order builds toward your final insight rather than seeming to reverse course midway.

Language and Grammar Corrections

A few recurring issues to address in revision:

Word confusion: "more that you have" → "more than you have"; "mear" → "mere"; "lead" → "led" (past tense) in "This can lead you into gambling" (should be "leads" for present tense consistency, or "led" if narrating a past pattern).
Capitalization: "christian" should be capitalized as "Christian" throughout (it's a proper adjective).
Verb form: "provide opportunities to educated the uneducated" → "to educate the uneducated" (infinitive form).
Spelling: "subservant" → "subservient"; "judgements" is acceptable in British English but should be consistent with "judgments" if using American spelling.
Sentence fragments/run-ons: Some sentences (eg, in paragraph 5) combine multiple ideas without clear punctuation. Reading aloud can help you hear where a comma or period is needed.

Polish the Conclusion

Your final line—"it is all in how it is used and how you allow it to use you!"—is a strong, memorable idea. Consider expanding it slightly into a full concluding sentence or two that restates your thesis and leaves the reader with a clear takeaway, rather than ending abruptly after the exclamation.

Summary

Your essay has a clear moral argument and a compelling final insight. Focus your revision on (1) stating a unified thesis upfront, (2) supporting claims with concrete examples, (3) smoothing the transition between your two main points, and (4) correcting the grammar issues noted above. With these changes, your essay will feel more cohesive and persuasive.

Feedback on "Crime Does Pay"

Overall Impression

Your essay takes a bold, provocative stance, and you clearly enjoy the persuasive challenge of arguing something counterintuitive. This is a strength—confident writers who defend unpopular positions can produce compelling work. However, in its current form, the essay reads as one-sided and lacks the balanced reasoning that argumentative writing at the university level requires. Below are specific suggestions for revision.

Argument and Evidence

The biggest weakness is that you present only the benefits of crime without acknowledging any risks or counterarguments. A strong argumentative essay anticipates objections and responds to them. Right now, a skeptical reader will immediately think: *What about arrest, imprisonment, violence, or the risk of being killed?* If you don't address these, your argument feels naive rather than persuasive. Consider adding at least one paragraph acknowledging the risks of crime and then explaining why, in your view, the potential rewards still outweigh them (eg, statistically, most crimes go unsolved; or many criminals never get caught).

Your numerical evidence also needs revision. In paragraph three, you calculate the criminal's earnings as "$300 without... deductions," but then in paragraph four you write "$300.00 per hour," which contradicts your earlier calculation. This inconsistency will confuse readers and undermine your credibility. Please recheck your math carefully:
Is $300 the criminal's daily, weekly, or hourly income?
Where does this figure come from? Right now, it appears to be invented rather than sourced. Even in an argumentative essay, using a hypothetical example is fine, but you should signal this clearly (eg, "Suppose a criminal earns...") rather than presenting invented numbers as fact.

Additionally, the claim "a criminal will always be employed" and "there is never a lack of work" are strong generalizations without support. Consider softening these claims or providing reasoning (eg, referencing recidivism rates, demand for stolen goods, etc).

Organization

Your essay follows a reasonable structure (introduction → job security → income comparison → retirement → conclusion), but transitions between paragraphs are abrupt. Phrases like "Then if we think about..." and "And finally..." are conversational but not strong academic transitions. Try using topic sentences that connect back to your thesis, for example: *"A second advantage of criminal life is financial: criminals can earn more in less time than the average worker."*

Your conclusion is very short and simply restates the thesis. Consider expanding it to briefly summarize your three main points (job security, income, retirement) and end with a stronger closing thought—perhaps acknowledging the moral complexity of your argument while reaffirming your economic reasoning.

Language and Grammar

Since English is your second language, here are some specific patterns to watch for:

Homophone/word confusion: "it's advantages" should be "its advantages" (its = possessive; it's = it is).
Word choice errors: "rather or not" should be "whether or not." Also "are a means" should likely be "or a means."
Subject-verb agreement: "Tax deductions will run... costs them" — check verb forms; "deductions... cost" (plural subject needs plural verb, not "costs").
Sentence fragments: "Then if we think about the work day of most working people" is a fragment—it lacks a main clause. Revise to: "Now let's consider the work day of most working people."
Punctuation with numbers: Be consistent with how you present dollar amounts (eg, "$300.00" vs. "$300"—pick one style).
Pronoun consistency: You often use "he or she," "they," and "him/her" inconsistently within the same sentence. Choose one approach (eg, consistently use "they" as a singular pronoun) for smoother reading.

Suggestions for Revision

Add a paragraph addressing counterarguments (risk of arrest, violence, moral consequences) and refute them.
Fix the mathematical inconsistency between hourly/weekly figures.
Clarify whether your statistics are hypothetical examples or real data; if hypothetical, label them as such.
Strengthen transitions and expand your conclusion.
Proofread carefully for grammar issues listed above—reading your essay aloud can help you catch missing words or fragments.

Final Note: Your creativity and willingness to argue an unconventional position show real potential. With more balanced reasoning and clearer evidence, this essay could become a genuinely persuasive piece rather than simply a provocative one. I encourage you to revise with these points in mind and feel free to bring a draft to office hours for further discussion.

Feedback Report on "Crime Pays" Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay presents a provocative and clearly-stated thesis with vivid, memorable imagery. You have a strong voice and clear conviction. However, the essay currently reads more like a persuasive opinion piece than an academically rigorous argument. To strengthen it for university-level work, you need to address issues of evidence, logical structure, and precision in language. Below are specific areas for revision.

Evidence and Support (Most Important Issue)

This is the essay's biggest weakness. Every claim you make is asserted but never substantiated:

"the shockingly low percentage of criminals who actually end up in jail" — What percentage? According to which source?
"Police are far outnumbered" — Compared to what? Do you have crime statistics or police staffing data?
"it is only a matter of time before he or she is free" — What is the average sentence length for non-violent crimes? What do parole statistics show?

Revision suggestion: For each major claim, find at least one credible source (government crime statistics, sociological studies, news reports) to support it. Academic argumentation requires you to *demonstrate* your claims, not simply assert them with confident language ("It is obvious that...").

Logical Structure

Your argument follows an interesting logic, but some connections need clarification:

You move from observing luxury goods among urban youth to concluding "crime" is the funding source. This is a causal leap—there could be other explanations (family wealth, employment, gifts, credit). Address counterarguments explicitly, even if you plan to refute them.
The claim that criminals are "merely working a job" is a striking reframing, but you should more explicitly justify this comparison rather than relying on the McDonald's example alone.
Consider adding a paragraph addressing objections: What would someone who disagrees with you say? How would you respond?

Tone and Word Choice

Some phrasing undermines your credibility as an academic writer:

"living larger" and "rolling wads of money into their designer jeans" are vivid but casual/slang expressions. Consider more formal alternatives: "living conspicuously wealthy lifestyles" or similar.
"Fortunately for the criminals" — the word "fortunately" reads oddly here; perhaps "notably" or "unfortunately for society."
The rhetorical questions ("How do these young people acquire funds?") are effective as hooks but overused. Limit them to one or two strategic moments.

Grammar and Mechanics

A few errors to correct:

"wether" → "whether" (paragraph 3)
"with no en in sight" → "with no end in sight" (paragraph 5)
Extra period after "hire them." (paragraph 3) — remove one.
"come from other criminals" → "comes from other criminals" (subject-verb agreement, paragraph 2)

These are minor but should be proofread carefully, as accumulated errors distract readers from your argument.

Racial Framing

Your opening sentence explicitly mentions "both black and white" youth, which seems intended to preempt accusations of racial stereotyping. However, the essay's focus on "inner-city youth," combined with mentions of specific urban fashion brands, may still invoke racialized stereotypes about crime and poverty. Consider:

Whether this framing is necessary to your argument, or if it could be removed/reframed to focus more directly on economic conditions rather than implicitly gesturing toward racial categories.
Consulting sociological or economic literature on urban poverty could help you make a more nuanced, defensible argument that avoids potential essentialism.

Conclusion Strengthening

Your conclusion restates your thesis effectively but doesn't move the argument forward. Consider adding:
A brief acknowledgment of the complexity of the issue (eg, systemic factors, education, policy failures)
A forward-looking statement: What are the implications of this argument? What should change?

Summary of Priority Revisions

Add credible evidence and citations for every major factual claim (most urgent).
Address counterarguments to strengthen logical rigor.
Revise casual language for more formal academic register.
Proofread carefully for grammar and spelling.
Reconsider racial framing to ensure your argument is grounded in economic analysis rather than implicit stereotype.

Your ideas are compelling and clearly argued in terms of voice—now the task is to back them with evidence and present them with the precision expected in academic writing. I'd encourage you to visit the writing center to workshop your evidence integration in particular.

Feedback on Your Essay: The Impact of Computers on Society

Thank you for sharing this thought-provoking essay. Your central idea—that computers have made life easier physically but harder mentally—is an interesting and debatable claim, which is exactly what a strong argumentative thesis should be. Below are some observations to help you strengthen the essay in revision.

Clarify and Tighten Your Thesis

Your "catch-22" framing is promising, but the connection between your evidence and your claim (physical ease vs. mental difficulty) is not always clear. For example, in paragraph 3, you argue that computers cause errors—but you then admit the error is actually human error, not the computer's. This weakens your point rather than supporting the idea that computers make things "harder mentally." Ask yourself: what exactly is the mental cost you want to prove? Is it that people no longer understand the math behind their tools (a loss of literacy/skill)? Is it anxiety about job loss? Is it cognitive overload from too much data? Right now these are blended together. Choose one or two clear mental effects and develop each with a specific example (a case, a statistic, or a scenario), rather than general statements like "mathematical models are riddled with errors."

Reconsider the Aside About "2nd & 3rd World Countries"

This parenthetical comment in paragraph 2 is dismissive and could be read as insensitive; it also does not add evidence to your argument. Since your essay does not otherwise discuss global inequality in technology access, this aside distracts from your main point rather than supporting it. Either remove it or, if you want to make an argument about unequal access to computers, develop it seriously with evidence.

Strengthen Paragraph Structure and Transitions

Your two-part structure (physical effects → mental effects) is logical, but the internal organization of the "mental effects" section is confusing. You label it "two-fold" but the two folds (loss of mathematical understanding; job displacement/human obsolescence) feel like separate essays rather than two connected sub-points. Consider adding a clear transition sentence at the start of each sub-point ("The first mental effect concerns..." / "A second, related effect is...") so readers can follow your logic. Also, each paragraph would benefit from a concrete example: a specific case where a calculation error caused real damage, or a specific industry where automation displaced workers, would make your claims far more persuasive than general statements.

Support Your Claims with Evidence

Much of the essay relies on assertion ("computers make errors all the time," "mathematical models... are absolutely riddled with errors") without evidence. As this is an argumentative essay, you need to substantiate these claims—even one or two brief examples (a news event, a statistic on job automation, a scholar's argument) would significantly strengthen your credibility.

Watch Your Use of Quotation Marks

You use quotation marks frequently for emphasis (eg, "told," "needless," "variables," "was able to do with a computer"). In academic English, quotation marks are reserved for direct quotations or to indicate a term is being used in a special, technical, or ironic sense—not for general emphasis. Overuse of quotation marks can make your writing seem uncertain or informal. Instead, use italics sparingly for emphasis, or simply trust your word choice without marking it. Revise these instances by removing the quotation marks or rephrasing the sentence.

Sentence-Level and Grammar Issues

There are several run-on sentences and comma splices, for example:
> "The computer has eliminated many jobs through automation of factories; accounting, engineering, and other scientific programs, etc"

This sentence is a fragment/list without a clear verb structure—consider rewriting: "The computer has eliminated many jobs through the automation of factories, accounting processes, engineering tasks, and other scientific applications."

Also check for consistent verb tense (you shift between present and present perfect) and avoid vague qualifiers like "etc" and "many," which reduce precision. Try to replace these with specific nouns or examples.

Strengthen the Conclusion

Your conclusion introduces new solutions (better math education, curtailing certain programming) that are reasonable, but they appear rather suddenly without being connected back to the specific problems raised in the body paragraphs. Consider explicitly linking each solution to the corresponding problem you identified earlier ("Since computer errors often stem from a lack of understanding, better mathematical education could address this specific issue...").

Summary of Priorities for Revision
Clarify your central claim about "mental difficulty" and make sure each paragraph supports it directly.
Remove or properly develop the aside on developing countries.
Add concrete evidence/examples to support generalizations.
Reduce unnecessary quotation marks.
Revise run-on sentences and improve transitions between ideas.

Overall, you have a genuinely interesting thesis with real analytical potential—focus your next draft on tightening the logic between your evidence and your central claim, and supporting your points with concrete examples.

Feedback Report on Draft Essay: "The Necessity of Computer Knowledge"

Overall Impression

Your essay addresses a relevant and interesting topic, and the anecdote about the auto-parts manager is a strong, concrete example that engages the reader. However, the essay needs revision in three main areas: (1) sharpening and maintaining a consistent thesis, (2) strengthening paragraph unity so that each body paragraph clearly supports your main argument, and (3) correcting grammar and word-choice errors. Below are specific suggestions organized by area.

---

Thesis and Overall Argument

Your introduction states that computer knowledge is "a necessary component of leading a successful life." This is a clear, arguable claim — good. However, your third body paragraph (on banking and hacking) drifts away from this claim. Instead of showing that *individuals need computer knowledge to succeed*, it discusses a *societal problem* (hacking) and gives *advice* (check your bank statements). This paragraph would fit an essay about "risks of computer dependence," but it does not clearly support your stated thesis.

Suggestion: Either (a) revise this paragraph so it clearly illustrates that *people who understand computers are better protected from problems like hacking or accounting errors*, thereby reinforcing your thesis, or (b) replace it with a different example that more directly shows the professional or personal necessity of computer literacy (eg, education, communication, job-searching).

---

Paragraph Development and Structure

Paragraph 2 (the manager example) is your strongest paragraph because it is narrative, specific, and clearly tied to your thesis. However, it would benefit from an explicit analytical sentence at the end explaining *why* this story matters — for example: "This case demonstrates that even experienced employees can lose their positions if they fail to adapt to new technology, proving that computer literacy is now essential for job security."

Paragraph 3 (banking) needs a clearer topic sentence that connects to your thesis (see above), and needs more development — right now it is only three short sentences and reads more like a list of loosely related facts than an analyzed example.

Conclusion is too brief and introduces a new, general idea ("keep abreast of new developments") without synthesizing the examples you've already given. A strong conclusion should restate your thesis in light of the evidence presented and perhaps suggest a broader implication (eg, what individuals or society should do in response).

Suggestion: Add transitional phrases between paragraphs ("In addition to employment, computer literacy is also essential in fields such as...") so the reader can follow your logical progression from one example to the next.

---

Language and Grammar

Several word-choice and grammar issues need correction. Here are the most important:

Spelling: "twenthieth" → *twentieth*; "succesful" → *successful*; "defecit" → *deficit* (note: "deficit" usually refers to money; you may mean *liability* or *drawback*).
Word choice: "One of the most persuasive discoveries" → computers are usually called an *invention*, not a *discovery* (discoveries are naturally occurring; inventions are created). Consider: "One of the most significant inventions of the twentieth century..."
Awkward phrasing: "internet working of large corporations" → likely you mean *networking* or *the interconnected systems of large corporations*.
Company name/typo: "auto-pasts company" → *auto-parts company*.
Article usage: "put all of their inventory on computer" → *onto a/the computer* or *into a computer system*. Chinese does not use articles the same way English does, so pay special attention to definite/indefinite articles (a/an/the) throughout — this is a recurring issue (eg, "found by examining the work" should probably be "found by examining the workplace" or "the world of work").
Sentence logic: "His inadvertent refusal to learn about computers cost him his job" — "inadvertent refusal" is contradictory (a refusal is usually a conscious choice, not accidental). Consider "unintentional oversight" or simply "failure to learn."
Run-on/fragment risk: "A vivid illustration of this can be found by examining the work." This sentence is vague — clarify what "the work" refers to (workplace? the job market?).

---

Next Steps for Revision

Rewrite your thesis statement so it explicitly previews the two or three areas your essay will discuss (eg, employment, finance, daily life).
Revise the banking paragraph so it clearly supports the thesis, or replace it with a more relevant example.
Expand your conclusion to synthesize your examples and restate the thesis with added insight.
Proofread carefully for spelling and article usage — reading your essay aloud can help you notice missing or misused articles.

You have a good foundation with an engaging example and a clear personal writing voice. With more attention to structural coherence and precision in language, this essay will be significantly stronger.

Feedback on Your Essay: The Impact of Computers on Society

Overall Impression

You've chosen an interesting and debatable topic, and your bilingual perspective gives you room to write thoughtfully about technology's dual nature. However, the essay currently reads more like a collection of personal observations than a structured academic argument. Below are specific areas to strengthen in your revision.

Thesis and Structure

Your introduction states that computers have brought "both bad and good" changes, but this is more a topic than a thesis. An academic thesis should tell the reader *what specific claim* you will defend and *how* you will organize your support. Consider revising to something like: "While computers have increased efficiency and saved lives through medical and safety innovations, they have also weakened human connection and fostered dangerous overdependence." This gives your reader a roadmap.

Additionally, your essay ends abruprtly in the middle of the "negative effects" paragraph. There is no concluding paragraph that synthesizes your argument. Every academic essay needs a conclusion that revisits your thesis and leaves the reader with a final, considered judgment—not just a hopeful wish tacked onto the last sentence.

Paragraph Development

Paragraph 1 (Introduction): The first three sentences repeat the same idea (computers are new, computers changed the world) without adding new information. Trim this down to 2-3 sentences that move efficiently toward your thesis.

Paragraph 2 (Positive effects): This paragraph is well-organized around clear examples (typewriters vs. computers, ATMs), which is good. However, it ends with a sweeping, unsupported claim: "Basically every facet of life has been affected by computers bringing about positive results." Academic writing needs specific evidence, not generalizations. Consider adding one more concrete example (eg, communication, education, or healthcare) to substantiate this claim, or narrow the claim to something you can actually support.

Paragraph 3 (Negative effects): This paragraph tries to do too much. You raise at least four distinct issues: (1) job loss, (2) loss of human interaction, (3) over-reliance/safety risks, and (4) computers' lack of emotional capacity. Each of these deserves its own paragraph with a clear topic sentence and supporting example. Right now they blend together, making it hard for the reader to follow your reasoning. I recommend splitting this into two or three paragraphs, each focused on one idea.

Evidence and Development

Most of your points rely on personal feeling ("I feel," "I miss," "I fear"). While your voice is a valuable part of persuasive writing, academic essays are stronger when personal impressions are paired with concrete evidence—statistics, real-world examples, or references to expert opinion. For instance, when you mention job losses, you could reference automation in a specific industry. When discussing over-reliance on computers, the "on-coming obstacle" example is good, but you could strengthen it with a real case (eg, airplane autopilot incidents) or scholarly discussion of "automation complacency."

Language and Mechanics

There are several recurring issues to address in revision:

Spelling: "Interraction" → interaction; "obstical" → obstacle; "live" → life (paragraph 2); "proceed" → process (in "proceed with the same transactions").
Repetition of hedging phrases: "I feel," "in my opinion" appear frequently. Use these sparingly; too much hedging weakens your authority as a writer.
Informal punctuation: Capitalizing "EVERYTHING" and using scare quotes around "impersonalized" and "fast enough" reads as informal/emotional rather than analytical. Choose precise vocabulary instead of relying on emphasis through capitalization or quotation marks.
Sentence fragments and awkward phrasing: "Not to mention how it will change people's life styles in the future" is a fragment that should be integrated into a full sentence. Similarly, "Gone are the days when a person who wanted money from a bank had to wait until it was open" is stylistically fine but could be tightened for clarity.
Comma splices: Watch sentences like "This, realistically, has been quite a new invention, and in its short life span has been able to change the world significantly" for run-on structure.

Suggestions for Revision

Write a clear, specific thesis statement that outlines your two main claims.
Trim the repetitive introduction.
Split the negative-effects paragraph into 2–3 focused paragraphs, each with one clear idea and supporting evidence.
Add a concluding paragraph that revisits your thesis and offers a final judgment.
Proofread carefully for spelling and reduce reliance on personal hedging language.
Wherever possible, replace general claims with specific, real-world examples.

You have good instincts about a genuinely debatable issue—now it's a matter of organizing and supporting your ideas with the precision academic writing requires. I look forward to seeing your revision!

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an important and timely topic—cultural awareness and the fight against prejudice—and your passion for this subject comes through clearly. However, the essay currently reads more like a general reflection or opinion piece than an *argumentative* essay. To strengthen it, you'll need to sharpen your thesis, provide concrete evidence, and improve grammatical accuracy. Below are specific areas to address.

Thesis and Argumentative Structure

Your introduction states that "the most significant discovery... will depend on the individual," which suggests a relativistic stance. But then you pivot to argue for *your own* choice (cultural awareness) as significant. This creates a slight contradiction: are you arguing that significance is subjective, or are you arguing that cultural awareness specifically deserves recognition as a major 20th-century "discovery"?

Suggestion: Commit to one clear, arguable claim. For example: *"While technological inventions often dominate discussions of 20th-century progress, the growing societal awareness of racial and cultural prejudice represents an equally significant—and often overlooked—achievement."* This gives you something specific to defend with evidence.

Evidence and Specificity

The essay relies heavily on broad, abstract claims ("society is moving in a new direction," "organizations and forms are being held") without concrete examples. An argumentative essay needs specific evidence—historical events, statistics, named organizations, or scholarly sources—to support claims.

Ask yourself: What organizations? What specific policies or movements (eg, Civil Rights Act, UNESCO initiatives, specific diversity programs)? Can you cite a scholar or researcher who supports your claim that "prejudice must be unlearned"? Adding 2–3 concrete examples would significantly strengthen your credibility.

Paragraph Development

Currently, you have three paragraphs: introduction, one long body paragraph, and a brief conclusion. Consider dividing your ideas into more focused paragraphs, such as:
One paragraph on the historical/past reality of discrimination
One paragraph on specific efforts or evidence of change
One paragraph explaining *why* this shift matters globally (your argument's significance)

This will help you develop each idea more fully rather than compressing multiple points into one dense paragraph.

Language and Grammar Issues

As a non-native speaker, you're doing well conveying complex ideas, but there are recurring errors worth reviewing:

Word choice/spelling: "discriminitive" → *discriminatory*; "excepted" → *accepted*; "enought" → *enough*; "breed" → *bred*; "burried" → *buried*; "avalibility" → *availability*; "existance" → *existence*; "sterotypes" → *stereotypes*
Word confusion: "more then" → *more than*
Apostrophes: "peoples lives" → *people's lives*
Subject-verb agreement: "Organizations and forms are presently being held" — did you mean "forums"? This word choice is unclear and should be revised.
Sentence fragments/run-ons: Several sentences are quite long and combine multiple ideas with commas rather than proper conjunctions or periods. For example: *"Numerous hidden talents, strong wise personal voices and the great knowledge and learning that results from human experience and suffering has been burried under the ignorance of our society."* Consider breaking this into two sentences and fixing the subject-verb agreement (the subject is plural, so it should be "have been buried").

Suggestion: After revising content, do a dedicated grammar/spelling pass, or ask a writing center tutor to review the final draft specifically for these mechanical issues.

Tone and Register

Some phrases feel more like casual spoken reflection than formal academic argument (eg, "I think that," "I greatly value all of humanity"). In academic writing, you can still express personal conviction, but try phrasing it more formally: instead of "I think," use "This essay argues that..." or "It can be argued that..."

Conclusion

Your conclusion introduces a new idea ("unlearning prejudice may not seem like a traditional discovery") that could actually work well as part of your *thesis*. Consider moving this idea earlier and using your conclusion instead to synthesize your argument and perhaps suggest broader implications (eg, what continued progress might look like, or what risks remain if we don't continue this work).

Summary of Next Steps

Revise your thesis to make a clear, specific, arguable claim.
Add concrete historical examples, data, or references to organizations/movements.
Restructure into 4–5 focused paragraphs.
Proofread carefully for spelling, word choice, and sentence-level grammar (or seek writing center support).

You have a meaningful argument here—now it needs sharper focus and stronger evidence to make it persuasive. Keep going!

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Thank you for sharing this draft. You are grappling with a genuinely interesting and important idea—the gap between America's stated values of equality and opportunity and the lived reality of inequality, intensified by media and technology. This is a promising topic, but the essay needs significant revision in three main areas: thesis clarity, paragraph organization, and sentence-level grammar/mechanics. Below is detailed feedback organized by these categories.

Thesis and Argument

Right now, your central claim is buried inside a long, complicated first sentence, and it's not clear what you are actually arguing. Are you claiming that the "American Dream" ideology is a myth? That media reinforces this myth? That inequality is masked by the pace of modern life? Each of these is a strong potential thesis, but the essay currently gestures toward all three without committing to one.

Suggestion: Write a single, clear thesis sentence at the end of your introduction that states your specific claim. For example: *"Although American culture promotes the belief that success depends solely on individual effort, this ideology obscures deep structural inequalities that mass media and the accelerating pace of modern life make easier to ignore."* Once you have this sentence, every paragraph should clearly support it.

Also, the phrase "nothing was ever equal" needs to be explained, not just asserted. Why wasn't it equal? Historically? Economically? You need at least one concrete example (eg, discriminatory laws, wealth gaps, access to education) to support this claim, or the reader will not trust the argument.

Organization and Paragraph Structure

The essay currently reads more like a stream of related observations than a structured argument. The first paragraph introduces the ideology of meritocracy and media; the second paragraph shifts to technology, pace of life, and individualism. The connection between these two ideas exists in your mind, but it is not yet visible to the reader.

Suggestions:
Add a topic sentence to each paragraph that states its main point clearly (eg, "One reason this ideology persists is that mass media constantly reinforces it through idealized images of success.")
After each claim, give a specific example. You mention TV commercials and programs—name one, describe it, and explain what message it sends.
Build an explicit bridge between paragraphs. Right now the shift from "media" to "technology and pace of life" feels abrupt. Consider a transition sentence: "This idealized image is reinforced not only by media content but also by the fast pace of life media and technology have created."
The final sentence—"The parts of the new machine that need oiled and regulated"—is a fragment and ends the paragraph without resolution. It reads as a powerful image, but it needs a complete sentence and a clear connection to your argument. Consider expanding this metaphor into a fuller comparison (society as a machine, people as parts) rather than leaving it as an isolated phrase.

Sentence-Level Grammar and Mechanics

There are frequent grammar and spelling errors that distract the reader from your ideas. I recommend careful proofreading and, if possible, reading your essay aloud to catch awkward phrasing. Some specific examples:

Run-on/comma splice: "A current ideology... one must follow all of the written & unwritten rules of society, everyone has an equal opportunity to succeed and when one does not do this one fails." This is one long sentence with multiple independent clauses. Break it into two or three sentences.
Capitalization: "americans" should be "Americans." Country/nationality names are always capitalized in English.
Spelling: "comercials" → commercials; "substitued" → substituted; "nolonger" → no longer; "unsuccessfull" → unsuccessful; "thier" → their; "realitive" → relative.
Abbreviations: "20th cen" should be written out as "20th century" in formal writing; avoid "&" in academic prose—use "and."
Article/preposition issues: "the values of/that the United States was founded on" — choose one construction; the slash indicates you were unsure, but this should be resolved before submission: "the values on which the United States was founded."
Word choice: "needs oiled" should be "needs to be oiled" (missing infinitive marker).

Overall Recommendation

Your ideas are ambitious and show real critical thinking about American culture, but the essay needs a clearer thesis, better-organized paragraphs with topic sentences and examples, and careful proofreading for grammar and spelling. I suggest you:

Write one clear thesis sentence.
Outline 2–3 body paragraphs, each with a topic sentence and at least one concrete example.
Revise sentence structure to avoid run-ons—read each sentence aloud and check it expresses one complete idea.
Proofread carefully for spelling and capitalization, or use a tool like Grammarly for a first pass before your final revision.
