Feedback on Your Essay: Technology as Friend and Nemesis

Thank you for sharing this thoughtful and personal essay. Your willingness to draw on your own experience—crossing an ocean, learning languages later than you wished, noticing your peers' unfamiliarity with world geography—gives the essay a strong, distinctive voice. However, the essay currently has some structural and logical gaps that make it hard for readers to follow your argument from start to finish. Below are the main areas to work on.

Clarify the Central Argument

Your introduction sets up a compelling thesis: technology is both humanity's "closest friend" and its "greatest Nemesis." Paragraph 2 supports the "friend" side well with vivid personal examples (travel, your grandmother's cancer treatment, your family's livelihood). But the essay never clearly returns to explain *how* technology becomes the "Nemesis." Instead, it shifts into a critique of American education—lack of geography classes, limited foreign-language options—without explicitly connecting this back to technology.

Ask yourself: What is the causal link between technology and American insularity? Right now, the connection is implied but never stated. Is your argument that technology *made the world feel smaller* while people's actual knowledge of it stayed shallow? Is it that convenience (GPS, translation apps, instant information) breeds a false sense of "self-sufficiency" that discourages people from learning languages or geography themselves? If so, say this directly—perhaps as a topic sentence at the start of paragraph 3, such as: *"Ironically, while technology has connected the world physically, it has allowed many Americans to remain culturally and geographically isolated."* This single sentence would tie your two examples together and make your thesis coherent.

Strengthen Paragraph Unity and Transitions

Each paragraph should open with a sentence that clearly signals its relationship to your thesis. Currently, paragraph 3 begins with an anecdote (children asking if Spain is in Michigan) before you've explained *why* this anecdote matters to your argument about technology. Consider reordering: state your claim first, then use the anecdote as evidence.

Also, the essay ends somewhat abruptly on the sentence: *"The effort of the United States has caused our minds to be heard, and a world that is growing while my country lags behind in its selfish self-satisfaction."* This sentence is difficult to parse (I suspect "heard" may be a typo for "closed" or "narrowed"?). A strong conclusion should restate your dual thesis (friend/nemesis) and reflect on what should change—perhaps a call for better education systems that use technology to *cultivate* global awareness rather than replace it.

Sentence-Level Clarity

Several sentences are grammatically ambiguous or overly compressed. A few examples:

*"The use of the means humans have developed is wherein the greatest problems lie."* This is abstract and hard to follow. Consider: "The danger lies not in technology itself, but in how humans choose to use it."
*"when they become welded with certain aspects of and certain people in our societies"* — "welded" is a striking word choice, but the phrase "certain aspects of and certain people in" is confusing. Try: "when technology becomes entangled with certain social attitudes or misused by certain people."
*"the same 'I am a self-sufficient- I don't need any help' type of mentality"* — the punctuation here (hyphen instead of comma or colon) disrupts the quotation. Revise as: *"the same 'I am self-sufficient; I don't need anyone's help' mentality."*
*"Our awareness of other people, other cultures, and other languages is very low on the priority list of our educators."* This is clear and effective—more sentences like this, direct and declarative, would strengthen the essay's clarity overall.

Watch Comma Splices and Run-ons

Sentences like *"Since I was twelve, I had an intense desire to learn Spanish, and because of these circumstances, that I mentioned before, I had no choice but to wait until I was twenty years old..."* pack too many ideas into one sentence. Consider breaking this into two: one sentence establishing your early desire, a second explaining the institutional barrier that delayed it.

Minor Points

Capitalizing "Nemesis" is a stylistic choice, but be consistent—make sure readers understand this is deliberate emphasis, not a typo.
"Geography classes" and other subject names should generally be lowercase unless part of a specific course title.
Double-check verb tense consistency, especially in paragraph 2, where you shift between present perfect ("have allowed," "have cured") smoothly, but later paragraphs mix tenses more loosely.

Summary

Your essay has a compelling personal voice and an ambitious central idea. To make it fully persuasive, (1) explicitly connect your examples of American insularity back to your technology thesis, (2) strengthen topic sentences and transitions between paragraphs, (3) revise a few unclear or run-on sentences, and (4) add a conclusion that returns to your "friend and Nemesis" framing. With these revisions, this essay will be both logically tight and emotionally resonant.

Feedback Report on Your Essay: "How Could People Live Without the Invention of the Computer?"

Overall Impression

You've chosen a relevant and relatable topic, and your enthusiasm for explaining how computers have transformed daily life comes through clearly. Your essay has a logical structure with an introduction, body paragraphs, and conclusion. However, the essay currently reads more like a description of computer use than an argument. To strengthen this piece, you need a clearer thesis, more specific evidence, and tighter organization. Below are detailed suggestions.

Strengthen Your Thesis Statement

Your introduction ends with "Computers have significantly changed people's lives," which is a true statement but not yet an *arguable claim*. Ask yourself: what is the specific argument you want to make?

For example, are you arguing that:
computers have made life *more efficient* but also *more dependent and vulnerable*?
the benefits of computers *outweigh* their drawbacks?
society's reliance on computers has fundamentally changed how we define productivity?

Your final paragraph actually introduces an interesting tension (dependency and vulnerability when computers fail), but this idea appears too late. Move this idea into your thesis so readers know from the start that you will discuss both benefits and drawbacks.

Revision suggestion: "While computers have made daily tasks faster and more efficient, this convenience has also created a deep dependency that reveals itself whenever technology fails."

Add Specific Evidence and Examples

Right now, your essay relies on general statements: "Computers speed up tasks," "Businesses use computers because they can be more productive." These claims need concrete examples to be persuasive.

Ask yourself:
*Which* laborious tasks are now automated? (eg, accounting, word processing, data storage)
*What kind* of information do students look up, and how has this changed learning?
Can you provide a specific example of computer failure and its consequences? (eg, a hospital system crash, an airport delay, a business losing customer data)

Specific examples will make your argument far more convincing than general claims.

Clarify a Factual Point

You mention "the calculator (the first computer)" — this equation may confuse readers. Calculators and computers are related but distinct inventions with different histories. If you want to discuss the *history* of computing, briefly clarify what you mean (eg, "early computing devices, such as calculators, laid the groundwork for modern computers"). Otherwise, consider removing this reference to avoid raising questions that distract from your main argument.

Improve Paragraph Focus and Transitions

Each paragraph should have one clear purpose. Currently:
Paragraph 2 discusses where computers are found.
Paragraph 3 discusses *why* people rely on computers.
Paragraph 4 discusses changes since computers were invented.
Paragraph 5 (conclusion) introduces the *drawback* of dependency — a new idea.

Consider restructuring so that:
One paragraph explains the *benefits* of computers (efficiency, productivity, job skills).
Another paragraph explains the *drawbacks* (dependency, vulnerability during failures).
Your conclusion synthesizes both sides and returns to your thesis.

This will make your essay feel more like a balanced argument rather than a list of separate points.

Watch for Repetition and Redundant Phrasing

Several sentences repeat the same idea in a slightly different way. For example, "Computers make things more efficient and often do things faster than humans" and "Computers speed-up tasks that used to take up a lot of time" say very similar things. Try to combine related ideas and avoid restating the same point three or four times across paragraphs.

Minor Language Corrections

A few small edits will improve clarity:
"calculators as usually found" → "calculators are usually found"
"laboreous" → "laborious"
"speed-up" → "speed up" (no hyphen needed as a verb)
The final sentence is missing a closing quotation mark: add `"` after "computer?"
Consider varying sentence openings — several paragraphs begin with "Computers" or "People," which becomes repetitive.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Sharpen your thesis to include a clear, arguable claim (benefits *and* drawbacks).
Add specific examples to support general claims.
Reorganize paragraphs so each has a distinct focus tied to your thesis.
Reduce repetition of similar ideas.
Proofread for small grammar and punctuation issues.

You have a solid foundation and an interesting angle already present in your conclusion — bring that insight about dependency earlier and develop it with evidence throughout. This will transform your essay from a general description into a compelling argument. Keep up the good work!

Feedback Report on Essay Draft

Thank you for sharing this ambitious and thought-provoking essay. You're grappling with genuinely difficult ideas—the philosophical implications of relativity theory—and your enthusiasm for the topic comes through clearly. However, the essay needs significant revision in several areas: argumentative structure, factual accuracy, clarity of expression, and mechanics. Let me address these systematically.

Central Argument and Structure

Your essay currently moves through several loosely connected claims: (1) relativity hasn't had its full philosophical impact, (2) it changed our notion of time, (3) atomic bombs are a negative application of Einstein's ideas, and (4) humanity should shift from fear to peace. Each of these could be a compelling thesis, but together they create a diffuse essay without a clear governing argument.

Suggestion: Choose ONE central claim and build your paragraphs to support it. For example, you might argue: "Einstein's theory of relativity fundamentally challenged humanity's assumption that ultimate knowledge is attainable, yet we have not fully absorbed this philosophical lesson—instead applying his discoveries toward destructive ends like nuclear weapons." This would let you connect your Socratic reference, your discussion of time, and your point about atomic bombs into one coherent argument rather than three separate points.

Factual Accuracy

Several factual issues need correction:
Special/General relativity was developed by Einstein alone, not "he and his wife" (Mileva Marić's contributions are debated by historians, but this is not standard attribution and requires careful sourcing if you wish to include it).
The atomic bomb was developed by many scientists (the Manhattan Project), not by Einstein himself—his relationship to it was indirect (he signed a letter urging research, but did not build the bomb). This distinction matters for your argument about "genius" being misused.
Japan was not a "winner" of WWII—it was defeated. Please revise this historical claim.

Suggestion: Since your argument rests partly on historical events, verify these details carefully, as inaccuracies undermine your credibility.

Clarity and Precision of Language

Some phrases are too abstract or private in meaning for an academic reader:
"Every point is a point of departure and all other points are in reference to that point of departure" — try explaining this more concretely with an example (eg, how simultaneity or measurement depends on the observer's frame of reference).
"Indwell the topic" and "sidphantha and his rock" — these references are unclear to readers unfamiliar with your personal vocabulary or the allusion (is this Siddhartha, the Buddha, and a related parable?). Please identify sources explicitly and explain the connection.
"Relatively speaking, fear has been the main point of departure" — this pun is clever, but risks feeling glib in a serious argument. Use wordplay carefully in academic writing.

Suggestion: For a general academic audience, unpack your metaphors and cultural/philosophical references so readers can follow your reasoning without needing to share your specific background knowledge.

Mechanics and Proofreading

There are frequent spelling and punctuation issues to address:
"Einstien" should be "Einstein" (repeated throughout)
"histories" → "history's"
"nostolgic" → "nostalgic"
"Hawkings" → "Hawking"
"socratic" → "Socratic" (capitalize proper nouns)
Semicolons are used where periods or commas would be more appropriate (eg, "USA-USSR; with Japan and Germany as the 'winners'")
Sentence fragments appear where full sentences are needed ("A shift in focus if you will." "But if humanity sought to understand relativity...")

Suggestion: After revising for content, do a dedicated proofreading pass focused only on spelling and punctuation. Reading your essay aloud can help you catch fragments and awkward phrasing.

Paragraph Development

Your second paragraph moves quickly from relativity to Socrates to "the philosopher's stone" without fully explaining the connections. Each of these ideas deserves 2-3 sentences of development so readers can follow your logic. Similarly, your final paragraph introduces the Cold War, nuclear geopolitics, and a call for "peace over fear" very rapidly—consider slowing down and developing one point at a time.

Overall Recommendation

This essay shows real intellectual curiosity and an attempt to synthesize science, philosophy, and history—an ambitious project. For revision, I recommend:
Narrow your thesis to one focused claim
Verify all historical/scientific facts
Explain your more abstract or personal references
Proofread carefully for spelling, capitalization, and complete sentences

I'd be happy to discuss these revisions further in office hours if that would help. Keep pursuing these big questions—they're worth exploring carefully.

Overall Impression

You've chosen a compelling, personal topic and clearly know the history of Kalamazoo well. Your argument—that suburban malls have deepened inequality by draining resources from downtown areas that poorer residents depend on—is a strong, debatable claim with real analytical potential. However, the essay needs revision in three main areas: sharpening the thesis and keeping it visible throughout, strengthening paragraph structure and logical connections, and cleaning up grammar and sentence-level issues. Below are specific suggestions.

Thesis and Argument Development

Your thesis states that malls are "more of a detriment than a blessing," but the middle paragraphs read mostly as historical narrative rather than argument. Ask yourself after every paragraph: *How does this paragraph prove malls are harmful?* Right now, readers must infer the connection.

For example, the paragraph about colleges and festivals attracting business to downtown is interesting, but it's unclear whether this supports or complicates your thesis. Are these efforts working? Are they enough to reverse the "disintegration" you describe? You should evaluate this evidence explicitly rather than just presenting it.

Also, several claims are asserted without support:
"Throughout history, the trend is for the rich to get richer and the poor remain poor" — this broad historical generalization needs either a citation, a narrower claim about Kalamazoo specifically, or removal.
"I believe it is only the storeowners who realize this process is occurring or enjoy the downtown district remain and stay in business" — this is a strong claim (that only *aware* or *loyal* owners stay) that needs evidence or should be softened.

Suggestion: Consider adding concrete evidence—statistics on downtown vacancy rates, specific store closures/relocations, or income data comparing suburban and downtown Kalamazoo residents. This would move the essay from personal observation toward persuasive argument.

Structure and Coherence

The essay lacks clear topic sentences that link each paragraph back to your central claim. Right now, paragraphs are organized chronologically (history → colleges/festivals → conclusion) rather than argumentatively (cause → effect → who is harmed → why it matters).

Also, watch for fragments that seem to be notes-to-self rather than finished sentences:
"and still could be!" — this appears to be an isolated fragment, disconnected from the previous sentence. Please integrate it into a full sentence or delete it.
The rhetorical question ("Why should we, say those with cars, go downtown...") is effective, but it appears mid-paragraph without clear framing—tell the reader *before* the question that you are about to voice the perspective of wealthier shoppers.

Suggestion: Try outlining your essay with one clear claim per paragraph:
Historical background (why the city was structured as it was)
How wealth and mobility caused the shift to suburbs/malls
Downtown's remaining assets and current struggles
The human cost: who is hurt and why this matters

Grammar, Word Choice, and Mechanics

Since English article usage and spelling can be tricky for Chinese speakers, here are patterns to watch for:

Articles: "the invention of the shopping mall" is fine, but check phrases like "near the central means of transportation" (awkward—consider "the main mode of transportation").
Homophone/spelling errors:
  - "clientel" → *clientele*
  - "There neighborhoods" → *Their neighborhoods*
  - "flu from the inner cities" → likely meant *flight* from the inner cities (this is an important error since it affects meaning!)
  - "caf​é" (encoding issue) → check for special characters not rendering correctly.
Comparative errors: "where the more poorer people are" → *where the poorer people are* (avoid double comparatives).
Subject-verb agreement: "the could keep a distance" → *they could keep a distance.*
Tense consistency: You shift between past tense (historical narrative) and present tense (current commentary) somewhat abruptly. This can work rhetorically, but make the shifts intentional—for instance, clearly signal when you move from past history to present-day analysis ("Today, this pattern continues...").

Style Notes

Your use of a hypothetical wealthy shopper's voice ("Why should we... go to a nice indoor mall") is a nice rhetorical technique—it adds voice and perspective. Consider using this technique once more, perhaps giving voice to a downtown resident, to create balance and emotional resonance in your conclusion.

Summary of Priority Revisions

Add clearer topic sentences connecting each paragraph to your thesis.
Strengthen or cut unsupported generalizations; add specific evidence where possible.
Fix the fragment "and still could be!" and revise "flu" to "flight."
Proofread for articles, spelling, and subject-verb agreement.
Reconsider paragraph order to build a cause-effect argument rather than a chronological narrative.

You have a genuinely interesting, locally-grounded argument here—with revision, this could become a compelling piece of persuasive writing.

Feedback Report: Essay on Television's Impact on Society

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an interesting and debatable topic—the paradox of television as both an educational tool and a societal distraction. You have a clear thesis and a logical three-part structure (introduction, potential vs. reality, consequences). However, the essay would benefit from more specific evidence, sharper argumentation, and careful editing for grammar and word choice. Below are detailed suggestions organized by area.

Thesis and Argument Development

Your central claim—that television *could* have enriched society but has instead become an "escape route"—is a strong, debatable thesis. However, the essay currently asserts this claim more than it proves it. For example, you state that "television has become the escape route of the masses" and that Americans regard TV as "diversion rather than educational tool," but you offer no concrete evidence (statistics, studies, expert opinions, or even specific examples of shows) to support these claims. Consider adding:
A statistic about average TV viewing hours per day/week.
A specific example contrasting an educational program (eg, a nature documentary) with popular entertainment (eg, a specific sitcom) to make your contrast vivid.
A brief acknowledgment of a counterargument (eg, "Some may argue that streaming services and educational channels have improved television's role...") followed by your rebuttal. This would make your argument feel more balanced and persuasive rather than one-sided.

Structure and Paragraph Coherence

Your structure is logical, but transitions between paragraphs could be smoother. Paragraph 2 discusses television's *potential*, Paragraph 3 discusses its *actual effects*, and Paragraph 4 is a conclusion—but the conclusion largely repeats Paragraph 2's ideas rather than synthesizing new insight. Consider:
Making the conclusion more forward-looking: What should society do about this problem? Should there be changes in TV programming, family habits, or regulation? A concluding paragraph that offers a brief recommendation or broader implication would leave a stronger final impression.
Paragraph 3 conflates two ideas—family togetherness and laziness—somewhat abruptly. Consider dividing this into two clearer points, each with its own support, or explicitly link them (eg, explain *how* laziness stems from convenience, and *how* that same convenience undermines family interaction).

Language and Grammar

As a non-native English speaker, you're doing well overall, but there are recurring issues to address:

Spelling errors:
"integrety" → *integrity*
"dificulties" → *difficulties*
"becomed" → *became* (also see verb tense note below)

Article and preposition usage:
"a closer look at current events than cannot be given by newspaper" — this sentence has a grammar error; it should read something like "a closer look at current events than can be given by newspapers." (Remove "not," and consider whether "newspaper" needs to be plural or preceded by "a/the.")
"00 years ago" — this appears to be a typo; you likely meant "100 years ago" or "a hundred years ago" (as used in your introduction). Please proofread numbers carefully.

Verb tense consistency:
"society has becomed more informed" — should be "has become." Watch for irregular past participles.
The essay shifts between present tense ("television has power") and present perfect ("has changed")—this is often appropriate for discussing ongoing trends, but check each sentence to ensure the tense matches your intended meaning (general truth vs. change over time).

Word choice/register:
"stare at a box" is a nice informal touch, but make sure your tone remains consistent. Elsewhere your language is formal ("cultural programs," "artistic expressions"), so an occasional colloquial phrase can work for emphasis, but use it sparingly and intentionally.

Pronoun agreement:
"The more time one spends watching TV, the less motivated he becomes" — using "he" here excludes half your readers. Consider using "one becomes" or "they become" for gender-neutral language, which is now standard in academic writing.

Style Suggestions

Avoid starting too many sentences with "However" (used four times). Vary your transitional phrases: "Nevertheless," "In contrast," "Yet," "Despite this potential..."
Sentences like "Millions of Americans can be found to be staring at the T.V. at any one time" could be more concise: "At any given moment, millions of Americans are watching television."

Acknowledge a counterargument briefly to strengthen your credibility.
Proofread carefully for spelling, verb tense, and the "00 years" typo.
Revise the conclusion to offer new insight or a recommendation, rather than repeating earlier points.

You have a clear, thoughtful argument here—these revisions will help make it more convincing and polished. Keep up the strong critical thinking!

Feedback on "The Polio Vaccine" Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an interesting and important topic—the transformative impact of the polio vaccine—and you clearly have a personal connection to the subject that comes through in your writing. However, the essay currently reads more like a first draft of ideas than a polished argumentative piece. Below are suggestions organized by priority to help you strengthen the next revision.

Thesis and Argument Development

Your introduction promises two main points: that the vaccine (a) "changed the population" and (b) "acted as a foundation for further medical advances." This is a strong, two-part thesis—but you only develop the first point in any depth. The second point appears again briefly in your final paragraph ("opened new doors") but is never fully explained or supported. 

Suggestion: Either commit to developing both points with equal attention (perhaps in separate body paragraphs), or narrow your thesis to focus on just one claim and explore it more thoroughly. Right now, the essay feels unbalanced.

Logical Clarity in the Population Argument

Your discussion of the Baby Boom is the most developed part of the essay, but the reasoning is difficult to follow. You state that the vaccine "did not CAUSE" the Baby Boom, but then suggest it contributed through increased "life span." You also introduce the idea that disease normally functions as "population control," which is a fascinating claim—but it appears without any support and somewhat undercuts your praise of the vaccine (if disease controls overpopulation, is eliminating it necessarily good?).

Suggestion: Slow down and walk your reader through the causal chain step by step. Ask yourself: What exactly is the relationship between the vaccine and population growth? Is it correlation or causation? A clearer topic sentence stating your specific claim before you explore its complexity would help orient the reader.

Organization

The essay would benefit from a clearer paragraph structure. Consider this framework:
Paragraph 1: Introduction with a focused thesis
Paragraph 2: First point (eg, demographic impact) with a clear topic sentence, evidence, and analysis
Paragraph 3: Second point (eg, foundation for future medical research—what specific advances followed from Salk's methods? mRNA vaccines? Other vaccination programs?)
Paragraph 4: Conclusion that returns to and synthesizes both points

Right now, the conclusion is very short and doesn't do the work of tying your ideas together.

Evidence and Specificity

Currently, the essay relies heavily on general impressions rather than concrete evidence. For an argumentative essay, you'll want to include:
Specific dates (When exactly was the vaccine introduced? 1955?)
Statistics (How many polio cases were there before/after? How much did infant/child mortality change?)
Named sources or historical context (What historians or public health experts have written about this?)

Adding this kind of evidence will make your claims more persuasive and academic.

Language and Mechanics

Since English is your second language, here are some specific issues to address:

Spelling: "mamed" should be "maimed" (this occurs twice)
Repetition error: "polio has never never been an issue" — remove the duplicate word
Run-on sentences: Several sentences are joined with commas where periods or conjunctions are needed. For example: *"With this new vaccination less children were dying, or being mamed, and had a greater chance of having a healthy childhood."* This could be split into two clearer sentences.
Comma splices: *"The effects of such a vaccine are countless and inequivalent to any other invention of the 20th century. Why was it so great?"* — consider whether "inequivalent" is the word you want; "unmatched" or "incomparable" may sound more natural.
Informal tone: Capitalizing "CAUSE" for emphasis is a technique better suited to casual writing. In academic prose, use italics sparingly or rephrase for emphasis (eg, "The vaccine did not directly cause the Baby Boom, but...").
Fragment: *"For sure there was an effect on the population as a whole..."* — "For sure" is conversational; consider "Certainly" or "Undoubtedly."

Suggestion: After revising for content, do a separate proofreading pass focused only on grammar and mechanics. Reading your essay aloud can help you catch run-ons and awkward phrasing.

Next Steps

Revise your thesis to clearly state both claims you intend to prove.
Add a full paragraph developing the "foundation for future medical advances" point with specific examples.
Clarify the logical steps in your Baby Boom argument.

Overall Impression

Thesis and Argumentation

Your introduction states that the airplane "has drastically changed the lives of many people," but this is a very broad, largely undisputed claim. A strong argumentative essay needs a thesis that takes a position someone could reasonably disagree with, or at least offers a specific analytical angle. Consider narrowing your focus: Are you arguing that the airplane's benefits outweigh its costs? That it has been the single most transformative invention of the modern era? That its impact has been uneven (benefiting global elites more than others)? Your concluding sentence actually gestures toward a more interesting claim—that the airplane brought both positive *and* negative change—but this tension is introduced only in the final paragraph. If this is truly your argument, it should be signaled in your introduction and explored more evenly throughout the essay, not just in the last section.

Additionally, each body paragraph currently offers one example and a general assertion, but doesn't explain *why* the example matters or *how* it supports your claim. For instance, in the business paragraph, you assert that "international business has increased" but don't explain the mechanism (faster travel enables face-to-face meetings, quicker deal-making, supply chain coordination, etc). Push yourself to add one or two sentences of analysis after each example.

Use of Evidence

Your personal example (Michigan to California travel times) is effective and concrete—more of this specificity would help. However, some claims rely on impressionistic personal experience ("I have seen this...") rather than evidence. For an academic essay, try to incorporate at least one outside source or statistic (eg, data on international trade growth, tourism statistics, or a historical account of the Pearl Harbor attack) to lend credibility. This would also help balance your reliance on personal anecdotes (your Spain trip, the Michael Jackson concert).

Consider expanding this section, or restructuring the whole essay so that "positive and negative effects" becomes your organizing framework instead of "travel/business/entertainment/politics." This would give your essay a more argumentative, less descriptive, structure.

Your conclusion is very brief and simply restates the last point. Consider adding a real concluding paragraph that returns to your thesis, synthesizes the four areas you discussed, and perhaps offers a broader reflection (eg, on how we might weigh benefits against harms, or what this suggests about technology in general).

Language and Mechanics

Because this is a formal essay, a few conventions should be adjusted:
Avoid contractions in academic writing: "would've" → "would have"; "I've" → "I have"; "I'm" → "I will."
Spelling: "efficeintly" → "efficiently"; "Habor" → "Harbor"; "musicans" → "musicians"; "choosen" → "chosen."
Word confusion: "effected" should be "affected" (to affect = to influence; to effect = to bring about). This appears several times.
"shorten" → "shortened" (past participle needed after "have been").
"previously possibly" is unclear—perhaps you meant "previously possible."
"perspective countries" → "respective countries."
Numerals: In formal writing, spell out numbers under 100 or that begin a sentence ("30-35 hours" is fine as data, but "1 week" should be "one week").
Punctuation: Watch comma splices, eg, "Travel times have been significantly shorten; for example..." (this one is fine), but check others like "This area has been positively and negatively effected." needs a comma before "too" in the next sentence, and quotation marks around "Peace Talks" are unnecessary unless quoting a specific source.
The ampersand (&) should not be used in formal academic prose; write out "and."

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an important and emotionally resonant topic, and your instinct to connect a medical phenomenon to social change (stigma, shifting attitudes toward sex and relationships) is a promising direction for an argumentative essay. However, the essay currently reads more like a collection of related observations than a tightly argued piece. Below are suggestions organized by argument, structure, evidence, and language.

Sharpen Your Thesis and Argument

Right now, it's unclear what you are actually arguing. Are you claiming that:
AIDS increased social prejudice and division, or
AIDS made people more cautious and responsible, or
Both, and one outweighs the other?

Your final paragraph suggests a fairly optimistic conclusion (people are more careful, relationships are valued more), but your middle paragraphs emphasize fear, ignorance, and cruelty (the Ryan White case). These are somewhat contradictory threads. Decide on your central claim—for instance: *"While AIDS initially fueled prejudice and fear, it ultimately pushed society toward more responsible and thoughtful behavior."* Then make sure every paragraph supports that specific claim rather than introducing new, loosely related ideas.

Strengthen Structure and Transitions

Each paragraph currently reads like a separate mini-topic (stigma → Ryan White → undetectable strains → changing sexual behavior) without clear logical connections. Consider:
Adding a topic sentence to each paragraph that ties back to your thesis.
Using transitional phrases ("This fear was intensified when...", "As a result of this stigma...") to show how ideas build on one another.
Combining the Ryan White paragraph with the stigma paragraph, since they make the same point (AIDS caused irrational prejudice), rather than separating them.
Writing a proper concluding paragraph. The essay currently ends mid-thought; you need a conclusion that restates your argument and reflects on its significance.

Develop and Support Your Evidence

Several claims need more support or qualification:
"It always leads to death and there is no cure" — this is a strong, sweeping claim. Even by the time period you seem to be describing, this needs a citation, and you should be cautious about absolute language ("always," "no cure") unless you can verify and date the claim precisely.
The Ryan White example is your strongest piece of evidence—use it more fully. When did this happen? What exactly did the school/community do? A brief, specific detail (dates, direct consequences) will make this far more persuasive than a general summary.
"Undetectable strands of AIDS" — this claim needs a source. Where did you learn this? Without evidence, it reads as speculation.
"Casual sex... rarely occurs [now]" — this is a significant, debatable claim about a whole society's behavior. You need statistical or sourced evidence to support it; otherwise, it will seem like unsupported personal opinion.

Watch Your Tone

Phrases like "I feel this has really made people think" and "I'm sure more changes will come about" are too informal and too personal for academic argumentation. Reframe these as evidence-based claims: instead of "I feel," write "Evidence suggests" or "This shift indicates." Save first-person reflection for a personal essay, not an argumentative one.

Language and Mechanics

Because English is your second language, here are some patterns to watch for in revision:

Apostrophe confusion: "it's" (contraction of "it is") vs. "its" (possessive). You use "it's" when you mean "its" throughout paragraph 1 ("it's presence," "it's consequences").
Spelling: "peoples" → "people's"; "awhile" → "a while"; "untill" → "until"; "devistating" → "devastating"; "definately" → "definitely"; "strands" → "strains" (a scientific term for AIDS variants).
Comma splices/run-ons: Several sentences join two independent clauses with only a comma (eg, "This kind of ignorant thinking is breaking us apart" is fine, but earlier sentences like "there is more of stigma... but they probably have AIDS too" are grammatically tangled). Read each sentence aloud to check if it expresses one clear idea.
Article and preposition usage: "there is more of stigma" should be "there is more stigma"; "In the early eighties 'casual sex'... was a widely accepted idea" is fine, but check subject-verb agreement throughout.

Summary of Next Steps

Write a clear, one-sentence thesis and revise paragraphs to support it directly.
Reorganize paragraphs so related ideas (stigma/fear vs. behavioral change) are grouped logically, with transitions.
Add specific dates, sources, or citations for factual claims.
Replace personal-opinion phrasing with evidence-based academic language.

Feedback on Your Essay: Nuclear Weapons as a Force of Historical Change

Thank you for sharing this thoughtful and ambitious essay. You are grappling with a genuinely significant historical question, and your personal reflections on growing up during the Cold War add an authentic voice to your argument. Below are some observations to help you strengthen the essay in a revision.

Argument and Content

Your thesis claims that nuclear weapons have been "the most significant factors of change in our lifetime." This is a strong, debatable claim—exactly what a good thesis should be. However, the essay currently *describes* Cold War fear and its recent thawing more than it *argues* for the superlative claim you've made. To convince a skeptical reader, consider:

Addressing the comparison implied by "most significant." Why nuclear weapons rather than, say, the rise of computing, decolonization, or globalization? Even a brief acknowledgment of competing candidates for "most significant change," followed by your reasons for ranking nuclear weapons above them, would make your argument more persuasive.
Supporting claims with concrete evidence. Phrases like "uncountable trillions of dollars" and "9 presidential administrations" gesture toward evidence but remain vague. A specific figure (even approximate), a named policy (eg, the arms race, SALT treaties, MAD doctrine), or a cited event would carry more weight than general impressions.
Clarifying the relationship between your two main points. Paragraph 2 argues that nuclear weapons bred fear and division; Paragraph 3 argues that this fear is now fading. Is your point that the *speed and scale* of this recent change proves how powerful nuclear weapons were as a historical force? Make this logical connection explicit—perhaps in a topic sentence at the start of paragraph 3.

Organization

Your three-paragraph structure (thesis → Cold War costs → post-Cold War change) is a reasonable skeleton, but each paragraph would benefit from a clearer topic sentence that ties back to your central claim. Currently, paragraph 2 opens with a description ("As missile technology... increased") rather than a claim, and paragraph 3 shifts into a fairly emotional, hopeful conclusion without fully returning to your original thesis about "significance." Consider ending with a short concluding paragraph that explicitly restates why this history matters and reasserts your thesis in light of the evidence you've given.

Language and Style

Because English article usage and sentence boundaries differ significantly from Chinese, here are some specific patterns to watch for:

Missing/incorrect articles: "the discovery and harness of atom" → "the discovery and harnessing of *the* atom." English nouns referring to specific, known things usually require "the."
Dangling/fragmented openings: "Though advances in communication, transportation, and information sharing, the world... has rapidly shrunk." Check whether you mean "Through" (indicating means) rather than "Though" (indicating contrast)—these have very different meanings and are easy to confuse. Also, the clause after the comma needs to connect grammatically to the introductory phrase.
Word choice/collocation: "arrived a standstill" should be "reached a standstill" or "come to a standstill." "Uncountable trillions" is unusual; try "countless" or simply state the number.
Spelling and compounding: "cheif" → "chief"; "commandpost" → "command post" (two words); "bombshelters" → "bomb shelters."
Subject-verb/number agreement: "the most significant factors" (plural) doesn't quite match "the discovery... and the... invention" (which could be read as one combined idea—consider "factor" singular, or rephrase as "have been among the most significant forces").
Mixed metaphor at the end: "our frigid planet and cold neighbors may once again be a warm and friendly people" mixes "planet," "neighbors," and "people" as if they were the same grammatical subject. Revise for clarity, eg, "our world may once again become warm and welcoming."

Suggestions for Revision

Read your essay aloud, sentence by sentence, listening for places where the grammar doesn't quite hold together (especially long opening clauses).
After each paragraph, ask: "Does this clearly support my claim that nuclear weapons are the *most* significant change-agent of our era?"
Add at least one concrete historical fact or figure per paragraph to move from impression to evidence.
Write a short concluding paragraph that circles back explicitly to your thesis.

You have real writing talent and a compelling personal perspective—this revision is about sharpening the logical scaffolding and tightening the language so your ideas land with full force. I look forward to seeing the next draft!

Feedback on "The Discovery of Penicillin" Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a fascinating topic, and your writing shows a solid grasp of the historical narrative surrounding antibiotic development. However, since this is meant to be an argumentative essay, the current draft reads more like a historical summary or informative report. My main suggestion is to identify a clear, debatable claim and restructure the essay to support it. Below are specific areas to address.

Missing Argumentative Thesis

Right now, your essay traces a sequence of events (penicillin discovered → other antibiotics found → industry grows → research continues today) without making a claim that someone could disagree with. Ask yourself: *What is my essay arguing?* Some possibilities:

Penicillin's discovery fundamentally *changed* the pharmaceutical industry's approach to drug development (from natural discovery to synthesis).
The commercialization of antibiotics, while beneficial, also raised concerns (eg, overuse, resistance) that are underexplored in your narrative.
The rapid growth of the antibiotic industry was driven more by economic incentive than by medical necessity.

Choose one focused claim, state it explicitly in your introduction, and make sure every paragraph works to prove it. Currently, your conclusion simply restates the sequence of events rather than asserting why this history matters or what it proves.

Lack of Evidence and Sources

You mention specific claims (eg, Upjohn's growth, university research teams, the shift to synthetic production) but provide no citations, dates, or figures. For an argumentative essay, you need concrete evidence: statistics on antibiotic production, named researchers or discoveries, or specific dates marking the shift from natural to synthetic antibiotics. Vague phrases like "a variety of new drugs" or "huge enterprises" should be replaced with specific examples (which drugs? which companies, and when?).

Structural Suggestions

Introduction: State your thesis clearly at the end of the first paragraph, after providing brief context.
Body paragraphs: Organize around *reasons* supporting your thesis, not strictly chronological events. For example, if your thesis is about the shift from natural discovery to synthetic production reshaping medicine, one paragraph could focus on the scientific shift, another on the economic/industrial shift, and another on the social impact (eg, easier access to antibiotics for common illnesses).
Conclusion: Don't just summarize—explain the broader significance of your argument. Why should the reader care about this shift? Does it have implications for how we think about drug development today (eg, antibiotic resistance, pharmaceutical ethics)?

Language and Style

Your English is generally clear, but there are a few recurring issues:

a) Overuse of passive voice: Phrases like "It was discovered that...," "It was also attempted...," and "It was now much easier..." appear repeatedly. This creates a flat, impersonal tone and obscures *who* is doing the discovering or attempting. Since this is an argumentative essay, using more active constructions will make your voice stronger and clearer:
Instead of: "It was also attempted to increase the production of the naturally-occurring antibiotics through synthesis."
Try: "Chemists also attempted to increase production of naturally occurring antibiotics through synthesis."

b) Word choice/grammar:
"attemped" → "attempted" (typo)
"a small bacteria" → "a small bacterium" (bacteria is plural; bacterium is singular)
"medicinal chemistry" vs. "medical chemists" — be consistent; typically we say "medicinal chemists" when referring to the field of medicinal chemistry.
"As a natural antibiotic, penicillin was discovered to have many uses..." — this phrasing is awkward. Consider: "As a naturally occurring antibiotic, penicillin proved useful in stopping and preventing the spread of infections."

c) Vague quantifiers: Words like "many," "a variety of," "huge," and "great" appear frequently but add little precision. Replace with specific data where possible (eg, "By the 1950s, over a dozen new antibiotics had been identified...").

d) Sentence variety: Many sentences follow a similar subject-verb-object pattern with heavy use of "caused," "led to," and "resulted in." Try varying sentence openings and combining some shorter sentences with subordinate clauses to improve flow.

Priority Revisions

Develop a clear, arguable thesis and revise your structure around it.
Add specific evidence—names, dates, figures—to support your claims.
Reduce passive voice to strengthen your argumentative voice.
Proofread carefully for small grammar errors (eg, "bacteria" vs. "bacterium," "attemped").
Revise the conclusion to explain the significance of your argument, not just summarize events.

You have strong raw material here—now it's a matter of shaping it into a persuasive argument rather than a historical account. I'd be happy to discuss thesis options with you in office hours if that would help.

   - "Many believe... people become junkies" — *Who believes this? What evidence supports it?*
   - "Some argue it is a way to bring the family together" — *Can you give a specific example or brief explanation of how?*
   
   Adding brief examples, statistics, or even hypothetical scenarios would make your arguments more persuasive and academic.

Punctuation:
   - "and and diminished" — remove the duplicate "and"
   - Consider using "and" instead of "&" in formal academic writing (you use "&" twice)

Sentence Fragments/Awkward Phrasing:
   - "This mode of communicating has been helpful in warning people of dangers, both natural and social" — this is good, but check similar sentences for clarity and completeness.

Overall

With more developed examples and careful proofreading for the grammar patterns noted above, this essay will be significantly stronger. I encourage you to revise using the suggestions above and feel free to bring your next draft to office hours if you'd like to discuss further.

Feedback on "The Washing Machine" Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay has a lively, engaging voice and a genuinely interesting idea buried inside it—the connection between labor-saving devices and reduced physical activity. However, the essay currently reads more like an informal reflection than an argumentative essay, because the central claim is not stated clearly, the humor sometimes overshadows the argument, and the reasoning in the conclusion needs more support. Below are suggestions organized by area.

Thesis and Argument

Right now, your thesis emerges only in the final paragraph: modern conveniences make life easier but reduce physical exercise, possibly contributing to weight problems. This is a promising and debatable claim—exactly what an argumentative essay needs. But because it appears so late, readers spend most of the essay unsure of your actual position. The first two paragraphs suggest a different focus (how indispensable washing machines have become), which creates a mismatch between introduction and conclusion.

Revision suggestion: State your real argument in the introduction. For example: "While inventions like the washing machine save time and labor, they may also be quietly contributing to a more sedentary, less healthy society." Then organize every paragraph to build toward that claim.

Structure and Organization

The essay currently moves: (1) history of invention → (2) exaggerated dependence on washing machines → (3) fear of hand-washing → (4) sudden pivot to exercise and diet culture. The connections between these sections are implied rather than explained. Readers have to guess how paragraph 3 (fear of hand-washing) supports paragraph 4 (obesity).

Revision suggestion: Add transitional sentences that explicitly link ideas—eg, "This fear of manual labor is not trivial; it reflects a broader pattern in which convenience replaces physical effort, with consequences for our health." Consider also adding a paragraph with concrete evidence (statistics on calorie expenditure from hand-washing vs. machine washing, or data on rising obesity rates alongside appliance ownership) to make the causal argument credible rather than anecdotal.

Evidence and Development

Much of your support relies on hypothetical scenarios (someone running from a fire clutching a washing machine, a panicked adult given a washboard). These are entertaining but function as humor rather than evidence. For an argumentative essay, you need at least some factual support—research findings, historical comparisons of labor and health, or expert commentary—to make the causal link between convenience and weight gain persuasive rather than speculative.

Revision suggestion: Keep one or two of your humorous examples for voice, but add a paragraph of actual evidence, even brief statistics, to ground the argument.

Tone and Style

Your voice is engaging, but the tone shifts unpredictably between comedic exaggeration ("clutching the machine tightly to their chests") and serious social critique (obesity, diet culture). This inconsistency can confuse readers about whether they should take the essay as satire or serious argument. Academic argumentative writing usually maintains a more consistent, measured tone even when using irony.

Also, avoid rhetorical questions stacked one after another ("What ever would a college student do without a washing machine? Where would we find time...?")—one well-placed rhetorical question is effective; several in a row can feel like filler rather than argument.

The ellipsis ("...But is that all that bad?....") and the ampersands ("&") throughout the essay are too informal for academic writing. Spell out "and" and use standard punctuation.

Language and Mechanics

A few recurring issues to fix:
Spelling: "continously" → *continuously*; "necesity" → *necessity*.
Word spacing: "with out" → *without* (appears twice).
Ampersands: Replace all "&" with "and."
Gendered generic pronouns: "man" and "his" are used generically throughout ("man has invented things," "he has invented"). Consider more inclusive phrasing: "humans have invented things" or "people have long invented tools."
Word choice: "housewives" may read as dated; consider "many people" or "many homemakers" depending on your intended emphasis.
Sentence fragments/run-ons: Some sentences are stylistically fragmented for effect ("But is that all that bad?"), which is fine occasionally, but check that this is a deliberate choice rather than accidental.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Move your real thesis (convenience vs. physical activity/health) to the introduction.
Add transitions that connect each paragraph explicitly to that thesis.
Include at least one paragraph of factual evidence to support the causal claim about exercise and health.
Choose a consistent tone—decide whether this is a witty, ironic essay or a more serious argumentative one, and adjust throughout.
Clean up mechanical errors: spelling, ampersands, ellipses, gendered generics.

You have a genuinely interesting angle here—refining the structure and adding evidence will transform this from an entertaining reflection into a persuasive argumentative essay.

Clarify the Argumentative Purpose
Right now, the essay reads more like a personal reflection or a "show and tell" than a formal argument. An academic argumentative essay typically needs a clear thesis statement that tells the reader *what claim you are making and why it matters*—not just "zip-lock bags are useful," but perhaps something like: "The zip-lock bag exemplifies how a simple, inexpensive invention can solve multiple everyday problems more effectively than specialized alternatives." Consider revising your opening paragraph to state this thesis explicitly, rather than opening with the anecdote about your family discussion (which could instead become a brief hook before the thesis, kept very short).

Strengthen Paragraph Organization
Each paragraph does introduce a new use for the zip-lock bag (freshness, odor control, travel, etc), which is good—but the transitions between them are weak or mechanical ("Another thing," "But, there are other uses"). Try using transitions that show logical relationships between ideas, such as "In addition to preserving freshness, zip-lock bags also solve a common problem: unwanted odors." This will help your paragraphs feel connected rather than like a list.

Consider a Counterargument
A strong argumentative essay often addresses an opposing view before refuting it. For example, you might acknowledge that some people see zip-lock bags as wasteful or environmentally harmful, and then argue why their practical benefits (or comparison to alternatives) still make them a worthwhile invention. This would elevate the essay from a list of uses to a genuine argument with depth.

Revise the Conclusion
Your conclusion ("What more could you ask for?") is engaging in tone, but it mostly summarizes rather than pushing your argument further. Consider ending with a reflection on why this "small" invention deserves more recognition, or connecting it back to a broader idea (eg, how the best inventions are often the simplest ones).

Watch Your Tone and Register
This essay uses a conversational tone (eg, "I cannot even count the number of times," "What more could you ask for?"). This works well for informal writing, but for an academic argumentative essay, you may want to strike a slightly more formal balance—keeping your personal voice while tightening some phrasing. For example, instead of "But, there are other uses for zip-lock bags," try "Zip-lock bags also serve several other practical functions."

Sentence-Level Notes

Watch comma splices and spacing errors, eg, "school to lunch,  and by the time lunch came around, I could smell the sandwich before I even opened my locker. ." Proofread carefully for double periods and spacing.
The bracketed aside "(onlies???)" is charming in a personal essay but should be removed or turned into a proper aside (eg, "dry-clean-only garments") in formal writing.
Double-check subject-verb agreement and article usage, which can be tricky when moving between Chinese and English grammar structures (eg, "This is an invention that I cannot live without" — good; but check phrases like "putting the surrounding food in the bag" for smoother phrasing).
Consider varying sentence openings—many paragraphs begin with "First," "Another," "But," which can feel repetitive. Try starting some sentences with the subject or an example instead.

Summary

Your essay has excellent raw material: real examples, a clear organizational logic (uses categorized by function), and an authentic voice. To make it more effective as an *argumentative* essay, focus on (1) stating a clear, debatable thesis, (2) connecting your paragraphs with stronger transitions, (3) considering a counterargument, and (4) polishing sentence-level issues. With these revisions, this could become a very persuasive and enjoyable piece of writing!

Keep up the great work, and don't hesitate to ask if you'd like help drafting a thesis statement or restructuring any specific paragraph.

Feedback on "The Computer: A 20th-Century Miracle"

Overall Impression

You've chosen an engaging topic, and your enthusiasm for the subject comes through clearly. However, this piece currently reads more like a description or reflection than an argumentative essay. To meet the expectations of academic argumentation, you'll need to sharpen your claim, support it with evidence, and address the fact that reasonable people might disagree with you. Below are specific areas to revise.

Thesis and Argument

Your thesis—that the computer is the most influential invention of the 20th century—is a strong claim, but it needs to be argued, not just asserted. Currently, you state the claim in the first paragraph and then largely restate it in different words throughout the essay. Ask yourself:

What is the argumentative task here? If you're claiming the computer is *more* influential than other inventions (eg, the airplane, antibiotics, the telephone), you need to briefly acknowledge those competitors and explain why the computer surpasses them. Right now, no comparison is made, so the "most influential" claim is unsupported.
Consider revising your thesis to be more specific and debatable. For example: "While inventions such as antibiotics and the airplane transformed daily life, the computer has had the most far-reaching impact because it did not just change *what* we do, but *how* we think, work, and communicate." This gives you a clear angle to defend.

Structure and Development

Your two body paragraphs each try to do too much—one covers technical history, the other jumps from business to future predictions. Consider organizing by *distinct lines of argument*, each with its own paragraph:

Paragraph 1: How computers changed business/economic productivity (with a concrete example: manufacturing, banking, communication).
Paragraph 2: How computers changed information access and daily life (again, use specific examples).
Paragraph 3: Brief counter-argument/rebuttal—acknowledge another candidate for "most influential invention" and explain why the computer still wins.
Conclusion: Tie back to thesis, perhaps end with your speculation about the future.

Evidence and Specificity

This is the biggest area for improvement. Right now, most sentences are broad generalizations:

"The impact of computers on the world has been great."
"the productivity of people has increased ten-fold."

Where does this number come from? Academic writing requires you to support claims with evidence—statistics, named examples, or cited sources. Even one concrete example (eg, "Banks that once required rooms of clerks to process transactions now handle millions of transactions per second through networked computer systems") would strengthen your credibility far more than a vague superlative.

Similarly, your closing paragraph makes predictions about the future ("every human being in the developed world will have their own PC linked by cellular modem…") that are speculative and undercut your argument if unsupported. Speculation can be part of a conclusion, but frame it explicitly as speculation, and keep it brief.

Language and Mechanics

A few recurring issues to address in revision:

Spelling: "occured" → "occurred"; "2st century" → "21st century" (also, spell out or format numbers consistently—see below).
Numbers: Academic style usually spells out numbers under 100 in text ("twentieth century" rather than "20th century") unless you're following a specific style guide (APA/MLA) that allows numerals—check your assignment guidelines.
Word choice: "expensive monsters" is a colorful but informal phrase; in academic writing, aim for more precise, measured language (eg, "prohibitively expensive machines that occupied entire rooms").
Quotation marks around "bright": Using scare quotes here is confusing—do you mean this ironically, or literally? If literal, remove the quotes; academic writing avoids using quotation marks for emphasis.
Comma splices/run-ons: Watch sentences like "This invention of the late 20th century has forever changed the way people live and work"—fine on its own, but check throughout for sentences that need semicolons or periods rather than commas.
Extra space before "and" in your last sentence—minor typo, but proofread carefully.

Tone

Your final line ("We are limited only by our imaginations and the future of this invention looks 'bright' indeed") is more suited to a promotional speech than an academic essay. Aim for a conclusion that reasserts your argument with confidence but avoids sweeping, unsupported optimism.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Sharpen your thesis to make it specifically arguable (not just descriptive).
Add concrete evidence—examples, data, or (if permitted) outside sources.
Restructure paragraphs around distinct supporting arguments.
Add a counter-argument and rebuttal to strengthen your position.
Proofread for spelling, number formatting, and informal phrasing.

You have real command of vocabulary and a clear passion for the topic—now it's a matter of directing that energy into a more rigorous argumentative structure. I look forward to seeing your revision!

Feedback Report: AIDS Essay

Overall Impression

This essay engages with an important and challenging topic, and your passion for the subject—particularly the injustice of social stigma—comes through clearly. However, the essay currently reads more like a series of observations and complaints than a structured argument. To strengthen it, you need a clearer thesis, more logical organization, and better-supported claims. I'll break down specific areas for revision below.

Reconsider Your Central Claim

Your opening sentence calls AIDS "one of the most important discoveries of the 20th Century." This is conceptually confusing: a disease is not a "discovery" in the way a vaccine or scientific breakthrough is. Do you mean:
The *discovery* of AIDS (as a medical/scientific event), or
The *social impact* of AIDS on public attitudes toward sexuality, mortality, and discrimination?

Your essay actually develops the second idea much more than the first. I recommend revising your thesis to focus on this: something like, "The AIDS epidemic exposed and reshaped American society's attitudes toward sexuality, mortality, and marginalized groups." A clear thesis like this will help you decide what belongs in the essay and what doesn't.

Improve Paragraph Focus and Transitions

Right now, the essay moves quickly between several ideas—stigma against gay men and IV drug users, children with AIDS, Ryan White, public education campaigns, abstinence-only education, and Magic Johnson—without clear logical connections. Each paragraph should serve your thesis directly.

For example, paragraph 3 shifts from discussing Ryan White's case to a debate about condom distribution in schools. This is a legitimate topic, but you need a transition sentence explaining *why* this follows from Ryan White's story. Something like: "His case sparked public education campaigns, but these campaigns quickly became entangled in a larger controversy over how—and whether—to teach children about sex." This kind of bridge sentence will make your logic visible to the reader.

Support Your Claims with Evidence

Several strong claims are made without support:
"No one really cared that gays would die."
"Children are becoming sexually active at the age of eleven."
"Most people are more careful about who they sleep with."

In academic writing, especially on a topic this significant, you need to cite sources—historical accounts, statistics, or scholarly analysis—to back up these assertions. Even one or two well-chosen statistics (eg, CDC data on AIDS cases by group in the 1980s, or documented public reactions to Ryan White's case) would make your argument far more persuasive and credible.

Clarify Your Argument About Abstinence vs. Education

You write that "abstinence is the only safe way to prevent AIDS, but society must be realistic," and then argue that children can't easily access condoms. This is an interesting tension, but it needs to be developed more fully. What is your actual position? Are you arguing for comprehensive sex education over abstinence-only programs? If so, state this directly and explain your reasoning with evidence, rather than leaving the reader to infer it.

Strengthen the Conclusion

Your conclusion introduces a new idea (Magic Johnson) very briefly and then ends somewhat abruptly. The sentence "Even if a cure is never found." is a sentence fragment—it needs to be joined with the following sentence: "Even if a cure is never found, AIDS victims should be accepted and given the respect they deserve." Consider expanding this conclusion to more clearly restate your thesis and reflect on the broader social lesson of the AIDS epidemic, rather than introducing a new example so late.

Language and Grammar Notes

A few recurring issues to watch for in revision:

Affect vs. effect: "no one really cared... they thought AIDS would never *effect* them" should be "*affect* them" (affect = verb, effect = noun in most cases).
Comma splices: Several sentences join independent clauses with only a comma, eg, "It was seen everywhere... on t.v., magazines, radio, & in the schools." Consider revising punctuation and avoiding "&" in formal writing—write out "and."
Consistency in verb tense: You move between past and present tense somewhat inconsistently (eg, "society *began* to care" vs. "society *must be* realistic"). Decide whether you're narrating history (past tense) or making a present-day argument (present tense), and be consistent within each section.
Word choice: "Everyone was in a panic" and "How wrong they were" are effective for informal writing but may feel too casual for an academic essay. Consider more analytical phrasing.

Summary

You have real insight into how AIDS changed public attitudes—now the task is to sharpen your thesis, organize your paragraphs around that thesis, and support your claims with credible evidence. I'd be happy to discuss a revision plan with you in office hours.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You have chosen a genuinely thought-provoking thesis — that only medical inventions of the twentieth century have improved human life — and your personal engagement with the topic comes through clearly. However, the essay currently reads more like a set of assertions than a fully developed argument. Below are suggestions to help you strengthen the reasoning, structure, and language of your draft.

Strengthen Your Argument with Evidence

The essay's biggest weakness is the absence of concrete examples. You make sweeping claims — that technology stems from "fear of death," that inventions are used "to dominate nature," that we build "better economic systems geared for killing and depriving other people" — but none of these claims is illustrated with a specific case. For an academic essay, each major claim needs support:

Which inventions of the 20th century do you have in mind as harmful (eg, automobiles, nuclear weapons, industrial chemicals)? Naming them and explaining *how* they damage nature or oppress people would make your argument far more persuasive.
Which medical inventions are you praising (penicillin, vaccines, MRI, insulin)? Concrete examples will help readers see exactly why you consider them uniquely benevolent.
Your claim about "economic systems geared for killing and depriving" is vague. Do you mean colonialism, capitalism, or something else? This needs clarification and evidence.

Address the Counterargument

Right now your essay treats "medical inventions" as entirely separate from the human impulse to dominate, but a critical reader will immediately think of counterexamples: medical experiments performed on vulnerable populations without consent, unequal global access to healthcare, or the use of medical technology in warfare (eg, biological weapons, battlefield triage designed to keep soldiers fighting). Acknowledging and responding to these complications would make your argument more sophisticated and credible. You don't need to abandon your thesis — but engaging with the strongest objections to it will show critical thinking rather than an overly one-sided view.

Improve Organization

Your essay currently has four short paragraphs but lacks a clear architecture:

The introduction does a good job stating your thesis but might rely too much on personal reflection ("this topic is very important to me") rather than framing the issue analytically for the reader. Consider opening with the broader question (what makes an invention "good" for humanity?) before narrowing to your personal stance.
The body paragraphs on domination of nature and domination of other people are underdeveloped — each is only two to four sentences. Expand them with examples, and add clear topic sentences that state the paragraph's main idea before diving into analysis.
Your final paragraph functions as your real thesis statement about medical inventions, but it appears where a conclusion should be. You need an actual concluding paragraph that restates your argument in light of the evidence you've presented and perhaps reflects on its broader implications (What should this insight change about how we approach future innovation?).

Refine Language and Mechanics

Since English is not your first language, here are some specific points to revise:

"occured" → *occurred*
"0the century" → *20th century* (likely a typo)
"truely" → *truly*
"affect" → *effect* ("inventions...have had...a negative effect")
Watch subject-verb agreement and article usage throughout (eg, "the topic is one that is very important" is fine, but check phrases like "our fellow man," which may sound archaic — consider "our fellow human beings").
Some sentences shift between abstract philosophical claims and concrete assertions without transition. Adding linking phrases (eg, "For example," "This is evident in," "By contrast") will help guide your reader.

Consider Tone

The opening lines ("Interestingly enough...very important to me") are conversational and personal, which can work well in a reflective essay but may feel out of place in a more formal argumentative essay depending on your assignment's expectations. If your instructor expects an academic register, consider revising the introduction to establish the stakes of the issue more objectively before revealing your personal investment in it.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Add specific, well-explained examples to support every major claim.
Address at least one counterargument to strengthen credibility.
Expand body paragraphs and add a genuine concluding paragraph.
Proofread carefully for typos and word-choice errors.
Reconsider the balance between personal voice and academic tone.

With more concrete evidence and a clearer structure, this essay has the potential to make a compelling and original argument. Keep developing your ideas — the core insight is interesting and worth defending rigorously.

Feedback Report

Thank you for sharing this essay on the satellite dish and its impact on global communication. You've chosen an ambitious and interesting topic, and I can see you're trying to cover a lot of ground. However, the essay currently needs significant revision in several key areas: thesis clarity, argument structure, evidence, and language precision. Below is detailed feedback organized by area.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

A strong argumentative essay needs a clear, debatable claim. Right now, your thesis seems to be "the satellite dish has changed communication significantly, for better and worse"—but this is too broad and not really debatable. Almost no one would disagree.

Revision suggestion: Narrow your focus. Choose ONE specific claim you can defend with evidence, such as: "While satellite technology has democratized access to information, it has simultaneously eroded critical thinking by prioritizing entertainment over substantive journalism." This gives you a real argument to prove.

Structure and Paragraph Unity

Each paragraph currently introduces a new topic (CNN, business, MTV, cable/military) without connecting back to a central argument. This makes the essay feel like a list of examples rather than a sustained argument.

Revision suggestion: After narrowing your thesis, ensure every paragraph explicitly supports that specific claim. For example, if your argument is about the "double-edged" nature of satellite technology, dedicate one paragraph to genuine benefits (with evidence) and one to genuine costs (with evidence), rather than mixing unrelated topics (business, entertainment, military, education) without clear logical connections.

This is the area most needing improvement. Several claims appear unsupported or factually questionable:

"40% of U.S. millionaires are entertainers" — What is the source? This seems inaccurate; please verify or remove.
"The intelligence of an average teenager is lowering itself due to cable viewing" — This is a strong claim with no evidence. How would you measure this? Is there research linking cable viewing to intelligence decline?
"Naval programs have wasted exceeding amounts of money" — Vague; provide specific examples or figures.
"STARWARS" — Do you mean the Strategic Defense Initiative? Be precise and explain its relevance.

Revision suggestion: For each major claim, ask yourself: "Can I cite a source or specific example?" Academic writing requires evidence—statistics, expert opinions, or documented case studies—not general impressions.

Language and Word Choice

There are several issues with word choice and mechanics that affect clarity and credibility:

"it's center" should be "its center" (possessive, no apostrophe)
"created in the last 0 years" — this appears to be a typo; did you mean a specific number?
"recieve" → "receive"
"panasonic and CORE" should be capitalized: "Panasonic"; also, what is "CORE"? Clarify or provide a real company name.
"millionares" → "millionaires"
"affect" vs. "effect" — check usage (here, "effect" as a noun is correct: "a strong effect")
"naval programs" — do you mean "national defense programs" or specifically "navy programs"? This is unclear.
"pro's and con's" — no apostrophes needed for plurals: "pros and cons"
"people lives" → "people's lives"

Suggestion: After revising content, do a careful proofreading pass focused only on grammar and spelling. Reading your essay aloud can help catch these errors.

Tone and Register

The opening sentence, while creative, is too informal and imprecise for academic writing ("as large as a small FIAT"). Academic essays generally favor precision over colorful comparison unless the metaphor serves a clear analytical purpose.

Revision suggestion: Consider replacing the introduction with a more direct statement of your topic and thesis, saving vivid language for when it strengthens (rather than distracts from) your argument.

Overall Priorities for Revision

Narrow your thesis to one specific, debatable claim
Reorganize paragraphs so each one clearly supports that thesis
Verify or remove unsupported statistics and claims
Add concrete evidence—specific examples, dates, and sources
Proofread carefully for grammar and spelling

You clearly have interesting ideas and a broad awareness of how satellite technology affects different sectors (media, business, entertainment, military). Overall Impression

This three-part structure gives your essay a strong backbone, and your historical examples (the Cleavers, the Vietnam War, the Challenger disaster) are vivid and well-chosen. With some revision to language, development, and mechanics, this essay could be quite strong.

Content and Argument

Strengths: Your thesis is stated clearly at the end of the introduction, and each body paragraph corresponds to one part of that thesis—this is good essay architecture. The examples you use are concrete and interesting; comparing a farmer in Iowa's knowledge of New York families to New Yorkers watching *Green Acres* is a clever illustration of "mass culturalization."

Areas to develop:
Your second body paragraph (commercialization) is noticeably shorter and less developed than the other two. You introduce an interesting idea—that advertisements have become an "art form"—but you don't elaborate. Consider adding a specific example (a famous ad campaign, a jingle, a slogan) the way you do in your other paragraphs.
The claim that "to white people, Ghetto life is slightly less of a mystery" is provocative but underexplained. Whose "ghetto"? Which show? This generalization needs a specific example or at least more careful qualification, since as written it risks sounding like an unsupported assumption about race.

Your conclusion introduces an important new idea—that television is "manipulator"—but this feels underdeveloped compared to how much space you gave to information and culture. If manipulation is "most important," as you say, it deserves more than two sentences.

Organization

The essay follows a clear and effective structure: introduction with thesis → three body paragraphs → conclusion. To improve flow, add transitional phrases between paragraphs. For example, your final paragraph starts abruptly with "Finally, & perhaps most importantly"—this is fine, but the shift from commercialization to information speed could use a linking sentence that shows *why* these two ideas are connected.

Language and Mechanics

This is where the most revision is needed:

Title/opening typo: "The 0th Century" should be "The 20th Century." Proofread your opening line carefully, as this is the first thing your reader sees.
