Spelling errors: "grupes" → "groups"; "breath" → "breadth"; "livingrooms" → "living rooms"; "inhance" → "enhance." Please proofread carefully, or use a spell-checker before submitting.

Capitalization: "vietnam war" should be "Vietnam War"; "the united States" should be "the United States." Be consistent and careful with proper nouns.

Sentence fragments: "The television a powerful and dangerous tool" is missing the verb "is." Please read your sentences aloud to catch missing words like this.

Consistency: You use both "mass culturization" (introduction) and "mass culturalization" (body paragraph)—pick one term and use it consistently throughout.

Word choice: "omniscient picture" is an interesting but slightly awkward phrase—"omniscient" usually describes a narrator or being with total knowledge, not a picture. Consider "idealized picture" instead.

Suggestions for Revision

Proofread line-by-line for typos and abbreviations—read the essay aloud slowly.
Expand your commercialization paragraph with a concrete historical example.
Clarify or soften the claim about "Ghetto life," adding a specific example.
Strengthen your conclusion by returning explicitly to the idea of "manipulation," since you flag it as most important.
Consider adding one sentence acknowledging a counterargument or limitation, to show more critical thinking.

You have a genuinely thoughtful argument here with strong topic sentences and effective examples. With careful editing for mechanics and a bit more development in your weaker paragraph, this essay will be significantly stronger. Keep up the good analytical thinking!

Feedback Report: "The Fall of the Berlin Wall"

Overall Impression

This is a vivid and emotionally engaging piece. Your personal memories—the excited conversations at school, the story of your classmate's father—give the essay authenticity and make abstract historical events feel immediate. However, as an *argumentative* essay, the piece currently reads more like a personal reflection or memoir. To strengthen it, you need a clearer thesis, more explicit reasoning connecting your evidence to your claim, and tighter control over grammar, punctuation, and logic. Below are specific areas to revise.

Clarify Your Argument

Your central claim seems to be that the fall of the Berlin Wall was the *catalyst* for the collapse of communism across Eastern Europe and the end of the Cold War. This is a strong, debatable claim—exactly what an argumentative essay needs. But it doesn't appear clearly until the final paragraph. Readers should know your thesis early, ideally by the end of your introduction. Consider opening with your personal anecdote (it's a great hook), but then explicitly state what you intend to argue before moving into supporting evidence.

Additionally, your argument currently relies almost entirely on personal testimony (your own memory, your classmate's father). For an academic argumentative essay, you should also incorporate historical evidence—dates, specific events in Hungary, Czechoslovakia, Poland, and Romania, statements from historians or political leaders—to support the claim that the Wall's fall *caused* subsequent uprisings, rather than simply coincided with a trend already underway. Right now you acknowledge this complexity ("Eventually, I think that things would have turned out as they did") but don't develop it. This is actually the most interesting analytical moment in your essay—expand it. Explain *why* you believe events would have unfolded similarly regardless, and *why* you still think the Wall's fall mattered. This kind of causal reasoning is the heart of argumentation.

Logical Consistency

There is a confusing contradiction in this sentence:
> "Without the 'events of Berlin Wall' history probably would not have taken a very different course."

This seems to negate itself. If you mean that history would have unfolded similarly with or without the Wall's fall, you likely want: "...history probably would not have taken a very different course" — but then your next sentence ("Eventually, I think things would have turned out as they did") repeats the same idea. Consider revising both sentences together so the logic is unmistakable, something like: "Some historians argue that even without the fall of the Berlin Wall, communism would eventually have collapsed. I am inclined to agree. However, the Wall's fall accelerated this process and gave it emotional and symbolic force that a slower collapse might not have had."

Pronoun Consistency

You introduce "a classmate whose father had escaped from Hungary," then refer to the father as "he" throughout the anecdote ("He had left his family," "I had asked him," "He told me"). But at the end, you write "her father's words were with me," revealing the classmate is female—information not given earlier. Please clarify your classmate's gender when you first introduce her, and make sure pronouns for "the father" and "the classmate" remain distinguishable throughout (eg, "her father," "my classmate, Anna," etc).

Punctuation

You use double dashes (--) at the end of almost every sentence, in place of periods. This is a significant issue: it blurs sentence boundaries, creates run-on structures, and looks unconventional in academic prose. Please replace these with standard periods, and use dashes only for their normal function (to insert an aside or emphasize a break in a sentence, eg, "The father—now living safely in Western Europe—rarely spoke of his escape.").

Grammar and Spelling

A few corrections to note:
"mesmirized" → mesmerized
"Tchekoslovaquia," "Roumania" → Czechoslovakia, Romania (these appear to be French spellings)
"as-President Bush" → then-President Bush (or former President, depending on when you are writing)
"one of those historic moment" → moments (plural)
Watch tense consistency: you shift between past tense (narrating 1989) and present tense ("Now, three years later, communism... no longer exists"). Make sure shifts are intentional and clearly signaled (eg, "Three years later, as I write this, communism...").

Style

The phrase "not in vain" appears twice in close proximity ("their efforts were not in vain," "will not have been in vain"). Consider varying your language to avoid repetition.

Summary of Priorities for Revision
State your thesis clearly and early.
Develop the causal argument (Did the Wall's fall *cause* the rest, or coincide with inevitable change?) with more historical evidence.
Fix dash/period punctuation throughout.
Correct pronoun inconsistency regarding your classmate.
Proofread for spelling and tense issues.

You have compelling material and a genuine personal connection to this history—now build the analytical scaffolding around it.

Feedback Report: Argumentative Essay on Television

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an engaging topic—the transformative power of television—and shows genuine enthusiasm for the subject. You have a clear opinion and provide some vivid, relatable examples. However, the essay would benefit from a sharper thesis, stronger paragraph organization, more developed argumentation, and careful editing for grammar and spelling. Below are specific suggestions organized by category.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your thesis—that television is the most significant invention of the twentieth century—is implied but not stated with full conviction. The phrase "Perhaps one does stand out a little more" is too tentative for an argumentative essay. Since your task is to *argue*, state your claim directly and confidently: for example, "Among these inventions, television stands out as the most transformative because it reshaped how humanity accesses information, entertainment, and connection."

Additionally, your final paragraph introduces a *new* argument—that television's power must be "controlled"—which shifts the essay's focus from "television is the greatest invention" to "television is powerful but dangerous." This is a compelling angle, but it needs to be integrated into your thesis from the start, or removed if it distracts from your main argument. Right now, it reads like a separate essay tacked onto the end.

Structure and Development

Your paragraphs move logically from radio (before television) to television's advantages (images, global connection) to television's risks. This is a reasonable structure, but each paragraph needs deeper development:

Paragraph 2 (radio): This is good context, but consider tightening it. You don't need to prove radio "worked"—readers will accept this quickly. Use the space you save to strengthen your television argument instead.
Paragraph 3 (images): This is your strongest paragraph. Consider adding a concrete historical example (eg, the moon landing, a presidential address, a war broadcast) to make your claim more persuasive and specific.
Paragraph 4 (global connection): Your examples are good, but they are asserted rather than developed. Choose one example (the Olympic race is vivid) and expand it: How does this compare to what radio alone could offer? What does this change about family life or community?
Paragraph 5 (power and control): This paragraph tries to do too much in too little space—business, media expansion, morality, and practical applications (medicine, politics, education) are all introduced without development. Choose one or two of these ideas and explain them, rather than listing many briefly.

Suggestion: Consider adding a paragraph directly comparing television to another major invention (the computer, the automobile) to strengthen your comparative claim, since your introduction promises a comparison ("some stand out as more significantly changing our mode of living") that isn't fully delivered.

Language and Grammar

There are several recurring errors worth addressing in revision:

Spelling: "vertually" → *virtually*; "boarders" → *borders*; "absorbtion" → *absorption*; "peak" (in quotation marks) should be *peek*; "spening" → *spending*; "olympic" → *Olympic* (capitalize); "necesary" → *necessary*.
Subject-verb agreement: "one lives easier" → *one's life easier* or *our lives easier*.
Pronoun consistency: You shift between "we," "the people," "our," and "its citizens." Choose a consistent point of view—likely "we/our" since this is an argumentative essay addressing readers directly.
Comma splices/run-ons: eg, "A system that seemed to work, many households throughout the nation owned..." This sentence fragment needs restructuring—perhaps: "This system seemed to work well; many households throughout the nation owned or had access to a radio..."
Article/preposition errors: "peak at what radio wasn't able to give them" is fine once "peak" is corrected, but check other prepositional phrases for accuracy (common challenge for Chinese speakers, since Chinese doesn't use articles/prepositions the same way English does). Reading your essay aloud can help catch missing "the," "a," or awkward prepositions.

Style Suggestions

Avoid overly casual phrasing like "The examples could be endless"—instead, briefly explain *why* you're limiting your examples, or actually extend the list with one more strong case.
The final sentence's wordplay ("keep the vision in television") is clever but appears abruptly; make sure your essay has built toward this idea rather than introducing it only in the last line.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Sharpen and commit to one clear thesis.
Decide whether "control" is part of your main argument—if so, foreshadow it earlier.
Develop each example with more specific detail and explanation.
Proofread carefully for spelling and grammar, especially subject-verb agreement and pronoun consistency.

You have strong ideas and a genuine argumentative voice—now it's a matter of tightening the structure and language to match that ambition. Keep revising with confidence!

Feedback Report: Argumentative Essay on the Computer

Overall Impression

This essay presents a clear, historically grounded argument about the computer's transformative impact on society. You've organized your ideas chronologically (from WWII-era mainframes to personal computers to portable computers), which gives readers an easy path to follow. Your topic is engaging and relevant. However, the essay needs revision in several key areas: thesis clarity, argumentative depth, sentence-level grammar, and evidence/support. Below are specific areas to address.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your introduction states that the computer "has had a great impact," but this is a descriptive claim, not an arguable thesis. An argumentative essay needs a position that a reasonable person could disagree with. Consider revising your thesis to something more specific and debatable, such as:

> "The personal computer, more than any other 20th-century invention, has restructured how ordinary individuals—not just corporations—access, produce, and control information, fundamentally decentralizing power that was once held by governments and large institutions."

This gives you something to *prove*, rather than simply *describe*.

Structure and Paragraph Development

Your essay reads more like a historical narrative than an argument. Each paragraph should support your thesis with a clear claim, evidence, and analysis—not just chronicle "what happened next" in computer history. For revision:

Paragraph 2 (pre-1980 computers): Good context, but ask *why does this matter to your argument*? Make the contrast with today more explicit.
Paragraph 3 (the PC revolution): This is your longest paragraph and contains the most content, but it lists effects ("faster, more sophisticated...") without deeply analyzing *any single one*. Choose 2-3 effects and develop them with specific examples (eg, a concrete scenario of a small business owner doing taxes before vs. after PCs).
Paragraph 4 (conclusion): This currently just restates your introduction. A strong conclusion should synthesize your argument and perhaps address a counterargument (eg, "Some might argue that information overload has made life more complicated, not easier, but...").

Fact-Checking

You state that "IBM Corporation started a revolution... in 1980" with "the invention of the personal computer." This is historically inaccurate. IBM released its PC in 1981, but earlier machines (Apple II in 1977, Altair 8800 in 1975) were already personal computers. Since this is a factual claim central to your argument, verify your dates and sources carefully. Academic writing requires accuracy—consider citing a source here.

Grammar and Language Issues

As you continue developing academic English, watch for these recurring patterns:

Subject-verb agreement: "There has been many inventions" → "There have been many inventions"
Article usage: "With the use of Modem" → "With the use of a modem" (also, lowercase "modem" as a common noun)
Spelling: "recieve" → "receive" (i before e except after c)
Comma splices/run-ons: "It was mainly used though, by corporations..." — the comma placement here is awkward; revise to "It was mainly used, though, by corporations..." or restructure the sentence.
Redundancy: "sort out, save, delete or add and receive information" — this list is vague; choose more precise, varied verbs.
Vague pronoun reference: "This meant that with the use of the computer and associated technologies... you could do anything from almost anywhere" — "anything" and "anywhere" are imprecise. Specify what tasks.

Tone and Register

You shift between formal academic language and second-person address ("you can sit in your home..."). Academic essays typically maintain a more formal, third-person voice. Consider revising "you" to "individuals," "users," or "one," eg:

> "You can, with a modem and a computer, receive and process all types of information" → "Users can, with a modem and a computer, access and process various types of information."

Evidence and Citations

Currently, the essay relies entirely on general claims without citing sources, statistics, or specific examples (eg, named databases, specific IBM models, user statistics). Academic arguments are strengthened by concrete evidence. Consider adding:
A statistic on PC adoption rates in the 1980s-90s
A specific example of a database or software program
A quotation from a historical or technology source

Summary of Priority Revisions

Sharpen your thesis into an arguable claim
Verify historical facts (PC invention date)
Deepen analysis in body paragraphs rather than listing effects
Add specific evidence/citations
Proofread for grammar (subject-verb agreement, articles, spelling)
Formalize tone (reduce direct "you" address)

Your ideas show real engagement with the topic—now let's give them the structural and linguistic precision they deserve. I'd be happy to discuss any of these points further in office hours.

Feedback on "The Century of Democracy"

Thank you for sharing this personal and thoughtfully engaged essay. Your enthusiasm for your topic comes through clearly, and the image of buying a piece of the Berlin Wall is memorable. However, the essay currently reads more like a personal reflection than an argumentative essay, and there are some structural and logical issues that need attention before this piece will fully persuade an academic reader. Below are the main areas to focus on for revision.

Alignment Between Thesis and Evidence (Most Important Issue)

Your introduction promises a survey of the entire 20th century—you mention the 1920s, the Depression, the 1950s, the 1960s and 70s—which sets up an expectation that your essay will trace the theme of democracy *across the whole century*. However, your body paragraphs discuss only events from the last 2–3 years (the fall of the Berlin Wall, the Soviet transition). This creates a mismatch between the scope of your claim and the scope of your evidence.

To fix this: Either (a) narrow your thesis to focus specifically on the *end* of the century and the post-Cold War moment, or (b) actually develop examples from earlier decades that support "democracy" as a defining theme (eg, decolonization movements in Asia and Africa, the expansion of voting rights, the fall of fascism in WWII). Right now, the essay's evidence doesn't back up its ambitious title.

Defining Your Key Term

You use "democracy" as though its meaning is self-evident, but the essay would be stronger if you briefly explained what you mean by it—political freedom? self-determination? the end of authoritarian rule? This would help readers understand exactly what pattern you're tracing across the century, and it would strengthen your argument's precision.

Balancing Personal Voice with Argumentative Structure

Your repeated use of "Perhaps it is because…" is a nice stylistic device for building suspense, but in an argumentative essay, it can make the piece feel more like a diary entry than a thesis-driven argument. Ask yourself: what is the *claim* you want to prove, and what is the *evidence*? Consider moving some of this personal reflection (the Berlin Wall purchase, your feelings on Nov. 4th) into supporting illustrations rather than the emotional core of the essay. This will help the essay read as more persuasive and less anecdotal.

Addressing Counterarguments

An argumentative essay is strengthened by acknowledging complexity. For instance: Not every nation became more democratic in the 20th century (consider ongoing authoritarian regimes, civil wars, genocides). Briefly acknowledging this—and explaining why you still believe "democracy" is the century's defining trend—would make your argument feel more credible and less one-sided.

Paragraph Development

Several paragraphs (especially the one on arms sales and the one on the Soviet Union) are quite short and would benefit from more development—specific facts, dates, or examples rather than general statements. For instance, "Arms sales have gone down" would be more persuasive with a specific statistic or source.

Sentence-Level and Language Issues

Since English is your second language, here are a few patterns to watch for:

Sentence fragments/run-ons: "For change is inevitable, and as difficult as it may be to pinpoint any one accomplishment..." — this sentence is long and starts with "For" used as a conjunction, which is a stylistic choice best used sparingly in formal writing. Consider breaking it into two sentences.
Word choice: "A very capricious decade" — "capricious" usually describes people or behavior that is unpredictable/whimsical; for describing a historical era, you may want a word like "turbulent," "unpredictable," or "eventful."
Tone consistency: Phrases like "I'm feeling very patriotic these days" are conversational; in an academic essay, you might rephrase as "I find myself feeling a renewed sense of patriotism" to maintain a more formal register.
Transitions between paragraphs: Try using transitional phrases that link back to your thesis (eg, "This democratic momentum was not limited to Europe...") so each paragraph clearly builds the argument rather than simply listing events.

Strengthening the Conclusion

Your conclusion ends on a hopeful but vague note ("maybe together, we can learn..."). Consider making your final statement more assertive and tied directly back to your thesis—restate clearly why democracy, in your view, is *the* defining achievement of the century, and perhaps gesture toward what challenges remain as the world moves into the new millennium.

---

Summary of priorities for revision:
Narrow or expand your evidence to match your century-wide claim.
Define "democracy" clearly early on.
Shift some personal reflection into an argumentative frame.
Acknowledge counterexamples.
Polish sentence-level word choice and tone.

You have a strong personal connection to this topic — with some structural revision, this essay's passion can be matched by an equally strong argumentative foundation. I look forward to reading your revision!

Feedback on "The Discovery of Atomic Energy" Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an ambitious and important topic, and your passion for environmental protection comes through clearly. However, the essay currently reads more as an emotional appeal than an academic argument. To strengthen it, you need a clearer thesis, more logical organization, factual accuracy, and evidence to support your claims. Below are specific areas to revise.

Clarify Your Thesis and Argument

Right now, the essay conflates two different things: nuclear weapons (destructive power) and nuclear energy (power generation). These have different risks, benefits, and policy debates. Your essay jumps between them without clearly distinguishing:

Is your argument that nuclear weapons should be abolished?
Is it that nuclear power plants are too dangerous to use?
Or is it about humanity's general apathy toward environmental destruction (which broadens the topic further)?

By the conclusion, you say nuclear energy is "only a small piece" of the environmental problem—but the whole essay before this has treated it as the central issue. This creates a logical inconsistency. Choose one focused argument and develop it consistently.

Check Your Facts

You state that Oppenheimer "unveiled to the world his invention" in 1945 "in the form... of the Atomic bomb." This is historically imprecise. Oppenheimer led the scientific team (the Manhattan Project) that developed the bomb; he did not personally "unveil" it to the world. The bombs were dropped on Hiroshima and Nagasaki by the U.S. military. In academic writing, historical claims must be accurate and, ideally, cited. Consider researching this event more carefully or citing a historical source.

Strengthen Your Evidence

Your claims are currently supported by broad, emotional statements rather than specific evidence:
"Never before had the world seen something with such a capacity to kill" — how many people died? Compare bomb casualties to other weapons.
"We run the risk of contaminating our land... rendering our seas useless" — what specific incidents support this (eg, Chernobyl, Fukushima)? What do scientists say about nuclear plant safety records versus fossil fuel pollution?
"The lack of sight from our leaders is almost appalling" — which leaders? What policies or lack of policies are you referring to?

Academic essays require concrete evidence: statistics, historical events, expert opinions, or scholarly sources. Without these, your argument feels like opinion rather than persuasion grounded in fact.

Structure and Paragraphing

Your paragraphs are very short and each introduces a new idea abruptly, without strong transitions. Consider this structure:
Introduction: Historical context + clear thesis statement
Body 1: The destructive power of nuclear weapons (with evidence)
Body 2: The risks of nuclear energy production (with evidence)
Body 3: Counterargument — acknowledge nuclear energy's benefits (low carbon emissions, energy security) before refuting
Body 4: Call for policy action, with specific recommendations
Conclusion: Restate thesis, broaden to larger environmental context

Adding a counterargument section is especially important for academic argumentative writing—it will make your final position more persuasive.

Language and Grammar

A few recurring issues to watch for:

Comma splices/run-ons: "The discovery of the Atomic energy, is undoubtedly..." — remove the comma before "is."
Repeated words: "the the most awesome" — typo, delete extra "the."
Word choice: "awesome" is informal in modern English (associated with casual speech); consider "extraordinary" or "formidable."
Spelling: "routed" should be "rooted" (rooted in fear); "irreversable" should be "irreversible."
Capitalization: "It is considered" should not be capitalized mid-sentence unless starting a new sentence—check punctuation around it.
Subject-verb agreement: "the world's need... augments" is correct, but double-check tense consistency throughout (you shift between present and past).
Imprecise/unscientific phrasing: "blowing holes in our sky" — this is a nice image but not accurate scientific language. Use "damaging the ozone layer" instead.

The Conclusion

Ending with "Write your local congressman" is a strong, direct call to action, but it appears very suddenly after a more philosophical, "sad tragedy" tone. Consider building toward this call to action earlier in the essay so it feels like a natural conclusion rather than an abrupt shift in register.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Narrow and clarify your thesis (weapons vs. energy vs. broader environmental apathy).
Verify historical facts and consider citing sources.
Add concrete evidence (statistics, case studies, expert opinions).
Include a counterargument.
Proofread carefully for grammar, spelling, and word choice.

You have a compelling topic and clear passion—now it's a matter of grounding that passion in structured, evidence-based argumentation. I'd be happy to discuss any of these points further in office hours.

Feedback on Your Essay: The Impact of Television

Overall Impression

You've chosen an interesting and manageable topic, and your enthusiasm for the subject comes through. However, the essay currently reads more as a general description of television's importance than as a focused argument. To strengthen this piece, you'll need to sharpen your thesis, ensure your paragraphs consistently support that thesis, and revise several sentences for clarity. Below are specific suggestions organized by area.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your introduction promises two things: (1) television is the most influential invention of the 20th century, and (2) it "has several downsides" and "can be used in a useless way." This sets up an essay that should balance benefits against drawbacks. However, your body paragraphs focus almost entirely on benefits (shrinking the globe, war coverage, connecting populations), and the downside is compressed into a single vague sentence near the end: "It is also used in ways that can desensitize one's sense of what is actually being portrayed."

Suggestion: Decide what your essay is actually arguing. If your real claim is "television is the most influential invention of the century," then remove the promise of discussing downsides, or dedicate a full paragraph to examining them seriously (eg, desensitization, misinformation, decreased attention spans, passive consumption). Right now, the imbalance makes the essay feel unfinished.

Paragraph Organization

Each paragraph should have one clear, guiding idea stated early. Your second paragraph opens with: "Not even truly dealing with the amount of television that is watched by the average person, there are several other important ways that television had changed people's lives." This sentence is confusing—readers don't yet know what "other ways" refers to, and the double negative construction ("Not even truly dealing with...") obscures your point.

Suggestion: Try a direct topic sentence, such as: "Beyond entertainment, television has transformed public awareness of world events, particularly during wartime." This tells the reader exactly what the paragraph will cover.

Similarly, your third paragraph tries to cover three ideas (television shrinking the globe, television as a communication tool, and television's harmful effects) without transitions between them. Consider separating these into distinct paragraphs, each with its own evidence.

Development and Evidence

Several claims need more support. For example:
"This was one of the factors that led to much of the protesting" (regarding Vietnam) — *which factors? What evidence connects television coverage to protest movements? A brief example or statistic would strengthen this.*
"this has led to public concern and effort, for instance, in Somalia" — *This is vague. What specific effort? Consider naming the humanitarian response or a specific event (eg, the "CNN effect" is a well-known concept you might research and cite).*

Adding even one concrete detail per claim (a date, a statistic, a named event) will make your argument far more persuasive.

Sentence-Level and Grammar Issues

A few patterns to watch for:

Word order: "it has made smaller the dimensions of the planet" → Consider: "it has made the planet smaller" or "it has shrunk the dimensions of our planet."
Awkward phrasing: "like all inventions can be used in a useless way" is unclear. Do you mean "misused" or "used carelessly"? Try: "like all inventions, it can be misused."
Proper nouns: "vietnam war" should be capitalized: "Vietnam War."
Wordy openings: "One of the most significant inventions of the 20th century has to do with the speed at which information is now communicated" could be more direct: "One of the most significant developments of the 20th century is the speed at which information now travels across the globe."
Vague phrasing: "unknown and unheard of areas" — consider "previously unknown or unreachable regions" for more precision.
Run-on/comma splices: "This simple fact has changed in many ways how we live, and in a way it has, and continues to shrink the planet we live on." This sentence is repetitive ("in a way... in many ways") and could be split: "This simple fact has changed how we live in many ways. In a sense, it has continued to shrink the planet we live on."

Conclusion

Your final sentence, "Either way, however, that it is used, there is no doubt that it has been one of the most significant inventions of the 20th century," is grammatically unclear. Consider: "Whether used for good or ill, television remains one of the most significant inventions of the 20th century." This restates your thesis more clearly and gives the essay a stronger sense of closure.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Clarify your thesis: decide whether this is primarily a "benefits" essay or a "benefits vs. drawbacks" essay, and follow through accordingly.
Add a substantive paragraph on drawbacks if you keep that claim in your introduction.
Strengthen topic sentences so each paragraph's purpose is immediately clear.
Add specific evidence (dates, named events, statistics) to support broad claims.
Proofread for word order, capitalization, and run-on sentences.

You have a strong foundation and clear passion for the topic—now it's a matter of tightening the argument and clarifying your sentences. I look forward to seeing your revision!

Overall Impression

However, your body paragraphs only address the first two ideas—and even these are somewhat merged into a single paragraph. Suggestion: Either revise your thesis to reflect only the ideas you actually discuss (travel/cultural perception and international relations), or add a paragraph exploring the significance of flight itself—perhaps discussing how aviation changed human self-perception, inspired scientific/technological advancement, or became a symbol of progress in literature and culture.

Additionally, the essay currently has no conclusion. It ends abruptly after discussing postal communication. A concluding paragraph should restate your thesis in new words, synthesize your main points, and perhaps offer a broader reflection on the airplane's legacy today.

Development and Evidence

Your claims are broad and would benefit from more specific support:

In paragraph 2, you mention learning "a lot" about your own culture in Spain, but you don't specify what you learned. Adding one or two concrete examples (a cultural difference you noticed, a moment of realization) would make this anecdote more persuasive and vivid.
In paragraph 3, you assert that air travel improved "international business endeavors" and "international trade," but you offer no historical evidence, statistics, or examples (eg, growth of global tourism, express air-mail delivery, diplomatic summits made possible by quick travel). Language and Grammar

As you continue developing academic English writing, here are some specific issues to revise:

"The extend to which" → should be *"extent."* This is a common confusion between the verb "extend" and the noun "extent."
"astronomical" is a vivid word, but in academic writing, more precise alternatives like *"immense," "profound,"* or *"far-reaching"* may fit better.
"The frequency that which foreign countries are able to communicate..." → This phrase is grammatically tangled. Consider: *"The frequency with which foreign countries can communicate and negotiate face-to-face has drastically improved international trade."*
"has improved drastically the establishment of international trade" → Word order should be: *"has drastically improved the establishment of international trade."* In English, adverbs like "drastically" typically come before the verb they modify, not between the verb and its object.
"Life with airplanes is more rapid paced." → This is a good closing idea but needs elaboration—rapid-paced *compared to what?* Consider integrating this into a fuller concluding thought rather than leaving it as a short, disconnected sentence.

Paragraph Transitions

Paragraph 3 begins with a repetition of the "doors" metaphor from the introduction, which feels mechanical rather than organic. Try varying your transitional strategy—perhaps by directly connecting the previous paragraph's idea (cultural understanding) to how that same speed and access also transformed diplomacy and commerce.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Add a paragraph addressing the "flying" concept mentioned in your introduction, or revise the thesis to match your actual content.
Write a conclusion that ties your ideas together and offers a final insight.
Add specific examples/evidence to strengthen your claims about culture and international trade.
Revise grammar issues, especially word order, article usage, and word choice ("extend"/"extent").
Vary your transitional language to avoid repeating the same metaphor.

With these revisions, your essay will have a clearer structure, stronger evidence, and more polished language—qualities that will make your argument far more persuasive. You have a good foundation; now it's a matter of building it out with more precision and depth.

Feedback on Your Essay on AIDS

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an important and still-relevant topic, and I can see you care about the subject. However, the essay currently reads more like a series of loosely connected observations than a unified argument. To strengthen it, I'd suggest revising in four areas: thesis clarity, organization, development of ideas, and language/mechanics.

Thesis and Central Argument

Your opening paragraph sets up a contrast (medical progress vs. AIDS as an unsolved problem), but it's not clear what claim you want to defend. Is your argument:
That education is more effective than medicine in fighting AIDS?
That public misconceptions about who is "at risk" are dangerous?
That society needs a combined approach of education and continued medical research?

Right now, the essay touches on all three but commits fully to none. I'd recommend choosing one clear, arguable thesis and stating it explicitly at the end of your introduction. For example: *"Because medical treatments for AIDS remain limited, public education about transmission and risk is currently our most effective tool for prevention."* Every paragraph should then work to support this single claim.

Organization

The paragraphs currently jump between topics without clear transitions:
Paragraph 2 introduces education/support centers.
Paragraph 3 briefly mentions school assemblies (only two sentences—this feels underdeveloped and could be merged with paragraph 2).
Paragraph 4 shifts to *who* can carry AIDS and includes an aside about drug use as "suicide."
Paragraph 5 shifts again to casual sexual behavior.
Paragraph 6 is a strange, almost self-referential aside ("Obviously, our culture has begun to educate its population or I would be unable to write about this particular topic") that doesn't clearly connect to your argument and could be cut.
Paragraph 7 restates the introduction rather than offering a strong conclusion that ties your points together.

Suggestion: Try outlining your essay before rewriting it. Group related ideas (eg, all points about who is at risk into one paragraph; all points about education initiatives into another). Use topic sentences that clearly state each paragraph's main idea and explicitly link back to your thesis.

Development of Ideas

Several claims need more support or explanation:
"Due to the ineffectiveness of medicines" — this is a strong claim. Has AIDS treatment truly been "ineffective," or has it simply not produced a cure? This distinction matters and could be nuanced (antiretroviral therapy, for instance, has extended lives significantly, even if it hasn't cured the disease).
The dentist example ("forget to put on their plastic gloves") is vague and unsupported—where does this come from? Consider replacing anecdotal or unverified claims with actual statistics or cited sources about transmission routes.
The comparison of drug use to "suicide" is an interesting rhetorical move, but it needs more explanation. Why does injecting drugs specifically raise AIDS risk (needle-sharing)? Spell out the logic for your reader.
You mention "our culture has begun to educate its population," but you don't give concrete evidence of *how* (beyond hospital support centers and one school assembly example). More specific, researched examples would strengthen your credibility.

Suggestion: Since this is an argumentative essay, each paragraph should include a claim, evidence (ideally from credible sources), and analysis explaining how the evidence supports your thesis. Even one or two outside sources (statistics from health organizations, expert quotations) would significantly strengthen your argument.

Language, Style, and Mechanics

Because this is academic writing, a few style issues need attention:
Avoid text-message abbreviations like "w/," "&," and "b/c." Spell out "with," "and," and "because" in formal writing.
Watch subject-verb agreement: "The AIDS viruses was discovered" should be "The AIDS virus was discovered."
Comma splices/run-ons: Sentences joined by "however" need a semicolon or period beforehand, not just a comma: "...ignore that it exists; however, it does..." (this one is correct) but check others carefully, eg, "people interact sexual w/ complete strangers" needs "interact sexually with."
Word choice: "victims of AIDS" may be considered outdated or stigmatizing; "people living with AIDS" is more commonly used in current writing.
Tone: Phrases like "selling their lives short" are informal/colloquial; academic essays generally favor more precise, objective language.

Next Steps

Write a one-sentence thesis and check that every paragraph supports it.
Reorganize paragraphs by topic, using clear topic sentences.
Add at least 2–3 credible sources (statistics, expert opinions) to support your claims.
Do a full proofreading pass to fix abbreviations, subject-verb agreement, and sentence boundaries.

You have a genuine argument here—it just needs sharper focus and stronger support. I'd be glad to look at your revised draft once you've reworked the structure.

Feedback on "Contact Lenses" Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay is engaging and personal—readers can tell you genuinely love contact lenses, and your enthusiasm comes through in vivid details like feeling your way to the bathroom mirror. However, the assignment asks for an argumentative essay, and right now this reads more like a personal reflection or narrative. To meet the goals of the assignment, you'll need to shift the focus from "why contact lenses changed my life" to "why contact lenses deserve to be called one of the most important inventions of the 20th century." Below are specific areas to revise.

Strengthen Your Argument (Content)

Your thesis claims contact lenses are "one of the most important inventions of the 20th century," but your evidence is entirely personal (basketball, swimming, waking up and seeing clearly). This is a common issue: personal experience is compelling, but it doesn't prove a *general* claim about importance on a historical or societal scale.

To fix this, consider:
Adding evidence beyond your own life—eg, how contact lenses have helped athletes, workers in certain professions, people with severe astigmatism, or those who cannot have LASIK surgery.
Explaining *why* "important" is the right word—compare contacts to other vision technologies (glasses, LASIK) and explain what unique problem they solved.
Addressing counterarguments. A strong argumentative essay acknowledges the other side. For example: some people argue LASIK surgery is more "important" because it eliminates the need for lenses altogether; others point out risks like eye infections from improper contact lens care. Bringing these up—and responding to them—will make your argument far more persuasive than simply asserting "glasses are a thing of the past."

Reconsider Your Organization

Right now the essay is organized chronologically (hard lenses → soft lenses → your own experience → disposables → colored lenses). This works for a narrative but not for an argument. Try organizing by reasons instead:
Reason 1: Contacts improved quality of life for athletes/active people (use your basketball/swimming story as *supporting evidence*, not the main point).
Reason 2: Innovations like disposables made lenses more practical and hygienic.
Reason 3: (broader impact) Contacts gave people more than convenience—confidence, safety, freedom.

Each paragraph should open with a claim, not a personal anecdote. Save your stories to *support* the claim.

Revise the Conclusion

Your conclusion ("glasses are a thing of the past!") is an overstatement that isn't fully supported by your evidence—many people still wear glasses successfully. Consider softening this claim or explaining more precisely: perhaps argue that contacts *transformed* vision correction rather than *replaced* glasses entirely. This will make your argument more credible and less absolute.

Language and Style Issues

Since English is your second language, here are some patterns to watch for:

Ampersands (&): Academic writing requires "and" spelled out, not "&". This appears throughout the essay.
Comma splices/run-ons: Sentences like "I had been wearing glasses since the time I was 10 & I was tired of being limited..." should be split into two sentences or joined with a conjunction ("...I was 10, and I was tired...").
Subject-verb agreement: "a style called 'hard lenses' were the only kind" → should be "*was* the only kind" (singular subject "a style").
Spelling consistency: You alternate between "lense" and "lens." The correct singular form is "lens" (plural: "lenses"). Please proofread for this throughout.
Tone: Exclamation points and rhetorical questions ("If glasses were my only form of seeing better, I'd be blind most of the time!") work in informal writing but should be minimized in academic argumentative essays. Aim for a more measured, persuasive tone.
Informal phrasing: Words like "craze" and phrases like "who am I to say!" are casual. Consider more formal alternatives suited to academic writing.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Add a clear, arguable thesis with supporting reasons (not just personal preference).
Reorganize by argument, not chronology.
Include a counterargument and rebuttal.
Broaden your evidence beyond personal anecdotes.
Edit for grammar, punctuation, and tone—especially ampersands, comma splices, and consistent spelling.

You have wonderful personal material here—the key is to use it as *support* for a broader, well-reasoned argument rather than letting it *be* the argument. I look forward to seeing your revision!

Feedback Report: "Feminism as a Discovery"

Overall Impression

You've chosen an intriguing and original angle for this essay—framing feminism as a "discovery" akin to technological inventions. This is a creative conceptual move that could make for a memorable essay. However, the essay in its current form is underdeveloped: at roughly 350 words, it doesn't have the space to fully argue this central comparison, support its claims with evidence, or address counterarguments. Below are suggestions organized by argument, organization, and language.

Argument and Development

The central analogy needs more justification. You open by comparing feminism to cars, telephones, and nuclear energy, but you never really explain *why* a social movement should be understood as a "discovery" in the same sense as a technological invention. Is feminism a discovery because society is uncovering pre-existing truths about inequality? Or is it more like an invention—a newly created way of organizing society? These are different claims, and your essay would be stronger if you took a clear position and defended it explicitly, rather than leaving the metaphor implicit.

Claims need evidence and citations. Several factual claims are asserted without support:
The statistic that women earn "60 cents for every dollar" needs a source and a date (this figure has changed over time and varies by context).
The claim that women won the right to vote "in the 1800s" is historically inaccurate for the U.S. (the 19th Amendment passed in 1920); please verify your dates.
The characterization of the 1970s ERA as "the first significant equal rights movement" overlooks earlier waves of feminism (eg, suffrage itself, or the women's rights conventions of the mid-1800s). This claim actually contradicts your own parenthetical mention of 19th-century suffrage.

I'd recommend fact-checking these details and citing at least one or two credible sources. This will strengthen your credibility as a writer.

The essay needs a counterargument. Strong argumentative essays anticipate objections. For instance, some readers might argue that feminism doesn't belong in the same category as scientific/technological discoveries because it is a political and social process rather than an empirical fact. Addressing this objection—even briefly—would make your argument more persuasive and show critical engagement with your own thesis.

Paragraph 3 (on image and pornography) feels underdeveloped. You raise an important point about how language and media construct images of women, but this paragraph is only three sentences and lacks a concrete example. Consider adding a specific case (an advertisement, a common phrase, a media trend) to illustrate your point.

Organization

Your essay has a clear four-part structure (intro, historical progress, image/representation, conclusion), which is good. However:

The topic sentences of paragraphs 2 and 3 could more explicitly connect back to your "discovery" metaphor. Right now, once you move into historical detail, the central conceit disappears until the conclusion.
Consider adding a transition sentence between paragraphs 2 and 3 that explains the shift from economic/legal equality to representational equality (eg, "Beyond legal and economic status, feminism also concerns something less tangible: how women are seen.")
The conclusion is quite short. Since this is your chance to fully deliver on the "discovery" metaphor, consider expanding it—explain more precisely what "completing" this discovery would look like, and perhaps return to your opening comparison with technology one more time for symmetry.

Language and Style

Your English is generally clear and grammatically strong, which is impressive. A few notes:

Typo: "0th century" should be "20th century."
Article usage: "the woman is still only earning" should be "women still earn only..." (avoid unnecessary definite articles with generic plural nouns).
Wordiness: "It's only recently that we've 'discovered', or begun to think about rather, how much..." could be tightened to: "Only recently have we begun to recognize how much..."
Punctuation: Check comma placement around "As recently as the 1950s, it was still the most typical as well as the ideal to have..." — this sentence is a bit awkward; consider: "As recently as the 1950s, the ideal—and the norm—was for women to remain at home, keeping house and raising children."

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Expand the essay significantly—aim for full development of each point with evidence.
Clarify and defend the "discovery" metaphor explicitly.
Verify all historical facts and cite sources for statistics.
Add a counterargument and response.
Strengthen transitions between paragraphs.
Proofread for minor grammatical issues (articles, wordiness).

This is a promising start with a genuinely interesting thesis—the next draft should focus on depth, evidence, and argumentative rigor.

Feedback Report: Argumentative Essay on Environmental Awareness

Overall Impression

Your essay addresses a compelling and relevant topic, and your enthusiasm for environmental issues is clear. However, the essay currently reads more like a list of environmental concerns than an argument about how this idea changed people's lives in the 20th century. To strengthen this piece, you'll need to sharpen your thesis, deepen your analysis, and revise several grammar and style issues common to academic writing.

Thesis and Focus

Your opening claim—that environmental awareness is the most significant idea shaping 20th-century life—is a strong, arguable thesis. However, the body paragraphs don't consistently return to this claim. Instead of just describing *what* people do now (recycle, turn off lights, etc), each paragraph should explain *why* this represents a meaningful *change* and *how* it has reshaped daily life, policy, or global thinking. Consider adding a sentence at the end of each paragraph that explicitly ties the example back to your central argument.

Additionally, the first paragraph on nuclear weapons and waste feels disconnected. Nuclear weapons are primarily a political/military issue, not strictly an environmental one. If you want to keep this content, reframe it explicitly as an environmental concern (eg, radioactive contamination, testing sites, long-term ecological damage) so it clearly supports your thesis.

Organization and Development

Structurally, your five body paragraphs (nuclear issues, nature/resources, recycling, energy, animal rights) are reasonable subtopics, but they are underdeveloped. Right now, each paragraph offers a general claim ("we realize X") without concrete evidence—dates, statistics, historical events, or named organizations/policies. For example:

Instead of "many groups have formed to plant more trees," name a specific movement (eg, the Green Belt Movement) or reforestation policy.
Instead of "companies are no longer able to freely dump their wastes," reference a specific law (eg, the Clean Water Act, 1972) to ground your claim historically.

This kind of specific evidence will make your argument far more persuasive and demonstrate genuine engagement with the 20th-century timeline you're arguing about.

Language and Style

A few recurring issues need attention:

Informal abbreviations: Academic essays should avoid "&," "w/o," and "b/c." Always spell out "and," "without," and "because."

Redundant/incorrect grammar:
   - "countries that which have many weapons" → should be "countries that have many weapons" (delete "which").
   - "We realize that conserving is not beneficial to our environment but it is also saves money" — this sentence is logically and grammatically tangled. You likely mean: "Conserving energy is not only beneficial to the environment but also saves money."

Word choice: "considerable of our animal life" should be "considerate toward animal life." Also, "topic" and "issue" are overused—try varying your vocabulary (concern, matter, phenomenon).

Repetitive sentence openings: Several paragraphs begin with "We realize..." or "People have become..." Vary your sentence structures to improve flow and demonstrate stronger control of English syntax.

Argumentation and Tone

Your conclusion makes a very strong, somewhat unrealistic claim: that by the 21st century "we will have fixed everything we have destroyed." This kind of absolute statement weakens your credibility as a writer, since it's not something you can support with evidence. Academic arguments should be measured—acknowledge progress, but also note remaining challenges or ongoing tensions (eg, between economic growth and conservation). This will make your conclusion more sophisticated and analytically honest.

Suggestions for Revision

Add a clear analytical thread in each paragraph that connects your example back to the thesis about *changed lives*.
Incorporate specific historical evidence—dates, laws, organizations, statistics—to substantiate general claims.
Eliminate informal abbreviations and revise grammatically awkward sentences.
Revise the conclusion to avoid overly optimistic, unsupported predictions; instead, acknowledge both progress and ongoing challenges.
Reconsider the nuclear weapons paragraph—either reframe it as clearly environmental or replace it with a more directly relevant example.

Final Note

You have chosen a rich, meaningful topic with real argumentative potential. With more specific evidence, clearer paragraph-to-thesis connections, and careful editing of grammar and tone, this essay can move from simply *describing* environmental awareness to *arguing persuasively* about its transformative impact on 20th-century life. Keep developing your ideas—you're already asking the right questions.

Feedback Report: Draft Essay on Computers

Overall Impression

Your essay offers genuine, personal examples of how computers have changed your life, and your enthusiasm for the topic comes through. However, the essay currently reads more like a personal reflection or narrative than an *argumentative* essay. To strengthen it, you'll need to sharpen your thesis, tighten your organization, and address academic writing conventions more consistently.

Thesis and Argument

Right now, your central claim is very broad: "Computers have definitely effected [sic] people's lives." This is true, but it's not really debatable—few readers would disagree. An argumentative essay needs a claim that requires defense, such as: *"Computers have fundamentally transformed personal record-keeping and international communication in ways that make daily life more efficient, though this convenience comes with a dependency we rarely acknowledge."* Consider narrowing your focus (eg, to *organization and efficiency*, or *access to international opportunities*) and making a claim that invites discussion, not just agreement.

Organization

The essay has two loosely connected sections: (1) your personal computer use at home, and (2) your experience arriving in Madrid. The connection between these two ideas is implied but never explicitly argued. Ask yourself: what is the *single* point these two examples are meant to prove? Currently, the transition ("I'm now here in Madrid. How did I get here?") is abrupt and reads as a narrative device rather than an analytical bridge.

Suggestion: Add a clear topic sentence at the start of each paragraph that states the specific point that paragraph will support. For example, Paragraph 2 might begin: "Computers have also made international education and travel more accessible, as my own experience studying abroad demonstrates."

Development of Evidence

Your examples (checkbook balancing, word processing, the Madrid program application) are relevant, but they remain descriptive rather than analytical. For each example, push yourself to explain *why* it matters:
How much time did the computer actually save you?
What specifically would have gone wrong without computer processing at the International Programs Office?

Right now you assume computers were involved ("I assume my info was processed by a computer")—admitting uncertainty weakens the argument. Either find out how the process worked, or reframe this as a hypothetical scenario more confidently.

Grammar and Mechanics

There are several recurring issues to address in revision:

Word confusion: "effected" should be "affected" (affect = verb, to influence; effect = noun, a result).
Verb tense/agreement: "my life has change" → "has changed"; "would inevitably inundated" → "would inevitably be inundated."
Article usage: "applied to the this program" → "applied to this program."
Comma splices: Many sentences run together independent clauses with only a comma, eg, "I no longer need to keep these records but only to transfer them to my computer." Consider breaking these into separate sentences or using semicolons/conjunctions correctly.
Sentence fragments and run-ons: The long sentence beginning "I'm able to store almost all the information..." tries to do too much. Break it into 2–3 shorter sentences for clarity.

Register and Formality

Academic essays should avoid the informal abbreviations you've used throughout: "w/", "&", "info." Please write these out in full ("with," "and," "information"). This is one of the easiest fixes and will immediately make the essay look more polished and appropriate for an academic audience.

Conclusion

Your final sentence ("Computers have had a most significant impact on the people in the latter half of the 20th century") restates your opening claim almost exactly, which suggests the essay hasn't developed much between introduction and conclusion. A strong conclusion should synthesize your specific examples and return to your (revised, sharper) thesis with new insight—perhaps reflecting on what this dependency means for the future, or how it has changed the relationship between individuals and institutions (like universities).

Revision Priorities

For your next draft, focus on:
Narrowing and sharpening your thesis into a debatable claim.
Adding clear topic sentences that connect each paragraph back to that thesis.
Eliminating informal abbreviations and fixing comma splices.
Making your Madrid example more analytical rather than narrative.

You have strong personal material to work with—the task now is to shape it into a more focused, formal argument. I'd be happy to discuss thesis options with you before your next draft.

Feedback on Your Essay: The Satellite and the Tiananmen Square Example

Overall Impression

You've chosen a compelling topic and a powerful historical example. The idea that satellites bring "nowness" to global events is genuinely interesting, and your emotional investment in the Tiananmen Square example comes through clearly. However, the essay currently reads as a single, dense paragraph with several structural and language issues that obscure your argument. Below are specific areas to work on.

Structure and Paragraphing

Right now, the entire essay is one paragraph. This makes it hard for the reader to follow your reasoning. Consider breaking it into:
Introduction: State your thesis clearly (eg, "The satellite is the most important invention of the century because it allows the world to witness events as they happen, which has changed how nations are held accountable.")
Body paragraph 1: Explain what makes the satellite unique compared to radio, TV, and videotape.
Body paragraph 2: Develop the Tiananmen Square example in detail—what happened, how satellite coverage mattered, what the effects were.
Conclusion: Restate your argument and its broader significance.

Also, "As it happens- the most profound byproduct of the satellite" is a sentence fragment, not a complete sentence. Revise it into a full sentence, such as: "This immediacy—the ability to witness events *as they happen*—is the satellite's most profound byproduct."

Strengthening the Argument

Your central claim—"the satellite is the invention of the century"—is a bold, sweeping statement. To make it convincing:
Acknowledge counterarguments. Other inventions (the internet, the computer, antibiotics) might also claim this title. Briefly explain why the satellite's contribution is more significant, or narrow your claim to something like "the satellite has had the most transformative effect on global political awareness."
Avoid overstatement. You claim that "without the satellite, the movement for democracy... would have been lost" and that governments will now be "less tempted to do wrong." These are strong causal claims. Consider softening them slightly (eg, "may have received far less international attention") and briefly explaining *why* this is true, rather than simply asserting it.
Develop the example further. You mention the Tiananmen Square protest but don't fully explain the causal chain: How exactly did satellite footage reach the world? What specific reactions did it provoke from other governments? Adding one or two concrete details (eg, international condemnations, sanctions, media coverage) would make your argument more persuasive.
Clarify the final image. The sentence "when China is placed down the block, who wouldn't want to help his neighbor?" is a metaphor, but it's unclear. Explain what you mean—perhaps that satellite technology makes distant countries feel like immediate neighbors, inspiring a sense of shared responsibility. Spell this connection out for the reader.

Language and Mechanics

There are several spelling and word-choice errors to correct:
"evention" → invention
"preceed" → precede
"openned" → opened
"Teniman Square" → Tiananmen Square
"litterally" → literally
"he young hero" → the young hero
"sensorship" → censorship
"mor" → more
"a like" → alike
"now the world functions" → how the world functions
"Freedom US. the tank of injustice" → likely meant "Freedom vs. the tank of injustice" — write out "versus" or use "vs." correctly with a period and space.

I recommend using a spell-checker and reading your essay aloud slowly to catch these errors before submission. Since English spelling doesn't always match pronunciation, keeping a personal list of frequently misspelled words (like "invention," "precede," "literally") can help you catch these patterns over time.

Clarifying Key Terms

You use the term "geo-centric satellite," but I suspect you mean geostationary satellite (a satellite that stays fixed relative to a point on Earth, enabling continuous broadcast). If this is correct, use the accurate technical term, as it will strengthen your credibility on the topic. If "geo-centric" is intentional, briefly define it for readers unfamiliar with the term.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Break the essay into clear paragraphs with a stated thesis.
Strengthen and qualify your causal claims about the satellite's effects.
Expand the Tiananmen Square example with concrete details.
Correct spelling and grammar errors (see list above).
Clarify the final metaphor about "neighbors."

Your instinct to use a real historical event to support an abstract claim is strong—this is exactly the kind of move that makes an argumentative essay persuasive. With clearer structure and more precise language, this essay has the potential to be very effective. I'd be happy to review a revised draft.

Overall Impression

Your essay addresses an interesting and relevant topic, and your enthusiasm for the subject comes through. However, the essay currently reads more as a general description of how computers are useful rather than an argumentative essay. Your introduction states that computers have had "a tremendous influence," but this is not really an arguable claim—few readers would disagree. A strong argumentative essay needs a thesis that takes a specific, debatable position. For example, you might argue:

Computers have fundamentally changed education for the better, despite concerns about reduced critical thinking skills.
The benefits of computer technology in business and education outweigh the risks of job loss or over-reliance on machines.

Currently, your essay simply lists benefits (data storage, easier writing, teaching tools) without engaging with any opposing view or deeper complexity. Consider adding a counterargument paragraph—for instance, addressing concerns about screen time, misinformation, or job displacement—and then explaining why your position still holds. This will make the essay feel more like a persuasive argument and less like a list of advantages.

Organization and Development

Each body paragraph introduces a good topic (businesses, students, teaching tools) but is very short and underdeveloped. Right now, most paragraphs contain only two or three sentences with general claims and no specific evidence. To improve:

Language and Mechanics

There are several recurring issues that need attention before this essay is ready for submission:

Spelling errors (likely due to typing quickly or auto-correct being off):
"definantly" → definitely
"accesible" → accessible
"cosm graph" → this is unclear—did you mean "cosmology" or "coordinate graph"? Please clarify this term.
"previosly" → previously
"imp" → this looks like an abbreviation left in by accident (perhaps "important"?)
"extend" → extent (paragraph 5)

Grammar issues:
"they is clearly imp" → subject-verb agreement error; should be "they are clearly important."
"enabled to students to learn" → should be "enabled students to learn" (remove "to" before students).
"w/o" → avoid text-message abbreviations in academic writing; write "without."

Sentence-level clarity:
The sentence "Students no longer have to spend hours trying to type their paper w/o making spelling errors, now they can focus..." is a run-on/comma splice. Consider breaking it into two sentences: "Students no longer have to spend hours typing without making spelling errors. Now they can focus their time on writing and let the computer handle spelling errors."

Register/tone:
Phrases like "a god send" and "who knows where these machines will take us" are too informal for academic writing. Replace with more formal phrasing, such as "an invaluable tool" and "the long-term implications of this technology remain to be seen."

Use of Evidence

Right now, all claims are unsupported by outside sources. Since this is an argumentative essay, including at least one or two citations (statistics on computer use in schools, quotes from educators or businesspeople) would significantly strengthen your credibility and move the essay beyond personal opinion.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Sharpen your thesis into a debatable claim.
Add a counterargument and rebuttal.
Expand body paragraphs with specific examples and evidence.
Proofread carefully for spelling, subject-verb agreement, and informal language.
Strengthen the conclusion with a clear restatement and forward-looking insight.

You have a solid foundation and clear enthusiasm for the topic—now it's a matter of tightening the argument and polishing the language. I'd be happy to review a revised draft once you've made these changes.

Feedback on Your Essay: The Impact of Television

Overall Impression

You've chosen an excellent topic, and your ideas about television's role in creating a "global village" are thoughtful and relevant. You clearly understand the significance of this invention. However, the essay needs stronger organization, clearer sentence structure, and more developed argumentation to fully convince your reader. Below are specific areas to focus on for revision.

Content and Argument

Strengths: Your central idea—that television enables instant, unmediated access to information, which allows people to form independent judgments and connects distant cultures—is a strong and interesting claim. The "global village" reference is a nice touch that shows engagement with existing ideas about media.

Areas to develop:
Add concrete examples. Right now, your argument stays abstract and hypothetical ("if something is happening... every person in the world can watch"). Consider naming an actual historical event that was witnessed live on television (eg, the moon landing, the fall of the Berlin Wall, a major sports event) to ground your argument in reality and make it more persuasive.

Balance your argument more evenly. You spend most of the essay praising television, then briefly acknowledge its negative effects (glorified violence, unrealistic portrayals) in the final paragraph—but you dismiss this concern quickly ("this sense of reality is not so distorted") without strong evidence. If you want to make this counterargument, give it more space and support; otherwise, consider removing it or integrating it more smoothly with your main thesis.

Clarify the relationship between your two main points. You discuss (1) television as a news medium and (2) television as entertainment, but the connection between these two functions isn't explained. Are they equally important? Does one support the other? A clearer transition or unifying statement would help.

Organization

The essay's structure needs strengthening:

Introduction: Your first paragraph does a lot of work (introduces TV, explains its news function, discusses global unification) but lacks a clear thesis statement that previews your whole argument, including the entertainment function you introduce later. Consider adding one sentence near the end of paragraph 1 that outlines both main points (news/global connection AND entertainment/life lessons).

Paragraph 2 (the "global village" paragraph) feels like an aside rather than a developed point. Since it's only two sentences, consider merging it into paragraph 1 as a concluding thought, rather than giving it a separate paragraph.

Paragraph 3 shifts to entertainment somewhat abruptly. Add a transition sentence explicitly linking this new idea back to your thesis (eg, "Beyond its role in the news, television also shapes how people understand life through entertainment.").

Conclusion: The essay ends rather suddenly with "TV can be an excellent tool for understanding the world we live in." This works as a concluding thought, but you need a fuller conclusion that ties together your two main points (news + entertainment) and perhaps returns to your opening idea about a "smaller, more unified" world.

Language and Sentence Structure

This is the area needing the most attention. You have many long sentences joined by commas and dashes where periods or conjunctions are needed. For example:

> "...now they can see it happening, for themselves, as it is happening, this allows them to make their own, informed judgment about the event; and if a reaction is necessary..."

This sentence contains a comma splice (two complete sentences joined only by a comma) and could be split into two or three shorter sentences for clarity:

> "Now they can see events happening for themselves, in real time. This allows them to make their own informed judgments. If a reaction is necessary..."

Specific corrections:
"thru" → "through" (avoid informal spellings in academic writing)
Reduce dash usage; dashes are appropriate for emphasis but overusing them makes sentences feel unstructured. Try replacing some with periods or semicolons used correctly (a semicolon should join two complete, related sentences, not connect a sentence to a fragment).
Watch for run-on sentences throughout paragraph 1 especially. Reading your work aloud can help you hear where natural sentence breaks should occur.

Suggestions for Revision

Add a clear thesis statement at the end of paragraph 1.
Break up long, comma-heavy sentences into shorter, clearer ones.
Add at least one concrete historical example to support your point about global news.
Either expand your counterargument about TV's negative effects with real support, or shorten it further.
Write a stronger conclusion that ties both main points together.

You have a genuinely interesting argument here—with revision focused on sentence-level clarity and stronger organization, this essay could be quite compelling. Good work overall; keep refining!

Feedback on "The Microwave Revolution" Essay

Overall Impression

This essay demonstrates creativity and ambition—the extended metaphor comparing culinary history to "calm seas" disrupted by a "storm" is a striking opening move, and your enthusiasm for the topic is clear throughout. However, the essay needs significant revision in three areas: argument development, organizational clarity, and language accuracy. Below are specific suggestions organized by category.

Argument and Content

Strengthen your central claim. Your thesis seems to be that the microwave represents a revolutionary "break from tradition" in cooking history. This is an interesting claim, but you need to more directly define what counts as a "revolution" and then systematically prove the microwave meets that standard. Right now, the essay lists impressive facts (no fire, faster cooking, safer for children) without building toward a cumulative argument.

Address counterarguments. A stronger essay would acknowledge that the microwave has limitations (eg, it doesn't replace all cooking methods, some argue it produces different flavor/texture) before showing why it's still revolutionary despite these limits. This would make your argument more persuasive rather than one-sided.

Verify your scientific claim. You state that microwaves work by "radiation to excite water particles in food, thus creating friction." This is a common simplification, but consider being more precise—microwaves cause water molecules to rotate rapidly, and this molecular movement generates heat. Since this is a key piece of evidence for your "revolutionary science" claim, it's worth getting exactly right, and possibly citing a source.

Organization

Trim the extended metaphor. The ocean/storm metaphor in your opening paragraph is vivid, but it runs long and delays your actual argument. Consider condensing it to 2-3 sentences, then move quickly to your thesis statement.

Clarify paragraph purposes. Currently:
Paragraph 2 argues the microwave is a "break in tradition" (cooking method)
Paragraph 3 argues the microwave saves time
Paragraph 4 restates the thesis

This structure is reasonable, but paragraph 3 tries to cover *too much*—time-saving for working mothers, reheating leftovers, AND child safety. Consider splitting this into two focused paragraphs: one on time efficiency, one on safety. Each deserves fuller development.

Strengthen your conclusion. Your final paragraph largely repeats earlier points rather than synthesizing them or offering a new insight. Consider ending with a forward-looking statement: What does the microwave's success suggest about future kitchen innovations? Or: What does this case teach us about how technological "revolutions" happen?

Language and Mechanics

You have talent for imagery, but frequent errors distract from your ideas. Here are patterns to address:

Spelling:
"culunary" → culinary
"consistant" → consistent
"rythm" → rhythm
"twentyth" → twentieth
"differents perspectives" → different perspectives
"comodity" → commodity
"dizzing" → dizzying

Grammar/Verb tense consistency:
"Progress has move slow" → "Progress has moved slowly" (also: use adverb form)
"the constant rythm...have showed" → subject-verb agreement issue; should be "has shown"
"A mother who works, can come home can whip up" → remove one "can"; fix comma splice: "A mother who works can come home and whip up something..."

Register/Tone:
Avoid ALL CAPS for emphasis in academic writing (eg, "FIRE"). Instead, use italics sparingly or simply strong word choice: "Flame—the ancient source of cooked food—is entirely absent."
Casual phrases like "bewitched" or "under the influence of heavy drugs" are colorful but may be too informal for academic argumentation. Consider whether hyperbole serves your argument or undermines its credibility.

Sentence fragments and run-ons:
"For As I have said before, there is no fire!" — This is a sentence fragment with awkward capitalization. Revise to something like: "As noted earlier, the absence of fire eliminates this risk entirely."

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Sharpen your thesis to clearly state *why* the microwave qualifies as revolutionary (not just *that* it is).
Reorganize paragraph 3 into two focused paragraphs (time-saving; safety).
Verify and refine your scientific explanation.
Proofread carefully for spelling and subject-verb agreement—reading your essay aloud can help catch these.

You have a genuinely interesting argument here. With more structural discipline and careful proofreading, this essay could be very effective. I'd be happy to look at a revised draft.
