Citing a specific historical example (eg, FDR's fireside chats, the 1938 "War of the Worlds" broadcast panic, radio's role in WWII propaganda)
Comparing radio's speed/reach to newspapers with a concrete example, rather than asserting it
Using your grandmother's example more analytically rather than just descriptively

Language and Mechanics

Since English is your second language, here are patterns to watch for:

Apostrophes: "don' t," "it' s" (should be *don't*, *it's* or *its*—note "it's" = "it is," while "its" is possessive: "the machine gun...except for those that it's killed" should be *it's killed* is correct here, but check other instances)
Capitalization: "Marshall Law" should be *martial law*
Spelling: "forcast" → *forecast*; "se�ora" appears to be an encoding error for *señora*—make sure special characters transfer correctly in your final draft
Subject-verb agreement: "the machine gun has not changed all that many lives" is fine, but check constructions like "I don't think any people... can even comprehend now our lives would be different" — this sentence is unclear; you likely mean *how* our lives would be different, and the verb tense needs adjustment (perhaps: "I don't think anyone in my generation can even imagine how different our lives would be without it")

I'd recommend reading your essay aloud, which often helps catch these issues, or asking a writing center tutor to review a revised draft with you.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Clarify your thesis—decide if this is about radio/power or radio/daily life, and commit to it throughout.
Develop your most interesting idea (passive vs. active citizenship) more fully.
Add concrete historical or factual evidence to support your generalizations.
Proofread carefully for apostrophes, capitalization, and spelling.

You have real insight and a natural, engaging voice—the "se​ñora" story, in particular, has great narrative potential. With sharper focus and revision, this essay could be quite strong.

Feedback on Your Essay

Overall Impression

You have a promising personal topic here — the microwave as an example of technology's impact on daily life — and your writing voice is warm and engaging. However, as an argumentative essay, the piece currently reads more like a personal narrative or reflection. To strengthen it, you need a clearer argumentative structure: a defined claim, organized supporting evidence, and a conclusion that returns to and reinforces that claim.

Thesis and Argument

Your opening sentence promises a broad discussion of 20th-century scientific advances and their impact on "people's lives," but the essay only discusses one appliance — the microwave. This creates a mismatch between your introduction's scope and your essay's actual focus. Consider one of two revisions:

Narrow the thesis to specifically claim something about microwaves (eg, "Although some resist household technology like the microwave, it has become an indispensable tool for modern families balancing work and home life").
Broaden the essay to discuss multiple technological advances, using the microwave as one example among several.

Right now, the essay presents *two* perspectives (pro-microwave and anti-microwave) but never clearly states which position you are arguing for, or how you reconcile them. The final line — "it has been a wonderful appliance for all of us" — suggests you favor the microwave, but this should be established as your thesis early on, not revealed only at the end.

Structure and Paragraphing

Many of your paragraphs are only one sentence long (eg, "In today's society..." "In my family, my parents work..."). While short paragraphs can be used for emphasis, overusing them makes the essay feel fragmented rather than logically developed. Try combining related ideas into fuller paragraphs:

Paragraph 1 (Introduction): State your thesis clearly.
Paragraph 2: Explain the general context (busy families, dual-income households) and how microwaves help.
Paragraph 3: Give your personal example in more depth.
Paragraph 4: Introduce the counterargument (your great-uncle's view) and explain it fully.
Paragraph 5: Respond to that counterargument — why do you still think microwaves are valuable, even though you understand his perspective?
Paragraph 6 (Conclusion): Tie the anecdote back to your thesis and broader point about technology.

Developing the Counterargument

Your great-uncle's story is the most interesting part of your essay, but you don't fully develop it into an argumentative move. Right now, you introduce his view, then simply report that he gave you the microwave — but you don't explain *why* his objection matters or *how* you would respond to someone who shares his opinion. Ask yourself: Is convenience worth losing tradition? Does using a microwave *prevent* people from appreciating food, or can the two coexist? Adding 2–3 sentences of analysis here would transform the anecdote from a story into an argument.

Evidence

Currently, all your evidence is personal/anecdotal. This is acceptable for illustrating a point, but a stronger academic essay would benefit from at least one additional type of support — a statistic about dual-income households, a brief reference to convenience-food culture, or even a rhetorical acknowledgment of your great-uncle's generation's habits in more general terms. This would show that your argument applies beyond just your own family.

Language and Mechanics

A few specific issues to correct:

Apostrophes: "peoples lives" → "people's lives"; "they' re done" → "they're done" (watch spacing).
Spelling: "recieved" → "received."
Redundant phrasing: "The point is, is that a microwave isn't right for everyone" — remove the repeated "is."
Verb form: "We each take turns cook" → "We each take turns cooking."
Capitalization: "Great Uncle and Aunt" should be lowercase ("great-uncle and great-aunt") unless used as direct address.
Informal transitions: Phrases like "In fact I have a little story to tell about that very thing" and "The long and short of the story is" are conversational. Consider more formal academic transitions, such as "This tension is illustrated by an experience in my own family" or "This example demonstrates that..."

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Clarify your thesis and make sure the whole essay supports it.
Combine short paragraphs into fuller, well-developed ones.
Expand your analysis of the counterargument — don't just narrate it, respond to it.
Proofread carefully for apostrophes, spelling, and redundant phrasing.
Consider adding one non-personal piece of evidence to broaden your support.

You have good instincts for storytelling — now channel that into a tightly argued essay with a clear, consistent claim. I look forward to seeing your revision!

Feedback on "The Impact of ATM Machines"

Overall Impression

Your essay addresses an interesting and relatable topic, and your enthusiasm for the subject comes through clearly. You have a functional structure (introduction, body, conclusion) and you attempt to acknowledge a counterargument, which is a sophisticated move for argumentative writing. However, the essay would benefit from a clearer thesis, deeper development of ideas, more logical organization, and attention to grammar. Below are specific suggestions organized by area.

Thesis and Argument

Your opening sentence identifies the topic (ATMs) but does not clearly state *why* they matter or what your essay will argue. A strong thesis should preview your main points. For example: "ATMs have transformed daily life by offering convenience, saving time, and increasing personal safety, benefits that outweigh concerns about job loss in the banking sector." This kind of thesis gives your reader a roadmap and makes your argument more persuasive.

Currently, your claims (convenience, time-saving, safety) are scattered across paragraphs without being tied together. Consider deciding on 2-3 main points early and structuring each paragraph around one point.

Organization

Your essay currently mixes ideas across paragraphs rather than dedicating each paragraph to a single, well-developed point. For instance:

Paragraph 1 mentions emergencies, time-saving, flexible hours, AND the counterargument about job loss—that's four ideas in one paragraph.
Paragraph 2 shifts to global availability and personal anecdotes about safety.

Suggestion: Reorganize using one idea per paragraph:
Paragraph 1: Introduction + thesis
Paragraph 2: Convenience and time-saving (with the "working men and women" point)
Paragraph 3: Flexibility (24-hour access) and global availability
Paragraph 4: Safety benefits (avoiding large cash amounts)
Paragraph 5: Counterargument and rebuttal (job loss)
Paragraph 6: Conclusion

This restructuring will make your argument easier to follow and each paragraph more focused.

Development of Ideas

Many of your points are introduced but not fully developed. For example, you mention that ATMs "save time" but do not explain *how* — is it because there's no need to wait in line for a teller? Because you don't need to visit during limited business hours? Similarly, the counterargument about job loss is raised but immediately dismissed ("we must also focus on the positive aspects") without any reasoning. A strong rebuttal should acknowledge the concern seriously and then explain *why* the benefits outweigh it—for example, by noting that new jobs were created in ATM maintenance and technology sectors, or that bank employees shifted to more specialized roles.

Your personal anecdote (forgetting your wallet at the grocery store) is a nice touch—concrete examples make arguments more convincing. Consider adding 1-2 more specific examples or even statistics (eg, "In the U.S., there are over X ATMs...") to strengthen your case.

Language and Grammar

Since English is not your first language, here are some common issues to address:

Verb tense consistency: You shift between present and past tense. Since ATMs still exist and are relevant today, use the present tense consistently ("ATMs appear in all areas of the world," not mixing with "of the late 1900's").
Sentence fragments/run-ons: "I can not comprehend how difficult it would be without this convenience at access to money" — this sentence is unclear. Revise to something like: "I cannot imagine how difficult it would be to access money quickly in an emergency without this convenience."
Article usage: Chinese does not have articles (a/an/the), so this is often tricky for Chinese speakers. Check phrases like "An invention of the 20th century" (correct) versus "the assigned banking hours" (should this be "fixed banking hours" without "the"? Check context).
Word choice: "entices robbers" is slightly awkward; consider "attracts robbers" or "makes you a target for robbery."
The dash before your final sentence ("-- The development of computerized technology...") creates a disconnected, abrupt ending. Integrate this idea smoothly into your concluding paragraph rather than appending it after a dash.

Conclusion

Your conclusion currently restates your appreciation for ATMs but does not fully synthesize your argument or leave the reader with a broader insight. Consider ending with a sentence that connects back to your thesis and gestures toward future implications (eg, how this technology paved the way for further advances like mobile banking).

Summary of Priority Revisions

Write a clear thesis statement with 2–3 specific claims.
Reorganize paragraphs so each focuses on one idea.
Develop your counterargument more thoroughly with a real rebuttal.
Proofread for verb tense consistency and sentence clarity.
Smooth the transition into your conclusion (remove the dash).

With these revisions, your essay will have a stronger argumentative structure and clearer, more persuasive prose. Your ideas are good—the next step is developing and organizing them more effectively.

Feedback on "The Cellular Telephone" Essay

Thank you for sharing this essay on cellular telephones. You've chosen a topic that was genuinely exciting for people in your generation, and your personal anecdote about the phone call across three cities is a nice touch. However, the essay needs significant revision in terms of argument, organization, and structure before its ideas can come through clearly. Below are my main suggestions.

Clarify Your Thesis and Argument

Your opening paragraph makes a strong claim: cellular phones are "a complete luxury and not a necessity." This is a debatable, arguable thesis—good! However, the rest of the essay does not consistently support or develop this claim. Instead, the body paragraphs drift into a general list of "ways cell phones have changed life" (convenience, accidents, cost, technological wonder) without clearly tying each point back to the luxury-vs-necessity argument. Ask yourself: does the accident-rate paragraph support your thesis that phones are a luxury? Does the anecdote about your family call support it, or does it actually work against it by showing how valuable/necessary the technology can be? Right now the essay reads more like a descriptive report on cell phones than an argumentative essay. For your revision, go through each paragraph and ask, "How does this prove my thesis?" If a paragraph doesn't answer that question, either cut it, revise it, or adjust your thesis to match what you are actually arguing.

Improve Paragraph Organization (One Idea Per Paragraph)

Right now your second body paragraph tries to do two things at once: it discusses accident rates *and* the cost of cell phones. These are two separate ideas and deserve two separate paragraphs, each with its own topic sentence, explanation, and (ideally) evidence or example. Consider this structure:

Paragraph 1: Introduction + thesis
Paragraph 2: Convenience/availability (luxury argument)
Paragraph 3: Safety concerns (accidents)
Paragraph 4: Cost
Paragraph 5: Personal anecdote (used as *evidence*, not just an amazing story)
Paragraph 6: Conclusion that returns to your thesis

Strengthen Your Conclusion

Your final paragraph shifts to a new topic (robots) and does not return to your original argument about cell phones as a luxury. A strong conclusion should remind the reader of your thesis and briefly restate how your evidence supported it. You can still end with a forward-looking thought about future technology, but connect it back to your main argument first—for example, "As technology continues to advance at this rate, luxuries like the cell phone may one day become necessities."

Use Evidence More Purposefully

Your personal story about calling your father is vivid, but its purpose is unclear. Is it meant to show that cell phones are amazing (supporting the "luxury" idea—something impressive but unnecessary), or is it meant to show they're useful and important (working against your thesis)? Make your purpose explicit: add a sentence explaining *why* you're including this story and how it connects to your argument. Also, throughout the essay, try to add more concrete evidence—statistics on accident rates, actual costs, or research findings would make your argument far more persuasive than general statements like "they are more likely to get into an accident."

Watch Your Tone and Word Choice

Phrases like "to me, it is a bit ridiculous" and "It is truly a mark of great expansion in technology" are conversational and enthusiastic, which is fine for a personal narrative, but an argumentative essay usually calls for a more measured, analytical tone. Try replacing subjective exclamations with more objective, evidence-based statements. For instance, instead of "I find it truly amazing," you might write, "This kind of connectivity illustrates how far communication technology has advanced."

Grammar and Mechanics

A few specific issues to fix:
Ampersands (&): Avoid using "&" in formal writing; write out "and."
Comma splices/run-ons: Sentences like "People are able to use their car phones in case of an emergency; if they are stranded or broken down" need revision. Consider: "People can use their car phones in emergencies, such as when their car breaks down."
Apostrophes/possessives: "friends houses" should be "friends' houses"; "it' s" should be "it's."
Capitalization and punctuation with dashes: "from Madrid - On his 'party line'" needs a clearer connector; consider rewriting as a full sentence: "I called my father at his office in Madrid. Using his 'party line,' he connected..."
Contradictory sentence: "Although most people who can afford to buy a cellular phone, can afford to pay the bills!" is unclear—do you mean this ironically or literally? Revise for clarity and remove the exclamation point for a more formal tone.

Summary

Your essay has an interesting, arguable thesis and an engaging personal example, but it needs tighter organization, clearer connections between evidence and argument, a conclusion that returns to your main point, and careful proofreading for punctuation and sentence boundaries. I recommend outlining your paragraphs before rewriting, with each one clearly answering "How does this support my thesis?" I look forward to seeing your revised draft!

Feedback on "The Creation/Evolution Controversy in the Classroom"

Overall Impression

You have tackled an ambitious and genuinely controversial topic, and your essay shows real effort in gathering sources and constructing a multi-part argument. However, the essay currently reads more like a summary of one side's talking points than an *argumentative* essay that critically evaluates competing positions. Below are the main areas to address in revision.

Thesis and Argumentative Stance

Your thesis claims that the two-model approach is "strong" and makes the opposition look "increasingly weak." This is a strong claim, but the essay never actually demonstrates this weakness—it simply reports what two-model advocates say and then reports what they say in response to objections. To argue persuasively, you need to:

Explain *why* the evolutionists' objections fail (logically, evidentially, legally), not just state that advocates "respond" to them.
Acknowledge the strongest version of the opposing view before dismantling it. Right now the opposition's arguments are presented very briefly and vaguely (eg, "some people feel "), which makes your refutation seem to defeat a weak opponent rather than a strong one. A convincing argumentative essay engages with the *best* version of the counterargument.

Use of Evidence and Citation

There are many bracketed placeholders () throughout the draft. These must be filled in with actual quotations and, crucially, cited properly (author, source, page/paragraph). As it stands, a reader cannot verify claims like "Bliss concluded that..." or the "anonymous quote" you mention. Since this essay relies heavily on outside authority (Darwin, Bergman, Carleton, Bliss), precise citation is not optional—it is the backbone of your credibility.

Also be cautious with the Newton/Einstein analogy. It is vivid, but the comparison is scientifically imprecise: Einstein did not "test" Newton's laws by studying rival theories side-by-side in a classroom; his work built on and extended Newtonian mechanics under specific conditions (high velocities), it didn't "disprove" or replace them in ordinary contexts. Using this as a model for how creationism and evolution should be taught side-by-side is a stretch that a critical reader will notice. Consider either explaining the analogy more carefully or choosing an example that maps more directly onto your argument about competing *scientific* theories.

Balance and Academic Tone

An argumentative essay on a controversial topic should demonstrate that you have seriously grappled with the strongest opposing evidence—particularly the scientific consensus that evolution is supported by extensive empirical evidence while creationism is generally treated as a religious rather than scientific claim. Right now, this crucial distinction (science vs. religion, testable vs. untestable claims) is mentioned only briefly ("evolutionists...believe creationism is religion") and never engaged with seriously. Strengthening this section—even if you ultimately disagree with the evolutionists' position—will make your own argument more credible.

Watch your tone in places. The parenthetical "(surprise)" when discussing Mississippi law is sarcastic and undermines the academic register you should be maintaining. Similarly, phrases like "the infamous equation" are informal. Aim for a more measured, analytical voice throughout.

Paragraph Structure and Transitions

Several paragraphs introduce a claim, then immediately shift to a quotation without analysis. For example, the paragraph on "well-respected authorities" strings together three quotations with almost no discussion of *how* each supports your argument beyond stating that Darwin's quote is persuasive "because he is the founder of evolution." This is an appeal to authority, which needs to be explicitly justified or at least acknowledged as such—why should Darwin's personal view override the current scientific consensus that has developed since his time?

Language and Mechanics

"E=MC�" — the exponent symbol did not render; check formatting (should be E=mc²).
"fro ma two-model approach" → "from a two-model approach"
"cur rent" → "current" (spacing error)
"where as" → "whereas"
Watch pronoun consistency: "his/her," "she/he" should be used consistently, or consider using "students" (plural) throughout to avoid the issue.
Some sentences are overloaded with clauses (eg, the opening paragraph's long sentence about schools/education). Break these into shorter units for clarity.

Conclusion

Your conclusion largely restates the two-model advocates' desires rather than synthesizing your argument or addressing the deeper stakes (educational policy, legal precedent, scientific integrity). Consider ending with a clearer, more analytical statement about *why* your readers should accept the two-model approach as the best solution, incorporating a brief acknowledgment of what would need to be true for the opposing view to be correct, and why you believe it is not.

Priority Revisions

Fill in and properly cite all missing quotations.
Strengthen and fairly represent the evolutionist counterargument before rebutting it.
Revise the Newton/Einstein analogy for accuracy.
Remove sarcastic asides; maintain formal academic tone.
Proofread for typographical and formatting errors.

With these revisions, your essay will move from *reporting* an argument to *making* one—which is the heart of academic argumentative writing.

Feedback on "Mercy Killing" Essay

Thank you for tackling such a challenging and controversial topic. You clearly did research and engaged with multiple sources, but the essay needs significant revision in four main areas: thesis clarity, logical consistency, source integration, and language accuracy.

Thesis and Argumentative Stance

Your central problem is that your position is unclear and, at points, self-contradictory. In your introduction, you say individuals should have "the freedom that God granted us... with the gift of life itself"—implying support for a patient's right to choose. But later, in your discussion of God's gifts, you argue that "it is not society's choice to decide when he or she has had enough of life," which sounds like an argument *against* euthanasia as a personal right.

By the conclusion, you present a conditional position (euthanasia is acceptable if the illness is incurable and suffering is severe, but not if recovery is possible). This is actually a reasonable, nuanced thesis—but it appears only in your final paragraph, almost as an afterthought. Move this nuanced claim to your introduction and make it your guiding thesis. Then revise every subsequent paragraph so it supports or complicates this specific claim, rather than summarizing "what articles say" in a general way.

The Values vs. Consequences Framework

You introduce an interesting analytical distinction—that euthanasia debates are framed as issues of *values* rather than *consequences*—but you don't fully develop it. Ask yourself: Why does this framing matter for your argument? Is the media's tendency to frame euthanasia as a "values issue" (rather than debating real-world outcomes/policy consequences) something you think is a *problem*? If so, say so explicitly and explain why. Right now this observation feels like a summary of your sources rather than a claim you are using to build your own argument.

Missing Source Material and Citations

There are several places marked with  where a quotation or specific detail seems to be missing (eg, "Some say that in cases of euthanasia they " and "The person suffering should remember "). These gaps make it impossible for a reader to follow your argument, since entire clauses are missing. Before your next draft, go back to your sources and fill in every one of these gaps with the specific information or quotation you intended to use. Also, make sure every direct quotation (like the Siegler quote and the "gift of life" quote) has a proper in-text citation (author's name, page number) so readers can locate the source.

Paragraph Organization

Several paragraphs introduce multiple ideas without transitions or clear topic sentences. For example, the paragraph beginning "Many advocates feel that the person will die anyway" moves through four different arguments (right to die, prolonging death, right to happiness, being "useless to society") without explaining how they relate to one another or to your thesis. Consider using one argument per paragraph, with a clear topic sentence stating the argument, followed by explanation and your own evaluation of it.

The Kevorkian paragraph is your strongest, because it engages with a concrete case and specific reasoning. Use this as a model: give context, explain the specific ethical/legal question, quote a source, then explain what that quotation means and why it matters to your argument.

Language and Grammar

As a note for revision (not the main focus, but worth addressing):
"an complex issue" → "a complex issue"
"no one has the right to take someone else life" → "someone else's life"
"en a painful life" — this seems to be a typo; check what word you intended (perhaps "end"?)
"Norms are usually classified as a standard of conduct" → subject-verb agreement: "Norms are usually classified as *standards*"
"God allows many people to destroy animals and plants because they are not persons" — this sentence needs revision for clarity; as written, it suggests animals/plants lack personhood *because* God allows their destruction, reversing your intended logic.
Watch for comma splices and run-on sentences, especially in paragraph 2.

I recommend reading your essay aloud after revising—this will help you catch missing words and awkward phrasing.

Summary of Priority Revisions
State one clear, consistent thesis in your introduction.
Fill in all  gaps with actual source content and proper citations.
Reorganize paragraphs around one argument each, explicitly connecting each to your thesis.
Resolve the contradiction between "personal freedom" and "God's ownership of life."
Proofread for grammar, especially subject-verb agreement and missing words.

You have real analytical instincts here—especially your insight about "values vs. consequences" framing. With a clearer structure and a consistent argumentative voice, this essay could be quite strong. I'd be happy to discuss a revision plan in office hours.

Feedback Report: Capital Punishment Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a complex, controversial topic with a clear personal stance, and your writing demonstrates a solid command of academic vocabulary and complex sentence structures for a non-native English speaker. The essay is organized in a logical for-then-against pattern, and your central image—that capital punishment "lowers the value of human life" for both victim and society—gives the essay a strong unifying thread. However, there are several areas where revision would strengthen your argument and its academic credibility.

Thesis and Purpose Statement

There is a slight mismatch between your stated purpose and your actual argument. You say the essay will "analyze the conflicting arguments... and show how the value of human life is affected by these arguments," which sounds like a balanced, analytical goal. However, you also state directly that "the argument opposing the death penalty is both stronger and more sensible." This is fine—it's a clear, defensible thesis—but you should revise your purpose statement to match this persuasive intent. Right now, the introduction promises even-handed analysis while the body delivers a clear argument favoring one side. Align these explicitly.

Missing Evidence

The bracketed placeholders () throughout the essay are a serious problem—in its current form, none of your key quotations from Hoyler, Nygaard, Conway, or Reiman are actually included. Since your argument leans heavily on citing scholars to prove points ("Advocates of the death penalty... support this claim by stating, "), the essay currently has no real evidentiary support. Before further revision, insert these quotations and, more importantly, follow each one with your own analysis explaining *why* the quote supports your point. Quotations should never stand alone; they need interpretation.

Argumentation and Logic

A few claims are stated as objective fact without support, which weakens your credibility:
"It is a known fact that for most people, the biggest fear in life is the fear of death."
"For many violent criminals there simply is no fear."
"Society simply craves violence."

These are assertions, not facts. Either provide evidence (studies, statistics, expert testimony) or soften the language ("many argue that...", "it seems likely that..."). Academic writing needs to distinguish between what is proven and what is your interpretation.

Additionally, the deterrence section could be stronger. You claim deterrence "does not have much evidence to support it," but then don't actually cite any studies or statistics comparing crime rates in death-penalty vs. non-death-penalty states—this is exactly where hard data would make your case far more persuasive than assertion alone.

Tone Shift in the Conclusion

The final paragraph shifts noticeably in tone—becoming more emotional and rhetorical ("Why should we be concerned with the life of a violent criminal?") rather than analytical. Phrases like "society begins to live on spite" and "the effective destruction of civilization" are dramatic claims that go beyond what your evidence has established. A strong conclusion should synthesize your argument, not introduce new, sweeping claims. Consider revising this paragraph to summarize your reasoning (deterrence is unproven; retribution devalues life) and end with a more measured call to reconsider capital punishment, rather than apocalyptic language.

Fairness to the Opposing View

You do acknowledge the pro-death-penalty argument's emotional logic (families of victims, the human desire for justice), which is good practice in argumentative writing. However, your treatment of the "for" side feels thinner than the "against" side—partly because the deterrence argument is dismissed fairly quickly. To make your rebuttal more convincing, spend a bit more time explaining the opposing argument's strongest form before refuting it. This will make your eventual rebuttal feel more earned.

Style and Language Notes

Watch repetition of the phrase "value of human life"—while it's your central theme, try varying the phrasing (eg, "moral worth," "sanctity of life," "human dignity") to avoid monotony.
Some sentences could be more concise. For example: "If the death penalty does indeed cause potential criminals to fail to carry out their planned actions, then the idea of deterrence is certainly a major benefit" could be shortened to sharpen the point.
Check paragraph transitions—phrases like "A continuation of this idea" are functional but could be more specific about what idea is continuing.

Summary of Priority Revisions

Insert and analyze all missing quotations.
Align your introduction's stated purpose with your persuasive thesis.
Support factual claims with evidence, not assertion.
Revise the conclusion to maintain an analytical tone rather than shifting into emotional rhetoric.
Strengthen the "for" side before refuting it, to make your rebuttal more persuasive.

With these revisions, this essay has strong potential to be a compelling, well-supported argument.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay tackles a genuinely difficult and important topic, and I can see real intellectual engagement with the complexity of affirmative action as a policy issue. You've moved beyond a simple pro/con structure to actually analyze how each side's argument can be strengthened, which shows sophisticated thinking. Below are areas to focus on for revision.

Structure and Argument Development

Your introduction does helpful work by laying out a roadmap, but the roadmap doesn't quite match what follows. You promise to (1) define affirmative action, (2) show how proponents defend its legality, (3) use both sides to show affirmative action is necessary, and (4) discuss how proponents can improve their argument. This is a reasonable plan, but the essay would be stronger if you signaled more clearly to the reader when you're transitioning between these sections. Consider adding brief transitional sentences ("Having established the legal foundation, I now turn to...") to guide readers through your logic.

More importantly, your central claim is a bit unclear. Are you arguing that affirmative action is necessary as currently practiced, or that it's necessary but poorly defended? Your conclusion suggests the latter, but this nuance gets lost in the middle sections. Consider stating this thesis more explicitly and consistently—perhaps in your introduction—so readers know from the start that your essay is really about *improving the argument* for affirmative action, not just defending it as-is.

Use of Sources

This is the area needing the most attention. You reference several authors (Shaw, Gillepsie/Gillespie, Beer) and even quote Kennedy's executive order, but the placeholder brackets  appear throughout, suggesting quotations were not filled in for this draft. Before your next revision, make sure:

All quotations are properly inserted with correct citations (author, page number, publication).
You introduce each source before quoting (eg, "As legal scholar Bill Shaw argues in [title]...").
You explain *why* you're including each quote—how does it support your specific claim in that paragraph?
Spelling of author names is consistent (you write "Gillepsie" in one place—double-check this against your source).

Right now, the quotes function as evidence you clearly have, but readers cannot yet evaluate your use of sources.

Language and Clarity Issues

Since English is your second language, here are specific patterns to watch for:

Word choice/spelling: "tents" should be "tenets" (paragraph 2); "stope" should be "stop" (paragraph on opponents' demands); "morals" likely should be "morale" or "standards."
Verb agreement/tense: "Opponents state that it is up to blacks to raise their morals" – check subject-verb agreement throughout, especially in longer sentences.
Pronoun clarity: In "A proponent of affirmative action, however, reminds the white male," it's unclear who this proponent is—use the author's name instead of vague reference.
Repetition: Words like "they argue," "they say," "they feel" appear very frequently as sentence openers. Vary your sentence structures—try starting with the claim itself, or combining sentences with subordinating conjunctions ("while," "although," "because").
Awkward phrasing: "Now that we have established the legality of affirmative action, let us assess the necessity" is clear, but some transitions like "How should we do it then?" feel abrupt. Consider smoothing these with fuller sentences.

Tone and Fairness

You do good work representing both sides, but be careful with characterizations like "opponents make unfair generalizations." This is a strong claim—make sure you've demonstrated *why* it's unfair with specific reasoning, not just assertion. Academic argument is more persuasive when you show your reasoning rather than simply labeling the opposition's claims as unfair.

Your final section (suggestions for how proponents can improve their arguments) is the most original and interesting part of the essay. Consider expanding this section, as it's where your unique contribution lies.
The historical framing (comparing affirmative action's 30 years to slavery's 200+ years) in your conclusion is a strong rhetorical move—effective and memorable.
You handle a genuinely divisive topic with balance and intellectual honesty.

Priority for Revision

Fill in all missing quotations and properly cite/introduce sources.
Clarify your central thesis and make sure structure matches your introduction's roadmap.
Proofread carefully for spelling, verb agreement, and sentence-opener repetition.
Expand your strongest section (improving proponents' arguments) since this is your most original contribution.

Feedback on Your Essay: "Yoda... Yoga"

Thank you for sharing this essay on the New Age movement. You clearly did substantial research and have a genuine interest in the topic. Below is feedback organized by area, with suggestions for revision.

Argument and Objectivity

Your central claim—that the case *against* teaching New Age ideas is stronger than the case *for* it—needs to rest on analytical criteria, not personal belief. Right now, your strongest piece of evidence for the "against" side is that it relies on the Bible, which you say is "very real and true." This is a significant problem: in academic argument, you cannot use your own religious conviction as proof that one side's evidence is more credible. A reader who does not share your faith will see this as the essay's biggest weakness, since it makes the whole argument sound like it is built on the author's personal values rather than objective analysis. Consider instead evaluating *how* each side uses evidence (specificity, verifiability, source credibility) rather than *whose values* are correct.

Similarly, be careful with claims like "the New Age movement is becoming popular... due to extreme political correctness and fear of offending others." This is presented as fact but is really your own interpretation. Signal it as such ("I would argue that...") or support it with a source.

Logical Structure: "Value" vs. "Consequence"

You introduce an interesting framework—arguments from *value* versus arguments from *consequence*—but it is not clearly defined for the reader early on. Define these terms explicitly in your introduction so readers can follow how you're applying them throughout. Right now the connection between the framework and your examples (eg, the Andrew Weil/tobacco anecdote) is asserted rather than demonstrated. Walk the reader through *why* a claim about tobacco counts as a "consequence" argument and *why* it fails.

Use of Evidence

Several important quotations are missing—marked only by . These are central to your argument (the four themes defining "New Age," the Andrew Weil quote, the Moody magazine testimony), so their absence weakens your case considerably. Please insert the full quotations with proper citations (author, title, page/date) before submitting a final draft.

Your anecdote about running to ask your neighbors whether tobacco is really the most destructive drug is engaging, but it functions as your own opinion, not evidence. To make this a stronger academic move, cite a source (eg, CDC or NIH statistics) confirming that alcohol causes more harm than tobacco. This will transform an entertaining personal moment into a persuasive rebuttal.

Also, be cautious with generalizations like "none of the authors had any books or hard facts to support themselves." This is a strong claim—make sure you can support it by naming specific texts you reviewed and explaining what was missing from each, rather than stating it in general terms.

Tone and Voice

The playful opening ("Yoda, that little green guy...") is a creative hook, but the tone shifts dramatically into a more formal academic register later. Consider whether this level of informality suits an argumentative essay for an academic audience, or whether a more measured opening (perhaps referencing the common confusion between the words, without the joke) would better set up the analytical tone that follows.

Phrases like "I honestly ran out of my room" and "stupendous job" are vivid but overly casual for academic writing. Aim for consistency: engaging but measured language throughout.

Language and Mechanics

Since you are writing in a second language, here are some recurring issues to review:
Apostrophes for possession: "authors opinion" → "author's opinion"; "that families values" → "that family's values" (this occurs multiple times).
Comma splices/run-ons: eg, "Because of the values stated... will come to the conclusion..." — check subordination.
Word choice: "it's" vs. "its"; "the trueness of the Bible" → "the truth of the Bible" or "the Bible's veracity."
Sentence fragments: "One magazine against the teaching of new age beliefs stated that indeed it was hard to define, but found four main themes..." works, but double-check punctuation around lists (colon usage before the missing quote).
Redundant transitions: You often restate "the case against/for teaching New Age beliefs" verbatim many times per paragraph. Vary your phrasing to avoid repetition.

Conclusion

Your conclusion largely restates the introduction. Try instead to synthesize *why* this distinction matters—what are the implications for how New Age ideas should (or shouldn't) be taught in schools? This would give your essay a stronger sense of purpose beyond comparing source quality.

Summary of Priorities for Revision
Remove/reframe reliance on personal religious belief as "proof."
Insert all missing quotations and citations.
Clarify and consistently apply your value/consequence framework.
Support key generalizations with specific textual evidence.
Proofread for possessives, run-ons, and word choice.

You have a strong analytical instinct and a distinctive voice—now channel that voice into an argument that would convince a skeptical reader, not just a sympathetic one. I'd be happy to discuss any of these points further in office hours.

Feedback Report: Nuclear Power Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You've tackled a complex, controversial topic and organized your essay around a clear rhetorical distinction: opponents appeal to values/fears, while proponents use facts/figures. This is a sophisticated analytical framework, and your body paragraphs (especially on cost, safety, and waste) are detailed and well-researched. However, the essay has significant issues with grammar, factual accuracy, structural balance, and argumentative consistency that need attention before this could be considered strong academic writing.

Major Issues to Address

Factual Error (Critical)
Your opening sentence states a nuclear bomb has "the explosive power of three hundred tons of TNT." This is inaccurate—modern nuclear weapons range from kilotons to megatons (thousands to millions of tons of TNT equivalent). Since this is your hook, an error here undermines credibility immediately. Please verify all statistics throughout, including the "20 percent cheaper" and "twenty and one hundred lives saved" claims—cite your sources so readers can verify them.

Grammar and Word Choice
There are several recurring errors:
"do to" should be "due to"
"analyzation" is not standard English; use "analysis"
"favors" should be "favor" ("strong in both favor" or better: "on both sides")
"meggawatts" → "megawatts"
"took" → "taken" ("precaution taken")
"then" vs. "than" — you consistently confuse these (eg, "more cost-efficient then," "cheaper then coal")
"act" should be "counteract" (one word)

I recommend using grammar-checking software (Grammarly) and reading your essay aloud to catch these patterns, since they repeat throughout.

Structural Inconsistency
You promise in your introduction to analyze both the opponents' and proponents' arguments, and you do explain the opponents' four points in paragraph 2. However, after that, the essay becomes almost entirely about the *proponents'* arguments, with opponents only reappearing briefly to be refuted. This creates an imbalanced essay that reads more as *pro-nuclear advocacy* than *rhetorical analysis of both sides*. 

Suggestion: Either (a) rebalance the essay to give equal, detailed treatment to both sides' rhetorical strategies, or (b) revise your thesis to reflect that this is primarily an analysis of the proponents' persuasive techniques, using the opponents mainly as a contrast point.

Blurring Analysis with Advocacy
Your conclusion ("nuclear power is much better for the environment... nuclear power will truly be an asset to our revolutionary country") reveals that you are not just *analyzing* the rhetoric—you're *endorsing* the proponents' position. This is a common issue in rhetorical analysis essays: it's easy to slip from "here's how proponents argue" into "and they're right." 

Ask yourself: Is this essay meant to analyze *how* each side argues (rhetorical strategy) or *which side is correct* (policy argument)? Your introduction promises the former, but your conclusion delivers the latter. Choose one focus and align the whole essay with it.

Unsupported Generalizations
Phrases like "one sided way Americans have been brought up" and characterizing all opponents as relying only on "fear" and being "closed minded" are overgeneralized and somewhat dismissive. Rhetorical analysis works best when you represent both sides fairly, even if you ultimately favor one. Consider acknowledging that some opponents' concerns (eg, long-term waste storage, accident history at Chernobyl/Fukushima) are evidence-based, not merely emotional.

Incomplete Sentence
In your waste paragraph: "Though simple, easy-to-understand facts such as:  the average American can easily relate to..." This sentence is broken—it appears text is missing. Please revise for completeness.

Strengths Worth Preserving
Your topic sentences are clear and guide the reader well (eg, "Another big factor is the safety measures...")
The specific examples (aspirin-sized waste comparisons, railroad cars of ash) are vivid and effective rhetorical illustrations
You demonstrate genuine engagement with the subject matter and have clearly done research

Revision Priorities

Fact-check all statistics and correct the TNT error
Rebalance the analysis to give opponents' arguments equal depth
Clarify your purpose: rhetorical analysis vs. persuasive advocacy—pick one
Proofread systematically for than/then, do/due, and similar recurring errors
Fix the incomplete sentence in the waste section
Add citations for all statistics to strengthen credibility

This essay shows real potential—your analytical framework is smart, and your research is substantive. With revision focused on balance, accuracy, and grammatical polish, this could become a much stronger piece of academic writing.

Feedback on "School Integration" Essay

Overall Impression

You've tackled a complex, historically significant topic with genuine engagement, and your personal narrative about the Chapter 220 program is a real strength—it grounds the abstract debate in lived experience. However, the essay needs revision in several areas: citation completeness, argumentative focus, and structural coherence.

Citations and Evidence (Most Urgent)

Several places in your essay show  where a quotation or citation should be. This is a serious problem in academic writing—readers cannot evaluate your evidence without seeing it, and unattributed claims (like "According to a thesis written by Howard Fuller...") followed by a gap undermine your credibility. Before anything else, fill in these citations properly, with author, source, and page number if using a specific style (MLA/APA/Chicago).

Additionally, when you introduce a source like *Desegregation and Beyond*, give the author's full name and a proper in-text citation format. Right now, sources are named informally ("a thesis written by," "a study done by two researchers from New York") without enough specificity for a reader to trace or verify them.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your introduction promises an essay organized around "value and consequence," but this framework disappears after the first paragraph. Consider making your thesis more explicit: What is *your* position? You gesture toward one in the conclusion ("I feel that there are both values and consequences...and if the program is going to be successful, it needs support from venues other than the school systems themselves"), but this should be stated clearly in your introduction, not just discovered at the end.

Right now the essay reads as a survey of the debate (academic benefits vs. harms, then racial relations benefits vs. harms) rather than an argument. Ask yourself: what do you want the reader to conclude? Revise your thesis to state this directly, then use each paragraph to build toward it.

Structure and Transitions

The essay's macro-structure is logical: academic effects → then racial relations effects → then community effects. However, transitions between paragraphs are weak. For example, the jump from discussing self-esteem/self-identity research directly into your personal narrative about Chapter 220 is abrupt. Add a transitional sentence explaining *why* you're moving from general research to a personal case study (eg, "The following account from my own schooling illustrates how these dynamics played out in practice").

Within paragraphs, some ideas are listed rather than connected. For instance, in paragraph 2, you present several claims (crime/drugs environment, timing of transfer, exposure to different learning styles) without clearly linking them causally. Consider using signal phrases like "This is significant because..." or "As a result..." to show logical connections.

Integrating Personal Narrative with Academic Argument

Your personal experience is compelling and adds authenticity, but it currently reads as a separate narrative rather than *evidence* for an argument. After each observation (eg, "220 kids were timid," "black students disassociated and formed groups"), add a sentence that ties the observation back to the broader debate. For example: "This pattern supports researchers' claims that late integration correlates with poorer social adjustment" (if that connects to research you cited earlier).

Language and Style Issues

A few recurring issues to address in revision:

Word choice: "productive to learning" should likely be "conducive to learning." "Hinderance" should be "hindrance."
Apostrophes/possessives: "the white communities reluctance" should be "community's reluctance."
Run-on sentences: Several sentences chain multiple clauses with "and" or commas where periods or semicolons would improve clarity (eg, the long sentence beginning "We did not experience many problems...").
Repetition: Phrases like "I feel" appear multiple times in the conclusion; vary your language to sound more analytically confident (eg, "This suggests..." or "The evidence indicates...").
Verb tense consistency: You shift between past tense (historical facts, personal narrative) and present tense (general claims). This is often necessary, but double-check each shift is intentional and clear.

Conclusion

Your conclusion introduces a new and interesting idea—that success requires "support from venues other than the school systems themselves"—but doesn't develop it. This could actually be a stronger, more original thesis than a simple "there are pros and cons" statement. Consider restructuring the essay to build toward this argument about community/family responsibility, which your Chapter 220 narrative and the New York Times Magazine citation both support nicely.

Priority for Revision

Fill in all missing citations.
Sharpen your thesis to state a clear position, not just "there are two sides."
Strengthen transitions between research-based claims and personal narrative.
Proofread for the specific language issues noted above.

You have strong raw material here—especially your personal experience—so the next draft should focus on argumentative clarity and integration.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Thank you for sharing this draft. You are tackling a genuinely important and complex issue—racial dynamics on predominantly white campuses—and your passion for the topic comes through clearly. Below are observations organized by area, along with suggestions for revision.

Missing Evidence (Most Urgent Issue)

The placeholders () throughout your essay mark spots where quotations, statistics, or study results should appear—for example, the opening anecdote about Alberta, the Penn State study, and the Steele quotation. Right now, your argument rests on claims like "evidence shows" or "it is proven" without the evidence itself being present. Before revising anything else, fill these in with properly cited sources. An argumentative essay on a topic this sensitive needs strong, specific, and attributed evidence—not just assertions that evidence exists.

Thesis and Structure

Your thesis—that integration, not separate organizations, is the solution to campus racism—is a defensible and interesting claim. However, it doesn't appear clearly until fairly late (end of paragraph one is close, but it gets buried in other claims). Consider stating it in a single, direct sentence early in the introduction, and then use each paragraph's topic sentence to signal how it supports that thesis. Currently, some paragraphs (eg, paragraph 2 on inferiority and equality) drift into related but tangential territory before returning to the main argument.

Logical Consistency

There are a few places where your argument works against itself:

You argue that black students should not separate because doing so lets white students "ignore the problem." But you also argue that "the white population is not going to be the one to step forward" and that black students "must be the ones to start the change." These two claims are in tension—if change depends on black students acting, but any form of black-led organizing is labeled "segregation," it's unclear what action you believe black students *should* take. Clarify this by explaining what integration would concretely look like, and what black students should do instead of forming their own organizations.
The claim about crime rates and poverty ("unaffected by skin color") is a strong empirical claim that needs a citation; without one, it risks feeling like an assumption rather than an argued point.
The phrase "race war" is a strong, loaded term that isn't really supported by the rest of your measured argument. Consider a more precise term ("racial tension" or "conflict") unless you intend to justify that stronger language.

Language and Style

Since English is your second language, here are patterns to watch for:

Hedging phrases: "It is true that," "It is obvious that," "It is proven that" appear frequently and weaken your authority. Academic writing is often more persuasive when you state the claim directly and let the evidence do the work (eg, "Poverty, not race, correlates with higher crime rates (Source, Year)").
Comma splices and run-ons: eg, "Not only are we thrown into an unfamiliar scene, with freedoms abound, we arrive there by ourselves..." Break these into shorter, clearer sentences.
Article usage: Watch for missing or misplaced articles (eg, "the problems can the problems begin to be solved" in the conclusion—this also looks like a leftover editing error).
Pronoun/reference errors: "As Steele mentions is her essay" — check whether Steele is "her" or "his," and proofread for typos like this and "e problem" (should be "the problem").
Word choice: "felling" should be "feeling." Small typos like this are easy to miss but add up; a careful proofreading pass will help.
Terminology consistency: You alternate between "black," "African American," and "person of color." Choose consistent terminology (checking current style conventions, including capitalization of "Black" when referring to racial identity) and use it throughout.

Paragraph Development

Several paragraphs end with strong, punchy one-sentence conclusions ("It is not dealing with the problem, but ignoring it and in some ways igniting it"), which is a good rhetorical technique. However, make sure each paragraph also *begins* with a topic sentence that tells the reader what point is coming, rather than starting with background narration. This will make your structure easier to follow.

Suggested Next Steps

Insert and properly cite all missing evidence.
Write a one-sentence thesis and place it clearly at the end of your introduction.
Resolve the tension between "black students must lead change" and "separate organizations are always segregation."
Do a full proofreading pass for typos, comma splices, and pronoun agreement.
Replace hedging phrases with direct, evidence-backed claims.

You have a clear point of view and real conviction—strengthening the evidence and tightening the logic will make this a much more persuasive essay. I'd be happy to discuss any of these points further in office hours.

Overall Impression

This is the most urgent issue. Several sentences are grammatically finished but conceptually empty:

"Surrogate Motherhood is when a ." — You need an actual definition here.
"Health services, however, argues that ." — What does this source actually claim?
"Opposing this view Annas argues ." — This is a direct citation gap; readers cannot evaluate your rebuttal without knowing what Annas said.
The conclusion also has unfinished claims ("helps the infertile woman to " and "Surrogacy has been looked upon as ").

Without this content, it's impossible to judge whether your evidence supports your claims. Complete these first, then revise for logic and flow.

Clarify Key Terms

You use "surrogate mother," "biological mother," and "adoptive mother" somewhat interchangeably, which confuses the reader. For example: "Surrogacy is unethical because biologically there are only two parents, excluding the adoptive mother." Do you mean the *intended* mother (the woman who will raise the child but has no biological link)? Define your terms early—surrogate, intended/adoptive parents, biological father—and use them consistently throughout.

Strengthen Your Reasoning

Some claims need more logical support:

"There are many people in the world who's lives have not been altered because they are infertile." This sentence is unclear—are you saying infertility doesn't have to be devastating? This needs explanation and perhaps evidence, because as written it seems to dismiss the emotional pain of infertility without justification, which weakens your credibility with a neutral reader.
"Just by creating safety standards and regulations for surrogate motherhood, it is not possible to lessen the emotional turmoil." This is asserted, not argued. Why can't regulation reduce emotional harm? A stronger essay would engage with this counterargument more seriously rather than dismissing it in one sentence.

Avoid Repetitive Phrasing

The phrase "Many people feel that..." appears at least five times, often introducing both sides of the debate without clear signaling of whose view is being presented. Vary your language (eg, "Proponents argue...," "Critics counter...," "According to [Author]...") and make sure each instance is followed by a properly cited source when you're referring to a specific position.

Improve Paragraph Unity

Each paragraph should develop one clear idea. Currently, some paragraphs shift between topics without transitions—for instance, the third paragraph moves from "the infertile couple's needs" to Annas's argument to biological need to the father's genetic inheritance to the mother's feelings, all in a short space. Consider breaking this into two paragraphs, each with a clear topic sentence and a logical progression of support.

Grammar and Mechanics

A few recurring issues to correct:

"who's" should be "whose" ("people whose lives...").
"mother hood" should be one word: "motherhood."
"substancial" → "substantial."
Comma splices: "By saying that surrogate motherhood fulfills a human longing... the author is claiming there is a void" — check punctuation carefully throughout for run-on sentences.
Watch subject-verb agreement: "Others feels that" should be "Others feel that."

Citation Format

You mention "Annas" and "Health services" as sources, but there's no indication of how you're citing them (in-text citations, a works cited page, etc). Confirm what citation style your course requires (MLA is common for this kind of essay) and apply it consistently.

Suggested Next Steps

Complete every  placeholder with real content.
Create a one-sentence definition of surrogacy and a clear list of the three parties involved (surrogate, intended parents, biological father) to use consistently.
Revise topic sentences so each paragraph argues one clear point.
Proofread for the grammar issues noted above.
Add a properly formatted Works Cited/References page.

You have a solid ethical stance and some strong intuitions (especially about the emotional cost to the surrogate mother), but the essay needs completion and tightening before its argument can be fully evaluated. I'd be happy to look at a revised draft once the missing sections are filled in.

Without this evidence, your essay currently reads as a summary of what the evidence *would* say, rather than an argument built on actual evidence. This is likely a formatting error from drafting, but it fundamentally weakens your paper's persuasive power. Please ensure every placeholder is filled with the specific data or quotation before submission, and double-check that each source is properly cited (author, publication, page/paragraph number) according to your assigned citation style (MLA/APA).

Strengths

Logical paragraph structure: Each body paragraph opens with a claim, offers evidence, and explains significance.
Concession and rebuttal: You appropriately acknowledge counterarguments (eg, the "fair is fair" argument, the constitutional legality argument) before refuting them. This shows critical engagement with the opposing side.
Varied evidence types: You draw on statistical studies, case examples, and philosophical/ethical reasoning—this range strengthens your argument.

Areas for Improvement

Source Integration and Attribution
Phrases like "Time magazine reported" or "Social Theory and Practice wrote" personify publications rather than authors. Revise to attribute claims to *authors* or *studies*, with the publication as the venue: "In a study published in *Social Theory and Practice*, [Author] argues that..." This is more academically precise and avoids the impression that a magazine—not a person—is making a claim.

Logical Gaps Requiring Explanation
Several claims need more explicit reasoning:
The "brutalization effect" section states criminals "model their behavior after the state," but you should explain *why* this happens (eg, what psychological or social mechanism is at work) rather than simply asserting it.
In the Dann study, you note "4.4 more homicides" occurred after executions but should clarify whether this correlation was statistically significant or merely suggestive, and address potential confounding variables (this will make your argument more credible to a skeptical reader).

Correlation vs. Causation
This is your most important analytical weakness. Several of your key statistics show *correlation* (eg, states with the death penalty have higher crime rates) but you present them as evidence of *causation* (the death penalty causes higher crime). A careful reader will notice this gap immediately. Consider adding a sentence addressing this directly: are there other factors that might explain both patterns? If your sources address this, include their reasoning; if not, acknowledge the limitation explicitly. This will strengthen, not weaken, your credibility.

Repetitive Sentence Openings
You frequently begin sentences with "This," "It is," or "Many people" — vary your syntax for better flow. For example, instead of "It is also stated that the state has, ," try integrating the quotation more smoothly into your own sentence.

Minor Grammar and Mechanics
"both sides cite" (paragraph 1) — capitalize "Both."
"put a stop to it all together" — should be "altogether" (one word).
"he examined states...This study also showed" — check for comma splices and ensure each sentence has a clear subject-verb relationship.
Proofread for consistent verb tense, especially when discussing historical studies (past tense) versus ongoing debates (present tense).

Strengthen the Ethical Section
This section relies more on assertion ("this idea is based on...") than the statistical sections do. Since you argue this is the "most well-known dispute," consider adding a named philosopher or ethicist (eg, utilitarian vs. retributive justice theorists) to give this section the same evidentiary weight as your deterrence argument.

Suggested Revision Priorities
Fill in all missing citations/data — this is essential.
Add explicit discussion of correlation vs. causation in your statistical evidence.
Revise source attribution to name authors, not just publications.
Proofread for capitalization, word choice, and sentence-opening variety.

Your argument is well-organized and your reasoning is generally sound—once the evidence gaps are filled and the causation issue is addressed, this will be a much stronger essay.

Feedback on "Prozac" Argumentative Essay

Your essay tackles a genuinely controversial topic and follows a clear structure: introduction with thesis, supporting evidence, acknowledgment of counterarguments, rebuttal, and conclusion. This is a strong foundation. However, there are several issues—some critical, some stylistic—that need attention before this essay is ready for submission.

Critical Issue: Missing Quotations

The most urgent problem is that every direct quotation is missing—you have left placeholder marks () throughout the essay where quoted material should appear. This happens at least seven times, including quotes attributed to Dr. Kramer, Dr. Auerbach, and Dr. Cass. Without these quotes, your evidence is essentially invisible to the reader. Since your argument relies heavily on expert testimony to establish credibility, this omission seriously undermines your paper's persuasiveness. Please locate your source material and insert the actual quotations, with proper in-text citations (eg, "Kramer states, '...' (45)"), before doing anything else.

Thesis and Argument Structure

Your thesis—"Prozac should continue to be prescribed... because of the positive effect it already has made in medicine and society"—is clear and appropriately debatable. The two-pronged structure (medical effectiveness, then social benefit) works well and is signaled early, which helps readers follow your argument.

However, consider narrowing your claim slightly. Right now you argue Prozac is safe, effective, socially beneficial, *and* that suicide risks are overstated *and* that side effects are tolerable. This is a lot of ground to cover in a short essay. You might strengthen the essay by choosing your two or three strongest points and developing them more fully, rather than touching on five or six points briefly.

Use of Evidence

Your case studies (Sam, Tess, the rabbi) are effective because they humanize the issue. However, you rely on these single examples to represent "many people," which is a common overgeneralization. Consider adding a phrase acknowledging that these are individual cases, eg, "While one case cannot represent all patients, Sam's experience illustrates a pattern reported by many users." This shows critical awareness without weakening your argument.

Your statistical reasoning about suicide rates also needs revision. Comparing "900 to 950 thousand prescriptions" to "a few confirmed deaths" is a reasonable move, but you should cite the actual numbers and source, and briefly explain why this comparison is meaningful (ie, that correlation is not causation, and that depressed patients—medicated or not—have elevated suicide risk to begin with). This will make your rebuttal more rigorous.

Counterargument Section

I appreciate that you devote a full paragraph to counterarguments (numbness, loss of "quirkiness," suicide concerns) rather than ignoring them—this is good academic practice. However, your response sometimes seems to dismiss these concerns too quickly by calling them "slight obstacles" without fully engaging with why patients like Sam experienced them as *losses*, not just side effects. A stronger paragraph would acknowledge the genuine trade-off (some emotional "flattening" in exchange for relief from depression) and then explain why, on balance, this trade-off is worthwhile for most patients.

Conclusion

Your conclusion currently ends with a rhetorical question ("what is wrong with making yourself better?"). This is common in Chinese rhetorical traditions, where a strong question can effectively close an argument, but in English academic writing, conclusions typically restate the thesis with new emphasis and end with a firm statement rather than a question. Consider revising the ending to something more declarative, such as: "Given its proven safety and demonstrated benefits, Prozac deserves continued use as a legitimate treatment for depression." This will leave readers with a more confident, authoritative final impression.

Language and Style Notes

A few smaller items to polish:
"banishment" is spelled "banish ment" (remove the space).
"his pas social lives" should likely be "his past social life."
"name Sam" should be "named Sam."
Watch article usage ("a," "the")—for example, "It treats depression by affecting one system of the brain, where a chemical called serotonin is" could be more naturally phrased as "one system of the brain, *in which* a chemical called serotonin is *located*."
Avoid starting too many sentences with "It" as a vague pronoun (eg, "It also has worked to stop bulimia..."). Specify "Prozac" for clarity.

Summary of Priorities

Insert all missing quotations immediately—this is non-negotiable for a passable draft.
Narrow and deepen your argument rather than covering many points superficially.
Strengthen your engagement with counterarguments.
Revise the conclusion to end with a declarative statement, not a question.
Proofread for minor grammar and word-choice errors.

You have a solid argumentative skeleton here—now it needs fuller evidence and sharper critical engagement to reach its full potential.

Feedback on Draft Essay: Homosexuals in the Military

Overall Impression

You have chosen a genuinely difficult and historically significant topic, and your essay shows real effort to engage with two sources in a comparative way. However, the essay currently reads more as an advocacy piece than a balanced academic argument, and there are several structural and grammatical issues that weaken your credibility. Below are specific areas to revise.

Thesis and Logical Consistency

Your thesis statement is confusing and even self-contradictory. You write "there should be a ban gays serving in the military" (grammatically incomplete—did you mean "a ban on gays serving"?), but your concluding paragraph states the opposite: "it was decided... that there was not reason why homosexuals should not be prohibited from serving" — this double negative is very hard to parse and may actually mean the opposite of what you intend. Before revising anything else, decide clearly what your position is and state it in one unambiguous sentence. Then check that every paragraph supports that single claim.

The Opening Paragraph

The reference to Dr. King and the women's liberation movement is a common rhetorical move, but as written it implies these are parallel or equivalent struggles, which is a strong claim you never actually defend. If you want to use this historical framing, you need to briefly explain *why* the comparison is relevant (eg, "some see this as the next civil rights battle, while others disagree that it is comparable"). Otherwise, cut it and open directly with the debate itself.

Fairness to the Opposing View (Wall)

This is the most serious content issue. Your treatment of Wall is dismissive rather than analytical. Phrases like "attacks the issue... with a lighthearted attitude" and "does not see the point" characterize Wall's argument rather than engaging with its logic. A strong argumentative essay must represent the opposing side accurately and charitably before showing why it is less persuasive. Right now, Wall's positions are presented only long enough to be knocked down—this can come across as a straw man. Ask yourself: what is the *strongest* version of Wall's argument? Address that.

Use of Quotations

You have left placeholders () throughout instead of actual quotations. This is a significant gap—your argument's credibility depends on precise, well-chosen textual evidence. When you insert the real quotations:
Introduce each one with context (who says it, why it matters)
Follow it with your own analysis explaining *how* the quote supports your claim
Avoid quotes that are too long or that do the analytical work for you

Right now the essay's structure ("He states that, . It is very natural to...") suggests you may be under-analyzing the quotes even once they are added—you tend to assert your interpretation without close reading of the language itself.

Logical Reasoning

Several of your inferences move quickly and could be challenged:
The claim that mixed-orientation barracks are inherently comparable to mixed-sex barracks needs more support—these are not obviously equivalent situations, and a skeptical reader will ask why.
The idea that allowing homosexuals to serve means homosexuality will be "virtually accepted" and thus uncontrollable is a slippery-slope argument. If you rely on this reasoning, acknowledge the logical leap and defend it explicitly.
The final rhetorical question ("If there is no trust amongst our troops, then how can we, as civilians, trust in them?") assumes the conclusion you're trying to prove (that trust is impossible with gay soldiers) rather than demonstrating it.

Grammar and Sentence-Level Issues

Some recurring patterns to watch for:
Subject-verb agreement: "James M. Wall's articles does not give..." (article/articles inconsistency; verb should match subject)
Verb tense shifts: "will make on change their mind" — likely a typo, but also check tense consistency between past/present when discussing the articles
Article usage: "there should be a ban gays serving" — missing preposition/article ("a ban on gays' serving" or "a ban on gays serving")
Comma splices and run-ons: several sentences combine independent clauses without proper punctuation (eg, the long sentence introducing Wall's article)

I recommend reading your essay aloud, sentence by sentence, to catch these—many are the kind of error that becomes obvious when heard rather than seen.

Conclusion

Your conclusion introduces a new argument (trust between troops and civilians) rather than synthesizing what you've already argued. Revise it to briefly restate your thesis, summarize your strongest points, and end with why this matters beyond the immediate debate—without introducing an entirely new claim you haven't yet supported.

Next Steps
Clarify and fix your thesis sentence.
Insert and closely analyze real quotations.
Strengthen your representation of Wall's argument before refuting it.
Proofread for subject-verb agreement and article usage.
Rewrite the conclusion to synthesize rather than introduce new claims.

Feedback on Your Essay: Mandatory Drug Testing in Sports

Your essay tackles an important and timely topic, and you've clearly done substantial research, gathering multiple sources to support your position. Below is feedback organized by key areas for revision.

Strengths
