This gives your essay a logical structure to follow. You also use concrete examples (Ben Johnson, the Borlange meeting, The Athletic Congress program) that ground your argument in real-world evidence.

Major Areas for Revision

Missing Quotations
This is the most urgent issue. Throughout your essay, you use  as placeholders where quotations should appear (eg, "he stated, "). These need to be filled in with the actual quoted material from your sources before this essay can be evaluated. Without them, readers cannot assess whether your quotes actually support your claims, and the essay currently reads as incomplete.

Paragraph Organization
Your body paragraphs tend to blend multiple sub-topics together. For example, your second and third paragraphs both discuss ethics, but the discussion feels scattered across them rather than developed in one focused section. Consider organizing body paragraphs strictly around your three thesis points:
Paragraph(s) on fairness/ethics
Paragraph(s) on health and safety
Paragraph(s) on role models

Currently, the Borlange and Athletic Congress paragraphs feel like separate case studies rather than integrated support for your three main claims. Ask yourself: which of your three reasons does each paragraph support? Make that connection explicit.

Transitions Between Sources
You introduce many sources (Chaplin, Thompson, Wadler, the Time and Sports Illustrated articles), but the connections between them are often abrupt. Instead of "The article also states," try phrases that show *how* each new piece of evidence builds on the previous one, such as "This concern is echoed by Dr. Wadler, who further argues that…" This will help your evidence feel like a unified argument rather than a list of quotes.

Grammar and Word Choice
There are several recurring errors to address:
"lesson" should be "lessen" (paragraph on Ben Johnson)
"meathod" should be "method"
"strickter" should be "stricter"
Subject-verb agreement: "The pros and cons... are discussed" is correct, but check similar constructions throughout
Article usage: "the National Collegiate Association of Athletes" — please verify this organization's exact name (it may be the NCAA, National Collegiate Athletic Association)
"Lial Alsado" — please double-check this name for accuracy (I believe you may mean Lyle Alzado)

I recommend reading your essay aloud or using grammar-checking software to catch smaller errors like missing articles ("a" and "the") and verb tense shifts.

Conclusion
Your conclusion largely repeats your introduction's thesis statement almost verbatim. A strong conclusion should synthesize your argument and perhaps end with a forward-looking statement or call to action. Consider: What should readers *do* with this information? What's at stake if mandatory testing isn't implemented? Push your final paragraph beyond restatement into a more persuasive close.

Counterargument Development
You raise the privacy objection early on, which is excellent—strong argumentative essays acknowledge opposing views. However, your rebuttal ("The importance of drug testing outweighs the impairment of the right to privacy") is asserted rather than fully argued. Consider dedicating a few more sentences to explaining *why* fairness and safety outweigh privacy concerns, perhaps using a specific example or comparison.

Specific Suggestions

Combine paragraphs 2 and 3: Since both address ethics, merging them with clearer sub-points would strengthen coherence.
Add topic sentences: Several paragraphs (especially those introducing new articles) launch directly into summary. A clear topic sentence stating *why* this evidence matters to your argument would help readers follow your logic.
Vary sentence openings: Many paragraphs begin with "The article states" or similar constructions. Varying these will improve readability.

Next Steps

Insert your actual quotations where  appears
Verify all proper nouns and organizational names
Reorganize paragraphs around your three main claims
Strengthen the counterargument/rebuttal section
Proofread for the specific errors listed above
Revise your conclusion to move beyond repetition

This essay has a solid foundation with real research and a clear position. With attention to organization, transitions, and the missing quotations, it will become a much more persuasive and polished piece of writing. I'm happy to discuss any of these points further during office hours.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay: Prayer in Public Schools

Overall Impression
You've tackled a genuinely complex issue and organized your essay around a clear structure (Conservative Religious Group → Civil Liberties Group → proposed solution). This block organization works well for comparing two sides before offering a synthesis. However, the essay needs revision in four main areas: thesis clarity, depth of analysis, evidence integration, and language accuracy.

Thesis and Organization
Your thesis sentence at the end of paragraph one is extremely long and tries to do too much at once—summarizing both groups' positions, evaluating three articles, and stating your own solution, all in one sentence. Readers will lose track of your main claim. Consider breaking this into two or three sentences: (1) a brief statement of the conflict, (2) your evaluation of the two articles' relative strength, and (3) your own proposed solution. A clear, separated thesis will make your argument easier to follow and will let readers anticipate your essay's structure.

Depth of Analysis
This is the most important area for revision. Throughout the essay, you tend to summarize a quote and then simply assert "This is a good argument" or "This is good reasoning" without explaining *why*. For example, after discussing the freedom-of-speech argument, you correctly note that a 1969 Supreme Court ruling doesn't necessarily apply to church-state separation—but you don't develop this insight. Push further: *Why* is this a logical gap? What would the author need to show to make the connection valid? The same is true for your treatment of Shriver's argument about minorities—you gesture toward the idea of respect but don't fully explain the mechanism by which mandatory prayer might "trap" a religious minority. Strengthening these explanations will transform your essay from summary into genuine argumentative analysis.

Evidence Integration
I notice several placeholders () where quotations should appear. Before submitting, make sure every quotation is fully inserted, properly introduced, and cited according to your assigned format (author's name and page number, eg, Klinghoffer 2). Right now it's impossible to evaluate whether your quotes actually support your claims. Also, work on varying how you introduce quotations—phrases like "As Klinghoffer states" and "According to Shriver" appear repeatedly. Try alternatives such as "Klinghoffer contends that…" or "This point is reinforced when Shriver writes…" to create more sentence variety.

Repetitive Sentence Patterns
You rely heavily on the formula "This is a good/weak argument by the author because…" This becomes monotonous and also signals that your analysis hasn't moved beyond simple evaluation. Try restructuring some sentences to lead with your own claim, then support it with the source: for instance, "Klinghoffer's claim about religion's educational value is undercut by his failure to propose any concrete curricular changes."

Grammar and Word Choice
There are numerous errors that a careful proofreading pass would catch:
"arrise" → arise
"know as" → known as
"torwads/torward" → towards/toward (this appears many times—please do a global search)
"conatin" → contain
"reaons" → reasons
"mionorities" → minorities
"stengthen" → strengthen
"ow" → how
"sustain from" → refrain from (this is a meaning error, not just spelling)
"titles" → titled
Article title inconsistency: you write "Prayer in Public Schools, It's Nothing New to Many" in one place and "Prayer in Public Schools? It's Nothing New for Many" in another—check the exact title and use it consistently.

I'd strongly recommend reading your essay aloud, or using a spelling/grammar checker, before your next draft. These errors are minor individually but accumulate to distract the reader from your ideas.

Conclusion
Your final paragraphs restate the moment-of-silence solution three times with similar wording ("gives the best solution," "the best choice for everyone"). Consider consolidating this into one strong concluding paragraph that also acknowledges a potential counterargument to your own solution—for instance, would some Conservative Religious Group members find a silent moment insufficient? Addressing this briefly would make your conclusion more persuasive rather than simply restating your preference.

Summary of Priorities for Revision
Rewrite your thesis into 2–3 clear, separated sentences.
Insert all missing quotations with proper citations.
Deepen your analysis—explain *why* each argument succeeds or fails, not just *that* it does.
Vary your sentence structures, especially when introducing quotes and evaluating them.
Proofread carefully for spelling and word-choice errors, especially "torward/torwads."

You have a solid grasp of the sources and a clear personal position—now it's a matter of sharpening the analysis and polishing the language so your strong ideas come through clearly. I'd be happy to discuss any of these points further in office hours.

Feedback on "The Death Penalty Debate" Essay

Overall Impression
You have chosen a genuinely debatable topic and taken a clear, defensible position. Your three main reasons—ineffectiveness, immorality, and systemic flaws—are all legitimate angles on this issue. With some revision to argument structure, evidence integration, and sentence-level clarity, this essay could be quite strong.

Content and Argumentation

Thesis-body alignment: Your thesis promises three reasons: the death penalty (1) won't stop crime, (2) is immoral, and (3) "may hinder the development of our society." However, your third body paragraph is actually about *procedural flaws* (unequal legal representation, appeals backlogs, wrongful convictions)—not really about "hindering development." Revise your thesis so it accurately previews what the body paragraphs deliver, or reframe the third body paragraph to match the original thesis.

Factual accuracy: You state that "many politicians, especially Liberal Democrats, feel that the death penalty is an integral part of the future well-being of America." Historically and politically, Democrats have been more associated with *opposing* capital punishment, while Republicans have more often supported it. Double-check this claim before submitting, as a factual error here undermines your credibility on a topic that requires precision.

Counterargument treatment: You mention opposing views (eg, "an eye for an eye") but dismiss them quickly ("No, to me no murder is right"). A stronger essay would engage more seriously with the strongest form of the opposing argument before refuting it—this shows critical thinking rather than simple assertion.

Evidence and Citation

This is the area needing the most attention. You reference Forst, Solomon, and Jim Gittings, but the actual quotations are missing (marked with ). Before submission, you must:
Insert the actual quotations or paraphrases you intend to use.
Provide proper in-text citations (author, year) and a Works Cited/References list.
Make sure each piece of evidence is followed by *your own analysis* explaining how it supports your claim (you do this well with Forst—keep that pattern).

Also, be careful with unsupported generalizations, such as "it has been proven that many criminals have been able to successfully appeal the death sentence because of the expensive, good lawyer." Proven by whom? Cite a specific case or statistic.

Organization

Your five-paragraph structure (intro, three body paragraphs, conclusion) is sound and easy to follow. To improve flow:
Add clearer transition sentences between paragraphs (eg, linking "ineffectiveness" to "immorality").
Within paragraph 3, the discussion jumps between money/lawyers, backlog, and wrongful convictions without transitions signaling the shift.

Language and Sentence-Level Issues

You have a good vocabulary range, but several recurring issues affect clarity:

Fragments/comma splices from "Whereas": "Whereas many politicians... feel that the death penalty is an integral part..." This is a sentence fragment because "whereas" needs to be joined to an independent clause, not stand alone. Combine it with the previous sentence using a comma, or replace "Whereas" with "However," / "In contrast,".

Missing question marks: "If somebody makes a horrible mistake of murdering someone else, then is it moral for the government to turn right around and murder them." This is phrased as a question—add a question mark, or restructure as a statement ("...it is not moral for the government to murder them in return").

Word form errors:
   - "the increases crime" → "increased crime" or "increasing crime"
   - "full-proof" → "foolproof"
   - "it necessity" → "its necessity"
   - "so ciety" → "society" (typo)

Informal register: Starting sentences with "Well" ("Well it has been proven...", "Well let's be tough...") is conversational and should be replaced with more formal transitions like "However," "In fact," or "Research suggests that..."

Run-on sentences: Several sentences chain multiple ideas with commas rather than periods or semicolons. Reading your paragraphs aloud will help you hear where sentences should break.

Priority Revision Checklist
Fill in all missing quotations and add proper citations.
Verify the political-party claim about death penalty support.
Align your thesis statement with your three body paragraphs.
Fix fragment/run-on issues, especially sentences starting with "Whereas" or "Well."
Add a question mark where you pose rhetorical questions.
Proofread for typos (foolproof, society) and word-form errors.

Your argument has real substance—focus your next revision on precision, evidence, and sentence mechanics, and this will become a persuasive, well-supported essay.

Overall Impression

Your topic sentences are strong, and the rebuttal-based structure (claim → counter-evidence → refutation) shows sophisticated argumentative thinking. However, there are several issues to address before this essay is ready for submission.

The most urgent issue is the numerous  placeholders throughout the essay where quotations should appear. These need to be filled in with the actual source language and proper in-text citations (author, page number, or paragraph number depending on your citation style). Right now, readers cannot evaluate whether your paraphrases accurately represent your sources, and the essay cannot be assessed for proper citation practice. Please also double-check that every source mentioned (the "Say a Little Prayer" writer, "Mississippi Prays" writer, Klinghoffer, Quindlen, etc) appears on a Works Cited/References page.

Phrases like "There are some weaknesses in this reasoning," "This claim is quite weak," and "This prayer according to opponents is anything but..." follow a similar pattern each time. You write clearly overall, but here are recurring error patterns to watch for:

Word confusion: "writes" → "writers" (paragraph 3); "intrest" → "interest"; "parishoners" → "parishioners"; "oppotunity" → "opportunity"
Article usage: Chinese does not use articles the way English does, so this is a common area for L1-Chinese writers. Check phrases like "a religious infusion," "the alleged 25%"—these are correct, but watch for missing articles elsewhere, eg, "prayer will not be able to end the ills of society" is fine, but scan for singular countable nouns without articles.

Sentence fragments/run-ons: "The prayer is void of any feeling or sincerity As Klinghoffer writes" is missing punctuation—likely just a typo from an incomplete citation, but proofread carefully once quotes are inserted.
Redundant phrasing: "the wide-spread views this debate creates" could be tightened to "the wide range of views this debate generates."

Paragraph on Practical Solution

Your proposed compromise (voluntary before/after-school prayer groups) is a strong, constructive addition to the essay—this shows you're not simply refuting the other side but offering a real alternative. Consider moving this solution earlier in your conclusion or even flagging it in your introduction, so readers know from the start that you have a constructive answer, not just a negative argument.

Introduction and Conclusion

The narrative opening is engaging and effectively sets up the human stakes of the debate. However, consider trimming it slightly, as it takes a while to reach your thesis. In your conclusion, avoid simply restating your thesis ("prayer should not be a part of the daily schedule..."). Instead, briefly address the broader implication: what does this debate reveal about the limits of legislating morality, or about the balance between majority and minority religious rights in a pluralistic society?

Summary of Priority Revisions

Fill in all missing quotations and verify citations.
Deepen your logical analysis, especially the correlation/causation argument.
Vary transitional phrases between paragraphs.
Proofread carefully for article usage, subject-verb agreement, and word choice errors.
Foreground your proposed solution earlier in the essay.

This is a strong draft with clear argumentative bones—the revisions above will sharpen both the clarity of your reasoning and the polish of your prose.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay on School Prayer

Thank you for sharing this draft. You've chosen a genuinely debatable topic and you clearly have opinions about it. Below is feedback organized by area, along with suggestions for revision.

Missing Evidence (Most Urgent Issue)

Every one of your quotations appears as  rather than actual text. This is a critical problem because:
Readers cannot evaluate whether your evidence actually supports your claims
Your paraphrases *after* each quote (eg, "She is stating that school is not the place...") suggest you're telling us what the quote means rather than letting the quote speak for itself and then analyzing it
A strong argumentative essay needs the reader to see the primary evidence, not just your interpretation of it

Action: Before revising further, insert the actual quotations and make sure each one is followed by *analysis* (why does this quote matter for your argument?) rather than simple restatement.

Logical Consistency

This is your biggest conceptual problem. Your thesis argues that prayer should not be instituted in schools because it imposes religion and produces meaningless recitation. However, in your fourth paragraph, you pivot to advocating for after-school religious activities and prayer groups—essentially proposing a version of school-sponsored religious practice yourself. This weakens your argument because:
It concedes significant ground to your opponents
It's unclear whether you object to *any* government involvement with prayer in schools, or only to *mandatory, in-class* prayer
The transition from "government should not impose religion" to "government should institute after-school activities" needs much clearer justification, since students could organize such groups themselves without government action

Action: Clarify your central claim. Do you oppose:
(a) mandatory recited prayer only,
(b) any government role in religious activity on school grounds, or
(c) something else?

Choose one clear position and make sure every paragraph supports it consistently.

Distinguish Between Different Proposals

Throughout the essay, you move between several distinct ideas without clearly separating them:
A government-written universal prayer recited by all students
A moment of silent reflection
Voluntary prayer groups after school

These are very different policies with different implications. Conflating them makes your argument harder to follow and easier for a critical reader to attack (eg, "the moment of silence doesn't require a government-written prayer, so your objection to the *content* of a mandated prayer doesn't apply to silent time"). 

Action: Address each proposal separately, or explicitly state early on that you are grouping them together and explain why.

Counterargument Paragraph

Your "opponent could claim" paragraph is a good instinct—acknowledging opposing views strengthens credibility. However, it currently reads as though you're arguing *for* the opponent's position (you spend most of the paragraph explaining why after-school activities would help disadvantaged children) rather than refuting it. A stronger counterargument paragraph would:
State the opposing view clearly
Concede any valid point briefly
Firmly explain why your position still stands despite this concession

Sentence-Level Language Issues

I noticed some recurring patterns common among Chinese-to-English writers:

Article usage: "promote moral values i children" (typo for "in"); consider reviewing when to use "a/an/the" vs. no article, especially before abstract nouns like "religion" or "prayer."
Subject-verb agreement: "According to the author... she states" is redundant—choose one.
Word choice/idiom: "Even is there is a silent time" should be "Even if there is..."; "there is a little effect" should likely be "there is little effect" (no article) since you mean "very little," not "a small amount."
Redundant phrasing: Phrases like "professes racial and religious tolerance" and "large step away from our professed ideal" repeat "profess"—vary your vocabulary.

Action: After revising content, do a careful proofreading pass focused specifically on articles, subject-verb agreement, and redundant word choice. Reading your essay aloud can help you catch these.

Structure and Transitions

Your paragraphs each tackle a reasonable sub-point, but transitions between them are abrupt. Consider adding a sentence at the start of each paragraph that explicitly connects it to your thesis (eg, "Beyond the issue of parental responsibility, a second problem arises when we consider what a government-written prayer would actually contain...").

Summary of Priority Revisions
Insert and analyze your actual quotations.
Clarify and unify your central claim across the essay.
Separate the three different policy proposals (mandated prayer, silent time, after-school activities).
Revise the counterargument paragraph so it genuinely refutes rather than supports the opposing view.
Proofread for articles, agreement, and repetitive phrasing.

You have a solid foundation and clear personal investment in this topic—tightening the logic and adding real evidence will make this a much more persuasive essay.

Feedback Report: Animal Testing Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a genuinely difficult and interesting ethical dilemma, and you show enthusiasm for the topic. However, the essay currently reads more like a personal opinion piece defending animal testing than a balanced academic argument. Below are the main areas to revise.

Missing Citations — Urgent Fix Needed

The most pressing technical problem is the repeated use of  throughout the essay. It appears these mark places where you intended to insert direct quotations (from the Bible, from Herbert Waddams, from Dr. Traystman, from Allen Bromey) but never completed them. As it stands, sentences like "in the Bible, it says that humans were to have " are incomplete and confusing to the reader. Before anything else, go back and insert the actual quotations with proper in-text citations (author, source, page/verse). Academic writing requires that every quoted or paraphrased claim be traceable to a specific, cited source.

Thesis and Balance

Your introduction promises a discussion of how "we must be careful to determine which side is morally correct," implying you will weigh both sides fairly. However, the body of the essay is overwhelmingly one-sided in favor of animal testing, and the concluding question ("who's life is more sacred, the life of a three year old boy, or a pig?") frames the issue as a false binary rather than the nuanced ethical dilemma you set up at the start. 

Revision suggestion: Decide explicitly what your thesis is. If your argument is "animal testing is ethically justified because human benefit outweighs animal suffering in most cases," state this clearly early on, and organize the essay so that opposing views (animal rights activists) are presented fairly *before* you rebut them — not simply dismissed. Right now the activists' argument (paragraph on the Draize test) appears almost as an aside rather than a serious position you're engaging with.

Logical Issues to Address

Appeal to religious authority: Using the Bible as evidence that humans have dominion over animals will not be persuasive to readers who do not share that religious framework. In an academic essay, you need a secular ethical justification (eg, utilitarian cost-benefit reasoning, which you actually use later) rather than relying on scripture as proof.
Single anecdote as universal proof: The Timmy Baker story is emotionally effective, but one successful case does not logically prove that *all* animal testing is justified. Anecdotes should support your reasoning, not replace it. Consider explicitly acknowledging this limitation ("while this case does not prove testing is always justified, it illustrates...").
Inconsistent standard: You argue that testing is justified when benefits "substantially outweigh" animal suffering, and even concede that some experiments (the dog/depression study) fail this test. This is a strong, nuanced point — but then your conclusion reverts to an absolute stance ("we must continue the testing"). Make your conclusion consistent with the nuanced cost-benefit framework you built earlier.
Factual error: Thalidomide caused severe birth defects; it did not "heal" them. Please verify this fact and correct it, as it currently undermines your credibility.
Emotional language: Phrases like "Thank God for little Timmy" and rhetorical questions stacked one after another read as persuasive/emotional rather than analytical. Academic argument should rely on evidence and logic; save emotional appeals for the introduction/conclusion at most, and use them sparingly.

Structure

Consider a clearer paragraph-by-paragraph organization:
Introduction with clear thesis
Background/context on animal testing (statistics, definitions)
Case *for* animal rights (activists' position) — presented fairly
Case *for* animal testing (with the Timmy Baker example and Traystman research)
Where you draw the ethical line (the dog experiment paragraph is good — expand this into your synthesis)
Counterarguments addressed (slippery slope re: bacteria/viruses; comparison to hunting)
Conclusion that reflects the nuanced position you developed, not a return to a simple binary

Language and Mechanics

Several sentence-level issues need editing:
"Some of them easily justified" → "Some of them are easily justified"
"which friends side" → "which friend's side"
"the party activity involved" → "the party actively involved"
"medicine aides in the healing off birth defects" → check word choice ("aids," not "aides"; "of," not "off")
"One animal experiment" (not "On animal experiment")
Watch subject-verb agreement: "animals my not be conscious" → "animals may not be conscious"
Several run-on sentences and comma splices — read your paragraphs aloud to catch these.

I recommend doing a full proofreading pass after revising content, since fixing structure may change many sentences anyway.

Summary of Priority Actions
Fill in all  citations properly.
Clarify your thesis and make the conclusion consistent with it.
Present the animal rights position more fairly before rebutting it.
Remove/reframe emotionally loaded language.
Correct the thalidomide fact.
Proofread for grammar and word choice.

You have the raw material for a thoughtful essay — the cost-benefit framework you introduce mid-essay is your strongest analytical tool. Build the whole essay around that logic rather than around emotional anecdotes and religious appeals.

Overall Impression

However, the essay needs revision in three main areas: use of evidence, paragraph organization/argument logic, and language mechanics. Below are specific suggestions.

This is the most urgent issue. Your essay contains numerous placeholders () where quotations, statistics, or paraphrased information should appear (eg, "it was said that ," "Pat Summit... says "). In the current draft, these gaps make it impossible to evaluate whether your evidence actually supports your claims. Before your next draft:

Fill in every  with a specific, attributed piece of evidence.
Use a consistent citation format (MLA is standard for undergraduate essays) with in-text citations and a Works Cited page.
When you quote someone (like Linnea Smith or Pat Summitt), briefly explain who they are and why their perspective matters *before* quoting them, then explain *how* the quote supports your argument. Right now some quotes seem to appear and disappear without analysis.

Organization and Argument Logic

Your paragraph structure (claim → counterclaim → rebuttal) is a good rhetorical strategy, but a few places need tightening:

The "added pressures" paragraph is confusing. You raise the objection that equal treatment might burden women's teams with pressure, but your rebuttal ("women have avoided mistakes men have made") doesn't fully answer why *equal treatment itself* wouldn't create the same problems. Clarify: are you arguing pressure is inevitable but manageable, or that it wouldn't actually occur? Right now it reads as if you're agreeing with the counterargument more than refuting it.
Transitions between paragraphs are abrupt. Each paragraph introduces a new counterargument (feminine vs. athletic, lack of interest, money, pressure) but doesn't explicitly connect back to your thesis at the end of each paragraph. Add a concluding sentence in each paragraph that ties the point back to "equal respect, rights, and opportunities."
Consider merging or reordering the money/scholarship paragraph and the "added pressure" paragraph—they both deal with institutional excuses and could flow together more logically.

Language and Style

As a note for a non-native English speaker, your grammar is generally strong, but here are recurring issues to watch for:

Spelling/typos: "supposeively" → *supposedly*; "otherhand" → *on the other hand*; "Their lucky" → *They're lucky*; "fell" → *feel*; "Pat Summit" → *Pat Summitt* (check proper noun spelling); "women" vs. "woman" (you write "being women" where you mean "being a woman").
Subject-verb agreement: "Colleges may argue why they should give..." — this sentence needs revision; "argue why" should likely be "argue about why" or reworded as "Colleges may ask why they should give..."
Comma splices/run-ons: eg, "It's going...going..." works stylistically in the intro, but elsewhere sentences like "Almost every player is on a full athletic scholarship and are offered..." have subject-verb mismatches (a singular "player" with a plural "are").
Clichés: "lower end of the totem pole" is overused; consider a fresher phrase like "systematically overlooked" or "treated as an afterthought."
Tone: Parenthetical asides like "(at least we don't belong in the kitchen anymore)" are informal and slightly sarcastic for an academic argumentative essay. Consider cutting or rephrasing to maintain a more consistently persuasive, professional register.

Strengths to Preserve

Your introduction is genuinely engaging—the play-by-play narrative device works well to illustrate an abstract problem concretely.
Your thesis is clear and appears early.
You anticipate multiple counterarguments, which shows sophisticated rhetorical awareness even if the rebuttals need strengthening.
Your conclusion effectively broadens the scope ("sit in a boat, or pick up a bat"), reminding readers this is part of a larger pattern.

Suggested Next Steps

Locate and insert real, cited evidence for every .
Revise each paragraph to end with a sentence explicitly linking back to your thesis.
Proofread carefully for spelling, subject-verb agreement, and comma splices—reading the essay aloud can help you catch these.
Reconsider the "added pressure" section's logic so the rebuttal directly counters the objection.

With stronger evidence and tighter paragraph-level logic, this essay has real potential to be a persuasive, well-supported argument. Keep the passion—just make sure the structure and evidence carry it convincingly.

These are related but distinct claims. Consider explicitly connecting them: does increased awareness lead to delayed sexual activity, or to safer practices *within* sexual activity? Right now the logic jumps.

Depending on what evidence goes here, you risk *supporting* the opposition's point before refuting it. "adolescent male" should likely be "adolescent males" (plural).

Ending with Rygor's quote is a good emotional appeal (pathos), but make sure the quote itself is impactful and that you provide one sentence of framing *after* it, so the essay doesn't end abruptly mid-quotation.

Feedback on "Prayer in Public Schools" Essay

Overall Impression
You've built a clear, consistent structure: each paragraph identifies a pro-prayer argument and then dismantles it. This counterargument-rebuttal pattern is a strong organizational choice for a persuasive essay, and your thesis is easy to locate and understand. However, several issues—some structural, some linguistic—need attention before this essay is ready for submission.

The most urgent problem is the repeated  placeholders throughout the essay. Doing so will also force you to represent the opposing view more precisely and fairly, which will make your rebuttals sharper.

Logical Consistency
Correlation vs. causation (paragraph 3): Your point about "uncontrolled variables" is genuinely strong critical thinking—keep it. But notice that your topic sentence ("this fact strengthens the argument for not allowing prayer") appears *before* you've actually stated what "this fact" is. Reorder so the claim comes first, then your interpretation.
Absolute claims: Phrases like "no data shows," "no reason to allow," and "holds no validity" overstate your certainty. Academic argument is more persuasive when it acknowledges nuance ("the available evidence does not demonstrate..." rather than "no data shows..."). Since you're accusing the other side of relying on "shaky speculation," your own essay should avoid the same overconfidence.
Loaded language: Words like "fanaticism" in your thesis and "apostasy" in paragraph 5 are emotionally charged rather than analytical. An academic audience will find your argument more credible if you replace such language with precise, measured terms (eg, "unsubstantiated appeals to emotion" instead of "fanaticism").
Final paragraph confusion: The closing sentence—"the lack of apparent good resulting from discouraging religion diversity"—seems to contradict your thesis. Check this sentence carefully; I suspect you meant "encouraging" rather than "discouraging," or the sentence needs restructuring so your final point is unambiguous.

Paragraph-Level Development
Several paragraphs (3, 4, 5, 6) begin with near-identical templates ("A common strategy...," "In an attempt to...," "Opponents... adopt an attitude..."). This repetition, combined with short paragraphs, makes the essay feel like a list of rebuttals rather than a developed argument. Consider:
Combining paragraphs 5 and 6, since they both address the "forcing beliefs on others" theme.
Adding a concrete historical or contemporary example in paragraph 5 (a specific case, like *Engel v. Vitale* or *Abington School District v. Schempp*) to ground your reasoning in real legal precedent rather than abstract assertion.

Encoding error: "na�vet�" should read "naïveté." Check your document for other special characters that may not have converted properly (eg, "�").
Verb agreement/form: "The concept of God is not discourage in a public school" → should be "discouraged."
Word choice: "determent" should be "detriment."
Mixed metaphor: "moral tarnishes... blemished society" — pick one image (stains, blemishes, tarnish) rather than combining them.
Article usage: Watch definite/indefinite articles, eg, "starting the day off in a public school" vs. "the American flag" — mostly correct, but double-check phrases like "the ban of school prayer" (should likely be "the banning of school prayer" or "the ban on school prayer").
Sentence-level clarity: Some sentences pack too many ideas together (eg, the final sentence of the essay). Consider breaking long sentences into two for clarity.

Suggestions for Revision

Vary paragraph openings to avoid repetitive structure.

Feedback Report: AIDS Discrimination Essay

Overall Impression

This is a passionate, well-researched essay with a clear moral stance and strong use of real-world case studies (Woodley, Lentz, the Valhalla pharmacist). Your commitment to the topic is evident, and you've clearly done research to support your position. However, there are several issues—some structural, some logical, and some related to language—that need attention before this essay is ready for submission.

Missing Citations — Urgent Fix Needed

The most pressing issue is the repeated use of  throughout the essay. These placeholders appear where quotations should be, but they are never filled in. As it stands, a reader cannot evaluate your evidence at all—we're told quotes exist, but never see them. This undermines your entire argument, since so much of your essay relies on quoted testimony (Woodley's letter, Margolick's reporting, Underwood's article, etc). Please insert the actual quotations with proper in-text citations (author, source, date) before this essay can be assessed on its evidentiary merits. This is your top priority for revision.

A Critical Logical Error

There is a serious contradiction in the paragraph beginning "This is more than enough proof that HIV, and later AIDS, is not, and cannot be transmitted through casual contact." You then write: "Therefore, co-workers, patients, friends, family, or anyone else in close contact with the victim is in danger of contracting the disease." This is the opposite of what you mean to say. Given your evidence (and your thesis), the sentence should conclude that people in *casual* contact are not in danger. This is a significant error because it appears in your rebuttal section and directly undercuts your argument. Please revise this sentence carefully—re-read your paragraphs for logic, not just grammar, especially around negation ("not," "cannot," "nothing").

Structure and Argument Flow

Your organization follows a reasonable pattern: introduction/thesis → case studies → rebuttal of opposition → emotional appeal → legal framing → conclusion. This works, but a few adjustments would strengthen it:

Thesis clarity: Your introduction contains multiple claims (discrimination is happening; it's morally wrong; it's legally wrong; the opposition's argument is a "fallacy"). Consider tightening this into one or two clear thesis sentences so readers know exactly what you will prove.
Case study proportion: The Woodley story receives disproportionate space (nearly a full page) compared to Lentz and the Valhalla pharmacist. Since Woodley is a "shining example," it makes sense to develop it fully, but ensure the other two examples get enough analytical space, not just narrative summary. Right now, you tell the story but don't always explain *how* each case proves your thesis.
Rebuttal placement: Your paragraph addressing "the predominant argument of employers and insurance companies" is a strong structural choice (concession + rebuttal), but it arrives quite late (paragraph 6). Consider signaling this the opposition's argument in your introduction, then developing the rebuttal earlier so it doesn't feel like an afterthought.

Language and Style Issues

Since you're writing in a second language, here are some patterns to watch for:

Redundancy: Phrases like "morally and legally wrong" and "legal and moral standards" repeat throughout. Vary your phrasing or trust the reader to remember the point after you've made it once.
Word choice/register: Some phrases are overly dramatic for academic argument ("a martyr to the AIDS community," "society would just as soon bury someone with AIDS alive"). Emotional appeals are legitimate in argumentative writing, but balance them with more measured, analytical language so your ethos as a credible arguer isn't undermined.
Comma splices and run-ons: Several sentences join independent clauses with only a comma (eg, "They backed this man into a corner, and left him no alternatives."). Review comma usage with coordinating conjunctions (and, but, so) versus semicolons for two complete sentences.
Minor errors: Watch for typos ("Wookley" vs. "Woodley," "fays" for "days," "car" for "care," "mo rally" for "morally," "A AIDS"). A careful proofreading pass will catch most of these.
Word "disease" vs. grammar: "AIDS is the most common disease" — check this claim; AIDS is not statistically the most common disease worldwide. Consider softening to "one of the most stigmatized diseases" or similar, which is more defensible and closer to your actual argument.

Suggestions for Revision Priority

Fill in all missing quotations with proper citations.
Fix the logical contradiction about casual contact and transmission risk.
Clarify and consolidate your thesis statement.
Proofread for typos and comma splices.
Balance emotional language with more analytical framing, especially in the concluding paragraphs.

You have a strong foundation with compelling case studies and a clear ethical stance—addressing these issues will make your argument much more persuasive and academically rigorous.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay: Orphanages and Welfare Reform

Thank you for sharing this draft. Your topic is timely and you clearly did substantial research. Below is feedback organized by area, with specific suggestions for revision.

Missing Citations (Most Urgent Issue)

Throughout the essay, you've left placeholders () where quotations or evidence should appear. This happens at least ten times, including in your introduction and conclusion. This is the single most important issue to fix before any other revision, because right now readers cannot evaluate your argument's evidentiary support. Please go back to your sources and insert the actual quotations, then make sure each one is followed by your own explanation of *why* it matters. A quotation should never stand alone—always add a sentence or two analyzing what it shows.

Thesis and Argument Structure

Your thesis appears at the end of the first paragraph: "many children have proven that growing up in one provides for a stable and nurturing atmosphere, therefore orphanages should be re instituted." This is a reasonable claim, but it's buried under a lot of introductory material about Newt Gingrich, welfare policy, and opposing views. Consider moving your thesis earlier and stating it more directly, so readers know your position from the start.

Also, be careful with your treatment of the opposition. You state that critics have "two reasons" (family breakup and cost), but you dismiss them quickly ("the truth is in the numbers") without much elaboration on what those numbers are. A strong argumentative essay should represent counterarguments fairly *before* rebutting them—right now it feels like you're minimizing the opposition rather than engaging with it.

Paragraph Organization

Several paragraphs mix multiple ideas without clear topic sentences. For example, the second paragraph moves from Scoppetta's quote, to 1960s historians, to "social control theory," to foster care's success, to the Baltimore study, to the Samuel Ready School—all in one paragraph. Consider breaking this into two or three paragraphs, each with a clear focus (eg, one on the history of anti-orphanage sentiment, one on recent scholarship that revises this view).

Similarly, the foster-care paragraph introduces a statistic (400,000 orphans), then jumps to homelessness, then to foster care's problems, then to Pia McKay's quote, then to St. Joseph's Orphanage. Each of these could be its own supporting point with a topic sentence like "Foster care itself presents serious problems that orphanages could address."

Use of Evidence

You cite several named sources (Scoppetta, Zmora, Kramer, McKay, Cooper), which is good practice. However, right now these citations function more as decoration than as argument. For each source, try this three-step pattern:
Introduce the source and their credibility (you do this well already).
Give the actual quotation or data.
Explain in your own words how this supports your specific claim.

Also double check: is the Baltimore study on Samuel Ready School representative, or could a critic say you're cherry-picking one success story? Consider addressing this potential objection directly.

Language and Grammar

Since English is your second language, here are some patterns to watch for in editing:

Apostrophes for possessives: "publics attention" → "public's attention"; "on it's own" → "on its own" (note: *its* without apostrophe is possessive; *it's* means "it is").
Compound words: "no where near" → "nowhere near"; "re instituted" → "reinstituted."
Word choice: "difficult to content with" — I think you mean "contend with" (to argue against) or "contest."
Typos: "and alternative" → "an alternative"; "he use" → "the use."
Comma splices: eg, "Many of these orphanages, did in fact provide..." — remove the unnecessary comma after "orphanages."
Sentence fragments: "with foster care, there is also a feeling of neglect" — check capitalization and whether this should attach to the previous sentence.

I recommend reading your essay aloud slowly, or using a grammar-checking tool, to catch these smaller errors after you've addressed the structural issues above.

Conclusion

Your conclusion has good instincts—acknowledging that orphanages aren't the *only* solution and that removal should depend on evidence of harm. This nuance is valuable and should perhaps appear earlier in the essay too, to make your argument feel less absolute. However, the conclusion currently ends on a placeholder quotation, so it's hard to assess how strong the final impression is. Make sure your last sentence is your own words, restating your thesis with conviction.

Priority for Revision

Fill in all missing quotations and analyze them.
Strengthen and fairly represent counterarguments before rebutting.
Break up overloaded paragraphs with clear topic sentences.
Proofread for possessives, word choice, and comma usage.

You have a solid foundation and clear passion for this topic—these revisions will help your argument come through more powerfully. I look forward to your next draft.

Feedback Report: Business Ethics Essay

Overall Impression

You have chosen an interesting and debatable topic—that business relationships matter more than pure profit—and you support it with a good range of sources (Fortune, Time, academic texts, case studies). However, the essay currently reads more like a collection of loosely connected examples than a tightly argued essay. My comments below focus on thesis clarity, structure, use of evidence, and language, with suggestions for revision.

Thesis Statement

Your introduction opens with rhetorical questions and general definitions, which delays your actual argument. The real claim—*"business relationships, built on trust and respect, are more important to long-term success than short-term profit"*—doesn't appear clearly until the end of paragraph one, and even then it is stated indirectly. 

Suggestion: Write one clear, direct thesis sentence near the end of your introduction that states exactly what you will prove. Everything afterward should visibly connect back to this sentence.

Organization and Paragraph Logic

Each body paragraph introduces a new example (producers/consumers, Sam Walton, advertising, B.F. Goodrich), but the connections between paragraphs are weak, and topic sentences often restate definitions rather than advancing an argument.

The B.F. Goodrich paragraph is introduced as something that "contrasts" your claim, but in fact it *supports* it (the company built a relationship first and profited later). This is a logical inconsistency—make sure your transition words ("in contrast," "however," "similarly") accurately reflect the relationship between ideas.
Several paragraphs repeat the same point in slightly different words (eg, "they are just greedy and out for self satisfaction" appears twice in paragraph 3). Trim repetition and instead build on the idea with new evidence or analysis.

Suggestion: Before revising sentences, outline your essay in one sentence per paragraph. Check that each sentence is a *new* point, not a restatement, and that transitions logically follow.

Use of Sources and Evidence

This is the area needing the most attention. Your essay contains many bracketed placeholders () where quotations or paraphrased evidence should be. In the current draft, it's impossible to evaluate whether your evidence actually supports your claims, because the evidence itself is missing.

Additionally, when you do use quotations, integrate them smoothly:
Introduce the source and its relevance *before* the quote.
Explain *after* the quote how it supports your specific point (don't just say "this supports my claim"—show *why*).
Use proper in-text citation format for all sources (author, date, page if using APA/MLA).

Suggestion: Fill in every placeholder with a specific quotation or paraphrase, then write 1–2 sentences of your own analysis explaining its significance. A source should never stand alone without your interpretation.

Language and Sentence-Level Issues

Subject-verb agreement: "Ethics are the goals..." — decide whether you are treating "ethics" as a singular field of study or a plural set of principles, and be consistent (usually singular: "Ethics is the study of...").
Run-on/comma-spliced sentences: Several sentences string together multiple independent clauses with commas (eg, in paragraph 1, "In business there is more involved than just making a profit and getting ahead; it involves relationships..."). Break these into shorter sentences or use appropriate conjunctions.
Redundant phrasing: Watch for repeated ideas across sentences within the same paragraph (paragraph 3 especially). Combine or cut redundant clauses.
Article usage: Check article use with abstract nouns (eg, "ethics involves the relationship of what is right in business" — consider rephrasing as "ethics concerns what is right in business").
Rhetorical questions: Opening with a question ("When someone thinks of business what do they think of?") is a common technique, but overusing it (again in paragraph 4) can weaken academic tone. Consider replacing at least one with a direct statement.

Conclusion

Your final paragraph begins with another placeholder quote and doesn't clearly synthesize your argument. The last sentence ("Money can not buy you friends, health of happiness, but good relationships can get you places in life") is a nice sentiment but reads as a cliché rather than an analytical conclusion.

Suggestion: Restate your thesis in new words, briefly summarize how each example (Sam Walton, Goodrich, advertising) supported it, and end with a forward-looking statement about why this matters for business practice today.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Write a clear, one-sentence thesis.
Fill in all missing quotations/evidence and analyze them explicitly.
Remove repetitive sentences; tighten paragraphs.
Fix transition logic (especially the Goodrich paragraph).
Proofread for run-ons and subject-verb agreement.
Rewrite the conclusion to synthesize, not merely restate.

You have strong ideas and relevant real-world examples—the next draft should focus on clarity, evidence integration, and logical flow. I'd be happy to look at a revised draft once these changes are made.

Feedback Report: Rhetorical Analysis of Shriver and Methvin

Overall Impression

You've taken on a genuinely challenging task: comparing two arguments about school prayer and evaluating their rhetorical strength. Your organizational instinct—analyzing claim, evidence, and treatment of opposition for each author—is sound and shows real understanding of what makes an argument effective. However, the essay needs revision in several key areas before it fully succeeds.

Strengths

Your comparative framework is a strength. You consistently apply the same criteria (clarity of claim, quality of evidence, handling of counterarguments) to both authors, which creates a fair and systematic comparison. Your point about Methvin's overreliance on quotation rather than original reasoning is a sharp observation, and your identification of the appeal-to-authority fallacy shows good critical thinking.

Areas for Revision

Missing Textual Evidence
This is the most urgent issue: your essay contains multiple  placeholders where quotations should appear. Without the actual quotes from Shriver and Methvin, readers cannot evaluate your claims. Every time you say "he states, ," you are asking the reader to trust your interpretation without evidence. Before revising anything else, insert the actual quotations and make sure each one is followed by *your own analysis* explaining why it is strong or weak. Right now, the essay's argument exists in outline form; the quotes are the flesh that make the argument persuasive.

Deepen Your Analysis of *Why*, Not Just *That*
You often assert that something is weak or strong without fully explaining the reasoning. For example: "This piece of supporting evidence is strong, but once again he does not elaborate on its importance." This is a good instinct, but push further—what *should* elaboration have looked like? What specifically is missing? Similarly, when you praise Shriver's biblical quotation, explain *how* it functions rhetorically (does it appeal to a shared moral framework? Does it preempt a religious objection to his own argument?). Analysis should show your reasoning process, not just your verdict.

Clarify and Strengthen Your Own Thesis
Your thesis—that Shriver's argument is stronger because it relies on facts rather than opinion and avoids fallacies—is a good start, but consider being more specific in your introduction about *which* fallacy Methvin commits and what kind of "facts" Shriver uses. This will give your essay a sharper analytical edge from the outset.

Sentence-Level and Grammar Issues
As a note for revision at the sentence level, watch for:
Subject-verb agreement and tense consistency: "people and learned through war" should be "people learned"; "praying has should not be allowed" needs correction to "praying should not be allowed."
Article usage: English articles (a/an/the) are notoriously tricky for Chinese speakers since Chinese lacks this grammatical category. Watch phrases like "the persecution of unpopular beliefs" (correct) versus "he state a claim" (needs "a" or "the" depending on context—check throughout).
Word choice/collocation: "he presents a religious argument" is fine, but "This ruling ha become" (typo for "has") and "so far he has only gathered momen tum" (spacing error) need proofreading.
Run-on and comma-spliced sentences: For example, "Shriver's argument is a stronger argument when compared to Methvin because..." is grammatically fine, but several other sentences string multiple ideas together without clear connectors. Read your sentences aloud to catch places where a period or conjunction is needed.

I recommend using a grammar-checking tool (like Grammarly) for a first pass, followed by careful proofreading focused specifically on articles and verb tense, which are common areas of difficulty when transitioning from Chinese syntax to English.

Paragraph Transitions
Your paragraphs move logically, but transitions between them are abrupt. For instance, moving from Methvin's section to Shriver's section could be smoothed with a sentence like, "In contrast to Methvin's weak claim, Shriver structures his argument very differently from the outset." This helps guide readers through your comparative structure.

Conclusion
Your conclusion effectively summarizes your position, but it could also briefly acknowledge the real-world stakes of the debate (why does it matter whether prayer is allowed in schools?) to leave the reader with a stronger sense of the argument's significance.

Next Steps

Insert all missing quotations and follow each with substantive analysis.
Expand your explanations of *why* each piece of evidence succeeds or fails.
Proofread carefully for grammar, especially articles and verb tense.
Add transitional sentences between major sections.

With these revisions, this essay has strong potential—your analytical instincts are good; now the evidence and language need to catch up to your ideas.

Feedback Report: Media Access to Executions Essay

Overall Impression
Your essay tackles an interesting and legally significant question, and you've clearly done research into a specific case (Robert Alton Harris) to support your argument. However, the essay needs revision in several key areas: completing missing citations, strengthening the connection between evidence and argument, and refining sentence-level grammar. Below are suggestions organized from higher-order concerns to sentence-level issues.

Missing Quotations and Citations
The most pressing issue is the number of unfilled placeholders () throughout the essay. These appear where you intended to insert direct quotations from Vasquez, the media brief, Brelsford, the First Amendment text, Sherwood, and Morain. As it stands, the essay cannot be evaluated as a finished argument because critical evidence is missing. Before revising further, please:
Insert the actual quotations you intend to use.
Add proper in-text citations (author, publication, date) so readers can verify your sources.
Include a Works Cited/References list at the end.

Thesis and Scope
Your opening claim—that "the public has a right to be informed about anything and everything that they want to be informed about"—is very broad and somewhat overstated. This absolute phrasing ("anything and everything") invites easy counterexamples (eg, classified national security information, private medical records) that weaken your credibility. Consider narrowing the claim specifically to executions and public accountability of the justice system: eg, "Because executions are carried out by the state on behalf of the public, citizens have a right to witness and verify how this power is exercised." This makes your thesis more defensible and directly tied to your case study.

Balance Between Narrative and Argument
A large portion of the essay (paragraphs 2–4) is devoted to summarizing the facts of the Harris case. This background is useful, but currently it reads as reporting rather than argumentation. For each factual paragraph, add a sentence or two explicitly connecting the facts back to your thesis. For example, after explaining that Judge Schnacke overruled the media ban, explain *why* this matters for your argument (eg, "This ruling establishes that unsubstantiated security concerns cannot justify restricting press access, reinforcing the principle that transparency should be the default unless specific risks are proven.")

Engagement with Counterarguments
You mention the opposing view (that media coverage is sensationalistic) but dismiss it quickly with a single line: "this simply does not override the First Amendment." This is not a strong rebuttal—it asserts rather than argues. Strengthen this section by:
Explaining *why* the First Amendment interest outweighs the dignity/decorum concern (perhaps using constitutional case law or scholarly commentary).
Directly addressing the security arguments you list later (staff identification, inmate agitation, equipment risk) instead of treating them as a separate, later point. Since these are legitimate concerns, integrate them earlier as concessions your thesis already accounts for (ie, "reporters yes, cameras no").

Tone and Register
The concluding line—"If you find the event grisly and gruesome, turn it off; it is that simple"—is too informal and dismissive for an academic argument. Academic essays generally avoid direct reader address ("As you can see," "If you find") and colloquial phrasing. Revise your conclusion to restate your thesis formally and synthesize your strongest points, perhaps closing with a broader reflection on the balance between transparency and safety in capital punishment cases.

Sentence-Level and Grammar Issues
Some specific corrections to address:
"However, there access should be restricted" → "their access"
"One such case which receive national attention" → "received"
"The reason Schnacke made the decision that he did was because the warden...had made to effort" — this sentence is grammatically tangled and unclear. "inmate are more tense" → "inmates are more tense"
Avoid redundant phrasing like "reason...is because" → use "is that."
Watch for repeated ideas (eg, staff retribution concerns are mentioned twice in the same paragraph)—consolidate for conciseness.

Summary of Priorities for Revision
Fill in all missing quotations and citations.
Narrow and sharpen your thesis statement.
Integrate analysis throughout the factual narrative, not just at the end.
Deepen your engagement with counterarguments, especially regarding security.
Revise the conclusion for a more formal academic tone.
Proofread for grammar (articles, subject-verb agreement, redundant phrasing).

With these revisions, your essay will move from a case summary with an argument attached to a fully integrated, persuasive argumentative essay. You have strong source material—now the task is to make your analytical voice equally strong.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Thank you for sharing such a personal and honest essay. Your willingness to connect your own experience to a public policy argument is a real strength, and it gives your writing emotional weight that many academic essays lack. However, to make this a more persuasive piece of argumentative writing, there are several areas you should revise: thesis clarity, evidence, structure, balance between narrative and argument, and grammar/mechanics.

Thesis and Argument Focus

Your thesis—that schools should teach sexual morals and values because parents and churches are failing to do so—is clear by the end of the introduction, but it gets diluted by ambiguous claims elsewhere. For instance, you state that "casual sex is unacceptable, degrading, animalistic," which is a strong moral judgment presented as fact rather than argument. Since your essay's purpose is persuasive, you need to *argue* for this claim, not assert it. Consider softening the language or explicitly stating this is your personal belief, then use evidence to support why schools should teach these values rather than simply declaring certain behaviors immoral.

Also clarify: are you arguing that schools should teach *abstinence-based morals*, or a broader *sex education* that includes safety and ethics? At times you seem to argue for abstinence-until-marriage teaching; at other points you mention "safe sex" and condom use. These are somewhat different educational philosophies, and the essay would be stronger if you clarified which position you are defending and acknowledged the tension between them.

Evidence and Citations

This is the most urgent issue. Several key claims are marked with , indicating missing statistics or citations (eg, "the latest statistic available before 1989 claimed there were "). An academic essay cannot include placeholder data—every statistic, study, or quotation needs a real, cited source. Please:

Locate actual statistics on teen pregnancy, STI rates, and sex education outcomes.
Cite them properly using MLA or APA format (introduce the source, give the data, and include an in-text citation).
Make sure the numbers are current, not from decades ago, unless you are making a historical point.

Right now, the essay relies heavily on personal experience and general assertions ("results of these studies show...") without demonstrating what those results actually are. Concrete evidence will make your argument far more convincing to a skeptical reader.

Structure and Paragraph Unity

The essay would benefit from a clearer organizational plan. Right now, ideas about parents, media, church, and schools are somewhat scattered across paragraphs. Consider organizing your body paragraphs by *reason*, such as:

Parents are failing to educate children (with evidence).
Media promotes sexual content without guidance (with evidence).
Churches are also stepping back from this role (with evidence).
Therefore, schools are the last remaining institution capable of filling this gap (with evidence of effectiveness).
Counterargument and rebuttal (you already have this—strengthen it).

This would make the logical progression of your argument easier to follow.

Balancing Narrative and Argument

Your opening and closing personal narratives are compelling, but consider trimming them slightly so they serve the argument rather than becoming the primary content. Right now, entire paragraphs (like the one detailing your marriage and divorce) function as storytelling rather than evidence. You might integrate a sentence or two connecting these experiences explicitly back to your thesis—for example, explaining *how* better sex education might have changed the outcome of your own situation.

Grammar and Mechanics

There are a number of grammatical issues to address in revision:

Apostrophes: "bird's and the bee's" should be "birds and the bees." "Youths'" should often just be "youths" or "youth."
Pronoun capitalization: "Him" in "critical for Him" should be lowercase "him."
Comma splices and run-ons: eg, "This type of behavior teaches our youngsters very little respect for sexual behavior" is fine, but several earlier sentences run together ideas without clear punctuation. Reading your essay aloud can help you hear where a sentence needs to be split.
Word choice: "casual sex is unacceptable, its degrading" — "its" should be "it's" (a contraction of "it is").
Consistency of tense: You shift between past tense (narrative) and present tense (argument) frequently; make sure transitions between these are intentional and clearly marked.

Tone

Because this is an argumentative essay for an academic audience, try to move from emotionally charged language ("hiding behind their bedroom doors," "turning their backs on our youth") toward more measured, evidence-based phrasing. Emotional appeals can be effective, but they should be balanced with logical and factual support so the essay doesn't read as one-sided moralizing.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Fill in all missing statistics/citations with real, credible sources.
Clarify your specific policy position (abstinence-based vs. comprehensive sex ed).
Reorganize body paragraphs around clear, evidence-supported reasons.
Trim personal narrative to keep focus on the argument.
Proofread carefully for apostrophes, pronoun capitalization, and sentence boundaries.

You have a genuinely compelling personal story and a clear stance—now the goal is to build the scaffolding of evidence and structure around it so readers are persuaded by argument, not just moved by narrative. I'd be happy to discuss any of these points further in office hours.

Feedback on "The Right to Refuse Life Support"

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a genuinely difficult ethical question, and your final position—that the decision to refuse life support belongs to the individual—is clear and defensible. You also do a nice job breaking the topic into manageable sub-questions (What is life? What is death? What do different people want?). However, the essay currently reads more like a personal reflection than an argumentative essay, because your claims are rarely supported with evidence, and the reasoning between paragraphs is not always explicit. Below are suggestions organized by area.

Thesis and Argumentative Structure

Your thesis appears at the end of the introduction: "The right to refuse life-support is an individual one." This is a good, specific claim. However, the body paragraphs mostly *describe* different attitudes toward life and death rather than *arguing* for why the decision must be individual. Ask yourself after each paragraph: "How does this support my thesis?" For example, the paragraph on defining death is interesting background, but you need a sentence explicitly connecting it back to autonomy—something like, "Because there is no single, universally accepted definition of death, no outside authority can claim to know better than the individual when life has effectively ended for them."

Consider also acknowledging a counterargument (eg, family members' or physicians' perspectives, or religious objections to refusing treatment) and responding to it. This would strengthen your claim that the decision is *individual* by showing you've considered who else might claim a stake in it.

Development and Evidence

This is the area that needs the most work. Many important claims are asserted but never supported:
The history of how death has been defined (breath → heartbeat → brain activity) is a real and interesting fact, but it needs a source or at least more specific detail (dates, examples, medical context).
The claim that coma patients may feel pain is a scientifically contested and important point—it deserves a citation or at least a hedge ("some researchers suggest...").
The mention of cryogenics is intriguing but underdeveloped; give a concrete example or statistic.

Right now the essay relies almost entirely on generalizations ("Some people...," "Others...") and rhetorical questions. A few concrete examples—a real case (eg, Karen Ann Quinlan, Terri Schiavo), a statistic on advance directives, or a quotation from a medical ethicist—would make your argument far more persuasive and academic.

Paragraph Organization and Coherence

Several paragraphs (especially the one on defining "life") consist of short, list-like sentences that repeat the same idea in slightly different words ("Our memories... Our personality... Our memories, our personality..."). Combine these into fewer, more complex sentences with clear logical connectors (*because, therefore, as a result, in contrast*). This will also help you avoid redundancy.

The paragraph on physicians ends without a clear conclusion tying back to your thesis—why does the physician's perspective matter to your argument about individual choice? Make that connection explicit.

Point of View and Tone

You shift between "we," "I," "you," and "people/others," sometimes within the same paragraph (eg, "We must consider..." vs. "Where do you draw the line..."). Choose one consistent stance—academic essays typically avoid direct address to "you." Since your final paragraph reveals a personal position, you might frame the whole essay in first person ("I argue that...") or keep it more objective throughout ("This essay argues that...") rather than mixing "we" and "you."

Also, phrases like "cheat death" are conversational; in an academic essay, replace with more formal phrasing ("delay death" or "resist death").

Language and Mechanics

A few specific corrections to watch for:
"to use" → "to us" (typo)
"people o want" → "people who want"
Be consistent with hyphenation: "life support" vs. "life-support" (choose one)
Some sentences are fragments or run together without connectors (eg, "The cost here is more than money. Consider the price of emotions as well..."). Combine with transitions: "The cost involves more than money; it also includes emotional costs..."
Rhetorical questions are used heavily (at least eight in the essay). Use them sparingly—one or two for emphasis—and otherwise convert them into assertions ("We must ask whether someone else has the right to decide for us" rather than "Would we want someone else to make this decision...?").

Suggested Revision Priorities

Add at least 2–3 concrete examples, cases, or citations to support key claims.
Strengthen transitions between paragraphs so each explicitly returns to your thesis.
Choose a consistent point of view (first person or general/academic).
Reduce rhetorical questions; replace most with direct statements.
Proofread for typos and consistent hyphenation.

You have a thoughtful, humane argument here—the next revision should focus on giving it firmer intellectual scaffolding and more concrete support.

Thank you for sharing this deeply personal and passionate essay on welfare reform. Roosevelt, not Herbert Hoover. You have two  placeholders where quotes should appear (Hoover's statement, and the social worker's exact words). These are crucial pieces of evidence—please insert them and cite sources where appropriate (especially for Hoover's quote, which needs a source).

Are there studies, sociological data, or statistics you can cite? Right now this reads as an assertion rather than an argued point. Consider: Are there other factors (deindustrialization, systemic racism, urban policy) that scholars link to family instability? Even briefly acknowledging alternative explanations would strengthen your credibility.

Tone Toward Welfare Recipients
While you clearly care about families and want to help them, some phrasing risks sounding judgmental in ways that may undercut your compassionate goals: "become indolent and/or chemical abusers," or the claim that children "grow up thinking they could have whatever they wanted...without working." "It's purpose" → "Its purpose" (its = possessive; it's = "it is")

"or financially able" — check this sentence for parallel structure

Also check:
"welfare benefits and the building" — comma usage in lists could be more consistent
"food rent, and medical assistance" → missing comma: "food, rent, and medical assistance"

I recommend using a grammar checker (like Grammarly) specifically to search for "it's" vs "its" throughout, as this is your most frequent error.

Paragraph Transitions
Some paragraphs begin abruptly (eg, "There are many families who have been dependent..."). Consider adding transitional phrases that explicitly connect back to your thesis, such as "This dependency illustrates the deeper problem I mentioned above..."

Suggested Next Steps

Verify and correct the Hoover/Roosevelt historical claim
Fill in the missing quotations
Add at least one external source (data, study, or expert testimony) to support your causal claims about welfare and family breakdown
Revise word choices that stereotype recipients; focus on systemic explanations

Your voice and personal investment in this topic are real assets—with these revisions, your argument will be both more persuasive and more academically rigorous. Well done on tackling such a complex, personally meaningful topic.

"off spending" → "of spending"
"he well have to consider" → "he will have to consider"
