Overall Impression

This is a passionate, well-researched essay that tackles a genuinely difficult ethical question. You clearly did your homework on the case timeline, Erikson's developmental theory, and adoption law reform. The essay's greatest strength is its emotional resonance—readers will care about Richard. To move this from a strong draft to a polished argumentative essay, focus on tightening your thesis, integrating sources more rigorously, and refining sentence-level grammar.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your central claim—that the Illinois Supreme Court erred in transferring custody—doesn't appear clearly until paragraph 4 ("The Illinois Supreme Court made an immense mistake..."). Move this thesis statement to your opening paragraph so readers know your position from the start. Right now, the introduction is mostly narrative summary of the case, which delays your argument.

Also consider narrowing your claim. You currently argue three related but distinct points: (1) the ruling harms Richard psychologically, (2) it harms the Does emotionally, and (3) it damages public trust in adoption. Each is a legitimate concern, but make sure your thesis promises all three so the structure feels intentional rather than like a list of grievances.

Missing Citations

I notice several bracketed placeholders () throughout the draft where quotations or paraphrased evidence should appear—for example, the Erikson quote, the Mark Hansen quote, and the timeline of events. These must be filled in before submission. An argumentative essay relying on expert authority (Erikson, legal journalists) needs the actual quoted material, properly cited (author, publication, page/date), not placeholders. Right now, a reader cannot evaluate whether your paraphrase of Erikson accurately represents "initiative vs. guilt," which weakens your most important psychological argument.

When you insert these quotes, follow them with your own analysis—don't just drop a quote and move to the next idea. For instance, after the Hansen quote, explain *specifically* what in the article supports your claim before transitioning to the Uniform Adoption Act.

Use of Counterargument

You do address a counterargument (the Baby Jessica comparison) effectively—this is your strongest paragraph. Consider adding at least one more: Why might someone believe biological parenthood should take precedence regardless of the child's age? Addressing this makes your case more persuasive and shows critical distance from your own position.

Rhetorical Questions and Tone

Phrases like "Nobody likes to be hurt, so why go through with it?" and "Think back to when you were four years old..." are effective for engaging readers but are more common in persuasive/opinion writing than academic argumentation. If this is for a composition class emphasizing academic register, consider replacing some of these direct-address questions with analytical statements. For example, instead of asking the reader to imagine being four, you could write: "Developmental psychologists note that children at this age lack the cognitive tools to process separation from primary attachment figures without significant distress."

Sentence-Level Language Issues

You write clearly overall, but there are recurring grammar patterns worth reviewing:

Capitalization after "when": "when Daniela Janikova gave birth" should begin with a capital letter since it starts a new sentence after the period following "complicated."
Comma splices/run-ons: "The Illinois Supreme Court made an immense mistake in transferring custody... Before we discuss the case..." — check paragraph breaks for sentences that should be separated or joined with conjunctions.
"whom are referred to" should be "who are referred to" (subject pronoun needed here, not object pronoun).
"vary from state to state" — you wrote "very from state to state." Easy typo to fix.
"ripping him away form the only family" — "form" should be "from."
Article usage: Watch for missing articles before singular countable nouns (eg, "the point in his life" is correct, but double-check similar constructions throughout, as English articles are famously tricky for Chinese speakers, since Chinese doesn't use articles the same way).

I recommend reading your essay aloud or using a grammar-checking tool for a final pass—these are minor issues that a careful edit will resolve.

Structural Suggestion

Consider adding a brief concluding paragraph that goes beyond restating the court's error and instead looks forward: What should happen now? What reforms would you recommend? Your mention of the Uniform Adoption Act sets this up nicely—capitalize on it in your conclusion rather than ending on the court's timeline of events.

Summary of Priority Revisions

State your thesis explicitly in the introduction.
Fill in all missing quotations/citations with proper attribution.
Add at least one counterargument beyond the Jessica comparison.
Proofread for the grammar patterns noted above.
Strengthen your conclusion with forward-looking analysis.

You have compelling ideas here—these revisions will help your argument match the strength of your convictions. Good work overall!

Feedback on Your Euthanasia Essay

Your essay tackles a genuinely difficult ethical question, and I can see you've put thought into organizing three distinct lines of argument. However, there are significant issues with clarity, evidence, and logical structure that need attention before this essay is fully persuasive. Let me walk through the main areas for revision.

Thesis and Argument Structure

Your introduction promises three ideas, but as currently written, they overlap significantly and aren't clearly distinct. Idea 2 ("too difficult to determine suffering") and Idea 3 ("the law is too vague") are essentially restating the same concern about the impossibility of judging "worth" of life. Consider consolidating these into one sharper argument, and using the freed-up space to develop a truly distinct third point. Also, your introduction states the opposing view's constitutional argument but never returns to directly refute it—if you mention the Declaration of Independence argument, make sure you circle back to address it head-on.

Evidence Problems (Important)

This is the most serious issue in the essay:

The monkey example is vague and unverifiable. Who conducted this? Where? When? Without a citation, this reads as anecdotal or possibly invented. Academic writing requires you to name your source (eg, "According to [source], in [year]..."). Right now, a skeptical reader cannot check this claim, which undermines your credibility.
You have three placeholder markers  where quotations should be. These must be filled in with actual quoted material before this essay is submission-ready. Right now, the paragraph about the law's vagueness is essentially built on missing evidence, which weakens your argument considerably.
The Roe v. Wade comparison is thought-provoking, but you assert the parallel between euthanasia and abortion rather than proving it. You state they are similar because both involve "ending a life," but a critical reader will want you to address obvious *differences* (consent, personhood debates, etc) before accepting the comparison as valid for building your argument.

Logical Leaps

Several claims move from evidence to conclusion too quickly:

The monkey/dog examples show that *some* Americans oppose euthanasia for animals. This does not firmly establish that "America is not ready" for human euthanasia — you're generalizing from a protest group to the entire nation. Acknowledge this is one indicator, not proof.
The abortion violence comparison assumes that because both involve ethical questions about "ending life," similar violence will follow with euthanasia. This is a slippery-slope argument. Strengthen it by finding actual instances of euthanasia-related violence (if they exist) rather than just predicting them from a different issue.
The sentence "if politicians can not find enough support... there must be some consensus that safety standards are not adequate" is a leap — lack of political support could stem from many causes unrelated to safety standards specifically.

Grammar and Word Choice (Common Patterns to Fix)

I noticed several recurring errors typical of transferring sentence patterns from Chinese to English. Here are the main categories with examples:

Article usage: "a very controversial topic," but later "God-given life" needs consistent article checking throughout. Chinese doesn't require articles the way English does, so proofread specifically for missing "a/an/the."
Subject-verb agreement: "the doctor and the family has little to say" → should be "have." "There is a large gray areas" → should be "are... areas" or "is... area."
Homophone/spelling confusion: "there own lives" → "their own lives"; "a live is worth living" → "a life"; "my America" → "by America"; "wholes" → "holes."
Word form: "American Against Human Suffering describes" — check organization name accuracy and capitalization consistency.

I recommend reading your essay aloud, or using a grammar-checking tool, specifically hunting for these five patterns.

Tone and Framing

Phrases like "God-given life" introduce a religious framework that may not fit an otherwise secular, policy-based argument. Consider whether this phrase serves your argument's logic or whether it's an assumption not everyone shares. If you keep it, acknowledge that not all readers share this premise.

Feedback on Your Essay: Televised Executions

Your essay tackles an interesting and ethically complex issue, and your opening anecdote about Amy is a strong hook that personalizes an abstract debate. However, the essay needs significant revision in several areas before it fully succeeds as an academic argument. Below are the main areas to address.

Missing Citations
This is the most urgent issue. Throughout the essay, you have placeholders () where quotations should appear—for example, "As KQED states, " and "Sherman states one reason... He stated, ." Without the actual quotations, your evidence is invisible to the reader, and claims like "these are concrete reasons" are unsupported. Please insert the full quotations with proper in-text citations (author, page/paragraph number) before you submit this essay again.

Clarify Your Central Framework
You introduce the rhetorical distinction between arguments of "value" and arguments of "consequence," but this framework is never clearly defined for the reader. You assume we already understand these terms. Consider adding a brief explanation early in the essay: What makes an argument one of "value" versus one of "consequence"? Why does this distinction matter for evaluating the strength of an argument? Right now, the claim "this is a matter of value" feels asserted rather than demonstrated.

Strengthen the Thesis
Your thesis sentence is long and difficult to parse: "The argument that executions should be televised... is established as an issue of consequence and is not a strong argument because the support for the claim is not as concrete..." Consider revising for clarity and directness. For example:

> "Although KQED argues that broadcasting an execution is protected by the First Amendment, this argument is weaker than the opposition's case because it relies on an abstract principle rather than concrete evidence of consequences."

A clear, front-loaded thesis will help guide your reader through the rest of the essay.

Avoid Circular Reasoning
In paragraph two, you write: "This is not a valid argument because only one aspect is being considered and is therefore not a valid argument." This sentence repeats itself without adding new reasoning. When you claim an argument is invalid or weak, explain *why* with specific logical or evidentiary reasons—don't just restate the conclusion.

Organize Paragraphs Around Clear Claims
Some paragraphs (especially the fifth and sixth) feel like lists of quotations without a clear organizing idea. For instance, the paragraph beginning "Watching executions on television will be both well-received and rejected..." introduces Pat Clark's view but doesn't explain how it fits into your overall argument. Ask yourself: What point is this paragraph making, and how does it support my thesis? Each paragraph should open with a topic sentence that states its main claim, followed by evidence and analysis of that evidence—not just evidence alone.

Develop Your Analysis Further
You often present a quotation and then move on without unpacking it. For example, after presenting Sherman's point about the Rainey Bethea hanging, tell the reader explicitly: What does this historical example prove? How does it counter KQED's First Amendment claim specifically? Strengthening this analytical step will make your comparison between the two sides' arguments more convincing.

Language and Mechanics
There are several errors to address in revision:
"usual routing" should be "usual routine."
"be drooms" should be "bedrooms."
"effect" should be "affect" ("how televising this execution will affect their ratings").
"pat Clark" should be capitalized: "Pat Clark."
Watch pronoun agreement: "their argument is more thoroughly defined" should likely be "Their argument is more thoroughly defined" (capitalize after a period).
Some sentences are run-ons or comma-spliced; reading your essay aloud can help you catch these.

Conclusion
Your conclusion returns nicely to Amy's story, which creates a sense of closure. However, it currently just restates your thesis rather than synthesizing your argument. Consider adding a sentence that reflects on the broader stakes of this debate—why does it matter whether we get this argument right? What are the implications for future cases like KQED's?

Summary of Priorities for Revision
Insert all missing quotations and citations.
Define your "value vs. consequence" framework clearly.
Revise the thesis for clarity and conciseness.
Eliminate circular reasoning.
Add topic sentences and analysis to underdeveloped paragraphs.
Proofread for grammar and mechanics.

You have a solid foundation with a compelling real-world hook and a genuine research base. Feedback on Your Abortion Essay

Overall Impression

You tackle a genuinely difficult, high-stakes topic, and your prose shows real effort to build a structured, evidence-based argument. However, the essay currently reads more as advocacy than balanced academic argumentation, and there are some structural and mechanical issues that need attention before this meets academic standards.

Thesis and Framing

Your introduction promises something the body doesn't deliver. You write, "Whichever position is taken, however, [a moral decision must be made]" — this phrasing suggests you will examine *both* positions fairly before reaching a conclusion. Instead, the pro-choice position gets one sentence, while the pro-life position receives multiple paragraphs of supporting evidence. If your goal is a one-sided persuasive essay, adjust your thesis to state this directly (eg, "This essay argues that abortion is morally impermissible because..."). If your goal is a balanced academic analysis, you need to give the pro-choice argument comparable depth and address its strongest points, not just summarize it in a single clause.

Loaded Language and Question-Begging

Several rhetorical moves weaken your credibility as an academic writer:

Putting "endure" in scare quotes, then immediately dismissing it, primes the reader before you've presented evidence.
Phrases like "senseless killing of an innocent human life" and "innocent child being denied a chance at life" assume the conclusion you are trying to prove. Academic argument should build toward these claims through evidence, not use them as premises.
Rhetorical questions ("Can one really justify...?") followed by "The answer is a resounding no" function as assertion, not demonstration. Consider replacing at least some of these with evidence-based reasoning: *why* is the answer no, according to which principle or source?

Missing Citations — Critical Issue

Your essay contains numerous placeholder markers () where quotations and statistics should appear — including your opening statistic, the biological description from "Calahan" (note: also spelled "Callahan" elsewhere — check spelling consistency), the Grisez quotation, the Hippocratic Oath excerpt, and your concluding quotation. This must be corrected before submission. An essay's persuasive force depends entirely on the specific evidence you cite; without the actual quotations, readers cannot evaluate your argument's strength. Please insert the full quotations with proper in-text citations (author, page number) and a Works Cited list.

Logical Slippage: Moral vs. Legal

You move between moral and legal registers without clearly distinguishing them. For example: "This is more commonly known as murder, which without a doubt is legally and morally wrong." Murder is a *legal* term with a specific definition (unlawful killing with intent); calling abortion "murder" assumes it meets that legal definition, which is precisely what is contested. Similarly, the Louisiana law you cite is a *legal* fact, not moral proof — using it as evidence for a moral claim conflates two different kinds of argument. Be more careful to signal when you're making a legal point versus a moral one.

Structure of the Body Paragraphs

Your organization is logical in sequence: (1) prove the fetus is human/alive → (2) prove it's a separate entity → (3) prove abortion is therefore murder → (4) address the doctor's moral responsibility. This is a reasonable skeleton. To strengthen it:

After presenting each piece of "biological proof," add a sentence explaining *why* this fact matters morally, not just biologically. Being "human and living" doesn't automatically settle moral status — many philosophical arguments distinguish between biological human life and moral personhood. Anticipate this objection.
Address the strongest pro-choice counterarguments (bodily autonomy, viability, situations of rape/health risk) rather than only refuting the "fetus is part of the mother's body" claim.

Conclusion Undermines the Thesis

Your conclusion shifts tone dramatically — introducing uncertainty ("These questions will probably never be answered...") and ending with the aphorism "You can't legislate morality." This final line actually contradicts the argumentative thrust of your entire essay, which has been built to *support* pro-life legislation (citing the Louisiana law approvingly). Reconsider whether this ending serves your thesis, or revise your conclusion to reaffirm your central claim more directly.

Language-Level Notes

A few recurring L2 issues:
"more ninety-eight percent" → "more than ninety-eight percent"
"A direct quote taken form the oath" → "form" should be "from"
Watch subject-verb agreement in longer sentences (eg, "and, therefore, be subject to")

Next Steps

Fill in all missing citations and check spelling of sources.
Decide whether this is persuasive or analytical, and adjust the thesis accordingly.
Strengthen the pro-choice section for balance, or explicitly frame the essay as one-sided in the introduction.
Revise the conclusion so it aligns with your thesis.

Feedback Report on Draft Essay: Medical Technology and the Value of Life

Thank you for sharing this ambitious and thought-provoking draft. You are tackling a genuinely difficult philosophical question—whether extending life through medical intervention paradoxically undermines the value of life—and there is a real argument here worth developing. However, the essay currently has significant problems in its use of evidence, its logical reasoning, and its organization that need attention before it will be persuasive to an academic audience. Below are the main areas to revise.

Missing Sources and Quotations

Throughout the essay you have placeholders marked "<\*>" where quotations or paraphrased evidence should appear (eg, after "the medical community derives its existence solely on the preservation of life," in the PKD section, and twice in the discussion of Heimer/Bosk). These gaps make it impossible to evaluate your evidence, and in the final draft they will make the essay look unfinished. Before revising further, please fill these in with the specific claims, statistics, or quotations you intend to use, and make sure each is properly cited (author, page number, publication) according to your assigned citation style.

The Central Argument Needs Sharper, Earlier Statement

Your thesis—that medical efforts to postpone death may degrade the value of life and strain societal resources—doesn't become clear until nearly the last paragraph. Readers need this claim, or at least a clear preview of it, in your introduction. Right now the introduction wanders through several ideas (constitutional rights, the death-and-taxes cliché, the Hippocratic oath, healthcare costs) without signaling which of these threads is your actual argument. Consider revising the introduction so that by the end of the first paragraph, the reader knows exactly what you will argue and how you will support it (genetic disease, antibiotic resistance, viral mutation, healthcare costs).

Logical and Scientific Problems Need Correction

This is the most serious issue in the draft, and it will undermine your credibility with an academic audience:

Misapplication of evolutionary theory. Your claim that PKD would be rarer today if medicine hadn't "interfered" with natural selection oversimplifies both evolution and human ethics. Natural selection does not optimize for "the good of the species" in a way that should guide medical or moral policy; applying it this way edges into territory associated with historical eugenics arguments, which is likely not your intention but will read that way to many readers. You need to either qualify this claim heavily, engage with the ethical objections to it directly, or reconsider using it as a pillar of your argument.

Conflating unrelated categories of disease. Genetic conditions (PKD), infectious diseases with resistance issues (antibiotic overuse), and viral mutation (HIV, Hantavirus) are governed by very different biological mechanisms. Treating them as interchangeable examples of "the same problem" weakens your argument—a knowledgeable reader will notice that antibiotic resistance, for instance, has nothing to do with "keeping weak immune systems alive to reproduce." Each example needs to be argued on its own terms, with the mechanism explained accurately, and you should be explicit about what connects them (is it economic cost? philosophical value? both?).

The passage about the parent quoted by Bosk. Calling the parent's decision "selfish" and suggesting they should have "adopted a child already born with a genetic defect rather than risk the life of their genetic child" is a strong ethical claim made without adequate context (we don't even see the quotation) or engagement with the real dilemmas parents face. This paragraph risks sounding harsh and judgmental rather than analytical. If you want to make a claim about reproductive ethics, you need to present the quotation, explain the parent's actual reasoning, and then argue against it with evidence and moral reasoning—not assertion.

Tone and Audience

The essay shifts between formal argumentative prose and casual, second-person address ("Do you continue to take the medication?" "I'm sure you've heard of the AIDS virus"). This creates an inconsistent voice. Direct address can work rhetorically, but use it deliberately and sparingly—perhaps confined to specific moments of illustration—rather than throughout.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

Watch capitalization consistency: "Doctors" is capitalized in some places, "doctor" lowercase in others; it should be lowercase unless part of a title.
"t decide" is missing a letter (should be "to decide").
"fallibility to disease" should likely be "vulnerability to disease" (fallibility usually refers to being capable of error, not illness).
Several long, comma-spliced sentences (eg, the opening sentence of the antibiotics section) would benefit from being broken into two sentences for clarity.

Suggestions for Revision

Fill in all missing citations and quotations.
Rewrite the introduction to state your thesis clearly.
Revise or remove the evolutionary-selection argument, or substantially qualify it with acknowledgment of its ethical complexity.
Treat each disease example with more precision, and explicitly connect them back to your central claim.
Rework the section on genetic counseling to present evidence before judgment.
Proofread for capitalization, grammar, and sentence length.

You clearly have strong critical instincts and a willingness to ask uncomfortable questions—qualities that make for compelling argumentative writing. The next draft should focus on grounding your bold claims in accurate evidence and more careful ethical reasoning.

Feedback on "Assisted Suicide and Euthanasia" Essay

Overall Impression

You have chosen a genuinely difficult and important topic, and it is clear you have done real research into the debate surrounding Dr. Kevorkian. Your essay shows an ability to identify multiple perspectives and to think critically about the strengths and weaknesses of each side's reasoning. This is a sophisticated goal for an argumentative essay. However, the essay currently reads more like a rhetorical analysis of both sides than a clear argument with your own position, and there are several structural, grammatical, and stylistic issues that need attention before revision.

Clarify Your Thesis and Purpose

Your introduction opens with two rhetorical questions and then moves into a fairly neutral description of the debate ("supporters believe... opponents believe..."). By the end of the introduction, you state that "both parties have strengths and weaknesses," but this is not really a thesis—it's an observation. An argumentative essay needs a claim that you will defend. Ask yourself: What do you actually think should happen? Do you support regulated assisted suicide, oppose it entirely, or advocate for a middle path (as your conclusion vaguely suggests with "we need to find a median")? Right now, the body paragraphs devote three sections to opposition arguments and only one to supporters, which unintentionally signals that you lean against Kevorkian—yet your conclusion never confirms or denies this. Decide on your position and revise your thesis statement to state it explicitly.

Balance the Argument

Because the essay is meant to weigh both sides fairly (or build toward your own stance), the current structure feels lopsided: three paragraphs argue against Kevorkian, one argues for him. If your goal is to demonstrate balanced analysis, add another paragraph presenting supporters' reasoning (for example, patient autonomy, quality of life, or the argument that suffering itself is a harm doctors should be permitted to relieve). If your goal is to argue against Kevorkian, then restructure the essay so this is clear from the start, and treat the "supporters" paragraph as a counterargument you then rebut—rather than leaving it as an equally-weighted, separate view.

Strengthen the Analysis of Strengths/Weaknesses

Your method—identifying a strength and then a weakness in each argument—is a good analytical structure. However, some of your evaluations are underdeveloped. For instance, you say the "steep" fast-food comparison is a weakness, but you don't explain *why* it fails to persuade (Is it because it's an exaggeration? Because it minimizes the seriousness of the issue?). Similarly, in the doctor's-oath paragraph, you claim the argument has "relatively no weaknesses," which is a strong claim that needs more justification—every argument has some limitation worth acknowledging, even a minor one. Push yourself to explain *how* and *why* each strength/weakness functions, not just to label it.

Missing Quotations

Several placeholders  appear where quotations should be. Since your feedback is being written before these are filled in, make sure that once you add the direct quotes from Hendin, Saunders, Garcia, Reich, and Kevorkian, you also introduce and explain each quote fully—state who the person is (their credentials/role in the debate) and explain how the quote supports your point *in your own words* immediately afterward, not just before.

Grammar and Language

As a non-native English writer, you're doing well with complex sentence structures, but there are recurring issues to address:

Word choice/spelling: "neglection" → *neglect*; "af raid" → *afraid*; "i doctors" → likely *in doctors*.
Article usage: Check for missing/extra articles (eg, "the neglection to name the consequences" → "the failure to name the consequences").
Sentence fragments and run-ons: eg, "While opponents believe that Kevorkian is trying to play God..." is a fragment because it starts with "While" but lacks an independent connecting clause—join it to the previous sentence.
Subject-verb agreement: "This makes it seems as if" → "seem."
Consistent pronoun use: You shift between third person ("society," "people") and first person plural ("we," "our lives"). Choose one voice—likely third person, since this is an academic/analytical essay—and stay consistent.

The Martin Luther King Jr. Comparison

This analogy is risky. Comparing Kevorkian's civil disobedience to MLK's suggests a moral equivalence between assisted suicide and the civil rights movement, which many readers will find unconvincing or even offensive. If you keep this comparison, explain the comparison's *limits* explicitly (eg, both broke laws to follow personal conviction, but the moral stakes and social consensus differ) rather than implying they are equally admirable.

Conclusion

Your conclusion largely repeats the introduction without synthesizing your analysis into a clear final stance or recommendation. Use the conclusion to state what *you* believe should be done, based on the strengths and weaknesses you've identified—this is where your argumentative voice should emerge most clearly.

Priority for revision: (1) clarify your thesis/stance, (2) balance the paragraphs, (3) proofread for grammar, (4) integrate quotations properly.

Feedback on Corporal Punishment Essay

Overall Impression

This is a promising draft with a clear analytical angle: rather than arguing for or against corporal punishment itself, you critique the *rhetorical effectiveness* of the opponents' arguments. This is a sophisticated approach, and your narrative opening about Tommy is vivid and engaging. However, the essay needs significant revision in three areas: (1) completing your evidence, (2) tightening the logical structure, and (3) correcting grammar and sentence-level issues.

Content and Argument

Missing Evidence (Critical Issue): Your essay contains several placeholders () where quotations should appear. These are the heart of your argument—you cannot claim that Jambor's and Johns/MacNaughton's definitions differ, or that Diamantes contradicts himself, without actually showing the reader the words. Before revising further, fill these in and make sure the quotations *clearly demonstrate* the inconsistency you're claiming. Otherwise, your central claim rests on assertion rather than proof.

Logical Consistency: Your thesis argues the opponents' case is weak due to (1) ambiguous terminology and (2) a wavering position. These are well-chosen categories, but the "vacillate on consequences" paragraph is underdeveloped—it merely restates the claim without a specific example (a quotation, a named author, a cited study). Compare this to your stronger paragraph on definitions, which names two specific authors. Strengthen the "consequences" paragraph with equally concrete evidence.

The "Suggestions" Paragraph: The paragraph beginning "How is it, then, that anyone would choose to believe..." shifts your essay's purpose—from critique to advice-giving. This is not wrong, but it changes the essay's register. Consider whether this belongs as a distinct section (perhaps introduced with a clearer transition, like "If opponents wish to strengthen their case, they might...") or whether it should be folded into your conclusion, since it currently interrupts the analytical flow between your body paragraphs and your ending.

The Conclusion: The statistic about the twelve states is interesting and relevant, but it arrives too late. Consider moving it earlier (perhaps into your introduction or first body paragraph) to establish stakes before your critique, rather than introducing new information in the conclusion. Because English is not your first language, I want to flag some recurring issues so you can watch for them in future writing:

Missing articles/prepositions: "took he seat" → "took her seat"; "subjected the paddle" → "subjected to the paddle." Chinese does not require articles the way English does, so these are easy to miss—read your final draft aloud slowly to catch them.

Invented words: "unteeteringly" is not a standard English word. Consider "unwaveringly," "firmly," or "without wavering."

Comma splices: "The authors who define the term do not apply the same definition, several of the authors fail to make any attempt at defining." This should be two sentences, or joined with a semicolon or conjunction: "...do not apply the same definition; furthermore, several authors fail to attempt a definition at all."

Ambiguous phrasing: "you probably know like Tommy, - maybe you were a tommy" is confusing—it's unclear whether "know like Tommy" refers to knowing a similar experience or knowing Tommy's feelings. Revise for clarity, eg, "you probably know how Tommy felt—perhaps you were a Tommy yourself."

Capitalization inconsistency: "Tommy" becomes lowercase "tommy" in places (intentional as a common noun, referring to any child in this situation)—this is a nice rhetorical move, but make sure it's consistent and perhaps clarify it explicitly the first time you use it this way, so readers don't think it's a typo.

Add clear topic sentences that echo your thesis vocabulary ("ambiguous terminology," "wavering position") so readers can track your organization.
Consider a brief paragraph acknowledging *why* this rhetorical weakness matters—you gesture at this with the reader's undecided position, but could develop it further as a bridge between your critique and your call to action.

Priority for Revision

Fill in all quotations and verify they support your claims.
Strengthen the underdeveloped "consequences" paragraph with specific evidence.
Proofread carefully for grammar (articles, prepositions, comma splices).

This essay has a strong conceptual foundation—keep developing the specific evidence, and the argument will become much more persuasive.

Feedback on "The Case for Increased Censorship" Draft

Overall Impression
You've clearly invested significant research into this essay, gathering compelling examples (the Huston study, Grace Under Fire, NYPD Blue, the Mapplethorpe case) that could make a persuasive argument. However, the essay's effectiveness is undermined by one major structural choice and several recurring language issues. Below are the key areas to revise.

Rhetorical Stance: Speak in Your Own Voice
The most significant issue is that you consistently write *about* what "advocates for censorship" or "proponents" argue, rather than making the argument yourself. Phrases like "the advocates for censorship effectively prove" or "the proponents can see" distance you from your own thesis and make the essay read like a summary of someone else's position rather than your argument.

Revise this pattern. Instead of: *"The advocates for censorship effectively prove how the decline in censorship is affecting America's youth,"* try: *"Case studies and program analysis reveal how the decline in censorship is affecting America's youth."* Let your evidence speak for your claims directly. This single change will make your essay feel far more confident and persuasive.

Thesis and Structure
Your thesis—that increased censorship is necessary to protect children—doesn't emerge clearly until deep into the essay. Consider stating it explicitly in your introduction: what specifically are you asking for (stricter content ratings? time restrictions? industry regulation?), and why, in one or two clear sentences.

Additionally, several paragraphs feel like they're circling the same point (children are impressionable; networks are irresponsible) without advancing to new territory. Consider organizing around clear sub-claims:
Children are demonstrably influenced by TV content (Huston study, Power Rangers)
Society has grown desensitized to sex/violence (Mapplethorpe, Florida jury case)
The industry resists self-regulation (Valenti/Senate example)
Counterargument and rebuttal

This would give readers a clearer roadmap.

Evidence Integration
You have strong sources, but they need better integration:
The bracketed placeholders () suggest missing quotations or citations—make sure these are filled in with the actual text before submission.
When introducing evidence, explain *before* the quote/example why it matters, then follow with analysis. Right now, some paragraphs list an example and then simply assert it proves your point without showing the reasoning.
Double-check your citation format for consistency (author name, title italicization, publication).

Counterargument Section
This is one of your strongest sections because you seriously engage an opposing view (parents should just turn off the TV). Consider expanding this section—right now it's compressed into one paragraph. You could also strengthen it by acknowledging what's *valid* in the opposing view before pivoting to your rebuttal, which builds credibility with skeptical readers.

Language and Grammar
Several recurring issues to watch for in revision:

Affect vs. Effect: You use "effect" as a verb multiple times ("what they view effects their behavior"). Effect (verb) means "to bring about"; affect (verb) means "to influence." You almost always want *affect* here.
Comma splices and run-ons: Many sentences join independent clauses with only a comma. Example: *"Censorship is a policy of control or standards upon something institution"*—this sentence needs restructuring for clarity and correct word choice (perhaps: "Censorship is a policy of control placed upon an institution—in this case, television—and the material...").
Subject-verb agreement: eg, "some parents use the television as the baby-sitter, not monitoring what their children see, so should the child be punished" — reconsider sentence boundaries here.
Apostrophes/plurals: "seventy's" should be "seventies"; "Psychologist, Aletha Huston" doesn't need a comma there.
Word choice: "caniving" (conniving?), "peak" (should be "pique"), "bout" (should be "about") — proofread carefully for typos.

I'd strongly recommend reading your essay aloud, sentence by sentence, to catch run-ons and missing words—this technique often helps non-native speakers catch errors that are hard to see silently.

Conclusion
Your conclusion largely restates the introduction. Instead, try synthesizing: what is the single most urgent reason your audience should care about this issue *right now*? End with a call to action or a forward-looking statement rather than a summary.

Next Steps
Rewrite in your own voice—remove "advocates say/prove" framing.
Fill in all bracketed citations and quotes.
Clarify thesis in the introduction.
Proofread for affect/effect, comma splices, and word choice errors.
Expand the counterargument section.

You have the raw material for a compelling essay—the revision work now is about *voice*, *clarity*, and *precision*. I'm happy to discuss any of these points further in office hours.

Feedback on Your Essay: Genetic Research and Ethics

Overall Impression

You've clearly done research and thought about the ethical dimensions of genetic research. However, there is a fundamental structural issue that needs to be addressed before we discuss anything else.

Major Issue: Meta-Argument vs. Actual Argument

Your essay currently describes *how one should construct* an argument against genetic research rather than *actually making* that argument. Notice phrases like "One should present the history..." or "This reference to the Nazis deals with..." You are narrating your argumentative strategy instead of executing it.

Compare these two approaches:
What you wrote: "Robert Sinsheimer says this subject deals with the horrors of eugenics as seen through Nazi Germany. This reference to the Nazis would be effective in an argument because..."
What you likely intended: "As Robert Sinsheimer argues, the history of eugenics—epitomized by Nazi Germany's genocidal programs—reveals humanity's capacity to misuse genetic knowledge. If even limited genetic understanding could fuel atrocities on this scale, we must ask whether modern genetic research, which 'far surpasses' what the Nazis had, poses similar dangers."

Revision suggestion: Decide whether your assignment calls for (a) an actual persuasive essay against genetic research, or (b) a rhetorical analysis of how such an argument *could* be built. If it's (a), rewrite every "one should" sentence into a direct claim. If it's (b), make this purpose explicit in your introduction and conclusion, and frame the essay as analysis rather than advocacy.

Missing Quotations

The placeholder brackets  appear seven times throughout the essay. These are essential—your entire argument rests on these quotations, yet readers cannot evaluate your claims without seeing the actual evidence. Please insert the specific quotes and cite them properly (author, publication, page/paragraph number).

Balance and Credibility

You state "no argument is absolute," yet your essay only presents one side. For an ethics debate this significant, briefly acknowledging the strongest counter-evidence (eg, the medical benefits of early disease detection) before refuting it would make your argument more credible and less like a one-sided op-ed. Right now, you assert that proponents' arguments can be "refuted," but you don't fully refute them—you mostly restate their concerns without decisively countering them (eg, the sickle-cell/malaria point actually supports *nuance* in genetic research, not blanket opposition).

Factual Accuracy

Mendel was an Augustinian monk, not "Gregorian" (Gregorian usually refers to the calendar or chant). Please verify this before submitting.
"Mendal" should be spelled Mendel.

These small errors can undermine your credibility with an academic reader, so a fact-check pass is essential.

Language and Grammar

As you continue developing your academic English, watch for these recurring patterns:

Subject-verb agreement: "One consequences deals with privacy" → "One consequence deals with..."
Awkward phrasing: "by doing three doings" is unclear—try "through three strategies" or "by taking three steps."
Article usage: "put modern medicine in the position to see future" needs an article: "the position to foresee future..."
Sentence fragments/run-ons: Some sentences are quite long and combine multiple ideas without clear connectors (see the second paragraph). Consider breaking these into shorter, clearer units.
Word choice: "malevolent" is an unusual choice to describe a gene; "harmful" or "detrimental" may fit better in context.
Repetition: The phrase "genetic research" appears extremely frequently. Consider synonyms like "this field," "such research," or "these advances" for variety.

I recommend reading your essay aloud—this often catches agreement errors and awkward phrasing that are easy to miss when reading silently.

Structure Suggestions

Your five-paragraph body structure (history → refutation of pro-arguments → consequences → monitoring/regulation) is logical and easy to follow. To strengthen it:

Add clear topic sentences that state the paragraph's claim directly, rather than describing what "one should" do.
Strengthen your transitions between paragraphs by linking back to your thesis (eg, "This danger to personal privacy represents just one of several consequences that make genetic research ethically fraught").
Your conclusion is solid but could more forcefully restate your actual position (whichever you choose per the meta-argument issue above).

Next Steps

Clarify your rhetorical purpose (direct argument vs. analysis of argumentation).
Insert and properly cite all missing quotations.
Fact-check historical/biographical details.
Do a full grammar and word-choice review, ideally reading aloud.
Add at least one strong counter-argument you directly rebut.

You have strong source material and clear organizational instincts—the main task now is to commit to a direct argumentative voice and polish the language mechanics. I'd be happy to discuss any of these points further in office hours.

Feedback Report: Comparative Rhetorical Analysis Essay

Overall Impression

This is a solid draft with a clear, well-defined thesis: that audience shapes how writers select and present facts, illustrated through JAMA and Newsweek's treatment of binge drinking. Your analytical framework—context and attitude—is appropriate and consistently applied across paragraphs. Below are suggestions to strengthen the essay in four areas: argument development, organization, evidence/citation, and language.

Argument Development

Your central claim is convincing, but some reasoning needs tightening:

The "good writer" claim needs qualification. You write that "making those assumptions is what makes a good writer" after discussing how Newsweek assumes readers want emotionally upsetting content. This risks sounding like you're endorsing sensationalism as good writing. Clarify whether you mean "effective for its purpose" rather than "good" in an absolute sense—this distinction matters for your argument's nuance.

Avoid overstating intent. Phrases like "getting people to read their article is [Newsweek's] concern... The problems that stem from this subject are really not of concern to them" assume the writers' motives rather than analyzing textual evidence. Consider softening to "the article's structure suggests a priority on reader engagement over comprehensive reporting" — this keeps your claim grounded in observable rhetorical choices rather than speculation about intent.

Distinguish context from attitude more sharply. Your context section (physical/social) and attitude section cover some overlapping ground—both discuss Newsweek's emotional framing. Consider clarifying in your topic sentences what attitude adds that context doesn't, or merge overlapping points to avoid repetition.

Organization

The essay follows a logical progression (introduction → factual comparison → audience assumptions → context → attitude → conclusion), which works well. Two suggestions:

The second paragraph (statistics on binge drinkers/non-drinkers) anticipates your social-context discussion later, creating some redundancy. Consider either moving this data point exclusively to the "social context" section, or using the early paragraph only to introduce the topic, saving detailed analysis for later.

Your conclusion is underdeveloped. It restates the thesis but doesn't synthesize *why* this comparison matters. Consider ending with a broader observation: what does this comparison reveal about how readers should critically evaluate media reporting on social issues? This would give your essay a stronger final impression.

Evidence and Citation

This is the area needing the most attention:

Missing quotations. Several placeholders () appear where specific statistics or quoted language should be. These need to be filled in with the actual wording/data from your sources before submission.

No formal citations. Since you're analyzing two specific published articles, you need in-text citations (author, year, page) and a Works Cited/References list, even in a rhetorical analysis essay. This is expected in academic writing and strengthens your credibility as a careful reader of sources.

Specify article titles and authors. Currently you refer generically to "the JAMA article" and "the Newsweek article." Naming the authors and giving full titles early on will make your analysis more precise and citable.

Language and Mechanics

Your control of complex sentence structures and topic-specific vocabulary (eg, "rhetorical situation," "physical/social context") is impressive. A few recurring issues to address in revision:

Apostrophes for possessives: "todays college campuses" → *today's*; "writers assumptions" → *writer's*; "readers attention" → *reader's*; "author's" is used correctly elsewhere, so check consistency throughout.

Word confusion – affect/effect: "any given time" is correct, but "an affect on how an article is written" should be *an effect on*. Effect (noun) = result; affect (verb) = to influence.

Subject-verb agreement: "this make for differences" → *this makes*; "56% or men" → *56% of men* (likely a typo).

Article usage: "the way in which the writer interprets" is fine, but watch phrases like "give the readers a complete report" — consider whether "the readers" needs the definite article or should be "readers" (general reference).

Redundant phrasing: Sentences like "This is the purpose of physical context, to enhance the articles initial appeal" repeat ideas already stated. Trim for conciseness.

Suggested Next Steps

Fill in all  placeholders with exact quotations/data.
Add proper in-text citations and a reference list.
Revise the conclusion to include a synthesizing insight about media literacy.
Do a careful proofreading pass focused on possessive apostrophes and affect/effect distinctions.
Consider whether the "context vs. attitude" sections could be tightened to reduce repetition.

Your analytical instincts are strong—the comparison itself is genuinely insightful. With attention to precision in language and completing the citation work, this essay will be significantly stronger.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You've tackled a genuinely complex, contested topic and demonstrated real engagement with source material—the specific examples (Dawson's study, the San Marcos statistics, Grady Webb-Wood's testimony) show good research. However, the essay has significant structural and clarity problems that undermine your argument. Let me walk through the main issues.

Unclear Thesis and Position

Your essay's biggest weakness is that your own position is never clearly stated. You spend the entire essay summarizing what "opponents of abstinence-only" argue, but it's unclear whether *you* agree with them, and if so, why. Academic argumentative essays require you to take a stance and defend it with your own reasoning, not simply report both sides' claims.

Revision suggestion: Add a clear thesis statement in your introduction that states your position explicitly. For example: "This essay argues that comprehensive sex education is more effective than abstinence-only programs because the latter relies on misleading statistics and incomplete information." Then structure each paragraph to support this claim.

Missing Content (The  Placeholders)

This is a serious problem. Your essay contains at least ten  placeholders where quotations or key evidence should be. Without this content, readers cannot evaluate your argument—we don't know what Xavier Flores actually said, what Pat Socia's misleading question was, or what "catchy phrases" you're referring to. Before your next draft, you must fill in all quotations and cited material. These aren't optional details; they are the evidence your entire argument depends on.

Confusing Attribution of Ideas

Throughout the essay, it's often unclear whose argument you're presenting. Sentences like "Proponents' support has turned into their own fallacies and misfortunes" are vague—*whose* fallacies? You alternate between reporting "opponents say X" and "proponents say Y" so rapidly that readers lose track of who holds which position. 

Revision suggestion: Use clearer signal phrases and keep a consistent structure: state the abstinence-only claim, then state the counter-argument, then (crucially) add *your own analysis* of why one is more convincing.

Logical Gaps and Unclear Reasoning

Some of your reasoning needs more explicit connection:
In paragraph 3, you note that only 14-year-olds showed the correlation Dawson's study claims, but you don't explain *why* this matters (ie, that a single-age correlation without similar results in other age groups suggests the finding may be spurious or coincidental rather than a real trend).
The birth control pill/condom comparison in paragraph 2 is a good example, but you need to state explicitly what point it's meant to prove (that Socia's question falsely implies no good pregnancy prevention existed before condoms became prominent).

Spell out the "so what" of each piece of evidence rather than assuming readers will draw the same conclusion you do.

Grammar and Word Choice

Some specific corrections to watch for:
"opponents claim" not "opponents of his claim" (paragraph 1—this phrase is confusing as written)
"birth control" (two words, not "birthcontrol")
"not being informed" → "not be informed" (paragraph 1)
"teens will not being informed" — subject-verb agreement issue
"since, why should" → remove the comma, or rephrase as "since neither is true, why should..."
"these [nouns] will eventually lead" — check that "these" has a clear noun referent (paragraph 4, before "will eventually lead")
"beared" → "borne" (paragraph 3: "other studies have borne this scrutiny")
"prevelant" → "prevalent" (final paragraph)
"lend any type of education" → likely "lend credibility to" or "provide legitimate"—the current phrasing is unclear

Paragraph Organization

Your paragraphs are organized by theme (misleading questions, inconclusive studies, facile reasoning), which is a reasonable structure. However, each paragraph would benefit from:
A clear topic sentence stating the specific claim being examined
Evidence (the quotations, once filled in)
Your own analysis explaining why this evidence supports your thesis
A transition to the next point

Currently, paragraphs end somewhat abruptly with a summary of the opponents' view rather than your own evaluative conclusion.

Conclusion

Your conclusion raises a good question ("Should we teach prevention methods in addition to abstinence?") but then doesn't fully answer it—you gesture toward comprehensive education being necessary but don't tie this back concretely to the evidence you presented. Strengthen this by explicitly restating your thesis and briefly summarizing your three strongest pieces of evidence.

Priority Action Items for Revision:
Fill in all  placeholders with actual quotations
Write a clear, explicit thesis statement
Add your own analysis after each piece of evidence—don't just report what others say
Proofread for grammar (subject-verb agreement, word choice, spelling)

You have strong research here—now you need to make your own voice and argument more visible. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback Report: Analysis of Adoption Rights Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

This essay demonstrates sophisticated critical thinking. Rather than simply arguing for one side of the biological-vs-adoptive-parents debate, you analyze the *quality of argumentation* on both sides—examining values, consequences, and rhetorical strategies. This is an ambitious and mature approach. However, the essay has significant structural and clarity issues that undermine this strong analytical framework. Below are key areas for revision.

Missing Source Material

The most pressing issue is the placeholder brackets  scattered throughout the essay (in the sections on public opinion, the NCFA statistic, and the DeBoer/Schmidt case). These gaps make it impossible for a reader to evaluate your evidence or follow your reasoning. Before any other revision, you must fill these in with actual quotations, statistics, or article titles. As it stands, a reader cannot assess whether your analysis is accurate because the primary evidence is invisible.

Clarify Your Thesis and Analytical Framework

Your thesis—that neither side uses strong values, consequences, or attempts to shift public opinion—is a promising and original angle. However, it needs to be stated more precisely in your introduction. Consider explicitly defining these three analytical categories (values, consequences, public opinion) early on, so readers understand the framework you'll apply to each case. Right now, the categories emerge somewhat haphazardly across paragraphs, making the essay feel like a list of observations rather than a unified argument.

Suggestion: Add a sentence like: "This essay evaluates each side's use of three rhetorical tools: appeals to *values* (moral/legal principles), appeals to *consequences* (predicted outcomes), and attempts to shape *public perception*—and finds both sides lacking in different ways."

Paragraph Organization

Your paragraphs jump between the two cases (Baby Jessica/Richard) and general claims without clear signposting. Consider reorganizing by your three analytical categories (values, consequences, public opinion) rather than alternating between "biological parents' arguments" and "adoptive parents' arguments." This would let you directly contrast how each side handles *the same category*, strengthening your comparative analysis.

Sentence-Level and Grammar Issues

As a non-native English speaker, you should watch for these recurring patterns:

Comma splices and run-ons: eg, "Blood is thicker than water. This is the main underlying value..." — consider combining or restructuring for smoother flow.
Pronoun ambiguity: "This is a somewhat strong argument" — *what* specifically is "this"? Be explicit rather than relying on demonstrative pronouns to carry meaning across sentences.
Article usage: Watch for missing/extra articles (e specifically "a" or "the") — eg, "one could argue" (typo: "on could argue").
Subject-verb agreement: "these two set out to show" — check consistency in verb tense throughout (you shift between present and past tense often, eg, "The Schmidt family is portrayed" vs. "They were visited").
Wordy transitions: Phrases like "This is the reasoning behind the supporters of biological parents" could be tightened to "This reasoning underlies biological parents' claims."

I recommend reading each paragraph aloud to catch awkward phrasing, or using a grammar-checking tool as a first pass before a final human review.

Strengthen Topic Sentences

Several paragraphs bury their main point in the middle or end (eg, the paragraph on psychological effects). Move your evaluative claim—*is this argument strong or weak, and why*—to the first sentence of each paragraph, then use the rest of the paragraph to support that claim with evidence.

Conclusion

Your conclusion effectively returns to the thesis, but it introduces a slightly emotional, unsupported claim ("these young children can be spared the guilt and pain") that shifts tone from analytical to sentimental. Consider ending instead with a sharper restatement of what stronger argumentation would look like, keeping the analytical register consistent with the rest of the essay.

Priority Revision Checklist

Fill in all  placeholders with actual evidence.
State your three-part analytical framework explicitly in the introduction.
Reorganize paragraphs around values/consequences/public opinion rather than case-by-case.
Proofread for tense consistency, pronoun clarity, and articles.
Sharpen topic sentences to lead with evaluation, not narration.

You have a genuinely interesting and original argument here—critiquing the *quality* of a debate rather than taking a side is a sophisticated move. With clearer structure and complete evidence, this essay could be very strong. I'd be happy to discuss any of these points further in office hours.

Feedback on Your Essay on Academic Cheating

Thank you for sharing this draft. You clearly have real engagement with this topic—your own experience as a student adds a valuable perspective—and you have researched several concrete strategies (honor codes, the "XF" grade, faculty involvement, legal action). Below are the main areas to focus on as you revise.

Clarify Your Argumentative Stance

The biggest structural issue is that it's often unclear whether you are (a) summarizing what "the authors" argue in your sources, or (b) presenting your own argument. Sentences like "The authors show that revamping the academic code and establishing strict honor codes are the two strongest methods" and "As a student, I believe that establishing honor codes is the best method" sit side by side without a clear signal of whose voice is speaking. For an argumentative essay, your thesis should be stated in your own voice early on, and source material should be clearly framed as *support* for your claim (eg, "According to Levine..." or "As the case of the University of Virginia demonstrates..."). Revise your introduction so the thesis is unmistakably your own position, and make sure every paragraph traces back to that claim rather than simply reporting what "the authors" say.

Missing or Incomplete Information

Several sentences end abruptly or seem to be missing content (marked with  in your draft, eg, "Mr Levine of Harvard believes that " and "It is hard for students to "). These gaps make it impossible for a reader to follow your reasoning. Before submitting, check that every sentence is complete and that every citation actually names its source and claim clearly. If you removed material by accident, this is a good opportunity to also add signal phrases like "Levine argues that..." so readers know where your evidence comes from.

Paragraph Focus and Transitions

Each body paragraph currently mixes description of a strategy, its evidence, its weakness, and your opinion, but the order shifts between paragraphs, which makes the essay feel unevenly organized. Try a more consistent pattern for each solution you discuss:
State the strategy in one sentence.
Give evidence/example.
Explain the weakness.
Explain your evaluation (is it fixable or not?).

Also add transition phrases between paragraphs ("In contrast to honor codes, revamping the academic code focuses on..."). Right now paragraphs feel like separate lists rather than a connected argument building toward your conclusion.

The paragraph on legal/corporal punishment solutions is underdeveloped compared to the honor code and academic code sections. You state that these methods "failed because they are unrealistic and irrational," but you don't explain *why*. Since this paragraph is meant to eliminate competing solutions and strengthen your case for honor codes, it needs the same level of detail (an example, a source, a clear line of reasoning) as your other paragraphs.

Language and Sentence-Level Issues

A few recurring patterns to watch for, common among Chinese-to-English writers:

Article use: "an honest academic environment" not "and honest academic environment" (typo in your conclusion); check "a" vs "the" throughout, eg, "the best method for combating cheating" is fine, but be alert to missing articles before singular nouns like "codes at universities" vs "the codes."
Word choice/precision: "too reluctant" not "to reluctant." "Eliminate" not "eliminated" in "find the best way to eliminated this problem."
Subject-verb agreement: "The strategies that they have developed are strong" — check throughout that plural subjects take plural verbs consistently.
Wordiness/redundancy: Several ideas are repeated almost verbatim (eg, the point that honor codes are "the best method" appears three times). Consolidate these into one strong statement, then build on it rather than restating it.
Run-on or fragment sentences: "This shows that their effectiveness can be increased by improving the faults" is vague — clarify what "their" and "the faults" refer to specifically.

Strengthen the Conclusion

Your conclusion currently restates your process ("After researching...") and repeats earlier points almost word-for-word rather than synthesizing your argument. A strong conclusion should briefly remind readers of your main claim, acknowledge the counterargument's best point, and then explain *why* your solution still wins — ideally adding a forward-looking or broader implication (eg, what should universities do next, or what does this mean for the culture of academic integrity).

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Fill in missing content and citations (the  gaps).
Establish a single, clear thesis in your own voice from the start.
Reorganize paragraphs with consistent structure: claim → evidence → weakness → evaluation.
Develop the paragraph on failed solutions with the same depth as your other sections.
Proofread carefully for articles, verb agreement, and repeated phrasing.

You have strong source material and a genuine personal stake in this topic — with clearer organization and sentence-level polishing, this essay can become a compelling, well-supported argument. Good work so far, and I look forward to seeing your revision.

Feedback Report on Your Fourth Amendment Essay

Overall Impression

You have chosen a genuinely interesting and complex case to explore, and your research into the specific details of the Simpson search (the fence-climbing, the glove, Fuhrman's history) shows real effort. However, the essay currently sits uncomfortably between two different genres: a rhetorical analysis of how each side argues its case, and an argumentative essay that takes and defends a position on how the Fourth Amendment should be interpreted. Deciding which genre you are writing—and committing to it—will be the single most important revision you can make.

Content and Argument

Your opening question ("How should the Fourth Amendment be interpreted?") promises an argumentative essay where you will state and defend a thesis. Instead, the body of the essay spends most of its time describing *how* the prosecution and defense each construct their rhetorical appeals ("The proposition attempts to pull sympathy…", "The opposition tries to cause skepticism…"). This is valuable analytical work, but it never resolves into your own claim. Your conclusion explicitly avoids taking a side: "Whether one side's reasons seem more credible… is not the issue." If this is meant to be an argumentative essay, this sentence undercuts your entire purpose. You need a clear thesis stating your own position on whether the evidence should have been admitted, and each body paragraph should build toward defending that position—not simply summarize both sides evenhandedly.

If, instead, your assignment is a rhetorical analysis (analyzing the strategies used in news coverage), then reframe your introduction accordingly, and be more consistent in signaling that you are describing others' arguments rather than making your own. Right now sentences like "This is a definite strength in the opposition's argument" or "sounds very realistic to people still angry at the LAPD" blur the line between reporting and endorsing—readers can't tell whether these are your judgments or your description of the rhetoric's effect.

Organization

The structural logic—background, then proposition's argument, then opposition's argument, then synthesis—is sound and easy to follow. However, some content is misplaced. The narrative of the crime and search (paragraph 2) is helpful, but it appears *after* your introduction has already summarized the same events, causing repetition. Consider moving all narrative background into one place, told once.

Your conclusion should do more than restate that "both sides argue effectively." A strong conclusion should return to your thesis, explain what is at stake in the interpretation debate, and (if argumentative) reaffirm your position with a reason drawn from your analysis.

Language and Mechanics

Several issues need editing attention:

Missing quotations: You have left placeholders () where quotations should be. Every one of these must be filled in with an actual quotation and cited before submission—an essay cannot be evaluated with gaps in its evidence.
Proper names: "Ronald Goldwin" should be "Ronald Goldman," and "Los Angelas" should be "Los Angeles." Double-check all proper nouns against your sources.
Verb tense consistency: You shift between past tense (narrating 1994 events) and present tense (analyzing the arguments) somewhat inconsistently. A common convention: use past tense for historical events, present tense for analyzing texts ("Ayres argues…").
Comma splices and run-ons: eg, "Since the detectives and the Los Angeles Police Department obtained much of the evidence without a warrant, they carelessly gathered it." This is a logical leap (lacking a warrant does not automatically mean "carelessly gathered")—revise for precision.
Capitalization: "one way the opposition tries…" should begin with a capital letter.
Word choice: "ethical reasons" is used loosely to mean "reasons" or "justifications"—ethics specifically concerns moral reasoning, so make sure you mean that term precisely.

Citation

You refer to article titles and authors in-text, which is a good start, but there is no Works Cited/References list, and in-text citations lack page numbers or consistent formatting. Follow MLA or APA style consistently (ask your instructor which is required) and include a full bibliography.

Suggestions for Revision

Decide: is this an argument for your own position, or an analysis of others' rhetoric? Rewrite your thesis to match.
If argumentative, add a paragraph near the end where you evaluate the two sides and explicitly state which interpretation of "exigent circumstances" is more legally and ethically sound, and why.
Fill in all missing quotations and add a Works Cited page.
Proofread carefully for proper nouns, verb tense, and comma splices.
Tighten the conclusion so it advances your argument rather than remaining neutral.

You have strong research instincts and a clear grasp of the case details—now the task is to sharpen your authorial voice and commit to a position.

Feedback Report: "Rational Suicide" Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a difficult and sensitive topic with real intellectual ambition. Your central strategy—examining specific arguments made by named authors and testing them for evidentiary support—is a sophisticated critical move. However, the essay currently struggles with a confused thesis, repetitive structure, and self-undermining logic that weaken its persuasive power. Below are the main areas to address in revision.

Clarify Your Thesis and Purpose

Your opening paragraph is contradictory and hard to follow. The sentence "Suicide means the end of all problems (Though those problems are usually solved easily.)" is confusing—do you mean problems are *not* usually solved by suicide, or that they *could* have been solved another way? This needs to be stated directly.

More importantly, your essay's actual argument is unclear. Are you: (a) arguing that suicide can be rational, (b) arguing that opponents of suicide reason poorly, or (c) arguing that we need more evidence-based research on suicide? Your conclusion suggests (c), but most of the body reads like (b). State your thesis explicitly in the introduction—something like: *"Although opponents of suicide raise compelling points, their arguments rely on assertion rather than evidence, and this essay will examine why that weakness matters."* This will give readers a clear lens for the rest of the essay.

Address the Repetitive Structure

Nearly every paragraph follows the same pattern: (1) introduce a quotation, (2) restate what the author means, (3) conclude "but there is no evidence." This formula becomes predictable by the third repetition and makes the essay feel mechanical rather than analytical. Consider:

Varying your critique. Not every argument fails for the *same* reason. Some might fail because of weak logic (like the slippery slope argument), others because of unsubstantiated claims, others because of unclear terms. Differentiate these problems more explicitly.
Consolidating redundant content. Paragraphs 1 and 2 both discuss "selfishness"—this could be merged into a single, more developed paragraph rather than two similar ones.

Strengthen the Logic

There's a significant logical problem you should address: your own central claim ("these arguments lack evidence") is itself unsupported by evidence. You assert repeatedly that arguments are weak without evidence, but you don't show us what *sufficient* evidence would look like, nor do you cite any studies, statistics, or psychological research yourself. This makes your critique vulnerable to the same charge you level at your opponents. Strengthening this would mean either (a) citing actual research on suicide motivations, or (b) being more explicit that your essay's goal is to identify a *gap* in the existing conversation, not to resolve it yourself.

Also examine the AIDS/slippery-slope paragraph. You assert the argument "commits the fallacy of the slippery slope" and cite Hurley's definition, but you don't explain *why* this specific argument fits that definition. Walk the reader through the reasoning rather than asserting the conclusion.

Fix the Placeholder Citations

The essay contains multiple  symbols where quotations should be—these need to be filled in before submission. Additionally, when introducing sources (Rauscher, Forbes, Ingram and Ellis, Parks and Weiss), give brief credentials up front rather than as an aside ("assuming that they are two people with experience..."). This will strengthen your ethos and avoid the awkward moment where you admit you're unsure who your sources are.

Language and Sentence-Level Issues

A few recurring issues to watch for:

Logical connectors between sentences: eg, "Others discuss the grief-stricken family. They feel no pity for the person who committed suicide either." The connection between these two sentences isn't clear—who is "they"? Make transitions explicit.
Tense consistency: "if the readers analyze their own experiences with those that attempt suicide, they can view" — check subject-verb agreement and tense throughout for consistency.
Wordiness: Sentences like "There is no escape from involvement with suicide" could be tightened: "Everyone will encounter suicide in some way."
Typo: "so lution" should be "solution."

Suggested Next Steps

Rewrite the introduction with a single, clear thesis statement.
Merge the two "selfishness" paragraphs into one.
Vary your critique of each source—identify *different* weaknesses, not just "no evidence."
Fill in all citation placeholders and add source credentials.
Consider adding at least one piece of actual evidence (a study, statistic, or documented case) to strengthen your own argument and avoid the self-referential weakness noted above.
Proofread for sentence-level clarity, especially around transitions and tense.

This essay has a genuinely interesting critical angle—testing rhetoric against evidence. With a sharper thesis and more varied analysis, it will be considerably more persuasive.

The  placeholders throughout suggest missing content or citations you meant to fill in—make sure these are resolved before submission.

Theoretical Application

The Marx/conflict theory section is weaker and less clearly connected to suicide specifically. The link between "dialectics" and "suicidal families" feels asserted rather than argued—walk the reader through *how* Marx's theory explains this connection.

Priority Revisions
