You have chosen an ambitious and interesting topic, connecting an environmental issue with sociological theory. Your research shows real effort—the statistics about disease outbreaks, beach closures, and the Clean Water Act add credibility. However, the essay currently reads more like a collection of facts and theory summaries than a unified argument. Below are specific areas to strengthen in revision.

Missing Citations and Unclear Thesis

The most urgent issue is the repeated ` <R>` placeholders throughout the essay. These appear to be citation markers that were never filled in. As it stands, readers cannot tell where your definitions of Rational Choice Theory, Exchange Theory, or your statistics come from. Before anything else, insert the actual sources and citations. This is not just a formatting issue—without them, key sentences are literally incomplete (eg, "The Rational Choice Theory is ___. This relates to..."). A reader cannot follow your logic when the definition itself is missing.

Related to this, your thesis is unclear. The second paragraph states the "focus" is on water pollution's relationship to two theories, but this is a description of topic, not an argument. Ask yourself: What is your *claim*? Are you arguing that sociological theory can help solve water pollution? That companies and individuals pollute because they lack information about consequences? That policy needs to leverage psychological/social incentives? State this clearly in your introduction and return to it in your conclusion.

Organization

The essay's structure is: intro → why water matters → types of pollutants → sources of pollution → Rational Choice Theory → Exchange Theory → conclusion. The first three body paragraphs (types and sources of pollution) function as background information, but they take up significant space without connecting to your theoretical argument. Consider either:
Trimming this background substantially and moving directly into theoretical analysis, or
Explicitly linking each pollutant type/source back to *why* individuals or companies choose to pollute (setting up your later theoretical discussion).

Right now, these paragraphs feel like an encyclopedia entry rather than part of an argument.

Application of the Theories

This is your strongest opportunity for improvement. You explain what Rational Choice Theory and Exchange Theory *say*, but the application to water pollution is repetitive and somewhat circular. For example, you write several times that "individuals need to be educated/aware" without showing *how* the theory predicts this, or what evidence supports it. Ask yourself:

Rational Choice Theory: If individuals/companies act on cost-benefit calculations, what specific costs and benefits are they weighing? You mention "low-cost disposal" and "profits" as rewards for companies—this is good, but develop it further. What would change the calculation? Fines? Reputational damage? Be concrete.
Exchange Theory: You conflate "reinforcement" with general awareness-raising. Try to distinguish it more sharply from Rational Choice Theory—right now they sound almost identical in your explanation. Exchange Theory's emphasis on *habituated* behavior (not consciously weighed choices) is a good angle, but you should develop the contrast: Rational Choice = conscious calculation; Exchange Theory = learned patterns of reward through repeated interaction.

Also, your two closing anecdotes (the Milwaukee AIDS patients, the 14,000 illness statistic) are compelling, but you don't explicitly connect them back to either theory. Every piece of evidence should be doing analytical work, not just providing shock value.

Language and Style

A few recurring issues to address in editing:
Apostrophes: "its' " should be "its" (no apostrophe for possessive "its"). This appears many times.
Subject-verb agreement: "The main source of these agents are" → should be "is."
Pronoun consistency: You switch between "she," "they," and "one" when discussing a generic actor. Choose one approach (plural "they" is now widely accepted for gender-neutral reference) and use it consistently.
Word choice: "war pollution" (likely a typo for "water pollution"); "Contemporary Journal reports that" is missing the actual content of what is reported.
Several sentences would benefit from combining or splitting for clarity (eg, the long sentence beginning "Some points to consider" packs in three separate statistics with semicolons—consider a bulleted list or separate sentences for readability).

Suggested Next Steps

Fill in all citations and missing content marked by ` <R>`.
Write a clear one-sentence thesis and place it at the end of your introduction.
Cut or condense the background sections on pollutant types/sources by about half.
Expand the theoretical analysis sections, sharpening the distinction between the two theories with concrete, specific examples.
Proofread carefully for apostrophes, subject-verb agreement, and pronoun consistency.

You have good raw material here—now the task is to sharpen the argument and make sure every fact serves your central claim. I'd be happy to look at a revised draft.

Feedback on Your Marijuana Legalization Essay

Overall Impression

You have tackled an ambitious topic and gathered a genuinely impressive range of historical, medical, economic, and legal evidence. Your command of factual detail is strong, and your attempt to apply sociological theory (Structural Functionalism, Exchange Theory, and Conflict Theory) shows real intellectual ambition. However, the essay currently reads more like an annotated collection of facts than a tightly argued paper. Below are the main areas to revise.

Thesis and Organization

Your thesis paragraph lists too many "this paper will focus on" statements in a row—this repetition is both stylistically weak and structurally confusing, since it reads like a table of contents rather than an argument. Consolidate this into one or two sentences that state your central claim clearly (eg, "This paper argues that criminalizing marijuana produces more social harm than legalizing it would, a claim best understood through structural-functional, exchange, and conflict theory"). Then briefly preview your three or four main supporting areas *once*.

Each body section (medicine, prisons, industry, judges, crime, revenue) currently functions as a separate mini-essay. You need clearer transitions that show *how* these sections build on one another logically, not just juxtapose different topics. Consider grouping them under two or three larger categories (eg, "Social Costs of Criminalization" and "Economic and Medical Benefits of Legalization") so the reader can see the architecture of your argument.

Use of Sociological Theory

This is the most important area to strengthen. Right now, the theories are applied like labels stamped onto paragraphs after the evidence has already been presented ("According to the Exchange Theory, rewards outweigh costs..."). To use theory analytically, you need to *explain the mechanism* the theory describes, not just assert that costs are less than rewards. For example: What specifically does Exchange Theory predict will happen when perceived costs of a policy exceed its rewards for those enforcing it? How does that dynamic explain judicial retirement specifically, rather than being a generic conclusion you could substitute into any paragraph? Similarly, your invocation of Marx's Conflict Theory to explain class-based enforcement disparities is promising, but you should cite data (arrest/conviction rates by race or class) to substantiate the claim rather than asserting it as self-evident.

Evidence and Citation

Your paper is full of <R> placeholders where citations should be—make sure every one of these is replaced with a proper citation before submission; as it stands, none of your claims (including statistics about prison populations, drug scheduling, or the $4–24 billion crop estimate) are actually sourced for the reader. Also be careful with your use of Grinspoon and the Harvard oncologist study: you present these as settled fact, but a strong paper acknowledges these are contested claims and cites the debate around them, rather than treating one advocate's book as scientific consensus.

Counterargument and Balance

You mention "a few negative aspects are brought up by the opposition," but you never actually state what these are or seriously engage with them. A persuasive academic essay must represent the opposing side accurately and then explain why your argument still stands. For instance, what are the strongest medical or public-safety arguments against legalization? Engaging with these—even briefly—will make your conclusion far more convincing than simply reasserting your own position.

Language and Mechanics

There are several recurring issues to fix in revision:
Spelling: "cannavis sativa" → *Cannabis sativa*; "heroine" → *heroin*; "POP" → *PCP*; "ganga" → *ganja*; "mar on drugs" → *war on drugs*; "foe" → *for*; "win" → *will*.
Punctuation: watch for stray hyphens and spacing errors ("a-plant," "1600 years ago- a," "1972 the Schafer Commission").
Word choice: "keeping pot ideal" should likely be "illegal."
Sentence-level clarity: some sentences run together multiple ideas (eg, the paragraph on PHS and AIDS patients). Break these into shorter, clearer sentences.
Consistency: decide whether you are calling the drug "marijuana," "pot," or "cannabis," and use terminology consistently within each section to maintain a formal register.

Conclusion

Your conclusion largely restates earlier points rather than synthesizing them. Use this space to explicitly return to your thesis and explain, in theoretical terms, why the accumulated evidence supports it. The closing statistic about no deaths from marijuana is compelling—move it earlier as supporting evidence, and use the conclusion instead to draw together your theoretical argument.

Summary of Priorities for Revision
Sharpen and consolidate your thesis.
Reorganize body paragraphs into clearer thematic clusters.
Deepen theoretical analysis—explain mechanisms, not just labels.
Insert real citations wherever <R> appears.
Add a genuine counterargument section.
Proofread carefully for spelling and sentence clarity.

With these revisions, this essay could become a much more persuasive, cohesive piece of academic writing. You clearly have done substantial research—the next step is shaping that research into a tightly reasoned argument.

Feedback Report: Essay on Homelessness

Overall Impression

This is an ambitious essay that attempts to trace the history of homelessness in America and analyze it through two sociological lenses (conflict theory and symbolic interactionism). Your effort to synthesize historical context with theoretical frameworks shows genuine engagement with the material. However, the essay currently reads more as a descriptive survey than an argumentative essay. To strengthen it, you need a clearer thesis, tighter organization, and more critical analysis rather than summary.

Thesis and Argument

Your introduction gestures toward multiple claims: that homelessness is a moral issue, that it is growing, and that the *method* of understanding it is contested. This is diffuse. A strong argumentative essay needs one central claim that everything else supports. For example, are you arguing that conflict theory offers the best explanation for homelessness? That societal labeling perpetuates the problem? Right now, the essay does not clearly commit to a position—it explains theories rather than *arguing* for one.

Suggestion: Revise your thesis to state clearly which theoretical lens (or combination) best explains homelessness, and why this matters for solving the problem. Then use the historical section only as background evidence for this argument, not as a self-contained narrative.

Structure and Organization

The essay has three distinct sections—history, causes, and theory—but they feel loosely connected. The historical narrative (paragraphs 2–5) is long and reads like a textbook summary. Since your real argument is about *theory*, consider condensing this history significantly (perhaps to one paragraph) and using the space saved to deepen your theoretical analysis.

The conflict theory section is your strongest and most detailed part. The symbolic interactionism section is shorter and less developed—consider balancing these, or explicitly comparing them (you gesture toward this in the final paragraph, but it arrives too late and too briefly).

Missing citation: You have a bracketed placeholder  where a quotation should be. This must be filled in with the actual quotation and properly cited before submission—right now the sentence is incomplete and confusing.

Use of Evidence

You cite only one source (Fantasia and Isserman) and refer to Cooley once. For an essay drawing on sociological theory, you need more engagement with scholarly voices—both to support historical claims (eg, statistics on homelessness, deinstitutionalization, housing costs) and to substantiate your theoretical claims. Right now, many assertions ("Real estate agencies have also conspired against the poor") are stated as fact without evidence. Strengthen these with citations.

Theoretical Analysis

Your explanation of conflict theory and symbolic interactionism is generally accurate, but it stays at the level of *summary*—explaining what the theory says—rather than *analysis*—showing how it illuminates something specific about homelessness that other theories miss. Ask yourself: What does conflict theory explain that symbolic interactionism cannot, and vice versa? Your final paragraph hints at this (macro vs. micro level) but needs to be expanded into a real comparative argument, perhaps as its own developed section rather than a brief closing note.

Language and Grammar

Since English is your second language, here are patterns to watch for:

Article usage: "the deinstitutionalization of the mentally ill" is correct, but elsewhere articles are missing or misused (eg, "This form of legislation was new" — check consistency in similar constructions).
Word choice errors: "consent unrest socked endures" is unclear—likely a typo or mistranslation; revise for clarity. Similarly, "Contact theorists" should be "Conflict theorists."
Sentence fragments/run-ons: Some sentences combine too many ideas ("This proves the symbolic interactionist's theory" as a one-line paragraph closer is abrupt).
Repetition: Phrases like "without a home" and "people without shelter" recur frequently; vary your vocabulary.
Typos: "lobs" should be "jobs"; "mayor cause" should be "major cause"; "establishement" should be "establishment."

I recommend reading your essay aloud or using a grammar-checking tool (like Grammarly) for a first pass, then focusing your own revision energy on argument and structure.

Conclusion

Your conclusion introduces a potentially interesting distinction (macro vs. micro-level analysis) but does so too briefly. Expand this into a genuine synthesis: tell your reader what your analysis reveals about *how we should approach solving homelessness*, not just how theories differ.

Priority Revisions

Sharpen your thesis into one clear, arguable claim.
Cut the historical narrative by half; use it as evidence, not narrative.
Fill in the missing citation.
Deepen theoretical comparison in the conclusion.
Proofread carefully for word choice and grammar.

You have strong raw material here—now the task is to shape it into an argument rather than a survey.

Feedback on Your Essay on Welfare Reform

Overall Impression

You have clearly done substantial research, and your decision to frame the debate through two competing sociological theories—Spencer's Social Darwinism and Merton's Matthew Effect—is a sophisticated organizing strategy. However, the essay currently reads more like a well-documented report than an argumentative essay. Below are the main areas to address in revision.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your introduction ends with a good question ("Why does welfare exist?"), but you never state your own position clearly at the outset. Readers must wait until the final two paragraphs to learn where you actually stand. In an argumentative essay, state your thesis explicitly in the introduction—for example, something like: "While conservatives argue for abolition based on Social Darwinist principles, this essay argues that welfare recipients face structural barriers that justify reform, not elimination." Then use the Spencer/Merton framework as *evidence* for this thesis, not as a substitute for it.

Proportion and Structure

Nearly half the essay (the historical background on ADC, AFDC, WIN, and the Talmadge Amendments) is descriptive rather than argumentative. This material is useful context, but it should be condensed to one or two paragraphs. Right now, the reader must wait a long time before your actual argument begins. Consider cutting the historical section by 50% and moving more quickly to the analytical core of your essay (the Spencer vs. Merton debate).

Balance in Presenting the Two Theories

You present Spencer's argument with vivid, extended examples (Aletha Townsend, the Riverside recipient, statistics on job training). But when you turn to Merton, your own critical voice becomes stronger and more skeptical of welfare recipients even while ostensibly defending them (eg, "welfare recipients simply never had the advantage..."). Make sure both sides receive comparably rigorous treatment, and be careful that your language doesn't unintentionally undercut the position you ultimately want to support. Right now, the essay's rhetorical energy leans toward the conservative/Spencerian view even though your conclusion sides with reform.

Missing Citations and Quotations

There are several placeholders in the draft—<R> and —where quotations, sources, or data appear to be missing (eg, "Ann Clark ... says," "she quit school because ," "Bill Bennett stated "). These must be filled in before submission. An essay with incomplete evidence cannot be properly evaluated, and readers will notice these gaps immediately. Double-check that every claim has a complete, properly cited source.

Use of Statistics

You include many strong statistics, but often list them without analysis. For instance, after citing the Holzer study on job openings, you could explain *why* this matters for your argument (eg, does this support Merton's "lack of opportunity" thesis, or complicate it?). Try the pattern: state the statistic → explain its significance → connect it to your argument.

Language and Grammar

Since English is your second language, here are some specific patterns to watch for in revision:

Typos/word confusion: "Me United States" → "the United States"; "deferent men" → "different men"; "he no useful place" → "has no useful place."
Awkward phrasing: "exploded in to a large and seemingly uncontained program" — revise to "expanded into a large, unwieldy program."
Run-on or fragmented sentences: Some sentences with quotations are incomplete due to the placeholders, making it hard to judge grammar there—but once filled in, read them aloud to check that subject-verb agreement and sentence boundaries are correct.
Comma splices: Watch sentences like "Thus, with so many Americans receiving welfare, one must wonder, what is wrong with society?" — punctuation before direct/indirect questions needs review.
Repetition of transitional words: "Thus" begins many paragraphs. Vary your transitions (eg, "Consequently," "As a result," "In light of this,") to improve flow.

I recommend reading your essay aloud paragraph by paragraph, or using a grammar-checking tool, to catch smaller errors like these systematically.

Conclusion

Your conclusion effectively humanizes the issue by mentioning child poverty, which is a strong emotional and ethical appeal. However, tie it back more explicitly to your thesis and the Spencer/Merton framework you built throughout. Right now it introduces a slightly new argument (about children) without fully integrating it into your established structure.

Summary of Priority Revisions

Add a clear, explicit thesis statement in the introduction.
Cut the historical background section significantly.
Fill in all missing quotations/citations.
Balance your treatment of Spencer and Merton more evenly.
Add analytical commentary after statistics, not just data.
Proofread carefully for grammar and word choice.

You have excellent research and a genuinely interesting organizing idea. With tighter structure and a clearer authorial voice, this essay could be very strong.

Feedback Report on "Divorce" Essay

Overall Impression

You have clearly done substantial research on the sociology of divorce, and your command of theoretical material (symbolic interactionism, functionalism, conflict theory, exchange theory) is impressive. However, the essay currently reads more like an informative survey of sociological perspectives on divorce than an argumentative essay. To meet the assignment's goals, you need a clearer central claim and stronger analytical connections between your evidence and that claim.

Thesis and Argument

Right now, the essay's purpose is difficult to identify. The introduction presents statistics but does not state what you are arguing (eg, "Divorce should be understood as a structural, not purely personal, problem" or "Among competing explanations, symbolic interactionism best explains why divorce rates have risen"). The sentence "This leads us to say that widespread personal troubles indicate a social problem" hints at an argument (a classic sociological move—linking private troubles to public issues, echoing C. Wright Mills), but this idea is never developed or returned to.

Suggestion: Revise your introduction to state explicitly what claim you are defending. Then use each theoretical section not just to *describe* a theory, but to show *how it supports your claim*. End each section with a sentence connecting back to your thesis, rather than simply moving to "another theory" or "another change."

Organization

The essay moves logically from causes → consequences → solutions, which is a sound structure. However, the transitions between theories are repetitive and mechanical ("Another theory," "Another change," "Another significant change"). This makes the essay feel like a list rather than a developing argument.

Suggestion: Vary your transitions and, more importantly, show relationships *between* theories. For example, do symbolic interactionism and conflict theory contradict each other, or can they be combined to explain divorce more fully? A brief comparative paragraph before your conclusion would strengthen the essay considerably.

The solutions section (near the end) largely repeats the causes section without offering new insight—it just restates each theory's implications. Consider synthesizing: which solution do you find most convincing, and why? An argumentative essay should take a stance here, not simply summarize.

Use of Evidence and Citations

You cite many specific studies (Ogburn; Burgess and Locke; Spanier and Thompson), which is good practice. However, all citations appear as a placeholder "<R>" rather than proper in-text citations. Before submitting, replace these with a consistent citation format (APA or MLA, per your instructor's requirement), including page numbers where possible, and add a reference list.

Also, some evidence is asserted rather than explained. For instance, the statistic that two-thirds of children will experience parental divorce is powerful, but you don't connect it back to your argument about *why* this matters sociologically (eg, long-term consequences for children, social policy implications).

Language and Grammar

Your writing is generally clear, and your vocabulary shows good command of sociological terminology. A few recurring issues to address in revision:

Word confusion: "than" vs. "then" ("get a divorce just because it is easier then working through problems" → *than*). "fob" should be "job." "coning" should be "coming." "wilt" should be "will."
Subject-verb agreement and plural forms: "put excessive pressure on husband and wives" → *husbands and wives*.
Awkward phrasing from direct translation patterns: "One partner cannot take for granted of what to expect" → *One partner cannot take for granted what to expect from the other.* "As a couple moves forward divorce" → *moves toward divorce.*
Unclear word choice: "Piety needs to make the family unit more a part of..." — this sentence is unclear; check whether "Piety" was meant to be "Society" or another word, as it doesn't fit the context.
Comma splices and run-on sentences appear occasionally (eg, in the exchange theory paragraph). Reading your paragraphs aloud can help you catch these.

I recommend using a grammar-checking tool (like Grammarly) for a first pass, but also reading each sentence aloud slowly—many of these errors are easier to hear than to see.

Depth of Analysis

Much of the essay summarizes theory accurately but doesn't critically evaluate it. Ask yourself: Which theory offers the most convincing explanation for rising divorce rates? Are there weaknesses in any of these theories? Do they overlap or compete? Adding even brief critical commentary (2-3 sentences per section) would transform this from a well-researched summary into a genuine argument.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Write a clear, arguable thesis and refer back to it throughout.
Add critical evaluation, not just summary, of each theory.
Fix citation formatting throughout.
Proofread carefully for the grammar issues noted above.
Strengthen the conclusion by synthesizing rather than repeating.

You have strong content here—now it needs a clearer argumentative spine to hold it together. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback Report on "Running the Score Up" Essay

Overall Impression

This essay tackles an engaging and specific topic—whether college football coaches are unfairly accused of "running up the score"—and shows genuine enthusiasm and detailed knowledge of the subject. You clearly have a strong grasp of the factual context (specific games, scores, rankings). However, the essay needs significant work in three areas: thesis clarity and organization, argumentative rigor, and language accuracy. Below are specific suggestions.

Thesis and Organization

Your central claim—that coaches are not "guilty" of running up the score because they have legitimate reasons (backup players, Heisman considerations, job security, fan satisfaction)—is buried and only becomes fully clear in your conclusion. Readers need to know your position early, ideally by the end of your first paragraph.

Suggestion: State your thesis explicitly near the beginning: something like, "Although the NCAA criticizes coaches for running up the score, this practice is often justified by competitive necessity, player development, and financial pressures facing college athletic programs."

Additionally, each body paragraph introduces a *different* justification (backup players, passing plays, Heisman trophy, job security, fan interest) without transitions connecting them to your thesis or to each other. Right now the essay reads as a list of separate reasons rather than a unified argument. Consider adding topic sentences that explicitly link each paragraph back to your central claim, and use transitional phrases ("Another reason coaches are not to blame is...") to guide the reader.

Argumentation and Evidence

Your opening anecdote about Spurrier and the Florida-Carolina game is vivid, but you use it in a confusing way: you first present it as evidence that coaches *do* run up the score, then pivot to defending Spurrier. This creates a mixed message. Are you agreeing that Spurrier ran up the score but arguing it was justified? Or are you arguing he *didn't* run it up? Clarify this distinction early—there is a difference between "coaches sometimes score a lot of points" and "coaches are not blameworthy for doing so." Right now these ideas blend together.

Some of your reasoning also needs stronger logical support:

The Heisman Trophy paragraph claims coaches keep players in "for a long time" to help them win the award, but this contradicts your earlier point that coaches pull starters early to spare opponents embarrassment. Consider addressing this tension directly.
The "job security" argument is compelling but would benefit from a source or statistic (eg, how much money bowl games actually generate, or a coach's public statement) to move it from assertion to demonstrated fact.
Your point about fans enjoying blowouts is a claim that needs support—do you have any evidence (attendance figures, fan quotes, ticket sales data) that fans of *weaker* teams prefer high-scoring losses? Right now this feels speculative.

Suggestion: For a stronger essay, consider citing at least one outside source (a sports journalist's article, an NCAA statement, or a coach's interview) to lend credibility to your claims. Right now the essay relies entirely on personal reasoning and general football knowledge, which weakens its persuasive power for an academic audience.

Language and Grammar

Since English is your second language, there are recurring grammar issues worth addressing systematically:

Subject-verb agreement: "The football program is the major money maker... but only if it has a winning season" is correct, but watch for slips like "so if he gets first down one after another" (unclear referent for "he").
Missing/incorrect punctuation: Commas splices appear frequently (eg, "Florida did score with two seconds left. He wasn't done yet because..."). Review comma usage after introductory clauses and before coordinating conjunctions joining independent clauses.
Word choice/idiom: Phrases like "getting their butts beat bad," "buying merchandise to support their team," and "it all tables up to" are either too informal for academic writing or imprecise (did you mean "adds up to"?). Consider replacing casual expressions ("jerk," "butts beat") with more formal academic language appropriate for this genre.
The bracketed  placeholder in paragraph three appears to be an incomplete sentence—please revise this before submission, as it currently reads as an error.
Sentence fragments/run-ons: Some sentences (eg, "So if he gets first down one after another, what can a coach do, but pass?") are grammatically unclear. Reading your essay aloud can help you catch these.

Tone

The essay's tone shifts between casual/conversational ("who could blame him," "coach Steve was a nice guy") and analytical. For an academic argumentative essay, aim for a more consistently formal register while still allowing your voice to come through.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Write a clear, one-sentence thesis and place it early.
Reorganize body paragraphs so each explicitly supports that thesis.
Resolve the contradiction in your opening anecdote.
Add at least one outside source for credibility.
Proofread carefully for comma splices, subject-verb agreement, and informal diction.

You have real command of the subject matter and a distinctive voice—with revision, this essay can become a much more persuasive and polished piece of academic writing.

Feedback on "The Confederate Battle Flag" Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a genuinely difficult and emotionally charged topic, and I can see you have a personal stake in this issue, which gives your writing energy and conviction. However, the essay's central argument needs significant clarification, and there are recurring language issues that affect clarity. Below are my main suggestions, organized by priority.

Clarify Your Thesis and Resolve the Internal Contradiction

Your introduction states clearly that the battle flag "should not be flying" over the capitol. However, your essay's actual proposal is more complicated: you want the battle flag replaced with a *different* flag (the "real" Confederate flag). This is a much more specific and debatable claim than your thesis suggests, and readers won't know this is your actual argument until several paragraphs in.

Revise your thesis to state your full position from the start: something like, "While the Confederate battle flag should be removed from the capitol, South Carolina should replace it with an alternative symbol that honors Southern heritage without the connotations of slavery and segregation." This is a more precise and interesting argument—make it visible early.

Address the Contradiction in Your Position

There is tension between two claims you make:
Early on: you personally value the battle flag as part of "Southern tradition."
Later: "The battle flag stands for nothing but racism and hatred... for all the wrong reasons."

If the flag means something different to you personally than the meaning you later insist it *actually* has, you need to explain this shift explicitly. Otherwise, readers will feel you have reversed your position without acknowledgment. Consider adding a sentence like: "Although I once viewed the flag through the lens of family tradition, I have come to recognize that its historical use tells a different story."

Support Your Historical Claims with Evidence

Two factual claims are central to your argument but are not supported by any source:
The distinction between the "battle flag" and the "real Confederate flag" (this is actually more complicated historically—the Confederacy had multiple official flags over time, and the "battle flag" was never officially the national flag, but it also wasn't simply "for fighting" while another flag represented "a way of life." This distinction, as you present it, may not hold up to scrutiny.)
The claim that the flag was raised in 1964, "the day after the civil rights movement started" (the flag was actually raised in 1961, coinciding with the Civil War centennial, and remained after 1962; the civil rights movement itself began well before 1964).

Since this is your strongest evidence ("This is probably the single largest reason"), it must be accurate and cited. Please verify these facts and add a citation (even informally, eg, "According to [source]..."). Inaccurate historical claims will undermine your credibility with readers who know this history.

Strengthen Organization and Reduce Repetition

Several paragraphs repeat the same point in slightly different words (eg, the battle flag = hatred is stated at least three times). Instead of repeating the claim, use that space to develop *new* supporting evidence, address counterarguments, or explain reasoning more deeply. Consider this structure:
Thesis (with full position)
Explain the two flags and their historical origins
Present evidence for why the battle flag specifically represents hate (with citations)
Acknowledge and respond to the heritage argument
Propose your solution and its benefits
Conclusion

Grammar and Language Notes

A few recurring issues to review:

Verb tense consistency: You shift between past and present tense when discussing history (e are "was the flag that was decided upon" vs. "stands for"). Decide whether you are describing historical facts (past tense) or ongoing symbolic meaning (present tense), and be consistent within each idea.
Spelling/word choice: "succeeded from the union" should be "seceded from the union"—an easy mix-up, but important given your topic.
Pronoun agreement: "No matter what one believes... they should agree" — "one" is singular; consider "he or she" or rephrase to plural throughout ("people... they").
Comma splices: Several sentences join two complete ideas with only a comma (eg, "It does not represent the southern way of life, but rather..."). Review comma splice rules, or use periods/semicolons to separate independent clauses.
Redundant phrasing: "many years of racism," "years of racism," "outdated theories" — try to vary vocabulary and combine ideas efficiently.

Final Suggestion

This essay would benefit greatly from a clear, single, well-supported thesis stated early, verified historical evidence, and tighter paragraphs that each add something new to your argument. I encourage you to revise with these priorities in mind, and feel free to bring a draft to office hours—I'd be happy to discuss the historical sources with you in more detail.

Feedback Report on Argumentative Essay: "Premarital Sex"

Overall Impression

You have tackled an ambitious and controversial topic, and it's clear you did research and tried to support your claims with sources. However, the essay currently reads more as a moral pronouncement than an academic argument. To strengthen it, you need to work on the objectivity of your tone, the logical connection between evidence and claims, and sentence-level clarity. Below are specific areas to revise.

Thesis and Argumentative Stance

Your thesis claims premarital sex is "overall destructive" and lists three effects: loss of respect for women, illegitimate families, and STDs. This is a strong, debatable claim—good for an argumentative essay. However, the essay's tone often shifts from "arguing a position" to "moral condemnation" (eg, "the foundation of this nation... has crumbled," "return to those moral and Christian values"). Academic argumentation requires you to persuade readers who may not share your religious or moral framework. Consider grounding your claims more consistently in evidence (social, psychological, statistical) rather than appeals to a specific religious tradition, which will alienate readers who don't share it. If you want to make a moral argument, you can, but you should acknowledge counterarguments and explain *why* your framework should be persuasive to others.

Use of Evidence

This is the area needing the most attention:

Misinterpreted statistics: You cite that "32% of men and 39% of women have had 11 to 30 different partners" as evidence that monogamy is "rare," but this statistic alone doesn't support that conclusion—it doesn't tell us what percentage report fewer partners or lifelong monogamy. Similarly, citing that "90% of people believe family is the most important institution" seems to *contradict* your argument that family structures are collapsing; you need to explain this apparent contradiction rather than gloss over it.
Correlation vs. causation: You state that "new idea families" produce children with "higher rate of crime and lower educational prowess," but provide no citation or explanation of *why* premarital sex—rather than poverty, divorce, or other factors—causes this. This is a common logical gap (a hasty causal claim) that a careful reader will challenge.
Citation format: Your parenthetical citations (eg, "Reiss 175," "Janus 205") are inconsistent in formatting and some appear to have typos (eg, "Janus 13 5"). Follow one consistent citation style (MLA is standard for literature/humanities papers) and double-check every citation for accuracy.
Selective evidence: You rely heavily on one source (the Janus Report) for many statistics. Diversify your sources and consider more recent data, since some sources (eg, statistics from the 1990s) may be outdated for a contemporary argument.

Organization

Each paragraph does focus on a distinct sub-claim (historical change, disease, pregnancy/family, disrespect), which is a reasonable structure. However:
The essay lacks clear topic sentences that connect back to the thesis. Start each paragraph by explicitly stating which part of your thesis it supports.
The conclusion introduces a solution ("return to moral and Christian values... use reason") that is not developed anywhere in the body. If this is your proposed solution, it deserves its own paragraph with support, not a last-minute assertion.

Language and Style

Because English is your second language, here are patterns to watch for in revision:

Article and preposition errors: "the lost of respect" → "the loss of respect"; "have sex" → "having sex."
Subject-verb agreement: "there bad side affects" → "their bad side effects" (note: *affect* is usually a verb, *effect* a noun—this confusion appears often in your essay; check each instance).
Run-on and fragment sentences: Several sentences (eg, in the paragraph on emotional effects) are very long and lose grammatical control. Break these into shorter, clearer sentences.
Informal/vulgar language: Words like "bitch," "piece of ass," and "BOOM" may be quoting cultural language to illustrate a point, but in an academic essay, you should frame these more carefully (eg, "terms such as ______, which reduce women to sexual objects") rather than using them as your own diction.
Overgeneralized rhetorical claims: Phrases like "no end in sight" or "the foundation of this nation... has crumbled" are hyperbolic. Academic writing benefits from measured, evidence-based claims rather than sweeping assertions.

Suggested Next Steps

Revise your thesis to make a claim you can fully support with evidence, and consider narrowing your scope (eg, focus on one effect—STDs or family structure—rather than three).
Re-examine each statistic and ask: *does this data actually prove my claim, or does it require more explanation?*
Have a native-speaking peer or writing center tutor help you proofread for grammar and sentence structure.
Revise the conclusion to connect logically with a solution you've actually argued for in the body.

You have a genuine argument here—now it needs sharper logic, more careful evidence, and a more academically balanced tone to be persuasive to a broad readership.

Feedback on "Women in Combat" Essay

Overall Impression
Your essay tackles a genuinely controversial and complex topic, and your passion for the subject comes through clearly. You have a clear position and return to it consistently. However, the essay currently relies heavily on personal feeling and rhetorical questions rather than developed argumentation, and it would benefit from more structural discipline, evidence, and precision in language. Below are specific areas to strengthen in revision.

Thesis and Argument Development

Your central claim seems to be: women can serve important non-combat roles in the military, but combat duty represents an inappropriate application of gender equality because it disregards physical and emotional differences. This is a reasonable position to argue, but right now it is asserted more than it is proven.

A few issues to address:

Define "equal." You use "equal" in shifting ways—sometimes meaning "identical," sometimes "deserving of the same opportunities." Much of your argument would be stronger if you explicitly separated *equality of value/dignity* from *equality of physical capability*, since conflating them is what critics of your position would immediately challenge.
Address counterarguments. Right now the essay doesn't engage with the strongest opposing view—for instance, that physical standards can be tested individually rather than by sex, or that many women do meet current combat standards. Bringing in and then responding to this kind of counterargument would make your position more persuasive and academically credible.
Support claims with evidence. Statements like "the standards... are sometimes lowered" or "physical demands of combat would not be feasible" are asserted without data, examples, or sources. Even one or two concrete references (military policy documents, physical fitness studies, historical examples) would substantially strengthen your credibility.
Reduce reliance on rhetorical questions. You ask many questions ("Why is it so important...?" "What consequences might there be...?") but rarely answer them. Questions can be a useful rhetorical device, but in academic writing they should lead to an argued answer, not stand in for one.

Structure and Organization

The essay would benefit from clearer paragraph-level organization:

Duplicate text: In your second paragraph, a large chunk of text is repeated almost verbatim ("and goes into the military, her intellectual capability to enter may be the same, however..."). This appears to be an editing error—please remove the repeated portion.
Topic sentences: Each paragraph should open with a clear claim that previews its content. Currently, paragraphs shift between ideas (physical differences, emotional differences, personal feelings) somewhat loosely. Consider organizing by: (1) intellectual equality — already achieved and appropriate; (2) physical differences — why they matter for combat; (3) emotional differences — why they matter; (4) response to the "equality as progress" narrative; (5) conclusion.
Conclusion: Your final paragraph mostly restates personal feelings ("I love having feminine characteristics...") rather than synthesizing the argument or pointing to broader implications. Consider ending with a clear statement of what policy or attitude you are recommending, and why it matters for readers who may disagree.

Language and Style

Since you're writing in a non-native language, here are some patterns to review:

Loaded language: Phrases like "weaker sex" carry strong ideological connotations and may undermine your credibility with readers who don't already agree with you. Consider more neutral phrasing such as "differences in physical strength."
Sentence-level grammar: Watch for subject-verb agreement ("a woman's...capability may be the same as a males" → "a man's"; "the physical body of a female is so different" — check article/number consistency throughout).
Run-on and comma-spliced sentences: Several sentences are quite long and combine multiple independent clauses without clear connectors (eg, "This could result in a distortion and/or deterioration of proper standards for warfare routines. In an effort to create this equal environment..."). Breaking these into shorter, clearer sentences will improve readability.
Overuse of "I think/I believe/I feel": These phrases appear very frequently. In academic argumentative writing, it's often more effective to state claims directly and support them with reasoning, reserving personal voice for moments where it adds rhetorical weight (eg, your final paragraph, where personal reflection is appropriate).

Suggestions for Revision

Remove the duplicated paragraph.
Add at least one concrete example, statistic, or cited source per major claim.
Include a paragraph addressing a counterargument directly.
Revise "weaker sex" and similar phrasing for a more academic, neutral tone.
Tighten sentence structure—read the essay aloud to catch run-ons.
Strengthen the conclusion by clearly stating your recommended policy position.

With these revisions, your argument's emotional sincerity can be paired with the analytical rigor needed for a strong academic essay. Good luck with your revision!

Overall Impression

You've tackled a complex, socially significant topic and drawn on relevant sources (Farrell, Masters, Friedan, Pollitt) to support your points. This is the most important issue to address. Your introduction states that culture should maintain gender identities "but only to a certain extent," suggesting a balanced, moderate position. However, the body of your essay presents almost exclusively negative consequences of traditional gender roles (men becoming estranged from families, boys suppressing emotions, women losing their sense of self, women dying rather than appearing "unfeminine"). Your conclusion then calls for people to be "who they want... to be," which sounds like a rejection of gender roles altogether, not a moderate endorsement of them.

Revise this by:
Deciding what your actual position is. Do you believe traditional gender roles have real *value* that should be partially preserved? If so, you need at least one strong paragraph showing concrete benefits that outweigh some costs.
Alternatively, if your true position (as your evidence suggests) is that rigid gender roles are harmful and should be replaced by individual choice, revise your thesis to reflect this more accurately, rather than promising a "middle ground" you don't deliver.
Make sure your topic sentences for the "advantage" paragraphs (eg, role models for boys, close-knit families) are given equal weight and space to the "disadvantage" paragraphs, or explicitly acknowledge that you find the disadvantages more compelling.

Structure and Paragraph Development

Several paragraphs introduce an idea and then immediately pivot to a rebuttal within the same sentence (eg, "Having the typical image is advantageous… But this unfortunately puts a lot of pressure…"). This pattern makes your paragraphs feel like they are arguing against themselves rather than building an idea.

Suggestions:
Give each main idea (advantage/disadvantage) its own fully developed paragraph with a clear topic sentence, supporting evidence, and a concluding thought.
Your paragraph on "both genders should work for financial stability" appears suddenly near the end and feels disconnected from the surrounding discussion. Consider integrating this point earlier, perhaps in your discussion of economic reasons (which your thesis promises but never clearly delivers—psychological and sociological reasons are discussed at length, but the *economic* argument is thin).
The paragraph referencing Katha Pollitt is incomplete (the placeholder  suggests you meant to explain her argument but didn't). Complete this thought—what specifically does she say about the "Smurfette Principle," and how does it support your argument?

Use of Sources and Citation

You mention several authors and studies (Farrell, Masters, Friedan) but do not cite them consistently. The placeholders  and `<R>` in your draft indicate missing citations or incomplete sentences. Before submitting a final draft:
Introduce sources properly (eg, "In *Why Men Are the Way They Are*, Warren Farrell argues that…") and cite page numbers according to whatever format your course requires (MLA, APA, etc).
Make sure every borrowed idea, statistic, or quotation is cited, and that no sentences are left unfinished.

Language and Mechanics

Since English is your second language, here are recurring error patterns to watch for:

Spelling/typos: "genre" → *gender*; "femine" → *feminine*; "famimlies" → *families*; "mascularity" → *masculinity*; "abnomal" → *abnormal*; "enstranged" → *estranged*; "inspite" → *in spite*; "alot" → *a lot*. Please proofread carefully or use spell-check before submission—these errors distract readers from your ideas.
Subject-verb agreement / verb form: "without being judge as bad wives" → *judged*.
Sentence fragments: "Meaning gentle, emotional, sensitive, loving…" is a fragment; combine it with the previous sentence: "On the other hand, a woman would be feminine—gentle, emotional, sensitive, and loving, with her future plans revolving around her husband and children."

Suggested Next Steps

Clarify and align your thesis with your actual argument.
Reorganize paragraphs so advantages and disadvantages are balanced and clearly signposted.
Complete all unfinished sentences and add proper citations.
Do a careful line-by-line edit for spelling, grammar, and punctuation—reading the essay aloud can help you catch errors.

You have strong source material and a genuine, thoughtful stance—now the essay needs a clearer argumentative spine and a more careful polish. I'd be happy to discuss any of these points further in office hours.

Feedback Report

Thank you for sharing this essay on residence hall policies. Your topic is engaging and you clearly have strong personal investment in the argument, which comes through in your passionate tone. Below is feedback organized around key areas for revision.

Thesis and Structure

Your essay currently lacks a clear, focused thesis statement. The introduction meanders through several ideas (college as a "maturing station," parental oversight, resident halls specifically) before settling on your actual argument. Consider opening with a concise thesis that previews your main claims, something like: "Resident hall policies—particularly visitation rules and quiet hours—undermine students' ability to develop independence and often apply standards unevenly across different groups of residents."

Your body paragraphs do address distinct sub-topics (visitation policy, roommate contracts, discrimination in policy enforcement, quiet hours), which is good organizational logic. However, the transitions between them are abrupt. Each paragraph should open by connecting back to your thesis and close by setting up the next point.

Argument Development

Your strongest paragraph is the one addressing the discrimination against heterosexual couples versus same-sex couples—this is a specific, well-illustrated example that supports your broader claim about unfairness. Use this paragraph as a model for the others.

However, several arguments need more rigorous development:

The "rapist" paragraph raises an important point about double standards in safety messaging, but the reasoning is somewhat unclear and the claims feel asserted rather than supported. Consider: What evidence do you have that warning signs appear disproportionately in female dorms? Could there be alternative explanations (eg, statistical patterns in reported assaults) that complicate this argument? Address this complexity rather than making a purely emotional appeal.

The "walking on eggshells" and "eyes watching" imagery in your introduction is vivid but needs concrete support later in the essay. You introduce this feeling but don't fully connect it to specific policy examples until later paragraphs.

The quiet hours paragraph ends with several rhetorical questions ("What is too loud?") that feel more like venting than argumentation. Convert these into an actual argument: propose what a fairer noise policy might look like, or cite specific inconsistencies in how "loudness" is judged.

Use of Evidence

You do use one concrete example effectively (the USC visitation policy times), which strengthens your credibility. To improve the essay further, add:
Statistics on the correlation (if any) between strict housing policies and student maturity/independence
Comparisons with other universities' policies to show variation
A counterargument acknowged more fully—you mention safety concerns but dismiss them quickly. Engaging seriously with the opposing view (before refuting it) would strengthen your credibility.

Grammar and Language

As a note for revision, watch for these recurring issues:
Subject-verb agreement: "guest have," "rules... has" — check singular/plural consistency
Verb tense shifts: You move between present and past tense; aim for consistency, especially in hypothetical scenarios (the lesbian/heterosexual resident example is a good place to check this)
Word confusion: "rolls" should be "roles"; "there" should be "their" (eg, "voice there opinions")
Missing articles: "guest of the opposite sex" should be "a guest" or "guests"
Comma splices and run-on sentences: Several sentences would benefit from being split or connected with appropriate conjunctions (eg, "It was supposed to build on what your parents taught you and prepare for the real world and beyond" runs together several ideas)

I recommend reading your essay aloud, which often helps catch these patterns.

Tone and Audience

Your essay would benefit from a more consistently academic tone. Phrases like "That's ridiculous!" and "Heaven forbid" are conversational and effective for emphasis, but overusing them throughout weakens the essay's persuasive authority. Save such moments for particularly climactic points, and replace others with more measured academic language.

Additionally, direct address to the reader ("I know you think...") is a useful rhetorical technique for anticipating counterarguments, but use it more sparingly and signal it explicitly as addressing a counterargument (eg, "Some might argue that...").

Revision Priorities

Write a clear thesis statement and adjust your introduction accordingly
Strengthen transitions between paragraphs
Add supporting evidence beyond personal example, where possible
Address counterarguments more substantively
Edit for grammar issues (verb agreement, tense, word choice)
Revise rhetorical questions into developed arguments

Your ideas are compelling and your organizational instincts are sound. With revision focused on clarity, evidence, and grammatical precision, this essay has strong potential.

Feedback Report on College Football Playoff Essay

Overall Impression

This is a well-researched and passionately argued essay that demonstrates strong knowledge of college football history and a clear position on the playoff debate. You've done impressive work gathering specific case studies (1992, 1993, 1994 seasons) to support your argument, and your use of direct quotations from coaches and journalists adds credibility. The essay shows genuine engagement with the topic. However, there are several areas where revision would strengthen both the clarity and persuasiveness of your argument.

Structural Issues

Imbalanced Development
Your three case studies (1992, 1993, 1994) take up roughly 70% of the essay, while your actual proposal for a playoff system—arguably your most important original contribution—receives only two short paragraphs near the end. Consider either:
Trimming one of the three historical examples (perhaps the 1992 case, which is the least controversial) to create more space for developing your playoff proposal, or
Expanding your discussion of *how* the playoff system would work, including specifics about seeding, revenue distribution, and addressing potential objections in more depth.

Underdeveloped Counterarguments
You mention opposing views (season length, bowl tradition, revenue concerns) only briefly near the end. A stronger essay would engage more substantively with these objections *before* your conclusion, showing why your playoff system overcomes them. For instance, you claim a playoff would generate *more* revenue than bowls, but you don't explain the financial mechanics that support this claim—television contracts, ticket sales projections, etc

Grammar and Language Issues

Since English is your second language, here are patterns to watch for in revision:

Verb tense consistency: You shift between past and present tense within paragraphs (eg, "college football *should* develop" vs. narrating past events). Keep your argumentative claims in present tense and historical narration in past tense.

Article usage: Watch for missing or unnecessary articles: "there was always some debated" should be "there was always some debate" or "there were always debates."

Preposition errors: "argued *from*" should likely be "argument *by*"; "couldn't really Florida State ahead" is missing a verb (likely "rank" or "place").

Sentence fragments/run-ons: Some sentences lack clear subject-verb agreement or contain repeated phrases, eg, "obligated to going to going to the Cotton Bowl" (repeated word, likely a typo, but also awkward phrasing—consider "obligated to attend the Cotton Bowl as").

Quotation punctuation: Your quotation marks are inconsistent (mixing "11" as an OCR/typing error for opening quotes). Please proofread carefully—these formatting errors appear throughout and disrupt readability significantly.

Citation and Evidence

Your citations (eg, "(18)," "(C3)") lack full source information. For an academic essay, include a Works Cited or References page with complete bibliographic details (author, publication, date, page numbers) so readers can verify your sources. Also, introduce your quotations more smoothly—rather than just dropping in Reilly's quote, explain *why* his perspective matters to your argument.

Argumentative Clarity

Your thesis is strong: playoffs would (1) generate revenue, (2) create fan excitement, and (3) produce a true champion. However, in the body paragraphs, you focus almost entirely on point (3)—the fairness/accuracy issue. Points (1) and (2) appear only briefly at the end. To fulfill your own thesis structure, weave references to revenue and fan excitement throughout your case studies, not just in a separate paragraph.

Specific Suggestions

Add a topic sentence to each case-study paragraph that explicitly connects back to your thesis (eg, "The 1993 controversy illustrates how the current system fails to produce a *true* champion").

Strengthen your transition sentences between sections—right now, paragraphs often begin with a new date/season without explicitly linking to the previous argument.

Revise the conclusion to briefly synthesize all three case studies together, showing the *cumulative* weight of evidence, rather than just restating the final call to action.

Proofread aloud—many of your grammar issues (missing words, repeated words) are the kind that become obvious when you hear the sentence spoken.

Final Thought

You have excellent command of the subject matter and a clear, defensible thesis. Your next revision should focus on (1) balancing space between evidence and proposal, (2) tightening grammar through careful proofreading, and (3) making sure every paragraph explicitly serves your three-part thesis (revenue, excitement, fairness). With these revisions, this essay could become a genuinely persuasive piece of sports journalism/argumentation.

Feedback on Your Go-Cart Safety Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a topic where you have genuine authority—your firsthand experience working at Frankie's Fun Park gives this essay credibility that many student writers lack. Your personal anecdotes (getting hit by a go-cart, watching kids get hurt) are the strongest parts of this essay because they make abstract "safety rules" feel real and urgent. With revision, this could be a compelling piece. Below are suggestions organized by priority.

Strengthen Your Thesis and Structure

Your introduction promises "many reasons" to follow rules, but the essay reads more as a list of rules than an argument built around 2-3 clear reasons (eg, personal safety, safety of others—especially children, and protecting equipment/reducing worker risk). Consider restructuring so each body paragraph clearly maps onto one of these reasons, rather than organizing paragraph-by-paragraph around individual rules (seatbelts, brakes, bumping, etc). This will make your argument feel more purposeful and less like an employee handbook.

Suggestion: Revise your thesis to something like: "Following go-cart rules matters for three reasons: it protects you from serious injury, it protects vulnerable riders like children, and it prevents costly damage that affects everyone's experience." Then organize accordingly.

Grammar and Sentence-Level Issues

Since English is your second language, here are recurring patterns to watch for:

Subject-verb agreement: "Having all these rules are" → should be "is" (gerund phrases take singular verbs). "The seatbelts shouldn't be taking off" → "taken off."
Article usage: "the annoucements" (spelling: *announcements*); "an innocent, little kid" is fine, but check phrases like "on the track" vs. "on track" for consistency.
Preposition errors: "The bumpers are not their for people" → this should be "there." Also "hurting people's back or neck" needs clearer connection—consider "which could hurt people's backs or necks."
Run-on sentences: Many sentences are joined with commas where periods or semicolons are needed. For example: "I was even lucky that when I got hit, I wasn't seriously hurt, I just bruised my arm real bad" — this has comma splices. Try: "I was lucky; I wasn't seriously hurt, just badly bruised."
Word choice: "real bad" is colloquial—use "quite badly" or "severely" in formal writing. "A good bit of people" → "quite a few people" or "many people."

I recommend reading your essay aloud, sentence by sentence, and pausing at every comma to ask, "Could this be a new sentence?" This will help you catch comma splices.

Tone and Audience

Your opening sentence ("I don't know how to express...") undercuts your authority right away. Since you *do* know—you work there!—start with confidence. Try something like: "After a year and a half working at Frankie's Fun Park, I've seen firsthand what happens when riders ignore go-cart safety rules." This immediately establishes your credibility.

Additionally, the essay shifts between "you" (direct address to reader) and general statements about "people" or "riders." This is fine for a persuasive, informal essay, but be consistent. Decide if you're writing a direct appeal to future riders or a more general argumentative essay, and adjust pronoun use accordingly.

Paragraph Development

Some paragraphs would benefit from a clearer topic sentence stating the *rule* before you explain *why* it matters. For example, your bumping paragraph is packed with important points (children's safety, equipment damage, escalating conflicts) but feels like it's listing consequences rather than building an argument. Consider breaking this into two paragraphs: one on physical danger, one on equipment/social consequences (fights, blame).

Conclusion

Your conclusion introduces a new point (worker fatigue—"seven hours a day dodging cars") that could actually be developed earlier as part of your "protecting workers" reason. Save your conclusion for synthesizing your argument and leaving the reader with a final, memorable statement—perhaps returning to your own injury story to close the loop with your introduction.

Priority Revision Checklist

✅ Rewrite the opening line for confidence and authority.
✅ Create a clear thesis naming 2-3 main reasons.
✅ Reorganize paragraphs around those reasons (not just listing rules).
✅ Fix comma splices—read aloud and listen for natural sentence breaks.
✅ Proofread for subject-verb agreement and homophone errors (there/their).
✅ Revise conclusion to circle back to your opening anecdote.

You have real, valuable experience here—the raw material is strong. Focus your revision on shaping that material into a tighter argument and cleaning up sentence-level errors, and this will be a compelling essay.

Feedback on "The Sentencing of Susan Smith"

Thank you for sharing this essay. You have chosen an emotionally powerful topic, and your compassion for both the victims and the defendant comes through clearly. However, the essay would benefit from stronger argumentative structure, more careful use of evidence, and attention to some factual and stylistic issues. Below are my main suggestions.

Clarify and Strengthen Your Thesis

Your central claim — that the jury was right not to sentence Susan Smith to death — doesn't appear clearly until paragraph four. In an argumentative essay, your thesis should appear in the introduction so readers know from the start what you are arguing and why. Consider revising your opening to state directly: "I believe the jury's decision to sentence Susan Smith to life in prison, rather than death, was the more just outcome, for both practical and moral reasons."

Check Your Facts

You state that "the Supreme Court did not impose the death penalty," but earlier you correctly say she was "tried" and sentenced by a jury. In the U.S. legal system, a jury (not the Supreme Court) determines sentencing in cases like this. Please verify this detail, as factual accuracy is essential to your credibility as a writer.

Resolve the Logical Tension in Your Argument

There is an inconsistency in your reasoning that readers will notice. You argue that Smith's traumatic childhood, depression, and psychological instability explain her "inability to make rational choices" — yet you also say, "If she had been thinking rationally as a mother she would have given her husband custody." These two claims work against each other: if she was not rational, how could she have made a different rational choice? You need to decide whether your argument is (a) she is not fully culpable due to mental illness, or (b) she made a terrible but understandable choice for which she still bears responsibility. Right now, the essay does both, which weakens your case. Choose one line of reasoning and develop it consistently.

Separate Emotional Appeal from Logical Argument

Your description of the boys' deaths and Smith's guilt ("the two small voices... pleading and crying for help") is vivid, but in an argumentative essay, emotional appeal should support your reasoning, not replace it. Right now, your strongest logical point — that a life sentence with guilt is a harsher punishment than death — appears only briefly ("Death would have been quick, easy, and more economical") and is not developed with evidence or examples. Consider expanding this idea: What does psychological research say about long-term incarceration versus capital punishment? Is there data on deterrence, cost, or psychological suffering you could cite to support this claim more rigorously?

Reconsider the Religious Argument for an Academic Audience

The final third of your essay shifts from a legal/ethical argument to a religious one, arguing from your Christian faith that Smith can be forgiven by God. This is a sincere and understandable personal belief, but in an academic essay written for a general or mixed audience, an argument based solely on religious doctrine will not persuade readers who do not share your faith. If you want to keep this dimension, consider reframing it as one perspective among several ("From a Christian perspective, some might argue...") rather than presenting it as the essay's central justification. Alternatively, you could strengthen the secular argument (economic cost, deterrence, rehabilitation, restorative justice) as your primary case, and mention the religious dimension more briefly as a personal reflection.

Fix the Missing Citations

You have two placeholders marked  where Bible quotations should appear. Please insert the actual verses and include a proper citation (eg, Exodus 20:13, NIV). Uncited quotations undermine your credibility, even in a personal reflection.

Proofread for Spelling and Word Choice

I noticed several small errors to correct in revision: "recieve" → "receive"; "prision" → "prison" (appears twice); "endowed with logical mental coherence" is unclear — consider simpler phrasing like "her decisions were not always rational." Reading your essay aloud can help you catch these errors.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

State your thesis clearly at the start.
Verify the fact about who sentenced Smith.
Resolve the contradiction about her rationality.
Develop your strongest logical argument (guilt vs. death) with real evidence.
Reframe or balance the religious argument for a broader audience.
Fill in missing citations and proofread carefully.

You have a genuine, thoughtful voice — with revision, this essay can present a much more persuasive and coherent argument. I look forward to seeing your next draft.

Feedback Report: Argumentative Essay on Lowering the Drinking Age

Overall Impression

You've chosen a genuinely debatable topic and organized your essay around three clear reasons (crime reduction, responsibility, and health). This three-pronged structure is a strength—it gives your essay a logical skeleton. However, the essay needs significant revision in the areas of evidence/citation, logical reasoning, and sentence-level accuracy before it will be persuasive to an academic audience. Below are the main areas to focus on.

Citations and Source Credibility

This is your most urgent issue. Several of your "sources" are not legitimate academic citations:

"Rick Finley@rfinleygmail.vt.edu" and similar email addresses are not citations. If you obtained information from an email correspondence, you need to explain who this person is (their credentials/expertise) and use a proper personal communication citation format. As written, this looks like an email address you copy-pasted, which undermines your credibility.
"WWW.mis.duke.edu/..." — website URLs should not stand alone as in-text citations. Use author/organization name and date if available.
Government sources (US Department of Health and Human Services, US Department of Justice) are good choices—but you must include page numbers or specific report titles consistently, and vary *how* you introduce them (eg, "According to a 2019 report by the Department of Justice...") rather than just tacking a parenthetical on.

Action step: Go through every citation and ask: "Would my professor recognize this as a legitimate, verifiable source?" Revise or remove any that fail this test.

Logical Gaps and Unsupported Claims

Several claims need stronger reasoning or evidence:

You assert that 18-year-olds "would act responsibly" once given back their rights, but you provide no mechanism for *why* this would happen. Your own essay notes that 18-year-olds "did not act responsibly" in the past—this seems to contradict your argument rather than support it. You need to reconcile this tension explicitly.
The claim that alcohol becomes "less glamorous" if introduced earlier is asserted, not proven. Consider citing research on how other countries' drinking cultures (eg, parts of Europe) correlate with lower rates of binge drinking, rather than relying on assumption.
The counterargument is largely absent. A strong argumentative essay acknowledges the opposing view (eg, "Critics argue that lowering the drinking age increases teen traffic fatalities, as seen after the 1984 Act") and then rebuts it. Adding this will make your argument far more convincing and show critical engagement.

Action step: For each major claim, ask "How do I know this is true, and what would someone who disagrees say?"

Repetition and Padding

Many sentences restate previous points in slightly different words (eg, multiple sentences about eighteen-year-olds being "included in society" or "given back rights"). This weakens the essay's forward momentum. 

Action step: After drafting, go through and highlight sentences that repeat an idea already stated. Cut or consolidate them. This will tighten your paragraphs considerably.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

There are recurring grammar patterns to address:

Apostrophes with plurals: "eighteen-year old's," "twenty-year old's" should be "eighteen-year-olds" (no apostrophe; also needs hyphens as a compound adjective/noun).
Capitalization: Sentences beginning with lowercase letters ("on most college campuses...").
Word confusion: "effect" vs. "affect" (paragraph 6: "would effect the United States" should be "would affect"); "amoung" should be "among."
Comma splices and run-ons: Several sentences string together independent clauses without proper punctuation (eg, "Adolescents are in an awkward place in society, being between childhood and adulthood").

Action step: Since these errors are consistent and patterned (likely influenced by differences between English and Chinese grammar structures), I recommend using a grammar-checking tool (like Grammarly) *in addition to* careful proofreading, and keeping a personal log of these recurring errors so you can watch for them in future essays.

Conclusion Needs Sharpening

Your conclusion currently restates your three body paragraphs almost verbatim. A strong conclusion should synthesize your argument and leave the reader with a broader implication (eg, what this means for policy going forward, or a call to action) rather than simply summarizing.

Priority Revision Checklist

Fix or replace all questionable citations (highest priority)
Add at least one counterargument with rebuttal
Strengthen the logical connection between "past irresponsibility" and "future responsibility"
Eliminate repetitive sentences
Proofread for apostrophe, capitalization, and word-choice errors
Rewrite conclusion to synthesize rather than repeat

You have a clear, organized argument here—the revision work now is about making your evidence trustworthy and your reasoning airtight. I'd be happy to look at a revised draft.
