Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay: "Mandatory Swimming Education"

Overall Impression
You have chosen a compelling, real-world topic, and your opening narrative is genuinely effective—it draws the reader in and personalizes the stakes of your argument. Your organization follows a logical structure (problem → proposed solution → implementation → conclusion), which is a strong foundation. However, the essay needs revision in three main areas: argument development, language accuracy, and academic tone.

Strengthening Your Argument

Your central claim—that swimming should be a mandatory part of P.E.—is reasonable, but it currently relies almost entirely on assertion rather than evidence. To make this a persuasive academic essay, consider:

Adding statistics. How many children drown each year? What percentage of Americans cannot swim? Citing a source (eg, CDC data) would make your opening scenario feel less like an anecdote and more like a documented problem.
Addressing counterarguments. A skeptical reader will immediately ask: What about the cost of transportation to pools? What about students who can't swim due to disability, religious dress requirements, or fear of water? What about liability if a student drowns during a school-sponsored lesson? Right now you don't acknowledge any obstacles, which makes the essay feel one-sided. Even briefly addressing and countering one or two objections would strengthen your credibility.
Clarifying your logic in places. The line "How often have you heard of dodgeball saving someone's life?" is a clever rhetorical move, but it needs a sentence of follow-up explaining *why* this comparison matters (ie, that curriculum time should be allocated to skills with real-life value).
Revising the conclusion. Right now it merely restates that the problem is "simple" and can be "remedied quickly," but you haven't actually proven it's simple—your body paragraphs raise several logistical solutions (partnering with pools, certifying instructors), which suggests it's not simple, just *feasible*. Reframe your conclusion to acknowledge the effort required while emphasizing that the benefits outweigh the costs.

Organization

The opening narrative is engaging, but it currently reads like a short story rather than an introduction to an argumentative essay. Consider adding a clear thesis statement at the end of the introduction (or in the second paragraph) so readers know exactly what you will argue.
The paragraph beginning "There is a way..." tries to do too much at once—it introduces the school system angle, states your thesis, and criticizes the lack of conversation on this topic. Consider splitting this into two paragraphs: one introducing your proposal, and one explaining the current gap in educational policy discussions.
Some transitions between paragraphs are abrupt (eg, jumping from "four weeks" to "many school systems have pools"). Adding transitional phrases will help the essay flow more smoothly from one supporting point to the next.

Language and Mechanics

Since English is your second language, I want to flag some recurring error patterns so you can watch for them in future revisions:

Spelling errors: *drownding* → drowning; *cericulum* → curriculum; *instuctor(s)* → instructor(s); *simlpe* → simple; *remidied* → remedied; *panicing* → panicking; *wuold* → would; *evryone* → everyone; *thaech* → teach; *employent* → employment. I recommend running your essay through a spell-checker before submission, but also keep a personal list of words you frequently misspell for extra review.
Capitalization: Sentences should begin with a capital letter (eg, "your only problem now..." → "Your only problem..."). Also, "News" should not be capitalized unless referring to a specific program title.
Run-on sentences and comma splices: For example: "The school system could contract out to local pools to use their facilities for the classes the pools could extend the season..." This needs a connector or a period: "...for the classes. The pools could extend the season..."
Subject-verb agreement and article use: eg, "This is not uncommon, most of your friends do not know how to swim" should be two sentences or joined with a conjunction: "This is not uncommon; most of your friends do not know how to swim."

Tone

The narrative opening uses second-person ("you") and informal language, which works well for a hook, but the rest of the essay should shift into a more formal, third-person academic register (eg, "students," "children," or "many Americans" rather than continuing with "you"). Consider revising the body paragraphs to sound more analytical and less conversational.

Summary of Priorities for Revision
Add data/sources to support your claims.
Address at least one major counterargument.
Insert a clear thesis statement.
Proofread carefully for spelling and sentence-boundary errors.
Shift tone from narrative/conversational to academic in the body paragraphs.

You have a strong, relatable idea here—with revision, this can become a persuasive and well-supported essay. Keep working on it!

Overall Impression

Insert the actual quotations from Masters, Johnson, and Kolodny; Friedan; and Farrell.
Introduce each source properly—not just with a name and title, but with context: What is the source of this claim? Why should we trust it? (eg, "In *The Feminine Mystique*, Friedan interviewed suburban housewives who...")

Some readers might argue that gender differences have biological roots, or that "traditional" roles provide social stability. Sentence-Level and Grammar Issues

> "...something must be wrong with them <R>. As a result, they were psychologically affected..."

and

> "...if these traits were abolished or became non-distinct, multi-gender characteristics, men would be relieved..."

Check every sentence beginning after a period to ensure it starts with a capital letter and is a complete, independent sentence. Also watch for:

Comma splices: "Even though these are strictly male gender identity characteristics, these women are forced to emanate these qualities" — this is fine, but some similar constructions elsewhere run two independent clauses together with only a comma.

Typo: "in order to succeed on order to succeed" — likely a typo for "in order to succeed."

Fix capitalization and sentence-boundary errors throughout.
Add at least one counterargument-and-response paragraph.
Vary repeated phrasing ("distinct gender identity").

Feedback Report on "The Media, Society, and the Myth of Perfect Beauty"

Overall Impression

This is a passionate, well-intentioned essay that tackles an important social issue: the damaging effects of media-driven beauty standards on women's physical and mental health. Your personal investment in the topic is clear, and you use a good range of evidence (statistics on models, eating disorders, plastic surgery, and workplace discrimination). However, the essay would benefit significantly from revision in four main areas: argument focus, organization, evidence integration, and grammar/mechanics.

Content and Argument

Your central claim—that media and society impose unrealistic beauty standards that harm women—is compelling, but it gets diluted by the sheer number of sub-topics you try to cover (models' measurements, anorexia, bulimia, plastic surgery, liposuction, beauty products, dental damage, obesity discrimination). Each paragraph introduces a new problem without fully connecting it back to your thesis. Consider narrowing your focus to two or three key consequences (eg, eating disorders and cosmetic surgery) and developing them in more depth, with clearer cause-and-effect reasoning.

Also, be aware of a tension in your argument: you state that "true beauty comes from within" and reject external standards, yet you admit you "only wish [you] could be as flawless as Cindy Crawford." This honesty is relatable, but you should explicitly address this contradiction rather than letting it sit unresolved—perhaps by explaining how even people who intellectually reject these standards still feel their pull, which actually strengthens your argument about the power of media conditioning.

Organization and Structure

The essay lacks a clear roadmap. Your introduction poses an engaging question, but the thesis is buried and vague ("Society and media shouldn't persuade us as to what true beauty is"). Consider crafting a thesis statement that previews the specific harms you'll discuss.

Many paragraphs read as lists of facts rather than developed arguments. For example, the paragraph on plastic surgery, liposuction, and bogus beauty products moves rapidly through three distinct topics in a few sentences each, without transitions or analysis of *why* this matters. Try the P-E-E structure (Point–Evidence–Explanation): state a claim, support it with evidence, then explain its significance before moving on.

The conclusion repeats ideas from the introduction almost verbatim ("beauty comes from within/inside"), which feels redundant rather than reinforcing. Try to end with a forward-looking statement or call to action instead.

Evidence and Sources

You use many statistics (model measurements, percentages of anorexia/bulimia cases, income gaps for obese women), which is a strength—but none of these are cited. In academic writing, you must attribute statistics to their sources (eg, "According to the National Eating Disorders Association..."). Without citations, readers cannot verify your claims, and the essay reads as informal opinion rather than researched argument. Please find and cite the original sources for every statistic.

Language and Grammar

As you continue developing your academic English, here are recurring issues to watch for:

Spelling: "adolecense" → *adolescence*; "Cambell" → *Campbell*; "enwnel" → *enamel*; "1 10 pounds" → *110 pounds* (likely a typo).
There/Their/They're confusion: "they are doing there job" → *their job*.
Subject-verb agreement: "females... inhibit symptoms" should likely be *exhibit* (word choice) and check plural agreement throughout.
Comma splices and run-ons: Many sentences join independent clauses with only a comma (eg, "Disorders so severe that there is such a strong dissatisfaction... they interfere with living healthy" is actually a sentence fragment—it has no main verb). Read sentences aloud to check they express one complete, connected thought.
Word choice: "inhibit symptoms" should be "exhibit symptoms"; "contain no self control" should be "have no self-control" or "lack self-control."
Redundancy: Phrases like "I mean if the model... so can I (yeah right.)" are conversational and undercut the academic tone. Consider revising toward a more formal register, even while keeping your personal voice.

Suggestions for Revision

Narrow your thesis to 2–3 specific consequences of media beauty standards.
Cite all statistics using a consistent citation format (ask your instructor which style is required—APA or MLA).
Revise topic sentences so each paragraph has one clear focus, connected explicitly to your thesis.
Proofread carefully for spelling, article usage (a/the), and comma splices—reading your essay aloud can help you catch these.
Strengthen the conclusion by proposing a concrete next step (eg, media literacy education, changing advertising regulations) rather than repeating earlier points.

You clearly care about this topic, and with more focused organization and stronger source integration, this essay could be very persuasive. I'd be happy to discuss any of these points further in office hours.

Feedback Report on Argumentative Essay: Talk Shows

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an interesting and debatable topic—the value and consequences of daily talk shows. You show a clear understanding of both sides of the controversy (entertainment value vs. potential harm), and the "Jenny Jones" incident you reference (the murder case) is a compelling piece of evidence. However, the essay currently reads more as a description of talk shows than an argument about them. To strengthen this piece, you need to sharpen your thesis, improve organization, and edit carefully for grammar and mechanics.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your introduction presents two opposing views (talk shows are harmless entertainment vs. talk shows are harmful/vulgar), but it never tells the reader which position you hold. An argumentative essay needs a clear, debatable claim that you will defend throughout.

Suggestion: End your introduction with a specific thesis statement. For example: *"While talk shows provide entertainment and occasionally useful information, their exploitation of guests' personal conflicts for ratings creates real risks that outweigh their benefits."* Everything in your body paragraphs should then work to support this single claim.

Organization

Currently, the essay moves between defending talk shows and criticizing them without clear signposting. This creates confusion about your stance. Consider organizing paragraphs by function:

Paragraph 1: Introduction + thesis
Paragraph 2: Acknowledge the "entertainment/helpful information" argument (this is your counterargument)
Paragraph 3: Explain how shows manufacture conflict (host "digging" for secrets, audience cheering)
Paragraph 4: Present the murder case as evidence of real danger
Paragraph 5: Conclusion that ties back to your thesis and makes a final judgment

Right now, the counterargument (talk shows are harmless/helpful) and your rebuttal are tangled together. Separate them clearly so readers can follow your reasoning.

Development of Ideas

Some claims need more support:
You mention shows provide "helpful information about health, financial matters" — give a brief example to make this concrete.
The murder case is powerful, but you introduce it abruptly. Consider connecting it explicitly back to your thesis: *this is why the "entertainment" framing is dangerous.*
Your final paragraph raises the issue of responsibility (host vs. guest) but doesn't reach a clear conclusion. Decide: Do you think the *shows* bear more responsibility, or the *guests*? State this directly.

Sentence-Level and Grammar Issues

There are frequent errors that affect clarity. Here are the most common patterns, with examples:

Comma splices/run-ons: "Many viewers, young and old tune in..." → needs a comma after "old" and possibly restructuring.
Subject-verb agreement: "differences in opinions leads" → should be "lead." "guest panelist" → should be "guest panelists." "each of the guest" → "each of the guests."
Sentence fragments: "Is it because talk shows show a part of the world they do not understand and are not .willing to know more about?" (stray period before "willing")
Word choice/spelling: "audeinces" → audiences; "agressively" → aggressively; "fueding" → feuding; "heath" → health; "nieghbor" → neighbor; "solitude" should likely be "solace" (solitude means being alone, not comfort).
Inconsistent verb tense: You shift between present tense (general description) and past tense (the murder story) — this is appropriate, but make sure transitions are clear when you shift.
Awkward phrasing: "The idea of each show is to build up the topic to a level where tempers may flare out of control and increasing the audiences' interest" — the verb forms don't match ("build," "increasing"). Try: "...to a level where tempers may flare and audience interest increases."

Recommendation: After revising content, read your essay aloud slowly, sentence by sentence. This will help you catch missing words, run-ons, and awkward phrasing that are easy to miss when reading silently.

Conclusion

Your final paragraph raises a good ethical question but ends without a strong final statement. Restate your thesis in new words and leave the reader with a clear takeaway: what should viewers, guests, or producers *do* differently?

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Add a clear, arguable thesis statement in your introduction.
Reorganize paragraphs so each one has a clear purpose tied to your argument.
Strengthen the connection between your evidence (especially the murder case) and your central claim.
Proofread carefully for grammar, spelling, and sentence boundaries—reading aloud will help.
End with a definitive conclusion that answers the question your essay raises.

You have strong raw material here—a vivid example, awareness of nuance, and genuine engagement with the topic. With a clearer argumentative structure and careful editing, this essay can be much more persuasive. Keep working on it!

Feedback Report on "The Legal Drinking Age" Essay

Overall Impression
You have a clear personal position and three distinct supporting reasons (military service, high school graduation, and crime reduction), which shows good essay planning. However, the essay needs substantial revision in three areas: logical consistency, argument development with evidence, and grammar/sentence structure. Below are specific suggestions.

Logical Consistency — Most Urgent Issue
Your conclusion says: *"Drinking should be prohibited to kids eighteen or older."* This directly contradicts your thesis ("Eighteen should be the legal age to consume alcohol"). I believe you meant to write that drinking should not be prohibited, or that eighteen-year-olds should be permitted to drink. Please reread your conclusion carefully — a contradiction like this can make readers doubt the entire argument, even if the rest of the essay is strong. Always restate your thesis clearly and check that your final paragraph matches your opening claim.

Argument Development
Your three reasons are reasonable, but each needs deeper development:

Military argument (¶2): This is your strongest point. Consider using real statistics (eg, the number of active-duty soldiers under 21) or a specific historical example to strengthen it. Avoid the word "condemned," which suggests punishment; you likely mean "sent" or "asked."
Diploma argument (¶3): This paragraph introduces a new criterion (education, not age) that actually weakens your main argument, since your thesis is based on *age* ("eighteen"), not *diploma status*. Consider either removing this idea or explicitly explaining how it supports the age-based argument rather than replacing it.
Crime argument (¶4): Your claim that "most crime among teenagers is drinking and driving" is an overgeneralization. Try to include a source, statistic, or reasoning chain (eg, "According to [source], X% of teen arrests involve alcohol..."). Without evidence, this reads as an assumption rather than an argument.

Addressing Counterarguments
You bring up one counterargument (teens without diplomas) but don't fully resolve why your solution is fair or practical. A strong argumentative essay should anticipate at least one more objection — for example, "Some argue that eighteen-year-olds' brains are not fully developed" — and respond to it directly. This will make your argument more persuasive to skeptical readers.

Sentence Structure and Grammar
There are several recurring issues to address in revision:

Sentence fragments: "Every since the wild west, the civil war, and the drafting of the Declaration of Independance." This is not a complete sentence — it lacks a subject and verb. Consider combining it with the previous sentence: "Drinking has been a custom in the U.S. for many decades, ever since the Wild West, the Civil War, and the drafting of the Declaration of Independence."
Spelling: "Independance" → *Independence*; "dieing" → *dying*; "gonvernment" → *government*; "affects" → *effects* (when referring to results, not action).
Word confusion: "two young" → *too young*; "condemned to go to war" → reconsider word choice, as noted above.
Redundant phrasing: "should give convince the government" — this sentence needs to be rewritten for clarity: "the military should convince the government to pass a law..."
Repeated ideas/wordiness: Several sentences repeat the same point (eg, hiding drinking from parents) without adding new information. Try to combine or trim these for conciseness.

Organization
Your five-paragraph structure (intro, 3 body paragraphs, conclusion) is appropriate for this argument. To improve flow:
Add transition phrases between paragraphs ("In addition to military service, another reason is...").
End your introduction with a clearer preview of your three reasons, so readers know what to expect.
Strengthen the conclusion by not just restating the thesis, but explaining its broader significance (eg, what changes might result from this policy).

Suggested Next Steps
Revise your conclusion first to fix the contradiction.
Add at least one credible source or statistic per body paragraph.
Read your essay aloud — this often helps catch fragments and grammar errors.
Consider using a grammar-checking tool (like Grammarly) *after* you've revised the content, so you can focus on ideas first, then polish language.

Your argument has real potential — the military service point especially could be very compelling with stronger evidence. Focus your revision on clarity, consistency, and support, and this essay will be much stronger.

Merge this into the paragraph that follows and lead with *your* claim, using the source as support.

Summary of Priorities for Revision
Write a clear, explicit thesis statement.
Rework paragraph openings so your own analysis leads, not the citation.
Fix all proper nouns and standardize your citation format.
Proofread carefully for spelling and word choice.

You have a thoughtful, well-researched argument underneath these issues — with revision, this essay could be quite strong.

Overall Impression

You've chosen a genuinely debatable and interesting topic, and you clearly have opinions and observations to share. However, the essay currently reads more like a series of loosely connected thoughts than a unified argument. To strengthen this piece, focus on three main areas: sharpening your thesis and scope, improving paragraph organization, and cleaning up grammar/mechanics.

Your introduction is inconsistent about what you're actually arguing. You open by saying "action needs to be taken on drugs," then pivot to "drugs are here to stay," then narrow to "legalization of some drugs like marijuana." Decide clearly: are you arguing for marijuana legalization specifically, or decriminalization of drugs generally? State this in one clear thesis sentence at the end of your introduction, and keep the scope consistent throughout.

Organization and Paragraph Structure

Several paragraphs lack a clear topic sentence and drift between ideas. For example, the paragraph on age limits moves from marijuana legalization to underage drinking/smoking to fake IDs—three related but distinct points that need clearer transitions or separate paragraphs.

Suggested structure:

Economic/criminal justice argument (jail overcrowding, police resources, tax revenue, jobs)
Civil liberties argument (personal freedom, the quotation)
Counterargument and rebuttal (age limits, health risks)
Conclusion that synthesizes rather than just poses rhetorical questions

Right now, related ideas (jobs, keeping people "off the streets") appear twice in different paragraphs. Consolidate these.

"A majority of drugs relate to or have deadly effects" — vague; which drugs, what effects?
"Most violent crimes stimulate from selling illicit drugs" — this needs a source or statistic, not just an assertion.
The AIDS/needle paragraph is a strong, concrete point but appears suddenly with no connection to surrounding paragraphs. Consider moving it into your health-benefits section and expanding it.
Your conclusion asks several rhetorical questions instead of stating a final position. Consider ending with a clear restatement of your argument and perhaps one memorable call to action.

Use of Sources

You include a quotation (attributed to "Wibotsky") that is a strong addition, but it needs:
Correct spelling of the author's name
A signal phrase introducing who this person is (eg, "As legal scholar [Name] argues...")
A proper citation format (check whether your instructor wants MLA, APA, etc)
Correction of the quotation itself, which contains typos ("injuries us all" should likely be "injures us all"; "harm" is misspelled as "hann" nearby, and "use" should be "us")

This is your only outside source—consider adding 1–2 more to strengthen credibility (eg, statistics on prison populations, marijuana-related health studies).

Language and Grammar

Since English is your second language, here are patterns to watch for in revision:

Subject-verb agreement: "The side effects of marijuana is" → "are"; "Jails are over crowded" (correct) vs. "The government claim" → "claims"
Word choice/spelling: "lease" → "least"; "hann" → "harm"; "stimulate from" → "stem from"; "over-the-counter drug" — you likely mean over-the-counter medications misused as drugs, which is unclear as written
Comma splices/run-ons: Many sentences join independent clauses with only a comma (eg, "The legalization of marijuana among other drugs, would keep some people out of the streets" — this sentence is also incomplete/unclear as a full thought)
Article usage: Watch for missing/extra articles ("get high off of," "a great a great opportunity" — repeated word)
Pronoun-antecedent agreement: "Everyone who is using... are just trying" → subject-verb mismatch

I recommend reading your essay aloud sentence by sentence—this often catches missing words and awkward phrasing that silent reading misses.

Next Steps

Rewrite your thesis to clearly commit to a specific, consistent claim.
Reorganize paragraphs so each has one clear focus with a topic sentence.
Expand underdeveloped points (violent crime, jobs) with evidence.
Fix the citation and correct typos in the quoted material.
Proofread for subject-verb agreement and run-on sentences—reading aloud will help.

You have real arguments here—regulation, tax revenue, civil liberties, comparison to legal substances—that are worth developing further. With clearer organization and cleaner language, this essay could be quite persuasive. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback on Your Essay: "Professors and Language Proficiency"

Overall Impression

You've chosen a compelling, relatable topic and use a personal anecdote effectively to draw readers in. Your argument—that professors should demonstrate English proficiency before teaching—is clear and reasonable. However, the essay would benefit from tighter organization, more varied evidence, and careful editing of grammar and mechanics. Below are specific suggestions organized by category.

Content and Argument

Strengths: The anecdote about your friend is vivid and establishes stakes early. Your comparison between professors and store transactions ("when a person buys an item from a store...") is a nice analogy that clarifies your tuition argument.

Areas to improve:

Thesis placement. Your actual thesis ("I completely agree that before a professor should be allowed domain over a classroom...") doesn't appear until the end of paragraph 3. Consider stating your claim more directly in your introduction so readers know your argument from the start.

Single-source evidence. The entire essay relies on one anecdote. Academic arguments are stronger with multiple types of evidence—consider adding data (how many international faculty at USC, any studies on student outcomes), a brief counterargument (eg, some might argue removing linguistic diversity harms research quality or diversity), and your rebuttal to it. Right now, the essay reads as one long extension of a personal story rather than a researched argument.

Underdeveloped solution. You mention "the Board of Trustees" and a "rigorous program" as if these details are already known, but you don't explain what this program would look like (a test? interview? teaching demonstration?). Specify what evaluating English proficiency would actually involve.

Repetition. Several paragraphs repeat the same point (tuition = right to be taught) using different phrasing. Consolidate these into one strong paragraph rather than restating across three paragraphs.

Your introduction (the narrative) is long, and the actual argumentative section doesn't begin until after several paragraphs. Consider trimming the anecdote to only the most essential details and moving toward your argument sooner.
Paragraph 4 (about the Board of Trustees) introduces new information (TA requirements vs. professor requirements) that could be its own well-developed point, but it feels tacked on. Consider making this comparison a central pillar of your argument, introduced earlier.
The conclusion mostly restates points already made. Try ending with a call to action or a projection of what will happen if nothing changes.

Language and Mechanics

This is where the most revision work is needed. Some specific issues:

Inconsistent pronouns for the professor. You refer to the professor as "she" throughout the anecdote, but shift to "he" in later general statements ("a professor who speaks a different language...he has a sufficient amount of knowledge"). Institution name inconsistency. You write "USC" but later spell out "University of South Carolina." Double-check which university you mean and use consistent naming throughout (USC most commonly refers to University of Southern California, so this may confuse readers).

Stray formatting errors. There are leftover artifacts from editing/copying, such as "her.  USC," "b t U she," "who-,@ spoke," and "large 1, university." These appear to be copy-paste or OCR errors. Proofread carefully to remove these—they distract readers and suggest carelessness.

Comma splices and fragments. Several sentences are fragments or improperly joined:
   - "A couple of classes later, when the course progressed... began-to have difficulty" (fragment with a stray hyphen)
   - "Along with other students in her class, she began. To ask questions aloud" (period should be removed)
   - Review sentences ending abruptly or split incorrectly by periods where a comma or continuation is needed.

Word choice. "Squander through complicated material" should likely be "struggle through." "Domain over a classroom" is slightly awkward—consider "authority in a classroom."

Overuse of long, comma-heavy sentences. Some sentences run very long with multiple clauses (eg, the paragraph about professors' role in teaching). Breaking these into shorter, clearer sentences will improve readability.

Revision Priorities

Proofread line-by-line to eliminate stray characters and fix comma splices/fragments.
Fix pronoun and institution-name inconsistencies.
Move your thesis earlier and tighten the narrative introduction.
Add at least one counterargument and rebuttal, plus one additional piece of evidence beyond the anecdote.

Feedback on "Business is Business" Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a compelling, emotionally resonant topic, and your passion for the subject comes through clearly. The essay raises an interesting ethical question about loyalty versus profit in professional sports. However, the essay needs revision in several areas: thesis clarity, organization, evidence documentation, and language precision. Below are specific suggestions organized by category.

Thesis and Focus

Your introduction poses several questions ("Is business just business or do fans, tradition, and loyalty count?") but never commits to a clear, arguable thesis statement. Right now, readers must infer your position. Consider adding one or two sentences near the end of your introduction that state directly: *what* you are arguing (eg, "Modell's decision to relocate the Browns was an act of pure greed that betrayed the fans who had shown Cleveland's investment and loyalty") and *why* it matters. This will give your reader a roadmap and give you a standard to organize each paragraph around.

Organization

The essay currently reads as a loose collection of facts and examples rather than a structured argument. The second paragraph, for instance, lists other team relocations (Oilers, Raiders, Rams) but doesn't explain how these examples support your specific argument about Modell and Cleveland. Consider either:
Cutting this paragraph down and using it briefly as context, or
Using these other cases as evidence of a *pattern* of NFL greed, explicitly connecting them back to your thesis.

Similarly, the paragraph about Cleveland's "beautification" (Gateway Project, Rock'n Roll Hall of Fame) is interesting but its purpose isn't clear until later—readers need a topic sentence explaining *why* this information matters (ie, that it shows Cleveland's genuine investment in the city, making Modell's betrayal worse). Add clear topic sentences to each paragraph that state its main point and link it back to your central argument.

Evidence and Documentation

This is the most important area for revision. You cite specific figures ($440 million, $80 million, $200 million stadium offer, etc) and quotations (Modell, Bob Costas) but provide no sources. In academic writing, especially when making factual claims, you must cite where this information comes from (newspaper articles, interviews, press releases). Without citations, readers cannot verify your claims, and your argument loses credibility. Please add in-text citations and a works-cited list.

Also, double-check your facts before revising:
The Colts left Baltimore for Indianapolis in 1984, not 1983.
The Houston Oilers moved to Tennessee and were renamed the Tennessee Oilers (later Titans), not the "Tennessee Cherokees."
"Los Angeles Rains" should be "Rams."
The Browns' new Baltimore team became the Ravens, not the "Baltimore Browns" (Cleveland actually kept the Browns name/colors for a future team).

Factual accuracy is essential for your credibility as a writer.

Counterargument

You briefly mention that "some" blame Cleveland or tell the city to "suck it up," but you dismiss these views very quickly without fully explaining or engaging with them. Strengthen your essay by giving the counterargument a full sentence or two of explanation, then respond to it with your own evidence and reasoning. This will make your rebuttal more persuasive.

Language and Grammar

Because English is your second language, here are some recurring issues to watch for:

Apostrophes: "it's" (it is) vs. "its" (possessive) — you use "it's" incorrectly several times.
Run-on sentences and comma splices: eg, "The Rock'n Roll Hall of Fame opened this year, and now they are building..." — break these into shorter, clearer sentences.
Word choice: "abdication" should likely be "betrayal" or "abandonment." "Consciously devastated" should probably be "completely devastated." "Scream rapid" is unclear—perhaps you mean "scream rabidly" or "roar loudly"?
Spacing errors: "inwhich," "sell outs," "10years" need correction.
Informal tone: Phrases like "one finger salute" and "suck it up and take it like a man" are too casual for academic writing. Consider paraphrasing these more formally, especially if you cannot cite the original source.

I recommend reading your essay aloud, sentence by sentence, or using a grammar-checking tool, to catch these small but frequent errors.

Conclusion

The essay ends abruptly with a factual statement rather than a conclusion. Add a final paragraph that restates your thesis, summarizes your key evidence, and perhaps reflects on the broader implications (eg, what this means for fans and cities in professional sports generally).

Summary of Priorities for Revision
Write a clear, explicit thesis statement.
Add topic sentences connecting each paragraph to your argument.
Cite all factual claims and quotations.
Verify and correct factual details.
Proofread carefully for grammar and word choice.
Add a strong concluding paragraph.

You have a strong emotional argument here—with more structure and precision, it will be very persuasive. Keep up the good work!

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You've tackled a genuinely interesting and complex topic—the tension between societal expectation and personal fulfillment—and you clearly have real insight into it, drawing on both literary sources and personal observation. The essay shows ambition and thoughtful reflection. However, the argument would be considerably stronger with a sharper thesis, tighter organization, and more careful editing. Below are the main areas to focus on as you revise.

Sharpen and Sustain Your Thesis

Your opening paragraph promises an essay about people who let *the advice of others* rule their lives, at the cost of their authentic wishes. This is a compelling angle. However, once you move into the Friedan and Farrell discussion, the essay shifts into a broader (and somewhat different) argument about the difficulty of balancing career and family. These are related but not identical claims. Ask yourself: is your essay about (a) the danger of following others' advice against one's own instincts, or (b) the impossibility of "having it all" in career and family? Right now it drifts between the two.

Revision suggestion: Reframe each paragraph so it explicitly returns to the "advice vs. authentic desire" thread. For example, when discussing Ralph, spell out *whose* advice he followed and why he chose to trust it over his own dream of playing baseball. This will keep the Friedan and Farrell examples working *for* your thesis rather than becoming separate mini-essays on gender roles.

Resolve the Internal Contradiction

You state at one point that "nothing has changed" between Friedan's generation and Ralph's, but earlier and later you also suggest that *attitudes are shifting*—men and women are "realizing" that sacrificing family for career isn't worth it. These two claims conflict. Decide which position you actually hold: Is society still trapped in the same pattern, or is change underway but incomplete? A more precise formulation—eg, "the *pressure* remains constant even as more people privately resist it"—would resolve this tension and give your conclusion more force.

Fix the Missing Quotations

You've left placeholder marks () where quotations from Friedan and Farrell should appear. These need to be filled in with actual quoted material, properly cited (author, page number if required by your citation style). Direct quotations are essential here because you're making claims about what these authors *specifically* argued—readers need to see the evidence, not just your paraphrase.

Strengthen the Conclusion

Your final paragraph ("There has to be a middle road...") is too brief and general to adequately close an essay of this scope. You raise excellent counter-examples in the second-to-last paragraph (Ralph as baseball player, Ralph as social-work lawyer, the woman who delays her career)—these are your strongest analytical moments. Bring their implications more explicitly into the conclusion: what does the "middle road" actually look like, based on the alternatives you've just described? A more concrete, specific conclusion will leave a stronger final impression.

Address Counterarguments and Unsupported Generalizations

Some claims are asserted without support and could weaken your credibility—for instance, "most men helped around the house" seems to contradict the essay's broader argument that women bore disproportionate burdens. Similarly, "few men want to risk the business world's scorn" is a strong generalization. Consider softening such claims ("some men," "many men report...") or briefly acknowledging exceptions, which will make your argument more persuasive to a critical reader.

Language and Mechanics

There are a number of recurring issues worth addressing in your next draft:

Word choice/typos: "erected of them" → "expected of them"; "reouired" → "required"; "tine-in" → "tied in"; "fear women" → "few women"; "actuality" → "actually."
Comma splices and run-ons: Several long sentences (eg, the one beginning "In the process, society witnessed...") would benefit from being broken into shorter units for clarity.
Consistency of tense: You move between past and present tense somewhat unpredictably when discussing Friedan's era versus "today." Decide on a clear temporal frame for each paragraph.
Article usage: Watch for missing or extra articles (a common challenge moving between Chinese and English), eg, "the man who is dissatisfied with his life as breadwinner" — check surrounding phrases for parallel structure.

I'd strongly recommend reading your draft aloud, or having a native-speaking peer read it, specifically to catch typos and awkward phrasing that are easy to miss when you already know what you meant to say.

Summary

You have the makings of a compelling, well-researched essay. Your next draft should focus on: (1) tightening the thesis around "advice vs. authentic desire," (2) resolving the change/no-change contradiction, (3) inserting real quotations, (4) expanding your conclusion using your strongest hypothetical examples, and (5) careful proofreading. Good luck with the revision!

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a provocative and interesting topic—the idea that celebrating differences between men and women can be socially beneficial rather than discriminatory. This is a bold thesis that could generate real intellectual engagement. However, the essay currently struggles to convince readers because the central argument is unclear, the examples don't consistently support one coherent claim, and there are frequent language errors that distract from your ideas. Below are suggestions organized by priority.

Clarify and Narrow Your Thesis

Your introduction promises an argument about "enjoying differences" increasing "social well[-being]," but the essay then moves through several *very different* kinds of "difference": athletic funding disparities, sexual entertainment (strip clubs, Hooters), advertising, and physical competition in sports. These are not the same issue. Conflating "men and women have different bodies" with "it's fine to underfund women's sports" or "objectifying women in ads is not sexist" weakens your credibility, because readers will feel you're using one soft claim (biological difference exists) to justify several much more contestable claims (funding inequity is acceptable; sexualized advertising is harmless).

Suggestion: Choose one domain—for example, physical/athletic difference—and argue that thoroughly. If you want to also discuss the entertainment industry, treat it as a separate essay or clearly signal that it is a *distinct* application of your thesis, with its own reasoning, not just another item in a list.

Strengthen Your Reasoning and Address Counterarguments

Right now, the essay makes assertions without seriously grappling with opposing views:

The football/volleyball example: You call the 50/50 funding split "communism" and imply it was unfair to football because it earns more revenue. But you don't address why volleyball fans might see this as reasonable, or why Title IX-style equity policies exist. A stronger essay would explain *both sides* before defending yours.
The Hooters section asserts "this is not discrimination" but doesn't explain the legal reasoning behind the lawsuits or why courts have sometimes sided against Hooters. Engaging with the actual counterargument (eg, "some argue that customer preference cannot justify sex-based hiring under Title VII, but I would respond...") would make your argument much more persuasive.
The Barkley/Madonna comparison is intriguing but underdeveloped—you assert they are "equally" deserving of respect without really explaining *why* nudity and athletic violence should be judged by the same standard.

Suggestion: For each example, add at least one sentence acknowledging the strongest objection to your view, then respond to it. This will make your argument feel earned rather than asserted.

Improve Paragraph Structure and Transitions

Each paragraph raises a new topic (sports funding → strip clubs → global models → advertising → Hooters → athletic competition) with little connective tissue explaining how they build on each other. Consider adding a topic sentence to each paragraph that explicitly ties back to your thesis ("This same principle—that difference can be a source of celebration rather than shame—applies to the advertising industry as well..."). Also consider reordering: right now the essay reads like a list of examples rather than a developing argument.

Language and Mechanics

There are frequent errors that a careful proofreading pass will catch:

Homophone confusion: "there" vs. "their" vs. "they're" (throughout); "seen" vs. "scene"; "hear" vs. "here"; "sexes" vs. "sexist."
Spelling: "resent" → recent; "underlining" → underlying; "tearn" → team; "retumed" → returned; "warring" → wearing; "Cline" → Klein; "sells" → sales.
Subject-verb agreement and verb tense: eg, "we can allow the enjoyment... to increase social well" should be "well-being"; check consistency of present/past tense in examples.
Sentence fragments/run-ons: Some sentences string together too many clauses without clear punctuation (eg, the paragraph beginning "A problem arose when...").

Suggestion: Read your essay aloud, or use a grammar-checking tool, focusing especially on there/their/they're and subject-verb agreement, since these recur most often.

Reconsider Tone in the Conclusion

The final paragraph ("Maybe open one called Buttocks for women") is meant to be playful, but given the seriousness of topics like workplace discrimination lawsuits, it risks undermining your credibility. Consider ending with a more measured restatement of your thesis and its real-world implications.

Summary of Priorities for Revision:
Narrow and clarify your central claim.
Address counterarguments directly.
Reorganize paragraphs around a clearer logical progression.
Proofread carefully for homophones and spelling.
Adjust tone in the conclusion to match the seriousness of your topic.

With focused revision on these points, your essay's original and thought-provoking angle can come through much more clearly and persuasively.

Feedback on Your Welfare Reform Essay

Your essay tackles a complex policy debate and shows real effort to synthesize source material and take a clear position. Below is feedback organized by category to help you revise.

Strengths

You've done solid research and found relevant, specific evidence (statistics, named examples like Princess Rowell, concrete program details like Project Match). Your thesis is clear: a compromise combining elements from both parties would work better than either party's plan alone. This is a reasonable, arguable claim.

Areas for Improvement

Thesis and Structure

Your thesis lists four elements (time limits, mandatory training, funding cuts, ending the "more children" incentive), but your essay doesn't give equal, organized treatment to each. Consider adding a clearer roadmap sentence after your thesis, and make sure each body paragraph opens with a topic sentence that clearly signals which element you're addressing. Currently, paragraphs sometimes drift between topics (eg, the training paragraph shifts into a Democrat-specific incentive at the end, which belongs more naturally in a "consequences" or "implementation" section).

Analysis vs. Summary

This is the most important area to strengthen. Right now, the essay is heavily weighted toward *reporting* what sources say, with less of *your own analysis*. For nearly every quotation, ask yourself: "So what? Why does this matter for my argument?" For example, after describing Princess Rowell's story, explain explicitly how her case supports your specific policy proposal—not just that training programs "help," but why mandatory participation (rather than voluntary) is the right approach, and what her story proves about that.

Similarly, the paragraph on ending extra payments for more children states the policy and quotes sources, but doesn't fully argue why removing this incentive is fair, effective, or better than alternatives. Push your analysis one step further after each quotation.

Quotation Integration and Citation Accuracy

Several quotations are just dropped into the text without introduction (eg, "Everyone agrees..." opens the essay abruptly). Consider framing: *As Silverstein notes, "..."*
There are formatting errors with quotation marks throughout (curly/smart quotes rendering incorrectly, commas outside quotation marks, stray apostrophes like "199911" instead of "1999," and "highschool" should be two words). Please proofread quotation punctuation carefully—in American English, commas and periods go *inside* closing quotation marks.
"Sliverstein" is misspelled in two places (should be "Silverstein")—check consistency of author names throughout, including your Works Cited page.
Attribute quotations to their actual source more precisely; "Economist 33" should probably be formatted according to your assigned citation style (check if magazine articles need a title or different in-text format, since "Economist" is unusual as an author name).

Paragraph on Time Limits

This section explains the Democrat and Republican proposals but doesn't fully justify *your* choice between them or how you're synthesizing them. Since your thesis promises a *combination* of ideas, tell the reader specifically: are you adopting the Republican five-year lifetime cap? The Democrat two-year second-chance model? Both? Be explicit about which elements you're keeping and why, otherwise the "compromise" feels vague.

Conclusion

Your conclusion is a bit thin and repeats claims (eg, "welfare is part of the problem") without tying back concretely to your four-part proposal. Consider ending with a brief restatement of your specific solution and a stronger closing statement about why compromise, specifically *your* compromise, is superior to either party's plan alone.

Sentence-Level Clarity

Some sentences have grammatical issues that a native Chinese speaker often encounters with English article usage and subject-verb agreement, eg:
"there would no chance" → "there would be no chance"
"make people support themselves" → "would make people support themselves" or "would encourage people to support themselves"
"get on welfare" and "get out of the system" are informal; consider more academic phrasing like "enter the welfare system" / "leave the welfare system."

Reading your essay aloud, or having a writing center tutor read it aloud to you, can help catch these small but noticeable errors.

Priority Revisions

Strengthen your own analytical voice after each piece of evidence.
Fix quotation punctuation and citation formatting throughout.
Make the "combination" logic in your thesis more explicit in each section.
Proofread for author name spelling and grammar issues.

You have strong raw material here—now the task is to make your own argumentative voice more prominent and precise. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay: Dorm Curfews and Overnight Visitation

Thank you for sharing this essay. Your topic is timely and relatable, and your personal experiences—especially the story about your cousin visiting before he deployed to Germany—give the essay real emotional weight. That said, the essay needs significant revision in several areas: thesis clarity, organization, logical consistency, and grammar/mechanics. Below are specific suggestions.

Sharpen Your Thesis and Central Argument

Right now, your position shifts throughout the essay. You begin by arguing that overnight visitation should be extended to all dorms since some already allow it. Later, you argue curfews should be eliminated entirely ("we shouldn't have one"). Then, in your conclusion, you soften this to "we could at least have overnight visitation on weekends." These are three different claims, and readers will be confused about what you are actually asking for.

Revision suggestion: Choose one clear, specific position and state it early—ideally in your introduction. For example: "Universities should extend overnight visitation privileges to all dormitories, not just designated ones, because current policies are inconsistent, unsafe, and infantilizing to adult students." Then make sure every paragraph supports that single claim.

Strengthen Organization and Paragraph Structure

Each paragraph currently mixes multiple ideas without clear topic sentences or transitions. For instance, your third paragraph moves from a general claim about maturity, to a story about a male classmate helping with homework, to a complaint about noisy dorm-mates. The connection between these ideas is left for the reader to infer.

Revision suggestion: Structure body paragraphs using this pattern: (1) topic sentence stating the specific reason supporting your thesis, (2) evidence or example, (3) analysis explaining how the example supports your point, (4) transition to the next idea. For example, your homecoming story is a strong emotional example—dedicate a full paragraph to it, and explicitly explain *why* this situation shows the current policy is unreasonable (eg, military deployment, limited/rare family visits) before moving to your next point (safety).

Address the Counterargument

You mention safety as a reason to abolish curfews (walking alone at night is dangerous) but curfews often exist *because* of safety concerns. This creates a contradiction you don't resolve. Similarly, you don't address why administrators might impose curfews (eg, protecting younger students, preventing disruptions, liability concerns).

Revision suggestion: Acknowledge the opposing view directly—for example, "Some administrators argue curfews protect students, particularly younger residents, from potential harm." Then explain why your proposal (or a modified curfew/sign-in system) still addresses those safety concerns better than the current policy. This will make your argument more persuasive and show critical thinking.

Use Evidence More Systematically

Your anecdotes (your friend at Bates House, the loud dorm lobby, your cousin's visit) are compelling, but they function more as isolated complaints than structured evidence. Consider adding:
Statistics or policies from other universities that allow flexible overnight visitation successfully.
A logical explanation of how a supervised sign-in system (like the one your friend uses) could work safely and formally, rather than "sneaking around."

This will move your essay from personal frustration toward a persuasive policy argument.

Grammar, Word Choice, and Proofreading

There are numerous errors that affect clarity and credibility. Please proofread carefully for:
Homophone/typo errors: "he" should be "be" (paragraph 1); "A" should be "I" ("A could, co-ed"); "bad" should be "had"; "sigu.her" is unclear—please rewrite this sentence.
Sentence fragments and run-ons: Many sentences lack clear subject-verb agreement or are fused without proper punctuation (eg, "Just because a dorm is co-ed doesn't mean overnight visitation is allowed. They still have a 2 a.m. curfew." — consider combining with "even though" or "since").
Capitalization/punctuation inconsistencies: eg, "In college now," "Into our own hands."
Informal diction: Phrases like "chill out" are too casual for academic argumentative writing. Consider "spend time" or "relax."

I recommend reading your essay aloud slowly, sentence by sentence, to catch these errors, or using a grammar-checking tool as a first pass before a final human proofread.

Conclusion

Your conclusion introduces a new, softer proposal (weekend visitation) that isn't developed earlier. If this is your real recommended compromise, build toward it throughout the essay rather than introducing it at the end.

Overall

You have a genuine, personal stake in this issue, and that passion comes through. Your next draft should focus on: (1) picking one consistent, specific claim, (2) organizing paragraphs around clear reasons with topic sentences, (3) addressing counterarguments, and (4) careful proofreading. I'd be happy to review a revised draft once you've reworked the structure.

Feedback on "Teachers Deserve More Recognition"

Overall Impression

This essay comes from a place of genuine feeling—your appreciation for your teachers is evident and sincere, and the personal anecdotes (the teacher who stayed after school, the elephant pins) give the essay warmth and authenticity. However, the essay currently reads more like a heartfelt reflection than a tightly argued essay. To strengthen it, I'd encourage you to focus on three areas: narrowing your thesis, organizing your paragraphs around single ideas, and tightening your sentence-level grammar.

Thesis and Scope

Your introduction promises an argument about recognition and reward for teachers, but the essay gradually expands to include pay and comparisons to doctors. Each of these is actually a separate, complex argument that deserves its own development. Right now, the essay tries to do three things at once, and as a result none of them is fully persuasive.

Suggestion: Choose one central claim. If you want to talk about pay and respect too, frame them as *sub-points* under the umbrella of "recognition"—eg, "Recognition can take three forms: verbal appreciation, material reward, and fair compensation." This way, the essay feels unified rather than like three different essays stitched together.

Paragraph Organization

Many paragraphs contain multiple unrelated ideas. For example, the paragraph beginning "Teachers should be appreciated because they make a difference..." moves from personal anecdote, to teachers being like mothers, to a general claim about admiration—three ideas without clear transitions.

Suggestion: Give each paragraph one clear topic sentence and stick to elaborating that single point. Ask yourself before each paragraph: "What is the ONE claim I am proving here, and what evidence supports it?"

Evidence and Development

Your strongest evidence is personal experience, which is valuable, but the essay relies on it *exclusively*. Claims like "high school teachers deserve the most recognition because students in high school seem to be the hardest to deal with" and "society should respect teachers just as much as they do doctors" are asserted but not really argued—we need reasoning or evidence for *why* these are true, not just repetition of the claim.

Suggestion: For each major claim, add at least one piece of outside support—a statistic on teacher retention, a quotation about teacher workload, or even a hypothetical scenario reasoned out logically. This will make the essay feel more persuasive to a reader who doesn't already share your view.

Repetition

The idea "teachers deserve reward/recognition" is restated many times (introduction, paragraph 3, paragraph 5, conclusion) without adding new insight each time. Similarly, the doctor comparison paragraph mostly restates the claim rather than developing it.

Suggestion: After you state a claim once clearly, move forward—add a *new* reason, a *new* example, or a *counterargument* you address. Avoid simply repeating the same sentence in different words.

Sentence-Level Language Issues

There are several recurring grammar patterns to watch for:

Fragments: "A material gift or a kind word of expression." This is not a complete sentence—consider combining it with the previous sentence: "The reward can be big or small, whether a material gift or a kind word of expression."
Run-ons/comma splices: "These teachers really care and.try to work with their students" (also a stray period/typo here).
Pronoun agreement: "if it weren't for teachers, some of us would not have the education that has gotten us the good jobs and degrees that we have" — this is fine, but check earlier: "help him with something unclear" should probably be "him or her" for consistency, since you use "they" elsewhere.
Word choice/idiom: "deserve their flowers while they live" is a nice image but is very colloquial (likely from AAVE idiom); in an academic essay, you may want a more formal equivalent like "deserve recognition while they can still appreciate it."
Missing words: "it teachers that have helped made these figures" should be "it is teachers who have helped make these figures."

Suggestion: After revising content, do a careful line-by-line edit. Reading your essay aloud can help you catch fragments and missing words.

Conclusion

Your conclusion returns nicely to the personal note of gratitude, which is a good rhetorical move. But consider ending with a call to action—what exactly should the reader *do* differently after finishing your essay? This will give the ending more persuasive force.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Narrow your thesis to one main claim.
Reorganize paragraphs around single ideas with clear topic sentences.
Add outside evidence, not just personal anecdote.
Eliminate repetitive restatements of your main claim.
Proofread carefully for fragments, run-ons, and missing words.

You have real passion and a clear personal stake in this topic—now the goal is to channel that passion into a more disciplined, evidence-based argument. I look forward to seeing your revision!

Feedback on "Violence on Television" Essay

Thank you for tackling this timely and complex topic. Television violence and its regulation involve legal, social, and psychological dimensions, and you've gathered relevant source material. However, the essay currently reads more like a collection of facts and quotations than a unified argument. Below are suggestions organized by priority.

Clarify Your Thesis and Argumentative Stance

This is the most important issue. Your introduction states that "some people disagree with its effects on children," which is vague and doesn't tell readers what *you* think. Then your body paragraphs describe legislative proposals (V-chip, Violence Report Card) somewhat neutrally, but your conclusion suddenly argues that violence "adds texture" to shows and that children will "have to deal with it" eventually—suggesting you may be against strict regulation. This shift is confusing because your opening paragraph frames violence as "a serious concern," which sounds like the opposite position.

Revision suggestion: Decide clearly whether you are arguing (a) that television violence is harmful and regulation is justified, (b) that current regulatory efforts are excessive or unnecessary, or (c) something more nuanced (eg, regulation is needed but current proposals are flawed). State this position explicitly in your introduction, and make sure every paragraph supports it.

Improve Paragraph Organization and Topic Sentences

Right now, paragraphs are organized by source rather than by idea. For example, paragraph 2 summarizes the V-chip and Senate Committee, paragraph 3 lists five proposals, and paragraph 4 shifts to a claim about adult appeal—without explaining how these ideas connect. Each paragraph should open with a topic sentence that states the point you're making, followed by evidence that supports it, and closing with an explanation of why that evidence matters to your argument.

Example revision: Instead of "The V-chip is an electrical device that blocks out violent television shows," you might write: "Despite the introduction of the V-chip, many lawmakers argue that voluntary technological solutions are insufficient to curb television violence." This immediately signals the paragraph's purpose.

Analyze Your Sources, Don't Just Report Them

You cite Hepburn, Stern, and Jensen accurately, but you rarely explain *why* their findings matter or how they support your argument. For instance, the statistic about 7¼ hours of daily viewing is powerful, but you don't connect it back to your thesis—does this number suggest children are especially vulnerable? Does it undermine parental oversight? Similarly, when you mention the NAB's criticism of "federal censorship bureaucracy," you don't evaluate whether this criticism is valid. Readers want your voice, not just quotations.

Revision suggestion: After each citation, add a sentence like "This suggests..." or "This is significant because..." to make your reasoning visible.

Resolve Logical Inconsistencies

Some claims contradict each other or lack support:
You state violence "can sometimes cause the child to become violent," but later argue violence merely "adds texture" and is harmless in the long run. Which do you believe?
You ask "how many parents actually watch television with their teenager?" implying parental neglect is the real problem—but this idea is never developed. Is your argument really about parental responsibility rather than television content itself?
The line "that is strictly up to the viewer to decide" (referring to cartoons) seems to undercut your entire premise that children are influenced by what they watch.

Consider whether parental responsibility, rather than government regulation, is actually your central argument. If so, restructure the essay around that claim.

Language and Mechanics

A few specific corrections:

Typo: "1995-1995 season" should likely be "1995–1996 season."
Subject-verb agreement: "if Congress had some support" — clarify what kind of support and from whom.
Word choice: "Some television shows show violence" — avoid repeating "show" in the same sentence; try "Some television programs depict violence because..."
Punctuation: "'sinister combat violence' pervades" needs the closing quotation mark placed correctly (you have it before "pervades," which is correct, but check consistency elsewhere, eg, "sinister combat violence' pervades" is missing an opening quotation mark).
Vague reference: "that is up to whomever is writing the television show" — "whomever" should be "whoever" (subject of the clause).
Wordiness: "The cause for television violence is to add texture" — consider "One reason producers include violence is to add dramatic texture."

Strengthen Your Conclusion

Right now, your conclusion introduces a new argument (that violence is inevitable and children must learn to cope with it) rather than synthesizing your essay's main points. A strong conclusion should return to your thesis, remind readers of your key evidence, and end with a clear takeaway—ideally a recommendation or call to action that follows logically from your argument.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Choose and clearly state a specific argumentative position.
Reorganize paragraphs around ideas, not sources.
Add analysis after every citation.
Eliminate contradictions between paragraphs.
Proofread for the grammar issues noted above.

You have good raw material here—now it needs a clear argumentative spine to hold it together. I'd be happy to discuss your revised thesis in office hours.

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

You've tackled a genuinely interesting and debatable topic, and your essay reveals real enthusiasm for the arts (the personal touch about painting/drawing is a nice moment). You've also organized your argument thematically, which shows good planning. However, the essay currently reads more like a loosely connected series of appreciative observations about art than a tightly argued case for *government funding*. Revision should focus on sharpening your central claim, strengthening your evidence, and cutting or reworking material that doesn't directly serve your argument.

Major Issues to Address

Keep your focus on the actual argument.
Your thesis is about *government funding* for the arts, but many paragraphs (anthropology, cave drawings, children's drawings, Louis XIV/Napoleon) discuss why art is valuable *in general*, not why government money specifically is necessary. A reader could agree entirely that art matters to human civilization while still asking, "So why can't private funding, ticket sales, or individual patrons cover this?" You need to explicitly bridge the gap between "art is valuable" and "therefore government should pay for it." Consider addressing counterarguments directly: Why is government support specifically necessary, rather than just desirable?

Strengthen and specify your evidence.
Several claims are asserted without support:
"As psychologists and psychoanalysts have told us for years..."
"As researchers have found..."
"Continual research has been done linking..."

These vague appeals to authority weaken your credibility. Since this is an argumentative essay, even brief, specific examples (a named study, statistic, or program) would carry far more persuasive weight than generalized claims. For instance, your point about prison art programs is promising—can you name a specific program or cite a specific outcome (recidivism rates, etc)?

Reconsider the pornography/art distinction paragraph.
This paragraph introduces a complex debate (what counts as "art") that isn't necessary for your argument and actually risks distracting from your main point or opening you up to challenges you don't have room to address. Unless you plan to connect this directly to funding decisions (eg, "government funding requires content guidelines"), consider cutting this paragraph or significantly shortening it.

The financial benefits paragraph needs development.
This is actually one of your strongest potential arguments (tourism, jobs, economic multiplier effects), but it's underdeveloped compared to the more abstract paragraphs on human communication. Since economic arguments are often persuasive to policymakers and skeptical readers, consider expanding this section with a concrete example (a specific museum, city, or economic study) and moving it earlier or giving it more prominence.

Conclusion is vague and repeats generalities rather than synthesizing your argument.
Phrases like "our human needs may dictate" and "should understand the interaction and basic needs" are abstract and non-committal for a concluding paragraph. Restate your specific claim (government funding is necessary/justified) and briefly remind the reader of your two or three strongest reasons.

Language and Style Notes

Word choice/register: The opening metaphor ("looms on the horizon like an ominous storm cloud") is vivid but slightly overwrought for academic writing; consider a more measured opening.
Spelling: "pschosanalysts" → "psychoanalysts"; "Necessarily" (paragraph on prisons) doesn't fit—perhaps you meant "Notably" or "Interestingly."
Comma splices/run-ons: Several sentences string together clauses with commas where a period or semicolon would clarify meaning (eg, the sentence beginning "Certainly, large museums...").
Consistency of pronouns: You shift between "he," "his," "himself" for general humanity (eg, "man... his world... himself"). Consider using more gender-neutral phrasing ("humans," "their world") for a more contemporary academic tone.
Transitions: Words like "Similarly," "However," and "Following from the idea of sharing" sometimes don't accurately signal the logical relationship between paragraphs. Check that each transition reflects the real logical connection (contrast, addition, cause-effect).

Suggested Revision Priorities

Revise your thesis/introduction to state clearly *why government* (not just society generally) should fund the arts.
Add a paragraph directly addressing a counterargument (eg, "Isn't private funding enough?").
Cut or drastically shorten the art-vs-pornography section.
Add concrete, specific evidence (names, numbers, studies) throughout, especially in the "productive/preventive" sections.
Rewrite the conclusion to synthesize rather than restate abstractly.

Your instinct for organizing by theme (anthropological, social, economic, therapeutic value) is sound—now make sure each theme circles back explicitly to the funding question. I'd be happy to discuss any of these points further before your next draft.

Feedback on "Should There Be a Salary Cap on Professional Football Players?"

Overall Impression

You clearly feel strongly about this topic, and that passion comes through in your writing. However, the essay would benefit from tighter organization, stronger evidence, and more careful reasoning to make your argument persuasive to a skeptical academic reader. Below are the main areas to focus on as you revise.

Structure and Organization

Your introduction promises three reasons for a salary cap: (1) players earn more than college graduates, (2) teams should spend money on facilities, (3) a cap would stop players from negotiating pay increases. This gives your reader a roadmap. However, the body paragraphs don't follow this roadmap closely. Instead, new topics appear that were never introduced—charity organizations, greediness, the O.J. Simpson trial, and player strikes. A reader following your outline will feel lost.

Revision suggestion: Either revise your introduction to preview *all* the points you actually discuss, or cut the tangential material (charity donations, O.J. Simpson, strikes) and stay focused on your three original reasons. A tighter essay with fewer, well-developed points will be more convincing than one that raises many ideas briefly.

Argumentation and Evidence

This is the area needing the most attention.

False dichotomy: You frame the issue as "doctors vs. football players"—as if we must choose one profession as more valuable than the other. This is a rhetorical strategy, but it's logically weak: society values (and pays) different professions differently for different reasons (market demand, entertainment value, training length, risk, etc). A stronger essay would acknowledge this complexity rather than implying football players are worthless ("does nothing but entertain," "doesn't do anything that is considered rewarding").

Unsupported claims: Several statements need evidence:
   - "Money and power didn't get involved until the 1950s" — where does this come from?
   - "The whole reason he was let off was because he was a popular football player" (about O.J. Simpson) — this is a serious legal claim that requires supporting evidence, not assertion. As written, it also doesn't logically support your salary-cap argument; it's a separate issue about celebrity culture.
   - "A person could not be smart enough to pass the second grade, but as long as he can play ball he is going to be rich" — this is an unfair generalization and weakens your credibility rather than strengthening your point.

Anecdote as evidence: The story about the broken seat and the restroom is a nice personal touch, but one anecdote doesn't prove that NFL revenue isn't being spent on facilities league-wide. Consider citing actual figures (team revenue, stadium spending, player salaries) to make this argument more persuasive.

Revision suggestion: For each major claim, ask "How do I know this?" and look for a statistic, expert opinion, or documented example rather than a personal impression or emotional appeal.

Paragraph-Level Coherence

Some paragraphs combine too many ideas. The long paragraph beginning "Is the college graduate the honored person these days?" moves through doctor/football comparisons, fairness, intelligence, charity, and greed—all in one paragraph. Break this into smaller paragraphs, each with a clear topic sentence and a focused point.

Similarly, the paragraph on player negotiations (Deion Sanders/Andre Rison) makes a good, concrete point but ends with a comparison ("this would be like me making more money than a friend") that could be developed further to strengthen the analogy.

Conclusion

Your conclusion introduces a new, unsupported historical claim (money entering football in the 1950s) rather than synthesizing your argument. Save new claims for the body; use the conclusion to reinforce your strongest points and restate your thesis with fresh wording.

Language and Mechanics

A few recurring issues to proofread for:
Word confusion: "affective" → *effective*; "wee" → *we*; "ant" → *any*
Subject-verb agreement: "the money that they make could be used for good causes" is fine, but watch constructions like "there are many things that the money could be used for"—consider revising for conciseness.
Sentence fragments/run-ons: Several short questions ("Why do they do this? The one reason why they go on strike is because of the money.") could be combined for smoother flow.
Repetition: Phrases like "why should Deion Sanders make more money" appear more than once; vary your sentence structures.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Align body paragraphs with your stated thesis reasons; cut or clearly integrate tangents.
Replace generalizations and emotional claims with researched evidence (salary data, stadium revenue reports, league policies).
Avoid oversimplified comparisons (doctors vs. athletes) and unsupported legal/historical claims.
Break up long paragraphs; give each one a single clear focus.
Proofread for word choice and grammar.

With more evidence and tighter organization, your genuine passion for this topic can become a much more persuasive academic argument. Good luck with your revision!
