Feedback Report: Gun Control Essay

Overall Impression

This essay tackles a complex, debated topic with genuine effort and a clear position: that gun regulation is both constitutional and necessary. You've done substantial research and cite multiple sources. However, the essay currently reads more like an information report than a persuasive argument. Below are specific areas to strengthen in your revision.

Structure and Organization

The biggest issue: Your first paragraph tries to make the *entire* essay's argument in miniature—covering crime, waiting periods, and constitutionality all at once. Then paragraphs 2 and 3 return to explain the constitutional argument again, and later paragraphs revisit statistics and regulation. This creates repetition rather than development.

Suggestion: Restructure so your introduction states your thesis in 2-3 sentences and previews your main points, *without* fully arguing them yet. Then devote one paragraph to each main point (constitutionality, statistics, existing regulations, proposed solutions) and build on it *once*, rather than circling back.

Topic sentences: Several paragraphs lack a clear opening sentence that tells the reader what the paragraph will argue (eg, the paragraph beginning "Firearms are used to murder nearly 12,000 people..."). Add a sentence that frames the data before presenting it.

Argumentation and Critical Analysis

You include strong evidence, but you often present it and move on without analyzing *why* it matters or how it supports your claim.

The paragraph on statistical persuasiveness (citing Cozic 198) makes an excellent point—that pro-control evidence is more verifiable than pro-gun claims. This is one of your strongest analytical moves. Expand it! Explain *why* "corpses and shattered limbs" are more verifiable than deterrence claims, and connect it explicitly back to your thesis.
The final paragraph shifts to abstract, emotional language ("Founding Fathers foresaw a great nation of peace and tranquility") that feels disconnected from the evidence-based tone of the rest of the essay. A strong conclusion should synthesize your strongest evidence and restate your thesis with new insight, not introduce a new rhetorical appeal.
You acknowledge the opposing view (pro-gun activists) in a few places, which is good practice. However, you could strengthen your rebuttals by explaining more precisely *why* the counterargument fails, rather than simply stating that it "lacks statistical verification."

Use of Sources and Citation

You clearly did the research, but citation mechanics need cleanup:

Inconsistent author name spelling: You cite "Cozic" throughout but also write "Cosec" and "Cosec 198-9" in a few places. Double-check every citation against your source list.
Garbled text: The sentence beginning "lnl981,JimBrady,W'hiteHousePresssecretary..." appears to be a formatting/OCR error. It needs to be rewritten as a proper sentence (eg, "In 1981, Jim Brady, White House Press Secretary, was shot...").
Over-reliance on quotation: Several paragraphs are built mostly from quoted or closely paraphrased material (eg, the Cozic citations). Try converting more of this into your own synthesis, using quotations only for particularly powerful or authoritative language.
Citation format: Confirm which citation style your instructor requires (MLA seems intended) and apply it consistently—author name only, no "The" unless part of formal title (eg, "CUF" vs. "The Close Up Foundation").

Language and Mechanics

Because English is your second language, a few recurring error patterns are worth flagging for careful proofreading:

Spelling: "definatly" → definitely; "riffles" → rifles; "licencing" → licensing (American spelling, since your essay otherwise uses American conventions); "sawed of shotguns" → sawed-off shotguns; "ant-gun" → anti-gun.
Capitalization: "Gun Control," "Firearms," and "Guns" are sometimes capitalized mid-sentence without grammatical reason. Reserve capitals for proper nouns (Second Amendment, National Rifle Association) and sentence starts.
Run-on/missing punctuation: Some sentences need commas for clarity (eg, "Instead of rushing out to obtain a gun while consumed with anger or depression, they would have time to rethink their intentions" — this one is fine, but nearby sentences merge clauses awkwardly).
Word choice: "prevent identification" likely should be "present identification."

I'd strongly recommend reading your essay aloud during revision—this often catches missing words, awkward phrasing, and run-ons that are hard to spot silently.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Reorganize to eliminate repeated arguments; give each point one clear "home."
Deepen analysis—don't just present evidence, explain its significance.
Fix citation consistency (author name, format).
Proofread carefully for spelling, capitalization, and the garbled sentence.
Rewrite the conclusion to synthesize evidence rather than shift to abstract appeal.

You have solid research and a defensible thesis—the next step is tightening the argumentative structure so your evidence does more persuasive work. Good progress so far!

Feedback Report on Abortion Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You have chosen a genuinely difficult and controversial topic, and it is clear you feel strongly about it. Your willingness to share a personal family story shows courage. However, the essay currently has significant issues with argumentative structure, logical consistency, tone, and language precision that undermine your credibility with readers. Below I offer feedback organized by category, with specific suggestions for revision.

Thesis and Logical Consistency

Your central claim shifts throughout the essay in ways that confuse readers. You open by saying abortion should be "regulated," then argue it should essentially be illegal ("no excuse for wanting an abortion"), then carve out exceptions (rape, health risk), then conclude with a story that seems to argue *against* forced pregnancy continuation for a minor. 

Suggestion: Decide on one clear, consistent thesis. If your position is "abortion should be illegal except in cases of rape or life-threatening health risk," state this clearly in your introduction and make sure every paragraph supports it. Currently, your cousin's story actually *undermines* your argument—she was forced by her mother, not choosing freely, and the essay doesn't clearly explain why this proves abortion should be "regulated by government" rather than showing the danger of *someone else* controlling a woman's reproductive choice.

Tone and Word Choice

Some phrasing will alienate readers regardless of their position on abortion:

"if a person decides to play the game" and "horseplay" trivialize sex and pregnancy, which conflicts with the seriousness of your topic.
"kill it" (referring to a fetus) and calling women who have abortions after consensual sex "murderers" who "should be put into jail" is an extremely harsh position stated without adequate legal or ethical support. If you truly hold this view, it needs careful, respectful argumentation—not casual phrasing.
The rape scenario description ("emits his vicious sperm into her system," "bottle ripped from a baby's mouth") uses graphic, sensationalized language that distracts from your point rather than strengthening it. Academic argument benefits from measured, precise language even when discussing emotional topics.

Suggestion: Revise for a more formal, respectful register throughout. Ask yourself: "Would this phrasing convince someone who disagrees with me, or would it make them dismiss my argument?"

Use of Evidence

Your statistics on contraceptive effectiveness are a good start, but they need citations. Where did these numbers come from? Unsupported statistics weaken academic credibility. Additionally, this section, while informative, doesn't fully connect back to your thesis—are you arguing contraceptive availability eliminates the *need* for abortion? Make this logical link explicit.

Your personal anecdote about your cousin is powerful, but it is used inconsistently. You use it to argue for stricter government control of abortion, yet the story is really about a mother making a unilateral decision *for* her daughter—this seems more like an argument for protecting a young woman's *own* choice, not restricting it. Consider what this story truly proves and adjust your conclusion from it accordingly.

Paragraph Organization

Right now, paragraphs blend multiple ideas (contraception methods, adoption, welfare, punishment for abortion) without clear transitions. Consider organizing with one main idea per paragraph:
Para 1: Introduction/thesis
Para 2: Contraception reduces "need" for abortion
Para 3: Adoption and welfare as alternatives
Para 4: Exception—rape
Para 5: Exception—health risk to mother
Para 6: Personal anecdote (revise its connection to thesis)
Para 7: Conclusion

Language and Grammar Issues to Review

Subject-verb agreement: "how are financially unable" → "who are"
Sentence fragments/run-ons: "I is does think" → likely "I do think"
Comparative structure: "The birth control pill is said to ninety-five percent effective" → "said to be ninety-five percent effective"
Watch verb tense consistency, especially in the narrative section (shifting between past tense forms)
"for a twenty-two year old married man" → clarify: do you mean she was pregnant *by* him?

I recommend reading your essay aloud or using a grammar-checking tool, then reviewing flagged sentences carefully rather than accepting all suggestions automatically.

Priority Revisions

Clarify and stabilize your thesis so all sections support one consistent argument.
Soften inflammatory language ("murder," "jail," graphic descriptions) or provide substantial legal/philosophical support if you keep this strong stance.
Reconsider what your personal story proves, and explicitly connect it to your thesis rather than assuming the connection is obvious.
Add citations for statistics.
Proofread carefully for grammar, especially subject-verb agreement and sentence completeness.

You clearly care about this issue—channel that passion into a tighter, more consistent, and more measured argument. I'd be happy to discuss any of these points further in office hours.

Feedback Report on "Sex in the Media"

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a timely and engaging topic, and your passion is clear throughout. You've made a genuine effort to support your claims with specific examples from television, radio, and magazines. However, the essay would benefit significantly from revisions in three main areas: thesis clarity and argument structure, paragraph organization, and grammar/mechanics. Below, I offer specific suggestions for each.

Thesis and Central Argument

Your title and opening claim that "sex is greatly overrated" in the media, but as the essay progresses, your real argument shifts toward a more specific and interesting claim: that the media's irresponsible portrayal of sex (without consequences, precautions, or communication) contributes to teen pregnancy and STDs. This second argument is actually more compelling and arguable than "there is too much sex in the media."

Suggestion: Revise your thesis statement to reflect this stronger claim. For example: *"By portraying sex as spontaneous and consequence-free, the media fails to show the responsibility that should accompany it, contributing to rising rates of teen pregnancy and STDs."* This gives your essay a clearer analytical direction and would tie your examples (TV shows, songs, ads) more tightly to your ultimate point about teen behavior.

Organization and Paragraph Development

Your essay currently reads as a list of examples (TV, then radio, then magazines) followed by a separate discussion of consequences. Consider restructuring so that each body paragraph explicitly connects the example to your argument about consequences, rather than saving that connection for the final paragraphs.

Paragraphs 1–3 (TV, radio, magazines) are largely descriptive. Ask yourself after each example: *So what?* Why does this particular example matter to your argument? Weave your analysis throughout rather than saving it all for the end.
The essay also becomes repetitive near the end, with several paragraphs restating "you can't wonder why teenagers..." Consolidate these into one strong paragraph rather than three that make similar points.
Your conclusion introduces a new and interesting idea—"understood" sex—but it appears too late. Consider developing this as a proposed solution earlier, giving your essay a more constructive, forward-looking argument rather than one that is purely critical.

Evidence and Support

Your examples (specific shows, songs, and advertisements) are a strength—concrete details make your argument more persuasive. However:

You make a strong empirical claim ("we wonder why there are so many pregnant teenage females") but provide no statistics or sources. Even one or two statistics (eg, from the CDC or a similar source) would strengthen your credibility considerably.
The Paul Mitchell and "Gravity" cologne examples are effective, but you left a placeholder () where the slogan should go—remember to fill this in before submitting.
Consider addressing a counterargument. For instance, some might argue that media reflects rather than shapes behavior, or that "sex sells" is simply a business reality. Briefly acknowledging and responding to this would make your argument more sophisticated.

Grammar and Mechanics

As a non-native speaker, you're doing well with vocabulary and sentence variety, but there are recurring errors to address:

Capitalization/typos: "Sex Is used" should be lowercase "is"; "R. Kellye" should be "R. Kelly"; "saids" should be "says."
Subject-verb agreement: "everyone on that show has or will have slept" is awkward—consider "By the end of the season, every character has slept with, or will sleep with, someone else on the show."
Sentence fragments: "Nothing but a sheet." and "That's not reality." are used for emphasis, but overuse weakens their effect. Use sparingly.
Comma splices/run-ons: Several sentences join independent clauses with only a comma (eg, "Now radio is almost as bad as television. Every time I turn on the radio, I either hear..."). Review comma splice rules, as this is a common issue in your draft.
Article usage: Watch for missing or unnecessary articles (eg, "in the large number of those" should likely be "among the many that…").
Word choice: "soak and wet" should be "soaked and wet" or simply "wet." "Precautions measures" should be "precautionary measures" or "precautions."

Suggested Next Steps

Rewrite your thesis to reflect your strongest claim (media's failure to show consequences/responsibility).
Reorganize body paragraphs so each example ties directly back to that thesis.
Add at least one statistic or external source to support your claims about teen pregnancy/STDs.
Proofread carefully for the grammar issues noted above—reading your essay aloud can help you catch comma splices and awkward phrasing.
Develop your "understood sex" solution more fully—this could become a strong, constructive conclusion.

You have a clear voice and a real argument here—with revision, this essay can become both more organized and more persuasive. Keep up the good work!

Feedback on "The Psychology of Fashion" Essay

Overall Impression

You've clearly done substantial research and gathered interesting material on how clothing relates to psychology, adolescence, aging, and modesty. However, the essay currently reads more like a summary of sources than an argumentative essay. To strengthen this piece, you need a clearer central claim that ties all your evidence together, and you need to make sure your own analysis—not just your sources—drives the paper forward.

Thesis and Argument

Right now, your essay covers many related but separate ideas: fashion identity, adolescent peer pressure, consumer behavior, aging, and modesty. Each is interesting, but the reader is left asking: What is your actual argument? Is it that clothing choices are primarily shaped by social pressure? That fashion reflects and shapes identity? That people have less individual control over their clothing choices than they think?

Suggestion: Write a single-sentence thesis that makes a debatable claim, and place it at the end of your introduction. For example: *"While people believe their clothing choices reflect personal identity, psychological research suggests that fashion decisions are largely governed by the need for social acceptance."* Every paragraph should then clearly support this claim.

Organization and Paragraph Structure

The essay moves topic to topic (adolescents → consumers → elderly → modesty) without transitions explaining *why* these topics are connected or how they build your argument. Each new section should open with a topic sentence that links back to your thesis, not just introduce a new group of people or a new source.

The Herrick poem is a striking inclusion, but it currently interrupts the essay's flow because you don't analyze it. A block quotation this long needs discussion afterward—what does "sweet disorder" suggest psychologically? Does it support your point about instability in fashion identity? Without analysis, it feels decorative rather than argumentative.

Similarly, the five statements from Flügel are quoted at length but not unpacked. Long lists of quoted material should be followed by your own explanation of their significance.

Suggestion: After every quotation or paraphrase, add 1–2 sentences of your own analysis connecting it explicitly to your thesis.

Use of Sources

You cite Davis, Ryan, Hollingworth, and Flügel, which shows good research effort. However:

Citations are inconsistent (sometimes just a page number, eg, "(84)," which is confusing without knowing which source you're referring to).
Ensure every citation follows a consistent format (author, page) throughout, per MLA or your assigned style guide.
You rely heavily on quotation and paraphrase; try to synthesize sources more (eg, "Both Ryan and Hollingworth suggest that adolescent identity is fragile, which explains why...") rather than presenting them one after another.

Personal Examples

Your hypothetical example ("if I were a person who dresses the way society wants me to dress...") is a good instinct—personal reasoning can strengthen an argument—but it should be clearly signaled as illustrative, and ideally replaced or supplemented with a concrete, real example (a personal anecdote, a celebrity, a cultural example) rather than a hypothetical "if I were."

Tone and Word Choice

Some word choices are too casual or potentially inappropriate for academic writing:
"slut," "pimp," "mean person" in the opening — consider more analytical terms like "provocative," "authoritative," or "unapproachable" to maintain a scholarly tone while still conveying your point about snap judgments.

Grammar and Language

Since English is your second language, here are recurring patterns to watch for:

Subject-verb agreement: "What a person wears can be a good indicator on how that person *feel*" → should be "*feels*." "she probably *feel* sexy" → "*feels*."
Article usage: "The way of expressing the way one dresses" is repetitive and awkward—consider revising to "This tension between self-expression and instability is captured in..."
Typos: "fell self-pity" should be "feel self-pity"; capitalization after periods (eg, "one doesn't know" should begin with a capital letter).
Comma splices and run-ons: Several sentences string ideas together without clear connectors (eg, "If the peer group doesn't accept what the friend is wearing, the person will change the outfit, never to be worn again" — clarify pronoun reference: whose outfit?).

Suggestion: After revising for content, do a separate proofreading pass focused only on subject-verb agreement, articles, and sentence boundaries. Reading your essay aloud can help catch these errors.

Priority for Revision

Develop a clear, arguable thesis.
Reorganize paragraphs so each explicitly supports that thesis.
Add analysis after every quotation.
Proofread carefully for grammar.

You have rich material here—now it needs a stronger argumentative backbone to unify it. Good luck with your revision!

Your thesis appears in paragraph 2: "the decision to have an abortion or not should be left entirely up to the woman." This is a solid, arguable claim. However, it gets buried in the middle of a paragraph rather than announced clearly in your introduction. Consider revising your opening paragraph so that it ends with a direct thesis statement. Right now, the introduction spends time explaining that people disagree without telling the reader what *you* will argue.

Also, be careful with factual claims used to set up your topic. "These debates began over two decades ago" is historically inaccurate—the abortion debate in the U.S. (and globally) goes back much further, at least to the 19th century, and intensified around *Roe v. Wade* (1973). Since this is an easily checked claim, consider removing it or replacing it with a more general statement, such as "This debate has continued for decades."

Organization and Paragraph Structure

Your essay would benefit from clearer paragraph unity. Right now, several paragraphs mix multiple ideas without transitions. For example, the paragraph beginning "The pro-choicers, as well as proffers..." is only one sentence and doesn't connect smoothly to the next paragraph about rape. Consider merging or transitioning explicitly: "One weak argument pro-life advocates use concerns cases of rape."

The paragraph about rape also has a confusing, seemingly incomplete sentence: "For example some anti-abortionist, think that even woman women who have abortions." This sentence doesn't finish its thought grammatically or logically—readers cannot tell what anti-abortionists actually *think*. Please revise this sentence carefully; it's currently the weakest link in your argument because your evidence doesn't logically connect to your claim.

Development of Counterarguments

You've chosen two counterarguments to refute: (1) the rape exception issue, and (2) the "genocide/Nazi" comparison. This is a good strategic choice—addressing opposing views strengthens your own position. However, both refutations need more development:

Rape argument: You assert that pro-life advocates "neglect" rape cases, but you don't cite any specific claim, source, or example of an anti-abortion argument that ignores rape. Strengthen this by referencing an actual argument or common pro-life position (eg, "some pro-life advocates argue that even pregnancies resulting from rape should be carried to term because the fetus is not responsible for the crime"). Then explain *why* this reasoning fails, rather than just asserting that it's "unrealistic."

Genocide argument: This section is your strongest, because you define the term and show a logical mismatch between "genocide" and "abortion." Nice use of definition as a rhetorical strategy. Consider citing where this "Nazi slippery slope" argument comes from (a specific pro-life writer, group, or article) to make your rebuttal more concrete and credible.

Evidence and Support

Currently, the essay relies entirely on general reasoning without any outside sources, statistics, or expert opinions. Since this is an argumentative essay on a heavily researched topic, adding at least one or two credible sources (medical statistics on unsafe abortions, psychological studies on unwanted children, or legal scholarship on bodily autonomy) would make your claims far more persuasive. For instance, your claim that unwanted children are "automatically psychologically damaged for life" is a strong, absolute statement that needs evidence to be convincing.

Grammar and Mechanics

There are several recurring issues worth focusing on in revision:

Comma splices/run-ons: "These debates began over two decades ago, they have done nothing..." should be two sentences or joined with a semicolon/conjunction.
Word confusion: "categories" is spelled correctly but "this categories" should be "these categories" (subject-verb/plural agreement). "Proffers" likely should be "pro-lifers."
Article/number errors: "even woman women who" — likely a typo; decide between singular/plural and revise.
Punctuation: Watch spacing around commas and dashes (eg, ",-genocide" should simply be "genocide").
Word choice: "obtains enough knowledge" is awkward; consider "has enough expertise" or "is qualified to judge."

Conclusion

Your conclusion restates your thesis, which is good, but it introduces a new argument (psychological harm to unwanted children) without development. Save this point for the body of the essay, where you can support it with evidence, and use the conclusion instead to synthesize your existing points and end with a strong closing statement about bodily autonomy.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Clarify and front-load your thesis in the introduction.
Fix the incomplete/confusing sentence in the rape paragraph.
Add concrete sources or examples to support your rebuttals.
Correct comma splices and grammatical errors throughout.
Move the "unwanted children" point into the body with supporting evidence.

You have a clear, defensible position and one genuinely strong paragraph (the genocide rebuttal)—use that paragraph as a model for developing your other points with the same precision and logical clarity.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Thank you for sharing this essay on a topic you clearly care about. Your passion for children's wellbeing comes through strongly, and your personal examples (especially about your own daughters) add credibility and warmth to your argument. Below is feedback organized by key areas, with suggestions for revision.

Content and Argument Structure

Your central claim—that television content is inappropriate for the amount of unsupervised viewing children do—is worth arguing, but the essay would benefit from a clearer thesis statement early on. Currently, readers must piece together your argument from scattered points. Consider opening with one or two sentences that state directly: what is the problem, and what should be done about it?

Your essay also lacks clear organization into cohesive paragraphs with topic sentences. Right now, ideas are grouped somewhat by association rather than by logical progression. I suggest restructuring around these clear sections:
The scope of the problem (statistics, viewing schedule)
Why children are vulnerable (lack of sexual education, cognitive development)
Specific examples (Ricki Lake, The Simpsons, your daughter's assumption about kissing)
Proposed solution (more children's programming, parental responsibility)

Each paragraph should have one main idea, introduced by a clear topic sentence.

Evidence and Credibility

Your statistic that "98% of television programs are not for children" is a strong claim, but it needs a source. Where did this number come from? Without citation, skeptical readers may dismiss it. The same applies to your programming schedule example (soap opera at 3pm, Oprah at 4pm, etc)—this is compelling, but tell readers where/when you observed this, or acknowledge it's illustrative rather than universal.

Your personal anecdotes (the kissing/marriage assumption, not allowing *The Simpsons*) are your strongest evidence because they're specific and vivid. Use *more* of these and trust them to do persuasive work, rather than relying on unsupported statistics.

Clarity and Focus

The essay currently tries to cover several related but distinct issues: sexual content, violence, profanity, disrespectful behavior (Bart Simpson), and homosexuality. This breadth weakens your argument's focus. Consider narrowing your scope—perhaps focus primarily on sexual content, since that's where your strongest evidence (Ricki Lake example, kissing anecdote) lies. You can mention violence and profanity briefly, but don't try to fully argue all three.

Language and Grammar

Since you're writing in a second language, here are patterns to watch for in revision:

Subject-verb agreement: "make the children to want" → "make children want" (remove "to" after "make")
Article usage: "On one channel at three o'clock a soap opera is on" is fine, but check phrases like "watch television more than adults do" for missing articles elsewhere.
Run-on sentences: Many sentences chain multiple ideas with commas where periods or semicolons would help. Example: "Approximately ninety eight percent of television programs are not for children. That only leaves a small two percent..." — good, but elsewhere you have long strings that need breaking up.
Consistent tense: You shift between present and past tense when discussing your daughter's experience. Keep anecdotes in past tense consistently.
Homophone/typo errors: "mom is" appears as "moms" and "moml"; "Opra" should be "Oprah"; "nigh time" should be "night time." A careful proofreading pass will catch these.
Word choice: "experiment" is used oddly (eg, "want to experiment and do what people on shows do")—do you mean "imitate" or "try out"? Consider revising for precision.

Suggestions for Revision

Write a one-sentence thesis and place it in your introduction.
Reorganize into 4-5 paragraphs, each with a clear topic sentence.
Narrow your focus primarily to sexual content, using violence/profanity as secondary support.
Cite your statistics or reframe them as personal observation.
Proofread for run-on sentences and typos—reading aloud can help you hear where sentences need to break.
Strengthen your conclusion by returning explicitly to your thesis and stating a concrete, actionable recommendation (eg, "Networks should reserve X hours for children's programming").

Your instincts about using personal experience and concrete examples are excellent—lean into these strengths as you revise. With tighter organization and more precise language, this essay could be very persuasive.

Feedback Report on "Recycling" Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay demonstrates genuine enthusiasm for the topic and impressive research effort—you've gathered substantial evidence from multiple sources about recycling different materials. However, the essay currently reads more like an informational report than an argumentative essay. To strengthen this piece, you need to sharpen your central argument, improve organization, and address several recurring language issues.

Structural and Argumentative Issues

Unclear Thesis and Argument
Your introduction mentions recycling generally, but you never state a clear, arguable claim. What exactly are you arguing? Possible options:
"Recycling should be mandatory rather than voluntary"
"Individuals bear more responsibility than corporations for recycling failures"
"The convenience of recycling should be prioritized in urban planning"

Currently, the essay simply *describes* how to recycle various materials (plastic, glass, batteries, oil, etc) without building toward a persuasive claim. Consider revising your thesis to state a specific position, then ensure every paragraph supports that position.

Repetitive, List-like Structure
The body paragraphs follow a monotonous pattern: introduce material → cite fact → move to next material. This creates a "laundry list" effect rather than a logical argument. Ask yourself: *Why am I discussing batteries right after glass?* Consider organizing by:
Severity of environmental harm (most dangerous materials first)
Ease of solution (easiest changes first, building to harder ones)
Type of actor responsible (individual vs. corporate vs. government action)

Uneven Paragraph Development
Some paragraphs (plastic, batteries) are well-developed; others (steel/glass) feel rushed and disorganized—note the broken sentence "Recycling is not only useful for saving room in the landfills but for saving" which trails off before introducing the light bulb statistic. Proofread carefully for these gaps.

Weak Conclusion
Your conclusion introduces a new comparison (recycling vs. gas station visits) that could be powerful, but it appears too late and isn't developed. Save your strongest, most memorable argument for the conclusion, not a brand-new idea.

Citation and Source Issues

You rely almost entirely on two sources ("Recycler's Bill of Rights" and "One Earth"). For a stronger argument, diversify your evidence with more sources, especially any offering opposing viewpoints (Is recycling always cost-effective? Are there criticisms of certain claims?).
Address counterarguments: Some environmentalists argue recycling has limitations (contamination, energy costs of transport, etc). Acknowledging this would strengthen your credibility.
The long block quote from "Superbag" should be integrated more smoothly with your own analysis, not just dropped in.

Language and Mechanical Issues

Encoding errors: There are strange symbols in the text (eg, "@ countless years," "fight a one hundred watt fight bulb," "will be fun," "win suffer," "m the normal trash"). These appear to be typos or copy-paste errors—please proofread carefully before submitting. They significantly distract from your credibility.

Comma splices and run-ons: eg, "Some marketing experiments... have resulted in damaged recycling plants, therefore, the shape..." Consider breaking into two sentences or using a semicolon.

Subject-verb agreement: "the human population needs to educate themselves" — "population" is singular; consider "itself" or rephrase as "humans need to educate themselves."

Quotation integration: Quotes are sometimes dropped in without proper lead-in (eg, the long indented quote). Use signal phrases like "According to the Superbag Corporation..." to introduce quotes smoothly.

Word choice: "uproar" in the opening sentence suggests conflict/anger, but you likely mean "growing concern" or "movement."

Specific Revision Suggestions

Rewrite your thesis to state a clear, debatable claim.
Reorganize body paragraphs around a logical principle (severity, feasibility, or responsibility) rather than an arbitrary list of materials.
Add a counterargument paragraph addressing skepticism about recycling's effectiveness, then refute it.
Fix the broken sentence in the glass/steel paragraph and proofread for encoding errors throughout.
Strengthen the conclusion by expanding the gas-station analogy and connecting it explicitly back to your thesis.
Vary sentence structure—many paragraphs follow "fact + citation + fact + citation," which becomes monotonous. Mix in analysis, transitions, and rhetorical questions.

Final Note

You clearly did solid research and care about this topic—that's the hardest part! Now the work is to transform this collection of facts into a persuasive essay with a clear argument that guides readers toward a specific conclusion. Focus your next draft on answering: "What do I want my reader to believe or do after reading this, and why should they believe me?"

Feedback on "Pink and Blue: Gender Color Stereotypes"

Overall Impression

You've chosen an interesting, relatable topic, and you clearly have a good eye for real-world examples (Skintimate, Secret, Huggies, Gerber). These specific details are a real strength—they make your essay concrete and persuasive. However, right now the essay reads more like a description of a phenomenon than an argument about it. To move this from a B-level draft to a strong argumentative essay, focus on the following areas.

Sharpen Your Thesis and Argumentative Claim

Your opening asks "why?" but the essay mostly *illustrates* that the pink/blue stereotype exists rather than *arguing* something about it. Ask yourself: What is your actual claim? Possible directions:
The stereotype is socially constructed and reinforced by media/marketing (not natural or biological).
This stereotype limits gender expression, especially for boys/men.
The double standard (girls can wear blue, but boys can't wear pink) reveals something troubling about how femininity and masculinity are valued differently.

Right now your final paragraph gestures at this last idea—the double standard—but it appears only in the last few sentences. This should be your central thesis, stated clearly in your introduction, with the rest of the essay built to support it.

Strengthen Organization Around Your Argument

Currently, the essay is organized by *topic* (women's products → babies → boys), which makes it feel like a list of examples. Since your strongest point is the *double standard*, consider reorganizing around that argument:
Paragraph 1: Introduce the stereotype and your claim about the double standard.
Body: Use your commercial examples to show *how* the stereotype is constructed and reinforced (this is your evidence).
A dedicated paragraph analyzing *why* the double standard exists (eg, femininity coded as flexible/acceptable, masculinity policed more strictly)—this is where your unique argument should live, not just in the conclusion.
Conclusion: Restate your claim and explain its significance (why should readers care about this double standard?).

Add Analysis, Not Just Examples

Each time you give an example (Skintimate ad, Huggies diapers), you describe it but don't explain what it *proves*. For instance, after describing the Secret deodorant ad, ask: What does this repetition across brands suggest? Is this simply reflecting existing preferences, or actively shaping them? Push yourself to explain the *significance* of each example, not just list it.

Consider Counterarguments and Deeper Causes

Right now you assert that pink = soft = feminine, but you don't explore *why* society links softness with femininity, or whether this association is universal or historically specific (interesting fact: pink was associated with boys in the early 20th century in the U.S.). Even briefly acknowledging that this coding is a cultural convention, not an innate truth, would make your argument more sophisticated and harder to dismiss.

Language and Mechanics

You have some recurring grammar issues worth addressing in revision:

Capitalization after periods: "on the Secret deodorant commercial" and "once a baby has developed" should begin with capital letters.
Comma splices/run-ons: "Pink is mainly associated with women. Pink is considered a feminine color." These are fine as separate sentences, but elsewhere you have long strings joined only by commas (eg, "The background has soft colors. on the Secret..."). Check each sentence for a clear subject-verb-object structure.
Informal aside: "(jam referring to babies)" appears to be a typo for "(I am referring to babies)." Avoid parenthetical asides like this in formal writing—integrate the clarification into your sentence instead.
Repetition: "soft colors" is used many times. Vary your vocabulary (gentle, muted, pastel, delicate) to avoid redundancy.
Vague pronouns: "As for males, baby boys especially, blue is the stereotypical color" is a bit unclear—revise for directness: "Blue is the stereotypical color associated with baby boys."

Add Sources

Since you're describing widely-recognized marketing patterns, this essay would benefit enormously from at least one or two outside sources—articles on the history of pink/blue gender coding, or scholarship on gender and color psychology. This will lend credibility and show you're engaging with existing conversation on the topic, not just personal observation.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Write a clear, arguable thesis (likely about the double standard) in your introduction.
Reorganize paragraphs to build toward that argument, not just list examples by category.
Add analysis after each example—explain *why it matters*.
Address the historical/cultural construction of these color associations.
Proofread for capitalization, sentence boundaries, and word choice.

You have strong raw material here—now it's a matter of shaping it into a focused argument. I'd be happy to look at a revised draft once you've reworked the thesis and organization.

Feedback on "The Case for Drug Legalization"

Overall Impression

You have chosen a genuinely debatable and socially significant topic, and your essay is organized around a clear structure: an introduction with a thesis, five body paragraphs each addressing a distinct benefit of legalization, and a conclusion that summarizes your points. This is a solid foundation. However, the essay currently reads more like an outline than a fully developed argument, because many of your supporting sentences are missing (marked with ). Below are suggestions to help you strengthen the content, organization, and language of your essay.

Content and Evidence

This is the most urgent issue. Several of your paragraphs contain placeholder marks () where explanation, evidence, or examples should be. For example, in your first body paragraph, you claim that legal drugs would be cheaper and harder to sell on the street, but the reasoning connecting these two ideas is missing. Similarly, your paragraph on crime reduction and the paragraph on "responsible for their own debts" have gaps that make your causal claims ("Therefore...") unsupported. Before revising the sentence-level grammar, please complete these missing sections with concrete reasoning or evidence (statistics, expert opinions, historical examples such as Prohibition). Right now, a reader cannot follow *why* lower prices would reduce street dealing, or why this specific "one third" statistic about drug users in prison is true.

Suggestion: For every claim, ask yourself "why?" or "how do I know this?" and make sure the answer appears in the text, ideally supported by a citation.

Use and Citation of Sources

You cite one source (Duke 231) at the very beginning, but afterward you make several specific factual claims — the number of people incarcerated for drug offenses, the effect of legalization on tobacco and alcohol use, the claim that "if drugs were to increase by a factor of two or three" deaths would still be lower — without any citation. These are exactly the kinds of claims that need source support in academic writing, both to make them credible and to give appropriate credit to the researchers whose work you are drawing on. Please integrate more citations throughout, not just in the introduction, and format them consistently according to your assigned style (MLA, APA, etc).

Organization and Coherence

Your paragraph topic sentences are generally clear ("Another benefit of legalizing drugs would be the decrease in crime"), which helps the reader anticipate each paragraph's focus. However, within paragraphs, the logical connections between sentences are sometimes weak or circular. For instance, in the health paragraph, the sentence "Similarly, when heroin, they become alcohol abusers" is incomplete and confusing — it's unclear who "they" refers to or what the full comparison is. Revise this sentence to state your point clearly, such as: "Similarly, when heroin is unavailable, some drug users turn to alcohol instead."

Also consider varying your transitions. You repeat "Another benefit" / "The next benefit" as openers for nearly every paragraph, which becomes repetitive. Try varying the language (eg, "Beyond reducing crime, legalization would also...") to make the essay flow more naturally and demonstrate more sophisticated paragraph linking.

Sentence-Level Language Issues

There are a number of grammar and word-choice issues to address in revision:

Comma splice/incomplete construction: "They have come to reality and decided that, drug prohibition has not worked in the past..." — remove the comma after "that" and consider rephrasing "come to reality" (perhaps "come to realize" or "recognized the reality").
Word choice: "beneficiary to America" should be "beneficial to America." "because of their socially and economically margin" is unclear — perhaps you mean "because of their marginalized social and economic status."
Sentence fragments: "Similarly, when heroin, they become alcohol abusers" needs to be completed as a full sentence.
Subject-verb and tense consistency: Check verbs like "If drugs were to increase by a factor of two or three" — do you mean drug *use* would increase, or drug *availability*? Clarify the subject of "increase."
Run-on causal logic: Sentences using "Therefore" should follow directly and logically from the previous sentence; in a few places the connection is assumed rather than shown.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Fill in all missing () content with clear reasoning and evidence — this is essential before anything else.
Add citations for factual/statistical claims throughout, not just in the introduction.
Clarify and complete incomplete sentences, especially in the health paragraph.
Vary paragraph transitions to avoid repetitive phrasing.
Proofread carefully for word choice, article usage, and subject-verb agreement, which are common areas of difficulty when moving between Chinese and English sentence structures.

You have a clear, arguable thesis and a logical essay structure — with revision focused on completing your evidence and tightening your sentences, this essay can become a much stronger piece of persuasive writing. Keep up the good work!

Feedback Report: Gun Control Essay

Overall Impression

You have chosen a topic you clearly care about, and the personal stories about Mr. Glover and your Uncle Edward give this essay real emotional power that many academic papers lack. Your willingness to draw on family history takes courage. However, the essay needs revision in several areas: sharpening the thesis, organizing evidence more logically, engaging with counterarguments, and polishing grammar and citation format. Below are specific suggestions.

Thesis and Argument Focus

Your thesis states that gun control would "reduce crime, save lives, and lessen fears," but the essay doesn't clearly organize around these three claims. Instead, it moves through a series of anecdotes (school shooting, bank robbery, two hometown killings) without explicitly connecting each one back to your three main points. Consider restructuring with clear topic sentences: one paragraph focused on how gun control reduces crime, using the bank robbery as evidence; one focused on saving lives, using the Glover and Spigner deaths; and one focused on fear, using the citizen quotes from Branchville. This will make your organization visible to the reader.

Use of Evidence

Your sources are compelling, but they need better integration:

Introduce quotations before dropping them in. Right now, quotations often appear abruptly (eg, "Toby R. Sincino, the 15-year-old..."). Add a sentence of context first: "One tragic example comes from Blackville-Hilda High School, where..."
Explain the relevance after each quote. You do this somewhat, but be more precise about *how* the example proves your specific claim (crime reduction? lives saved? fear reduced?).
The hate crime statistics paragraph is confusing. You cite robbery as 2.5% and murder as 16.3% of "hate crimes," but it's unclear whether these are gun-related specifically, and the numbers seem oddly small for such serious categories. Double-check your source and clarify what these percentages actually measure.
Personal interviews are valuable evidence, but academic essays typically ask you to explain *why* this source is credible (eg, "as an eyewitness to the aftermath, my mother's account illustrates...").

Counterargument

You mention the NRA's opposition in one sentence but dismiss it too quickly ("This opposing organization doesn't realize..."). A stronger essay would explain *what* the NRA's actual argument is (eg, Second Amendment rights, self-defense) and then respond to it with evidence or reasoning. This shows you've engaged seriously with the other side, which strengthens your credibility rather than weakening your position.

Citations

Your citation format is inconsistent and needs standardization (check with your instructor whether MLA or APA is required):
"(The Times and Democrat, pg.6A)" should include the author (if known) and a consistent format
"(Multimedia Resources-CD-Rom)" is too vague—what is the actual title of this resource?
Personal interviews should be cited consistently (eg, "Personal interview with [Mother's name], [date]")
Create a Works Cited/References page at the end listing full source information

Grammar and Sentence-Level Issues

I noticed several patterns worth reviewing:

Articles (a/an/the): eg, "government limitation" should be "a government limitation." Since Chinese doesn't use articles the way English does, this is a common area to double-check in revision.
Subject-verb agreement: "the acquaintace pulled out a handgun and started shooting and at least three of the shots hit" — check tense consistency throughout narrative sections.
Word form errors: "misue" (misuse), "techers" (teachers), "memebers" (members), "Resoureces" (Resources) — these look like typos rather than grammar issues; a careful proofreading pass will catch them.
Run-on sentences: Several sentences combine too many ideas with commas rather than periods or conjunctions (eg, the second sentence of paragraph one). Try breaking these into shorter, clearer sentences.
Quotation formatting: Some quotes have stray punctuation marks (¬ symbols) that appear to be formatting errors from copying—clean these up.

Conclusion

Your conclusion introduces a strong personal policy proposal (only police/military should carry guns), but this is a significant claim that appears without warning at the very end. Consider foreshadowing this position earlier, perhaps in your introduction, so it doesn't feel like a new argument tacked on. Also, the Brady Bill reference is excellent evidence—consider moving it earlier and analyzing it more (Did it work? What were its limits?) rather than mentioning it briefly at the end.

Next Steps

Revise your topic sentences so each paragraph clearly supports one of your three main claims.
Add 2-3 sentences addressing the NRA's counterargument directly.
Proofread for article usage, spelling, and sentence boundaries—reading the essay aloud can help catch these.
Standardize your citations and add a Works Cited page.

You have powerful material here—now let's make the structure and language match the strength of your content.

Feedback on Your Capital Punishment Essay

Overall Impression

You've built a clear, well-organized argument with a solid three-part structure: cost, retribution, and deterrence. Your reasoning within each section generally makes sense, and you're clearly engaged with the topic. However, the essay needs significant revision in two main areas: tone/rhetorical strategy and language mechanics. Let me address both.

Content and Structure

Strengths:
Clear thesis and organizational plan
Each counterargument is addressed with specific reasoning
The point about revenge vs. punishment (paragraph 2) is your strongest philosophical argument

Areas for Improvement:

Tone is too aggressive for academic writing. Phrases like "obscene form of punishment," "ridiculous," and directly addressing the reader ("Let me throw a couple of statistics at you") feel more like a spoken debate than an academic essay. Academic argumentation persuades through evidence and reasoning, not through mocking the opposing view. Consider revising:
   - "obscene form of punishment" → "this controversial practice"
   - "as ridiculous as the source they are trying to endorse" → simply remove this kind of dismissive language
   
Citations are missing. You mention statistics "gathered doing a paper last year" and reference "studies in states," but you never cite sources. For academic credibility, you need specific citations (author, year, publication) for all statistics and studies. Right now, a skeptical reader has no way to verify your claims, which weakens your argument significantly.

The deterrence paragraph needs more specificity. Which states? What time period? What was the study called? Vague references to "they have done studies" undermine your credibility.

Your conclusion just repeats the introduction. Try to end with a stronger call to action or a broader implication (eg, what should replace capital punishment? What should readers do with this information?).

Language and Mechanics

This essay contains frequent errors that need attention:

Spelling errors (proofread carefully or use spell-check):
"untill" → until
"it's" → its (this is used incorrectly multiple times—"its" shows possession, "it's" means "it is")
"Ounce" → Once
"individ ual" → individual
"inprison" → imprison
"recieves" → receives
"midevil" → medieval
"el,,,actly" → exactly
"statels" → states
"murdeers/murdeer" → murders/murder
"reason's" → reasons
"detramental" → detrimental
"deference" → deterrence (this is a meaning-changing error—"deference" means respect/submission, while you mean "deterrence")

Grammar issues:
"prols and con's" → pros and cons
Run-on sentences and comma splices appear frequently; read your essay aloud to catch places where you need a period instead of a comma
Watch subject-verb agreement in longer sentences

Suggestion: Since English is your second language, I strongly recommend:
Writing your essay, then setting it aside for a day
Reading it aloud slowly—this often catches errors your eyes skip over
Using a grammar-checking tool (like Grammarly) as a supplement, not a replacement, for careful proofreading
Visiting the writing center for a session focused specifically on proofreading strategies

Specific Revision Priorities

Revise the introduction to remove inflammatory language while keeping your clear thesis
Add proper citations for all statistics (find the original source from your previous paper)
Proofread systematically—perhaps line by line, checking one type of error at a time (first spelling, then punctuation, then grammar)
Strengthen your conclusion with a forward-looking statement rather than pure repetition

Final Thought

Your argument has real substance—the ethical point about hypocrisy (killing to punish killing) is genuinely compelling, and your three-pronged structure works well. With careful proofreading and a more measured tone, this essay could be quite persuasive. Focus your revision energy on making the *evidence* speak for itself rather than relying on charged language to make your case. Well-supported claims stated calmly are often more convincing than passionate claims stated aggressively.

Overall Impression
You've chosen a genuinely difficult and important topic, and you clearly did research to inform your argument. The essay shows good effort in explaining background (types of euthanasia, the Kevorkian case, the Hippocratic Oath). However, the essay currently reads more like an informative report than an argumentative essay. To strengthen it, you need to sharpen your thesis, tighten the connection between your evidence and your argument, and fix some structural and language issues.

Thesis and Argument
Your introduction ends with a question ("Should it be legalized...or should they be bound by their Hippocratic Oath?") rather than a clear thesis statement. An argumentative essay needs a specific claim you will defend throughout. Your conclusion *does* take a position — that doctors should not have the authority to initiate euthanasia but may cooperate with a patient's wishes — but this is a fairly nuanced middle position that isn't signaled early on. Readers spend most of the essay unsure what you're arguing for.

Suggestion: Revise your introduction so the final sentence states your actual position clearly (something like your conclusion's claim), so readers know from the start what you will prove.

Missing Sources — the "" Placeholders
This is the most urgent issue. There are five places where a quotation or specific detail is missing (marked ""): the Christian view of suicide, Judge Breck's statement, the wording of the Hippocratic Oath, and Fletcher's "two things." These are central pieces of evidence for your argument — without them, key paragraphs feel incomplete and unpersuasive. Please locate your sources and fill these in with the actual quotations, properly cited (author, year, page if using MLA/APA).

Organization
The essay's structure needs rethinking:
Paragraph 2 (types of euthanasia) is useful background but takes up a lot of space without connecting back to your argument. Consider condensing this into a shorter paragraph, since your essay is really about *physician-assisted* suicide specifically.
Paragraphs 3–4 (May's four forces, Kevorkian case) present a lot of historical/factual detail. After each piece of evidence, add a sentence explaining *why this matters for your argument* — right now they read as summary rather than analysis.
Paragraph 5 (Hippocratic Oath) and Paragraph 6 (Fletcher) are very short and underdeveloped compared to the historical narrative. Since these are central to your final argument, they deserve more space and explanation.
Final paragraph introduces your actual thesis but does so abruptly, without transition from the previous discussion of the Oath. It also doesn't fully engage with the counterarguments you spent four paragraphs building (Kevorkian, technology, medical profession). Make sure your conclusion explicitly responds to at least one opposing view you raised earlier.

Suggestion: After finishing a draft, write a one-sentence summary of each paragraph's purpose. If a paragraph's purpose isn't clearly serving your thesis, revise or cut it.

Language and Grammar
A few recurring issues to review:

Apostrophes/plurals: "Those patient's who desire" → should be "Those patients who desire" (no apostrophe needed; you're not showing possession).
Parallel structure: "would rather die than to continue" → "would rather die than continue" (drop "to" after "rather...than").
Word choice/idiom: "Many people leave in their will expressed wishes" is awkward — try "Many people state their wishes in a living will" or similar.
Pronoun consistency: You switch between "his," "their," "one's" when referring to a single patient/physician (eg, "he must be guided by his conscience" vs. "it is one's option as a physician"). Choose one style (many writers now use "they/their" for a generic singular) and use it consistently.
Sentence fragments/typos: "Re unmanageable pain" looks like a typo — please proofread carefully for stray characters like this.
Verb tense: You move between past tense (historical narrative about Kevorkian) and present tense (general claims). This is fine, but double-check each sentence individually — eg, "He still did not obey" should probably be "He still did not obey the order and continued to instruct..." for clarity.

Citation Format
Right now, sources are named in prose ("William F. May wrote...") but there are no formal in-text citations or a Works Cited/References page. Please add these according to whichever citation style your course requires (MLA, APA, etc) — this is both an academic integrity requirement and will help you organize your evidence.

Summary of Priority Revisions
Fill in all missing quotations (the "" marks).
Write a clear thesis statement in your introduction that matches your conclusion.
Add analytical sentences after your factual/historical evidence, linking it back to your thesis.
Proofread carefully for apostrophes, pronoun consistency, and typos.
Add proper in-text citations and a reference list.

You have solid raw material here — the revision work now is about making your *argument* visible and consistent from start to finish. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback on "The Wild Card in Baseball" Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen an engaging topic and clearly know the subject well—the level of specific detail (player names, statistics, standings scenarios) shows strong research and genuine interest. However, the essay currently reads more like an informative report than an argumentative essay. To sharpen your argument, please consider the following areas for revision.

Balance Explanation and Argument

Roughly the first half of your essay explains *how* the wild card system works (old vs. new playoff structure, home-field-advantage rules, etc). This background is necessary, but it is currently too long relative to your actual argument. Readers need this information to understand your claims, but they don't need every procedural detail (eg, the full home-field-advantage rotation rule). Consider condensing the explanatory sections into a single, tighter paragraph and moving more quickly to your three main claims: (1) more stars/teams in the playoffs, (2) added competitive excitement, (3) tougher competition for the championship. This will shift the essay's center of gravity from "reporting" to "arguing."

Strengthen Topic Sentences and Paragraph Unity

Some paragraphs blend multiple ideas without a clear controlling claim. For example, the paragraph beginning "There are now four more teams in the playoffs" moves from *why* more teams is good, to a list of star players, to an analogy about advertising, to city involvement—all in one paragraph. Consider splitting this so each paragraph makes one clear point and supports it with evidence, rather than accumulating loosely related observations.

Address Counterarguments

An argumentative essay is stronger when it acknowledges opposing views. For instance, critics might argue that the wild card weakens the meaning of winning a division, or that it dilutes pennant races by giving weaker teams a "second chance." Adding even one paragraph addressing and rebutting such a concern would make your argument more persuasive and show critical engagement rather than one-sided cheerleading.

Revise the Conclusion

The anaphora "Without the wild card..." is a nice rhetorical technique, but repeating it four times in a row makes the conclusion feel mechanical rather than climactic. Try varying sentence structure, and check content: you write "baseball would have remained behind football and basketball," but then conclude "brings baseball back to the forefront after losing popularity to football and baseball"—this seems to be a typo (should likely be "football and basketball" both times, or "football and basketball" vs. some other sport). Please proofread this passage carefully, as the inconsistency undercuts your final point.

Citation and Sourcing

Your parenthetical citations (Major League Baseball), (The Wildcard...), (Final 1995 Wild Card Standings) are inconsistent in format and lack page numbers or clear source identification. Please check your assigned citation style (likely MLA, given the era of this topic) and:
Use consistent author/title abbreviations
Include page numbers where available
Attach a Works Cited page listing full source information

This will help your reader verify your evidence and will meet academic expectations for source attribution.

Language and Sentence-Level Issues

A few recurring issues to watch for in revision:

Typos/word confusion: "National Leagued Championship Series" → "National League"; "the last day of the season this year... The only two wild car spots" → "wild card spots."
Missing words: "taking the team from each league with the best record the three Division winners" is missing a word or phrase—likely "besides" or "excluding" the three Division winners. Please revise for clarity.
Run-on/comma splices: Several sentences joined with commas need semicolons, periods, or conjunctions (eg, "The Rockies also managed to score nineteen runs in those four games, while the Reds only got a total of five runs...").
Repetition: You use "clearly" and "clearly was good for baseball" several times. Vary your language to avoid repetitive claims of certainty.
Wordy phrasing: Sentences like "The absence of these four teams would have meant the absence of some of baseball's brightest stars" could be tightened: "Without these four teams, some of baseball's brightest stars would have missed the playoffs."

Suggested Priorities for Revision

Trim background/explanation to make room for argument.
Add a counterargument paragraph.
Tighten paragraph focus (one idea per paragraph).
Fix the football/basketball inconsistency in the conclusion.
Standardize citations and add a Works Cited list.
Proofread carefully for typos and missing words.

You have excellent command of the subject matter and strong evidence—now it's a matter of reorganizing and tightening the argument so your analysis, rather than your summary, takes center stage. I look forward to seeing your revision!

Feedback Report on "The Media's 'Right to Know'"

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a compelling and still-relevant issue: the tension between the media's "right to know" and an individual's right to privacy. You clearly feel strongly about this topic, and that passion comes through in your writing. However, the essay would benefit significantly from a clearer argumentative structure, a more explicit thesis, and tighter organization of your many examples. Below are specific areas for revision.

Thesis and Central Argument

Right now, your main claim emerges only gradually and is scattered across several paragraphs (the Grenada anecdote, the Surgeon General nominee, Colin Powell, O.J. Simpson). A reader has to piece together that your argument is: *the media has expanded its "right to know" so far that it now endangers lives, destroys reputations unfairly, and erodes personal privacy—and this must stop.*

Suggestion: State this thesis explicitly and early, ideally at the end of your introduction. Right now, the introduction is a personal anecdote (the phone call from "The State") that doesn't clearly connect to your larger argument until several paragraphs later. Consider either cutting this anecdote or explicitly linking it to your central claim in the first paragraph, so readers know from the start what you are arguing.

Organization

The essay reads as a list of examples (Grenada, Desert Storm, Bosnia, Jocelyn Elders' successor, the *Spectator* incident, O.J. Simpson, Colin Powell, Reagan, Ford) rather than a structured argument. Each example is compelling on its own, but they are not clearly grouped by the *type* of harm they illustrate.

Suggestion: Consider organizing your body paragraphs around distinct categories of harm, for example:
National security harm (Grenada, Desert Storm, Bosnia)
Reputational/character harm (Surgeon General nominee, the English Department professor, Colin Powell)
Trial-by-media harm (O.J. Simpson case)
Everyday privacy erosion (hamburgers, sleep schedules, Reagan, Ford)

Grouping examples this way will make your argument feel more deliberate and will help readers see the logical progression from "national security" to "individual privacy."

Development of Examples

Some examples are stated but not fully explained, which weakens their persuasive power for readers unfamiliar with the specific event:
The "Jocelyn Elders" replacement paragraph doesn't name the candidate, making the example harder to verify or research.
The *Spectator* / USC English Department incident is vague—readers don't know what was actually reported or why it was unfair.
The O.J. Simpson paragraph is strong, but the rhetorical questions ("How did they know...?") could be followed by a sentence explaining what this excess coverage actually revealed about media priorities.

Suggestion: Add one or two concrete sentences per example (a name, a specific claim, or a consequence) to strengthen your evidence. This will also help balance the essay, since currently the essay leans heavily on personal opinion and repeated rhetorical questions rather than documented specifics.

Logical Consistency

There is a slight contradiction worth addressing: you criticize the media for reporting personal/private matters (Reagan's Alzheimer's, Ford's golf game) as trivial, but you also criticize them for reporting relevant public matters (the Surgeon General nominee's medical history, O.J. Simpson trial coverage) as invasive. A stronger essay would draw a clearer line: *what distinguishes legitimate public interest from harmful invasion of privacy?* Right now, the standard seems to be "whatever I personally disagree with," which weakens the argument's persuasiveness. Consider adding a paragraph that proposes a clearer principle (eg, "reporting should stop where physical safety or irrelevant personal history begins").

Tone and Rhetorical Questions

You use many rhetorical questions ("Was this a rejection or a crucifixion?" "How did they know...?"). These are effective in moderation but become repetitive when used in nearly every paragraph. Consider replacing some with direct statements to vary your rhetorical strategy and make the essay feel more argumentative rather than exclamatory.

Conclusion

Your conclusion effectively returns to your opening idea (money vs. quality journalism), which is good. However, the ending sentence ("we don't see any of it anymore") is a strong emotional note but could be strengthened with a final call to action or a suggestion for change (eg, media accountability standards, reader responsibility) to leave the reader with something more constructive.

Language and Mechanics

There are several typographical/OCR-type errors that need proofreading (eg, "successfiil," "hwnburgers," "gwne," "dodt," "fives" for "lives," "ad that" for "all that," "inunune" for "immune"). Please do a careful proofreading pass, ideally reading the essay aloud, to catch these before submission.

---

Summary of Priorities for Revision:
Write an explicit thesis statement early in the essay.
Reorganize examples into thematic clusters.
Add specific details to underdeveloped examples.
Clarify the principle distinguishing legitimate reporting from invasive reporting.
Proofread carefully for typos.

You have real conviction and good instincts for using examples—now it's a matter of shaping them into a more unified, persuasive argument.

Feedback on Your Marijuana Legalization Essay

Thank you for sharing such a personal and passionate piece of writing. Your voice comes through clearly, and it's evident you feel strongly about this topic. However, to strengthen this into a more persuasive academic argument, there are several areas that need revision.

Thesis and Argument Structure

Your central claim—that marijuana should be legalized—gets lost in personal narrative. The essay reads more like a memoir of your evolving views than a structured argument. Consider opening with a clear thesis statement that previews your main reasons (eg, "Marijuana should be legalized because it is a natural substance with medical benefits, its criminalization disproportionately targets low-level offenders, and prohibition serves government financial interests rather than public safety"). Each paragraph should then develop one of these points with a clear topic sentence, rather than following a chronological "as I got older" structure.

There's also an important internal contradiction: you write "I don't believe marijuana is a drug" but later refer to it as "this drug." Decide on your position and use consistent terminology throughout.

Logical Fallacies to Address

Several claims rely on reasoning that won't hold up to scrutiny:

Ad hominem/whataboutism: Pointing out that "politicians use drugs themselves" doesn't logically support legalization—it's a separate issue about hypocrisy, not policy merit.
Anecdotal evidence: "Dealers had nice clothes and drove nice cars" and "my friends... it only mellows them out" are single examples, not evidence of broader safety or benefit.
Appeal to popularity: Listing celebrities and public figures who use marijuana doesn't prove it's harmless—many harmful things are popular among famous people.
Unsupported causation: "They lit up before a study session and aced their tests" implies a causal link between marijuana and academic success that a single anecdote cannot establish.

For an academic essay, you need actual evidence: studies, statistics, expert testimony, or legal/economic data—not personal observations alone.

Evidence and Sourcing

You make several factual claims (marijuana treats eye ailments, is "highly non-habit forming," has "nearly no long-lasting side effects") without any citations. These are exactly the kinds of claims that need academic sources to be credible. Research glaucoma treatment studies, addiction research, or public health data, and cite them properly. Right now, these claims read as assertions rather than supported facts, which weakens your credibility with skeptical readers.

Tone and Audience

The essay's conversational tone ("Do you ever notice," "Did you know") works well in spoken argument but is too informal for academic writing. Direct address to the reader can be a rhetorical device, but overusing rhetorical questions can feel like you're avoiding the harder work of building an argument. Consider revising these into declarative statements supported by evidence.

The quote from Bob Marley is a nice touch but should be integrated more formally—introduce who he is and why his perspective is relevant, rather than simply dropping the quote as your final line.

Organization

The essay would benefit from a clearer structure:
Introduction with thesis
Body paragraphs, each addressing one argument (eg, medical benefits, criminal justice inequities, economic/government interests)
Counterargument section addressing why some believe marijuana should remain illegal, followed by your rebuttal
Conclusion that restates your position and calls readers to a specific action or shift in thinking

Currently, your personal history (parents smoking in the 70s, Black Moses, childhood memories) takes up significant space but doesn't clearly connect to a formal argument. Consider using one or two of these stories briefly as an introduction hook, then move quickly into your structured reasoning.

Language and Mechanics

Please proofread carefully for the following recurring issues:
their/there/they're: "and their are nearly no long lasting side effects" → "there are"
its/it's: "it's leafy form" → "its leafy form"
Spelling: "Columbian" → "Colombian," "critisize" → "criticize," "c ession" → "session"
Run-on sentences and comma splices appear frequently; break these into shorter, clearer sentences.
Capitalization: "european countries" → "European countries"

Overall Suggestions

This essay has the seeds of a compelling argument—you clearly have personal investment and some genuine points about drug policy inconsistency. For your revision, I'd recommend:
Writing a clear thesis statement
Cutting or condensing the personal narrative to make room for researched evidence
Finding 3-4 credible sources on marijuana's medical uses, legal statistics, and economic impact
Addressing at least one counterargument seriously
Proofreading carefully for grammar and spelling

Focus your next draft on turning personal conviction into persuasive, evidence-based argument. You have real potential here—now it needs academic rigor to match your passion.
