Feedback Report: Reporter's Privilege and Shield Laws Essay

Overall Impression

You've clearly invested significant effort in researching this topic, and your case studies (Susan Smith, Watergate, Procter & Gamble, Rodney King) provide compelling real-world evidence. However, the essay currently reads more like a collection of research notes than a unified argumentative essay. To strengthen this piece, you need to sharpen your thesis, improve organization, and more consistently connect your evidence back to your central argument.

Structural Issues

Unclear Thesis Statement
Your introduction explains the reporter-source relationship well, but it never clearly states your argument. What exactly are you claiming? Is it:
Shield laws should be strengthened/made federal?
Courts should never override shield laws?
The reporter-source trust bond should always take priority?

State this clearly in your first paragraph, and make sure every subsequent paragraph supports this specific claim.

History Overwhelms Argument
The historical background (Maryland's 1896 law, the full legal text quotation, South Carolina's legislative history) takes up a large portion of your essay but doesn't advance your argument. Consider:
Summarizing this history in 2-3 sentences rather than quoting the full 1896 statute
Moving quickly to your analytical argument
Using history only where it directly supports a specific point

Case Studies Need Analysis
You present many strong cases (Decker, Swasy, Woodward/Bernstein, Stuart, Serrano) but mostly *describe* them rather than *analyze* them. For each case, ask:
What does this case prove about your argument?
Why should the reader care?

For example, after the Decker case, explicitly state: "This case demonstrates that shield laws are dangerously narrow because..." Don't make the reader infer your point.

Logical Inconsistency
You use the *Charles Stuart* case to show reporters successfully protected their sources—but this seems to undercut your argument that shield laws are being eroded. Clarify why this case fits your thesis, or explain what makes it different from the other cases where journalists lost.

Paragraph-Level Concerns

Weak Transitions: Paragraphs often jump between cases without connecting sentences. Add transitions that show *relationships* between ideas (eg, "While Serrano avoided contempt charges, Judge Kamins found another way to punish him, revealing an even more troubling trend...").

The Doctor-Patient Analogy: This is a strong rhetorical move—use it more! Return to it later in the essay when discussing the consequences of broken trust.

Emotional Appeals Need Support: Statements like "how many stories will the American people miss?" and "Don't make people scared to talk" are persuasive but need to be *earned* through evidence, not simply asserted. Consider adding a specific example of an important story that was never written because a source feared exposure.

Sentence-Level and Grammar Issues

Quotation formatting: Several quotes have inconsistent or incorrect punctuation (eg, stray commas, mismatched quotation marks: `'Alecia Swasy,,'` `"deep Throat"` should be capitalized as "Deep Throat"). Proofread all quotations carefully against your sources.
Citation consistency: You cite (Henry 56) many times—vary your sentence structures so quotations don't feel repetitive. Consider paraphrasing some material instead of quoting directly.
Comma usage: There are several comma splices and missing commas (eg, "In a very recent example,, Twila Decker..."). Read your sentences aloud to catch these.
Word choice: "twisted the law 180 degrees" is a strong, vivid phrase—but ensure this level of informality is consistent throughout (parts of your essay are more formal, academic in tone).

Concluding Paragraph

Your conclusion ("Protect the Shield Laws...Don't keep them from doing their job") is more like a slogan than a synthesis. A strong conclusion should:
Briefly restate your central argument
Synthesize what your evidence *collectively* proves
End with a forward-looking statement (a call to action, a prediction, or a broader implication)

Suggested Revision Priorities

Write a clear, arguable thesis and place it at the end of your introduction
Cut the lengthy 1896 statute quotation—paraphrase instead
Add analytical sentences after each case study explaining its significance
Reorganize so evidence builds logically toward your conclusion, rather than listing cases chronologically
Proofread carefully for punctuation and quotation formatting

Final Note

Your research is impressive, and your topic is genuinely important and current. With sharper focus and more explicit analysis connecting your evidence to your argument, this essay could be very persuasive. I recommend revising with one guiding question in mind: *"How does this specific sentence prove my thesis?"*

Feedback on Your Essay: "Should Women Be Allowed to Attend the Citadel?"

Overall Impression

You have chosen a topic you clearly care about, and your passion comes through in your writing. Your basic argument—that tradition and unfounded stereotypes shouldn't justify discrimination in publicly funded institutions—is a strong and defensible position. However, the essay needs work in three main areas: structural organization, development of evidence, and grammar/word choice. Below are specific suggestions.

Structure and Thesis

Your introduction identifies the debate but never clearly states *your* position. Consider adding a direct thesis sentence at the end of your introduction, such as: "This essay argues that the Citadel, as a publicly funded institution, should admit women because tradition and stereotypes about physical ability are insufficient justification for gender discrimination." This gives your reader a roadmap for the rest of the essay.

Each body paragraph should also open with a clear topic sentence stating its main point. Currently, your paragraphs shift between several ideas at once (eg, paragraph 2 discusses tradition, equality, *and* physical strength together). Consider separating these into distinct paragraphs, each with one focused claim:
Paragraph A: Tradition is not a valid reason to exclude women.
Paragraph B: The physical-strength argument is flawed because ability varies by individual, not gender.
Paragraph C: Legal/funding argument (public schools cannot discriminate).
Paragraph D: Educational equality argument.
Paragraph E: The "distraction" argument is refuted by coeducational institutions.

Finally, your essay has no conclusion. It ends abruptly on the "distraction" point. Add a final paragraph that restates your thesis, briefly reviews your strongest points, and ends with a broader statement about why this issue matters (eg, implications for gender equality in society).

Developing Your Evidence

Many of your claims are asserted but not supported. For example:
You state that publicly funded schools cannot discriminate—this is a strong legal point, but you should name the actual law or principle (eg, Title IX, or the Equal Protection Clause) rather than just asserting it as a rule.
Your personal statement "as a woman I especially know that is not true" is not strong evidence in academic writing. Instead, cite an example or statistic showing inequality (eg, wage gap, representation in institutions).
The comparison to slavery is powerful but needs more development—explain *why* it's analogous (both were traditions defended on grounds that turned out to be discriminatory), rather than just listing it alongside "women as housewives."

Try to bring in concrete facts: Did the Citadel eventually admit women? What happened when they did? Referencing this real historical outcome (Shannon Faulkner's case, 1996) would significantly strengthen your argument and show you've researched the topic, not just reasoned from opinion.

Grammar, Spelling, and Word Choice

You have many spelling and word-choice errors that a careful proofreading pass will catch. Some examples:

"appearred" → appeared
"alao" → also
"desciniinate" → discriminate
"pubfically" → publicly
"eqaul" → equal
"learening" → learning
"hispanic or if you are poliish" → Hispanic or Polish (also, this comparison is a bit unclear—clarify what point you're making)
"less superior" → this phrase is contradictory; you likely mean "inferior"
"Every deserves" → Everyone deserves
"Men are just like women, some of them may be strong..." — awkward parallel structure; consider: "Like men, some women are strong or intelligent, while others are not."

I'd recommend reading your essay aloud, or using a grammar-checking tool (eg, Grammarly) as a first pass, then reviewing flagged errors yourself so you understand *why* each correction is made—this will help you avoid repeating the same errors in future essays.

Sentence-Level Clarity

Several sentences run on or contain comma splices, for example: "That may be true in some cases, but others might be able to handle the challenges, Everyone does deserve the chance to try." Break these into separate sentences for clarity: "That may be true in some cases, but others might be able to handle the challenges. Everyone deserves the chance to try."

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Add a clear thesis statement and topic sentences.
Separate overlapping ideas into distinct, focused paragraphs.
Add concrete evidence (laws, historical facts, statistics) to support claims.
Write a conclusion paragraph.
Proofread carefully for spelling and grammar.

You have good instincts about argumentation and counterargument—now it's a matter of organizing and supporting those instincts more rigorously. I look forward to seeing your revision!

Feedback Report on Your Essay: "Stay-at-Home Wives and Equal Ownership in Marriage"

Thank you for sharing this essay. Your topic is timely and important, and you clearly have strong personal convictions about it. You also use a nice mix of strategies—hypothetical scenarios, personal example, and observed social commentary—to support your position. Below are suggestions organized by area to help you strengthen the essay in revision.

Thesis and Argument Structure

Your central claim—that a stay-at-home wife's contributions are equally valuable and that possessions should be owned equally—is a good, debatable thesis. However, it currently appears buried in your second sentence rather than stated as a clear, focused claim. Consider opening with a concise thesis statement that previews your main supporting reasons (eg, unpaid domestic labor has real economic/emotional value; ownership should be joint by law and practice; social judgment of stay-at-home wives is unfair). This will help readers track your argument across six body paragraphs, which currently feel like separate reflections rather than parts of one unified case.

Development and Use of Examples

Your examples are promising but underdeveloped:

The TV show scene: This is a strong illustrative device, but the dialogue is missing (marked with ) in the version I received. Without the actual lines, readers cannot follow the exchange or judge whether it supports your point. Please fill these in.
The housekeeper/$4.25 wage point: This is a great empirical anchor—it quantifies unpaid labor. Push this further: calculate roughly how many hours per week domestic work takes and multiply by the wage to show a dollar figure. This would make your "equal value" claim more persuasive and less abstract.
Your family example: This is your most vivid and effective paragraph. However, it actually shows the *opposite* of your stated point at that moment—it describes an exhausted working mother, not a stay-at-home one. Clarify explicitly that you're using this as a contrast: staying home would give her *more* time for care and leisure, not just describe her current overload.
The gossiping women at the grocery store: This anecdote is relatable but reported very generally. Consider quoting the actual remarks (again, currently marked ) so the reader can feel the unfairness you're describing.

Organization

The essay would benefit from clearer paragraph purposes. Right now, several paragraphs restate the same idea (that the husband should have known/agreed to this arrangement before marriage) in more than one place. Try outlining your paragraphs by function:
Thesis and reasons overview
Domestic labor has real value (housekeeper wage example)
Ownership should be legally joint (mortgage/contract point)
Personal example illustrating labor imbalance
Premarital agreement argument
Social stigma against stay-at-home wives
Conclusion

This would reduce repetition and create a more logical progression from *value of labor* → *legal/practical fairness* → *social perception* → *conclusion*.

Language and Mechanics

Since English is your second language, here are recurring patterns to watch for in editing:

Comma splices and run-ons: eg, "The husband then went on to say that he viewed the car as his because he took care of it, She then took the argument..." Use a period or semicolon instead of a comma before a new independent clause.
Apostrophe errors: "her's," "',their'," "',stay home wife"" — check apostrophe placement and quotation mark pairing throughout; some quotation marks appear reversed or doubled (eg, `,their',`).
Subject-verb agreement: "Taking care of a house just as well as taking care of an income are both sources of income" — reword for clarity and agreement (perhaps: "Taking care of a house is a form of labor just as valuable as earning an income").
Word choice: "loose" should be "lose"; "t his wife" should be "to his wife"; "fixing on an automobile" → "working on."
Fragments: "In this sense then the wife's work should be viewed..." reads awkwardly as a paragraph opener disconnected from the prior paragraph—add a transitional phrase.

I recommend reading your essay aloud, sentence by sentence, to catch run-ons and missing words—this is often more effective than silent proofreading.

Conclusion

Your closing image ("What's good for the goose isn't good for the gander") is memorable, but the conclusion could more directly restate your three main reasons and end with a forward-looking statement (eg, a call for greater legal protection or social respect).

Overall, you have a clear personal voice and real conviction—the essay just needs a tighter thesis, completed examples, reduced repetition, and careful proofreading for grammar. Revising with these points in mind will make your argument much more persuasive and polished.

Feedback Report: Marijuana Legalization Essay

Overall Impression

You've tackled a controversial and complex topic with genuine enthusiasm, and your essay contains interesting factual claims that could support a compelling argument. However, the essay currently suffers from a significant contradiction between its stated purpose and its actual execution, along with issues in organization, evidence use, and language precision. Below are the key areas to address in revision.

Purpose and Consistency (Most Important Issue)

In your introduction, you explicitly state: *"The purpose of this passage is not to convince you to legalize marijuana... Rather, its purpose is to inform you on all the facts."* This is a claim of objectivity. However, your essay is clearly persuasive—you selectively present information favoring legalization, use rhetorical questions to guide readers toward one conclusion, and end with "there can only be one logical answer."

Revision options:
If you want to write a persuasive essay, revise your thesis to openly state your position (eg, "This essay argues that marijuana should be legalized because...").
If you truly want an informative/balanced piece, you need to include counterarguments and evidence against legalization (eg, effects on youth, addiction concerns, workplace safety, arguments from public health officials who oppose legalization).

Right now, the essay reads as persuasive but claims to be neutral—this undermines your credibility with readers.

Evidence and Citations

Your essay relies heavily on statistics and quotations, which is good practice for argumentation, but several issues need attention:

Unclear sourcing: Where do the 400,000 arrests, 150,000 alcohol deaths, and other figures come from? Academic writing requires you to cite sources (author, publication, year) so readers can verify claims.
Questionable claims: The assertion that "NO ONE has ever died from marijuana" (since 4000 BC) is a strong, sweeping claim that needs a credible source—this kind of absolute statement without citation weakens your argument's credibility.
Formatting: Use a consistent citation style (MLA/APA) for your quotations from the DEA and *Psychology and You*.

Organization

Your essay follows a logical sequence (history → physical effects → mental effects → comparison to alcohol → benefits of legalization), which is a good structural instinct. However:

Each paragraph introduces new information but rarely synthesizes it back to your central argument. Add transitional sentences explaining *why* this information matters to the reader's decision.
The paragraph about crime and government revenue feels rushed—this is actually one of your strongest points and deserves fuller development with specific examples or data.
Consider adding a counterargument paragraph addressing common objections (eg, "gateway drug" theory, effects on adolescent brain development) even if you ultimately refute them. This strengthens persuasive essays significantly.

Language and Mechanics

Since you're writing in a second language, here are recurring issues to watch for in revision:

Spelling errors: "govermnent" → government; "Pgychology" → Psychology; "goverwnent" → government; "eanount" → amount; "elevate" (likely meant "alleviate" or "reduce" crime, not "elevate" which means increase)
Comma splices: "The first thing to look at when deciding about a stance on Marijuana, the history of Marijuana in the United States" is missing a verb—should be "...is the history of Marijuana in the United States."
Subject-verb agreement: "suffers of glaucoma" should be "sufferers of glaucoma"
Word choice: "elevate much of the crime" should likely be "eliminate" or "reduce"
Comma overuse: You frequently insert commas that break the flow of sentences unnecessarily (eg, "Some short term effects of smoking Marijuana include, the 'high'..."—no comma needed after "include")

Suggestion: After revising content, do a dedicated proofreading pass focused only on grammar and spelling, perhaps using a grammar-checking tool or asking a native-speaking peer to review.

Tone and Rhetorical Strategy

Phrases like "ask yourself what is to be lost and what is to be gained, and you will see there can only be one logical answer" are effective rhetorical closings for a persuasive essay, but they contradict your claim of neutrality (see point #1). Academic essays generally avoid absolutist language ("can only be one logical answer") because it dismisses legitimate opposing views without engaging them.

Priority Revisions for Next Draft

Decide your purpose: persuasive or informative? Revise your thesis accordingly.
Add proper citations for all statistics and quotes.
Include at least one counterargument with a thoughtful response.
Proofread carefully for spelling and grammar (see list above).
Strengthen transitions between paragraphs to reinforce your argument's logic.

You have good instincts for using evidence and structuring an argument—now it's about aligning your stated purpose with your actual writing, and polishing the language for clarity and credibility. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback Report: Double Standards for Women Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an important and timely topic, and your passion for gender equality comes through clearly, especially in your personal reflections near the end. However, the essay currently reads more like a series of loosely connected observations than a tightly argued essay. To strengthen this piece, you'll need to work on establishing a clear thesis, improving logical organization, and addressing several language issues that currently obscure your meaning. Below are specific areas to focus on in revision.

Thesis and Argument Clarity

Your introduction states that "society has a double standard for women," but this claim needs to be sharpened into a specific, arguable thesis. What exactly are you arguing? Possible directions include:
Double standards persist today *despite* the feminist movement's gains
Media and cultural narratives (fairy tales, advertising) perpetuate double standards for new generations
Historical justifications for inequality (religious, biological) don't hold up to scrutiny

Right now, the essay moves chronologically through history, but does not build toward a unified claim. I'd recommend choosing one clear argument and using each paragraph to support it directly.

Important issue: Your conclusion contains a troubling contradiction. You spend the whole essay arguing that double standards are unjust, but then write, "Setting double standards in a society are like setting rules for a school, they should be adhered to." This seems to reverse your argument. Please revisit this sentence—I suspect this is a wording slip rather than your actual intended meaning, but as written it undermines your entire essay.

Use of Evidence

Several claims need support or qualification:
The Bible reference (Adam created before Eve) is presented as fact rather than as *one interpretation used historically to justify inequality*. Consider rephrasing to something like: "Some used religious texts, such as the biblical creation story, to justify men's authority over women." This is more accurate and academically careful.
Historical claims about "the early nineteen hundreds" need citations. Where did you learn about attitudes of that era? A history textbook, an article? Please cite sources.
There's a placeholder  before a quotation about "modern women's attitudes" that seems to be missing—please insert the actual quote and its citation.
Your Bill of Rights list is a strong addition, but it appears without integration—explain *why* you're including it and how it connects to your argument before or after the list, not just as a stand-alone quotation.

Organization

The essay would benefit from clearer topic sentences that state the main point of each paragraph before diving into examples. Currently, paragraphs often start with a general statement, then wander through multiple related-but-different ideas (eg, the paragraph mixing children's raising practices, dating chaperones, and cultural fairness all together). Consider breaking these into more focused paragraphs, each with one controlling idea.

Additionally, some paragraphs (like the one about media and Cinderella/Sleeping Beauty) contain excellent, specific examples—this is your strongest writing. Aim to bring this same level of specificity to your historical sections as well.

Language and Mechanics

Since English is your second language, here are some recurring issues to watch for in revision:

Missing subjects: "It stems back from the past when were strictly in the home" — missing "women" as the subject. Read your sentences aloud to check that each has a clear subject and verb.
Word choice/typos: The word "warm" appears repeatedly where you clearly mean "woman" or "women" (eg, "movement for the equality of warm," "what society deems a warm"). This looks like an autocorrect or OCR error—please proofread carefully for this.
Tense consistency: You shift between past and present tense within paragraphs discussing history (eg, "we had only one place in society" then "there was no equality"). Choose past tense consistently for historical description.
Informal phrasing: Expressions like "fine and dandy," "a little uppity," and "how fair was that?" are conversational and may not fit an academic register. If you're using irony deliberately to voice historical attitudes, consider signaling this more clearly to the reader (eg, with quotation marks and a framing sentence).
Comma splices and run-ons: Several sentences join two complete thoughts with only a comma (eg, "Women were getting restless, they no longer wanted..."). Use a period, semicolon, or coordinating conjunction instead.

Priority for Revision

Fix the contradictory concluding statement about double standards.
Develop one focused, arguable thesis and revise paragraphs to support it directly.
Replace "warm" with correct word throughout; proofread for missing subjects.
Insert the missing quotation and citation.
Add scholarly sources to support historical claims.

You have real insight and passion here—channeling it into a more focused argument will make this a much stronger essay. I'm happy to discuss any of these points further in office hours.

Feedback Report: Censorship Essay

Overall Impression

This essay tackles an important and genuinely debatable topic—book censorship in schools and libraries—and you clearly have strong personal convictions about intellectual freedom. Your research into specific court cases (*Salvail v. Nashua*, *Cary v. Board of Education*) and organizations (PAW, CEE) shows real effort to ground your argument in evidence rather than opinion alone. However, the essay needs significant revision in three areas: argumentative clarity, structural organization, and language mechanics. Below are specific suggestions.

Strengthen Your Central Argument

Your introduction poses questions ("Who has given these persons the power to ban books?") but never explicitly states your thesis. Readers must infer your position from scattered clues throughout the essay. Consider opening with a clear claim, such as: *"Censorship of literary texts in American schools and libraries violates constitutional protections of free speech and deprives students of valuable educational experiences."* Every paragraph should then visibly support this claim.

Additionally, some of your reasoning needs tightening. The claim that "if children do not read it, they will see it" (via cartoons, soap operas, or walks in the park) is an interesting point, but it needs development. Why does exposure elsewhere make censorship of *literature specifically* illogical or harmful? Right now this feels like an assertion rather than a developed argument.

Improve Structure and Paragraph Focus

Your case studies (Nashua, Arapahoe, Mesa) are your strongest material, but they are presented as a list of examples without clear analytical framing. For each case, consider structuring your paragraphs around three questions:
What happened? (the facts)
Why does it matter? (the underlying issue—First Amendment rights, lack of due process, community fear)
How does this support my thesis?

Currently, the Arapahoe paragraph ends abruptly with "nothing could be done," which is a missed opportunity to explain *why* this outcome is troubling and what it reveals about gaps in legal protection for teachers.

Your conclusion tries to do too much at once—summarizing, praising activists, discussing Maya Angelou, and quoting Vonnegut. Consider separating these into: (1) a restatement of your argument, (2) a brief closing reflection or call to action. The Vonnegut quote, in particular, seems to work against your argument (he says his job was "not to defend the First Amendment") and needs more explanation of how you're using it—otherwise it reads as a contradiction.

Citation and Evidence

You cite statistics (376 attacks, 348 demands, 44 states) and legal cases, which is excellent for building credibility. However:
You need proper in-text citations and a works-cited page, following whatever citation style (MLA is standard for literature-related essays) your course requires.
Introduce sources more smoothly. Instead of "Did you know that in the year 1991-92, the People of the American Way (PAW) reported...", try: "According to a 1991–92 report by People for the American Way (PFAW)..." (note: check the organization's correct name and acronym—you've written both "PAW" and later imply it differently).
Double-check factual details: Gutenberg's press date, Pope Leo X's condemnation of Luther, and the spelling of names (Gutenburg → Gutenberg) should be verified for accuracy.

Language and Mechanics

Since you're writing in a second language, focus your revision on these recurring issues:

Spelling/typos: "fireedom," "bum" (burn), "Attacks on the Freedom ofspeech," "curriculwn," "openingly," "baimed," "restrain" (restraint), "Steinbeck, Judy Blume" (missing comma consistency), "School of Board's" (should be "School Boards")
Punctuation: Several sentences have stray commas or missing ones (eg, "In New Hampshire,, the magazine"). Read your sentences aloud to catch these.
Verb agreement and tense: "the go as far as to bum them" should be "they go as far as to burn them"; "was allowed to ban five books" needs consistent tense with surrounding sentences.
Word choice: "provided all people with the fireedom of speech" → "guarantees all people freedom of speech." "Putting a restrain of the voices" → "Restraining the voices" or "Placing restraints on the voices."

I recommend reading your essay aloud slowly, or using a grammar-checking tool, to catch these smaller errors before submission.

Summary of Priority Revisions

Write an explicit, one-sentence thesis in your introduction.
Restructure body paragraphs to explain *why* each case matters, not just *what* happened.
Streamline your conclusion into two focused parts.
Proofread carefully for spelling, punctuation, and verb tense.
Add proper citations for all sources.

You have compelling material here—sharpening the argumentative thread and cleaning up the prose will make your passion for this topic much more persuasive. I'd be happy to discuss any of these points further during office hours.

Feedback on Your Essay: The Confederate Flag Controversy

Overall Impression

You have clearly done substantial research for this essay, gathering many quotes and sources on a complex, sensitive topic. This shows real effort. However, the essay currently reads more like a collection of quotations than an argumentative essay with a clear thesis. My feedback will focus on helping you develop a stronger argumentative structure, which is the most important area for revision.

Thesis and Argumentative Position

The biggest issue is that your argumentative position is unclear until the very last paragraph, and even there it is stated ambiguously. An argumentative essay needs a clear thesis stated early (usually in the introduction) that tells the reader what you will argue and why.

Your final paragraph suggests your position is that the flag should remain because of heritage, but this appears only after pages of quotations presenting *many* different viewpoints without your analysis connecting them to your argument. Ask yourself: What is my one main claim? Revise your introduction to state this clearly, for example: *"While the Confederate flag holds symbolic importance for many Southern descendants, this essay argues that..."*

Structure: Quotation vs. Argumentation

Currently, the essay is organized as: quote → quote → quote → quote → brief conclusion. This is a common challenge for students learning academic argumentation. In English academic writing, you must analyze and respond to each quote, not just present it. For each source, ask:
Why am I including this quote?
Does it support my argument or represent an opposing view I need to address?
What is my interpretation of what this person is saying?

For example, after Senator Campbell's quote about the American flag being linked to genocide, you need a sentence explaining *your* reasoning: Is this a fair comparison? Does it strengthen or weaken your argument? Right now, you present many opposing views (Moseley-Braun, Charles Blair, the poll data) without ever returning to engage with them critically in light of your thesis.

Balance and Selection of Evidence

You include a very wide range of voices (senators, poll respondents, historical figures, ordinary citizens). This is admirable research, but more quotes do not make a stronger argument — better analysis does. Consider:
Cutting some of the "survey" quotes (Laverne Thomson, Elena Minor, etc) and instead summarizing the range of opinion in 2-3 sentences, saving space for deeper analysis of 2-3 key sources.
The Alexander Stephens speech is a powerful historical primary source, but you don't explain its significance to your argument. Why does it matter that the Confederacy's founding document explicitly defended slavery? This needs direct discussion, especially since it complicates a "heritage, not hate" argument.

Language and Mechanics

There are frequent spelling and typographical errors that need careful proofreading:
"empathizes" (do you mean "agrees" or "sympathizes"?)
"fory percent" → forty percent
"siad," "todl," "an the Capital grounds" → said, told, on the Capitol grounds
"unacceptabe," "Sates," "Africa-Americans" → unacceptable, States, African-Americans
Inconsistent capitalization: "united Daughters of the Confederacy" should be "United Daughters"
Missing quotation marks and punctuation (check paragraph 4, which is missing a closing parenthesis)
"There is always going to be controversy for since we live in this world" — this sentence needs rewording for clarity (perhaps: "As long as people hold different values, controversy will exist.")

I recommend using spell-check and reading the essay aloud to catch these errors — many are the kind that a careful re-read will fix.

Citations

You cite sources with author and page number (eg, "Riley 30"), which is a good start, but there is no Works Cited/Reference list, and the citation style is inconsistent (sometimes just a publication name, like "The Post and Courier," rather than an author). Check with your instructor about which citation format (MLA, APA, etc) is required, and make sure every source is fully listed at the end.

Suggestions for Revision

Write a clear thesis statement in your introduction.
Reorganize body paragraphs around ideas, not sources — for example: "Arguments from Heritage," "Arguments from Racial Harm," "Public Opinion Data," and analyze each.
Add topic sentences to each paragraph that state the paragraph's purpose in relation to your thesis.
Follow every quotation with 1-2 sentences of your own analysis.
Write a stronger conclusion that returns to your thesis and explains what should be done, rather than a vague final claim.
Proofread carefully for spelling, capitalization, and punctuation.

Final Note

This is a topic with real emotional and historical weight, and you've clearly engaged with multiple perspectives — that's valuable. Your next step is to move from *reporting* what others say to *arguing* your own position using their words as support. I'd be happy to discuss thesis development further in office hours if that would help.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a timely and engaging topic—reforming the civil litigation system to reduce costs and delays. You clearly have strong opinions and some vivid, memorable examples (the inmate lawsuits are particularly effective for grabbing attention). However, the essay needs significant revision in three areas: structure, use of evidence, and language accuracy. Below are specific suggestions.

Thesis and Structure

Right now, your essay lacks a clear, explicit thesis statement. You open with a general observation about government trends, then pivot to the judicial system, but you never state directly what changes you are proposing until the final paragraph. Readers should know your argument and your proposed solutions from the introduction.

Suggestion: Add a thesis sentence near the end of your introduction, such as: "To reduce this financial and administrative burden, lawmakers should grant judges greater power to dismiss frivolous cases, require losing plaintiffs to pay court costs, and hold professionals who facilitate fraudulent claims accountable."

Your paragraphs also cover too much ground without clear topic sentences. For example, the second paragraph moves from inmate lawsuits to fraudulent car-accident staging without a transition. Each paragraph should focus on one type of frivolous lawsuit (eg, inmate suits, staged accidents, workers' compensation fraud, suits against police/schools) with a clear topic sentence and supporting details, followed by a transition to the next idea.

Evidence and Credibility

This is the most important area for revision. Your essay relies heavily on anecdotes and unverified claims ("I heard of a case last week," "seems like everybody is suing everybody"). While anecdotes can illustrate a point, they cannot carry the entire argument. Academic writing requires you to support claims with:

Statistics (eg, actual data on caseloads, average trial wait times, insurance premium increases)
Citations from credible sources (news articles, government reports, legal studies)
Attributed examples (name the case, date, and source rather than "I heard of a case")

For instance, your claim that civil cases take "three to five years" to try needs a citation. Similarly, the causal chain you build in paragraph four—frivolous lawsuits → court backlog → criminal case delays → jail overcrowding—is a serious logical claim that needs evidence at each step, not just plausible-sounding reasoning. As written, it risks looking like speculation rather than an evidence-based argument.

Tone and Bias

Words like "ridiculous," "ffivolous" (frivolous), and "begging" reveal strong personal bias. This is appropriate in casual speech but weakens academic argumentation, which should sound measured and objective even when arguing a strong position. Consider revising to more neutral, precise language: instead of "ridiculous cases," try "cases without legal merit" or "claims lacking sufficient evidence."

Additionally, a strong argumentative essay should acknowledge counterarguments. For example: some lawsuits that seem frivolous on the surface (like the inmate case about hair or clothing) may actually raise legitimate constitutional questions about civil rights in prisons. Addressing this complexity—even briefly, before refuting it—will make your argument more credible and persuasive to skeptical readers.

Language and Grammar

Because you are writing in a second language, some sentence-level issues need attention:

Article usage: English requires articles ("the," "a") more consistently than Chinese. Check phrases like "the trend seems to be moving" vs. "trend seems."
Comma splices and run-ons: Several sentences join two independent clauses with only a comma (eg, "Lawsuits seem to have become a way for a lot of people to make a living."—this one is fine, but check others like the sentence about insurance companies). Use a period, semicolon, or coordinating conjunction instead.
Verb tense consistency: You shift between present and past tense when discussing ongoing trends versus specific cases. Keep tense consistent within each type of claim.
Word choice/typos: There are several typographical errors that likely came from scanning or transcription (eg, "wn t~g," "ft~tes," "bmtes," "fiLI1"). Proofread carefully before submitting your final draft—these errors distract readers from your argument.

Conclusion

Your final paragraph functions well as a policy recommendation list, but the essay needs a true concluding paragraph that restates your thesis, briefly summarizes your main points, and ends with a strong closing statement about why this reform matters (eg, for taxpayers, for genuine victims waiting for justice, for the integrity of the legal system).

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Write a clear thesis statement.
Reorganize paragraphs around one theme each, with topic sentences.
Replace unverified anecdotes with cited evidence.
Tone down emotionally loaded language.
Add a counterargument and rebuttal.
Proofread for grammar and typographical errors.
Write a conclusion that reinforces your argument.

You have a compelling topic and genuine conviction—now it's a matter of building a more rigorous, evidence-based case around it. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback on Your Essay: The 1995 Government Shutdown

Strengths
You've chosen a compelling historical framework—bookending your essay with Jefferson's warning about partisanship gives your argument a strong conceptual anchor. You also did solid research, including specific numbers and dates that show you engaged seriously with the topic. Your instinct to explain the causes of the shutdown (the budget process, the veto exchanges, the political standoff) shows you're thinking about *mechanism*, not just describing events.

Thesis and Argument
Your core claim—that partisanship, as Jefferson warned, is directly responsible for the shutdown—is a good one, but it gets lost. Right now, it appears in the introduction, disappears for several paragraphs of historical background, and only returns explicitly in paragraph 4 ("I agree with Thomas Jefferson..."). Consider stating your thesis more directly at the end of your introduction, something like: *"The 1995 government shutdown demonstrates that Jefferson's 200-year-old warning about partisan division was not merely historical commentary but a prediction that would prove true."* Then, as you move through your narrative, periodically remind the reader how each fact supports this claim.

Organization
The essay currently reads as three loosely connected sections: (1) Jefferson/background on the fiscal process, (2) a chronological narrative of the 1995 shutdown, and (3) your personal reaction. I'd suggest restructuring around your argument rather than around chronology:
Introduction: Jefferson's quote + thesis
Background: How the budget process works (briefly—this can be condensed)
Evidence: The 1995 shutdown as a case study, told efficiently
Analysis: Why this proves Jefferson right—connect specific actions (Clinton's veto, Congress's veto, "mudslinging") explicitly back to the concept of partisan gridlock
Conclusion: Broader implications—what this means for how we should think about parties today

Several paragraphs are only one or two sentences (eg, the paragraph beginning "This temporary bill would last..."). Merge these into surrounding paragraphs.

Evidence and Sourcing
You use compelling statistics (800,000 workers, specific base layoffs), but none are cited. For an argumentative essay, especially one making historical and factual claims, you need to tell your reader *where* this information comes from (a news source, a government report, etc). This builds credibility and protects you from unintentional inaccuracy. Also double-check your facts: you say the 1962 shutdown lasted two days but don't source it, and shutdown counts/dates for U.S. government history are often disputed depending on how "shutdown" is defined—verify with a reliable source.

Academic Tone
You rely heavily on "I feel that..." to introduce your opinions. In academic argumentative writing, especially at the university level, we typically state claims directly and support them with evidence, rather than framing them as personal feelings. Compare:
*"I feel the American people have been unfairly made to pay..."*
Revision: *"The American people bore the cost of a political conflict they had no part in creating."*

This shift makes your argument sound more authoritative and less like personal opinion.

Grammar and Mechanics
This is where the most concrete revision work is needed. Some patterns to watch for:

Sentence fragments: "This statement that was made by Thomas Jefferson..." is not a complete sentence—it needs a verb (eg, "This statement *was* made by...").
Comma splices/run-ons: eg, "This event is the shutdown of the United States government and it will not end until government officials. decide to work together..." (also note the stray period before "decide").
Date formatting: Dates need consistent commas: "October 1, 1995" not "October1, 1995" or "October 1- 1962."
Apostrophes for possession: "presidents cabinet" should be "president's cabinet"; "Congress' bill" is correct, but check consistency.
Spelling: "govermnent," "figthing," "Thurrnond," "situations" (should be "situation")—run a careful spell-check pass.
Capitalization: Political party names ("federalist and democratic parties," "republicans," "democrats") should be capitalized: Federalist, Democratic, Republican, Democrat.
Subject-verb agreement: "government officials... decide" vs. singular/plural consistency throughout.

Next Steps
Write a clear one-sentence thesis and place it at the end of your introduction.
Reorganize paragraphs so each one has a clear purpose tied to your argument.
Add citations for all statistics and historical claims.
Do a full grammar/proofreading pass, focusing especially on fragments, run-ons, and possessives.
Revise "I feel" statements into direct, evidence-supported claims.

You have real command of the subject matter and a strong argumentative instinct—the revision work now is mostly about *structure* and *precision*. I'd be happy to look at a revised draft.

Feedback Report: Argumentative Essay on Abortion

Overall Impression

You've chosen a genuinely difficult and important topic, and your essay shows real conviction. You attempt to support your position with multiple types of evidence—statistics, legal precedent, economic reasoning, and moral argument. This range shows good instinct for building a case. However, the essay currently reads as a list of loosely connected claims rather than a unified argument. Below are specific areas to strengthen for your revision.

Thesis and Structure

Your thesis—"Abortion is the right of the people and it should remain so"—is clear, but the paragraphs that follow don't build on each other logically. Right now, each paragraph introduces a new, separate reason (rape, poverty, legal precedent, safety, cost) without transitions connecting them or ranking them by importance. Consider:

Grouping related arguments (eg, safety/practical consequences together; rights-based arguments together)
Adding transitional sentences ("In addition to the danger to women's safety, there is also a strong legal precedent...")
Ending with your strongest argument, not scattering your points evenly

Evidence and Credibility

This is the most urgent area for revision. Several factual errors will seriously damage your credibility with an academic reader:

"Roe versus Way" should be *Roe v. Wade* (1973). This is a foundational case—getting the name wrong undermines your authority on the topic.
The statistic "one in four women experience rape" needs a citation. Where does this come from? Academic writing requires sourced evidence, not general claims.
The claim that "lower income families have five children on average" also needs a source—this seems like an overstatement and could be seen as reinforcing a stereotype about poverty rather than a supported fact.
The $125,000 cost-per-child figure needs a citation and clarification (Is this a national average? From what country/year?).

Action step: Go back and find actual sources (government data, peer-reviewed studies, or reputable news organizations) for every statistic. Cite them properly.

Logical Weaknesses to Address

Some arguments, as currently written, could be challenged easily by an opposing reader:

The poverty argument ("abortion helps maintain a balance between lower and upper income families") could be read as suggesting society should reduce the *number* of poor children rather than address poverty itself. This argument needs more careful framing, or it may seem to devalue the lives of children born into poverty.
The "slippery slope" argument about rights ("if they remove this right, who's to say they won't remove others") is a recognized logical fallacy in formal argumentation. It can still be used, but you should acknowledge its rhetorical nature and perhaps replace it with a more direct rights-based philosophical claim (eg, referencing bodily autonomy).

Addressing Counterarguments

A strong argumentative essay directly engages with the *opposing* view rather than only asserting your own. Currently, you assume your reader agrees rape victims deserve special consideration, that legal precedent should stand, etc—but you never address common objections from the pro-life perspective (eg, concerns about fetal personhood, adoption alternatives, moral objections). Adding even one paragraph that raises a counterargument and then responds to it will make your essay far more persuasive and sophisticated.

Language and Mechanics

Since English is your second language, focus your proofreading on these recurring issues:

Spelling/typos: "Hapeo povertyg" (unclear—please revise this sentence, as I cannot tell what you intended), "eightteen," "sky racket alsop" (likely "sky-rocket also")
Subject-verb agreement: "Would be able to love him" → "Would *she* be able to love him"
Word choice confusion: "force" vs. "forced," "Rowe" vs. "Roe"
Punctuation: Check comma usage, especially before "because" and after introductory phrases
Sentence fragments: Some sentences lack clear subjects or verbs

Suggestion: After revising content, read your essay aloud slowly, or use a grammar-checking tool, to catch these mechanical errors before submission.

Priority Action Items for Revision

Fix the Roe v. Wade error and verify all statistics with real sources
Reorganize paragraphs with clearer transitions and logical grouping
Add one counterargument and your response to it
Proofread carefully for spelling, grammar, and word choice
Reconsider the poverty argument's framing so it doesn't inadvertently suggest poor children are less valuable

You have the beginnings of a passionate, multi-faceted argument. With stronger evidence and tighter organization, this essay could be quite persuasive. I'd be happy to discuss any of these points further in office hours.
